GEOMETRY OF MECHANICS Thanks: e - mail : narciso.roman@upc.edu / ORCID: 0000-0003-3663-9861.
Miguel C. Muñoz-Lecanda

Abstract




Abstract

The aim of this work is to study
the geometry underlying mechanics and its application
to describe autonomous and nonautonomous conservative dynamical systems of different types; as well as dissipative dynamical systems.
We use different geometric descriptions to study
the main properties and characteristics of these systems;
such as their Lagrangian, Hamiltonian and unified formalisms,
their symmetries, the variational principles, and others.
The study is done mainly for the regular case,
although some comments and explanations about singular systems are also included.

Keywords:

Symplectic manifolds, cosymplectic manifolds, Hamiltonian systems, Riemannian manifolds, Newtonian systems, contact manifolds, dissipative systems, Lagrangian formalism, Hamiltonian formalism, symmetries, conservation laws, actions of Lie groups, fiber bundles, variational principles.

MSC 2020 codes:

Primary: 53B20, 53D05, 53D10, 70H03, 70H05, 70H33.

Secondary: 53B21, 53D20, 53D22, 53Z05, 58A30, 70A05, 70F20, 70F25, 70G10, 70G45, 70G65, 70G75, 70H15, 70H20, 70H25.

Chapter 1
Introduction

The study of mechanics experienced a substantial advance during
the 18th and 19th centuries with the emergence of what was called

Analytical
or
Rational Mechanics
.
Many relevant mathematicians contributed to its development,
such as
L. Euler, W.R. Hamilton, C.G.J. Jacobi, J.L. Lagrange, A.M. Legendre, S.D. Poisson
, among others.
Their techniques are essentially based in the application of variational methods
to obtain the equations of motion of dynamical systems, and their subsequent development;
and are widely collected in a lot of classical treaties, such as

[
161
,
165
,
180
,
225
,
234
,
237
,
361
]

or, in a more modern perspective,
[
12
,
47
,
99
,
148
,
222
,
246
,
318
,
332
,
333
,
364
]
,
among many others.

Although the dynamical equations of Analytical Mechanics had been well-stablished
previously by
J.L. Lagrange
and others,
the connection between the variational methods and Mechanics was done
by the
minimum action principles

[
33
,
34
,
159
,
173
,
234
,
246
]
. Thus, the so-called

Hamilton principle
leads to the Euler–Lagrange equations
and the so-called
Lagrangian formalism
of mechanics.
Introducing the
Legendre transformation
and the “momentum coordinates”
the dynamical equations become the
Hamilton equations
,
originating in this way the
canonical
or
Hamiltonian formalism

of Mechanics. As in the Lagrangian case, these Hamiltonian equations
can also be derived from the so-called
Hamilton–Jacobi variational principle
.

In the second half of the 20th century,
an extensive group of mathematicians and physicists used
the techniques of differential geometry to formalize analytical mechanics
and physics in general,
and studied the properties of dynamical systems intrinsically,
giving rise to what is known today as
geometric mechanics
.
Among the most relevant books and contributions, we can cite

[
1
,
2
,
11
,
69
,
91
,
178
,
208
,
209
,
217
,
218
,
227
,
247
,
249
,
250
,
255
,
265
,
291
,
303
,
318
,
327
,
358
]
,
in addition to many others.

In the geometric formulation,
differentiable manifolds that model the phase spaces of dynamical systems
are endowed with different kinds of geometric structures,
which are used to study the properties of these systems.
Thus, for
conservative systems
,
the
symplectic manifolds
are used to describe

autonomous
or
time-independent
dynamical systems

[
1
,
7
,
11
,
91
,
138
,
247
,
249
,
250
,
265
,
276
,
327
,
346
,
347
,
358
]
;
meanwhile for the
nonautonomous
or
time-dependent
case
also other types of manifolds can be considered ,
such as
jet bundles
and
contact
and
cosymplectic manifolds

[
1
,
7
,
54
,
89
,
106
,
109
,
154
,
255
,
275
,
317
]
.
In particular, for variational dynamical systems;
that is, those of
Lagrangian type
,
their phase spaces have the geometric structure of a
tangent bundle
[
105
,
108
,
164
,
178
,
227
,
337
,
338
]
,
or a cotangent bundle in the dual canonical Hamiltonian formalism

[
1
,
11
,
91
,
249
,
337
,
339
]
.
These formalisms can also be described in a single unified description

[
322
,
323
]
.
Furthermore, for those dynamical systems which are called of
mechanical type
, their configuration spaces are also endowed
with a (semi)Riemannian metric
[
1
,
11
,
46
,
91
,
179
,
291
]

which is used to construct their Lagrangian functions in a canonical way.
Finally, symplectic mechanics has also been used to describe
nonholonomic systems
;
that is, systems subjected to constraints depending on positions and velocities,

[
11
,
100
,
127
,
131
,
165
,
248
,
282
,
344
]
;
and also
vakonomic systems

which consists in considering those systems but modifying the variational principle
using only the variations allowed by the constraints

[
11
,
34
,
159
,
273
]
.

In addition, there are other more general geometric structures
which are used to model some special types of dynamical systems.
These are
Poisson and Jacobi manifolds

[
249
,
256
]
or also

Lie algebroids

[
78
,
130
,
188
,
192
,
270
,
272
,
360
]
.
Finally,
dissipative
or
nonconservative dynamical systems
are modelled using
contact manifolds

[
38
,
40
,
93
,
114
,
117
,
141
,
169
,
224
,
235
]
.
In all these cases, there are deep relations between the differential equations
describing the dynamics and the underlying geometric structures.

Another very important fact that characterize the behaviour of dynamical systems is
the existence of symmetries.
The interest of studying symmetries arises as a consequence of the well known fact
that their existence is closely related to that of conserved quantities,
and the main result comes from the work of
Emmy Noether

[
283
]

(see
[
229
]
for a review of her results).
On turn, the existence of conserved quantities
facilitates the integration of the dynamical equations,
applying the suitable reduction procedures.
Concerning to this, it is interesting to mention the fundamental
Arnold-Liouville Theorem

for
integrable systems

[
11
]

(see also
[
19
,
277
]
), and also those by

J.E. Marsden
and
A. Weinstein
on the problem of the
(symplectic) reduction
by symmetries

[
266
]
(see also
[
263
,
265
,
267
,
292
,
293
,
294
]
and the references therein).

The physical systems for which these geometric approaches were initially developed
had the characteristic of being
regular

1
1

1

For Lagrangian systems, this means, locally, that the Hessian matrix with respect to the generalized velocities is regular everywhere.
.
The dynamical systems in classical analytical mechanics are of these kind.
Nevertheless, with the advent of Relativity Theory and the
development of field theories in modern physics, in general,
many systems appear for which that property does not hold; that is,
they are
singular systems
.
For autonomous systems, this feature of being regular or not manifests in the fact that
the underlying geometric structure is

symplectic
or
presymplectic
, respectively

[
60
,
63
,
72
,
73
,
184
,
185
,
186
,
196
,
258
,
280
,
281
,
324
]
.

The aim of this work is to do a detailed exposition of
the geometry underlying dynamical systems and how it is applied
to describe the different kinds of them: conservative autonomous and nonautonomous dynamical systems in general, and Newtonian systems in particular; as well as dissipative systems.
We use different geometric descriptions to study
the main properties and characteristics of these systems;
such as their Lagrangian, Hamiltonian and unified formalisms,
their symmetries, the variational principles, and others.
The study is done mainly for the regular case,
although comments and explanations about singular systems
are also introduced throughout the exposition and in some proposed problems.

The organization of the work is the following:

The first chapter of the exposition, Chapter
2
, is devoted
to present the most general geometric framework for autonomous mechanics;
that is,
symplectic manifolds
and their structures and characteristics
(
Poisson brackets, symplectomorphisms,
etc.),
as well as the fundamental notion of
Hamiltonian dynamical system
.
A general description of
symmetries, conserved quantities
and the

Noether theorem
is also done for this general situation.

In Chapter
3
, we continue the exposition about
symplectic mechanics

studying, in particular, autonomous mechanical systems of variational type
which are characterized by being described by means of time-independent Lagrangian functions.
The phase spaces of these kinds of systems are represented by tangent
and cotangent bundles of manifolds representing
the configuration spaces of the systems; so the canonical geometric structures
of these bundles are introduced first.
Starting from the Lagrangian function and using the canonical structures of the tangent bundle,
we can define a (pre)symplectic form and the
Legendre map

which allow us to establish the Lagrangian formalism,
the associated canonical Hamiltonian formalism for these systems,
and the equivalence between both formalisms.
We also state the foundations of a new geometric setting of the
Hamilton–Jacobi theory

for Hamiltonian systems.
In addition, we also give a description of the so-called Skinner-Rusk unified formalism,
which combines in a single framework the two previous formalisms.
The study of symmetries, conserved quantities and Noether’s theorem is
also performed for Lagrangian systems in both formalisms,
which lead to introduce some new types of symmetries.
As these dynamical systems are of variational type,
a section is devoted to introduce the variational formulation
and derive the dynamical equations from the corresponding variational principle.
Finally, two of the most classic examples in mechanics,
the
harmonic oscillator
and the
Kepler problem
,
are analyzed.

Chapter
4
is devoted to develop one of the most interesting
and generic geometric formulations for describing

nonautonomous dynamical systems
,
both the Lagrangian and Hamiltonian formalisms.
It is based in using
cosymplectic manifolds
,
which are presented in the first section of this chapter.
Then, the dynamics and symmetries for these time-dependent systems are studied
and the formalism is used to describe the classical examples introduced in the previous chapter
when external time-dependent forces act on the oscillator
or for variable mass systems in the case of the Kepler problem.
In this chapter we also give a brief presentation
of two other very common formulations of nonautonomous mechanics;
namely, the
contact
and the
extended symplectic formulations
,
and showing their equivalence with the cosymplectic picture.

Next, Chapter
5

deals with the study of those dynamical systems whose configuration spaces are
endowed with additional geometric structures, such as a
metric
.
First, we review the foundations of connections in manifolds and Riemannian geometry,
which are the geometric structures needed to develop what is called
Newtonian mechanics
.
In particular, the existence of a
metric allows us to construct what are known as

mechanical Lagrangians
. We analyze
different types of them; namely,
conservative
and
coupled systems
, and
systems with
holonomic
and
nonholonomic constraints
;
stating their dynamics and variational formulation.
As a particular situation, the case of
(nonautonomous) Newtonian systems
is
displayed along this exposition.

The last chapter, Chapter
6
, is an introduction to the study of
autonomous dissipative systems
,
using the
contact geometry
.
After reviewing the foundations on contact manifolds,
we establish the generic concepts about
contact Hamiltonian systems
.
and develop the Lagrangian, Hamiltonian and unified Lagrangian-Hamiltonian formalisms for these kinds of systems.
Next, we study symmetries and the concept of
dissipated quantities
for contact Hamiltonian and Lagrangian systems;
establishing the so-called “dissipation theorems”,
and showing how to associate dissipated and conserved quantities to these symmetries.
As in the above chapters, the examples of the damped harmonic oscillator and the Kepler problem with friction
are analyzed in this context.

The work ends with an appendix where various contents on other
geometric structures that appear throughout the exposition are collected;
in particular, tangent and cotangent bundles, and Lie groups and Lie algebras.

This work is intended for readers who have completed at least
the first courses of a degree on mathematics or physics,
and who have a basic training on differential geometry of smooth manifolds and analytical mechanics.

Along the exposition, all manifolds are supposed to be real, second-countable and
{\rm C}^{\infty}
.
All the maps and the structures are smooth.
The summation criterion for repeated crossed indices is adopted.

Chapter 2
Symplectic mechanics (I): Autonomous Hamiltonian dynamical systems

The general geometric framework for describing autonomous mechanical systems (with a finite number of degrees of freedom)
uses some particular types of differentiable manifolds to model the phase spaces of these systems,
they are the
symplectic
and
presymplectic manifolds
.
This formulation is known as
symplectic mechanics
. Taking these kinds of manifolds as phase spaces,
we have a very general setting to study dynamical systems,
from which other descriptions such as the Lagrangian and the Hamiltonian formalisms of Lagrangian systems can be analyzed as particular situations

1
1

1

This general formulation allows us to describe also
dynamical systems which are not of Lagrangian type,
for instance, the system of a classical spin particle
[
327
]
.
.

The symplectic description of (autonomous) Hamiltonian systems has been exposed in many works and books
(see, for instance,
[
1
,
11
,
138
,
209
,
231
,
249
,
250
,
265
,
327
,
358
]
and the references quoted therein).

In this chapter, after reviewing the fundamental concepts on symplectic geometry,
we present the symplectic description of the autonomous Hamiltonian systems
and we introduce several types of symmetries and their associated conserved quantities
from a geometric perspective.

2.1
Notions on symplectic and presymplectic geometry

In this section, we state the fundamental concepts and properties
of symplectic (and presymplectic) manifolds
(see, for instance,
[
1
,
3
,
50
,
208
,
230
,
249
,
358
]
).

2.1.1
Symplectic and presymplectic vector spaces

Let
E
be a finite dimensional real vector space.

Definition 2.1

A
symplectic inner product
, or a

linear symplectic structure
, on
E
is a non–degenerate skew symmetric bilinear function
\omega
on
E
.

We say that the pair
(\textbf{E},\omega)
is a
symplectic vector space
.

Non degeneracy means that, if
\omega(\bf{x},\bf{y})=0
, for every
\bf{y}\in\textbf{E}
, then
\mathbf{x}=0
.
Let
\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{r}
be a basis of
E
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}^{1},\ldots,\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}^{r}
its dual basis. If
\omega_{ij}=\omega(\mathbf{e}_{i},\mathbf{e}_{j})
, then the expression of
\omega
on these bases is

\omega=\omega_{ij}\,\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}^{i}\otimes\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}^{j}\ ,

being
(\omega)=(\omega_{ij})
the matrix of
\omega
in these bases.

The linear mapping
\omega^{\flat}:\textbf{E}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\textbf{E}^{*}
is defined by

(\omega^{\flat}(\mathbf{x}))(\mathbf{y})=\langle\omega^{\flat}(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{y}\rangle=\omega(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\ ,

and its matrix relative to these bases is
(\omega_{ij})
,
since
\omega(\mathbf{e}_{i})=\omega_{ij}\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}^{j}
.
By the skew symmetry of the bilinear mapping
\omega
, we have that the matrix
(\omega_{ij})
is skew symmetric. The non degeneracy gives us that the mapping
\omega^{\flat}
is one–to–one, hence an isomorphism, and
(\omega_{ij})
is regular.
As a consequence, the dimension of
E
is even, that is
r=2n
, because
(\omega)=-(\omega)^{t}
(the transpose matrix),
and
\det(\omega)=\det(\omega)^{t}=(-1)^{r}\det(\omega)
.

A basis
\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{n},\mathbf{a}_{n+1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{2n}
of
E
is called
symplectic
if

\begin{matrix}\omega(\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{a}_{j})=0\ ;&i,j=1,\ldots,n\ ;\\
\omega(\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{a}_{j})=0\ ;&i,j=n+1,\ldots,2n\\
\omega(\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{a}_{n+j})=\delta_{ij}\ ;&i,j=1,\ldots,n\ .\end{matrix}\ .

The existence of symplectic basis is given by the following Lemma:

Lemma 2.2

Let
({\bf E},\omega)
be a
2n
dimensional symplectic vector space.

1.

There exists a symplectic basis
({\bf e}_{k})
(
k=1,\ldots,2n
) on
{\bf E}
.

2.

If
(\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}^{k})
is the corresponding dual basis on
{\bf E}^{*}
,
then

\displaystyle\omega=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}^{i}\wedge\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}^{i+n}
or,
what is the same, the matrix of
\omega
in this basis is

\left(\begin{matrix}0_{n}&I_{n}\\
-I_{n}&0_{n}\end{matrix}\right)

where
I_{n}
denotes the identity matrix of order
n
and
0_{n}
the zero square matrix
n\times n
.

(
Proof
)
By induction on the dimension of
{\bf E}
. Let
\mathbf{e}_{1},\mathbf{e}_{n+1}\in{\bf E}
with
\omega(\mathbf{e}_{1},\mathbf{e}_{n+1})=\hskip-9.95845pt/\hskip 5.69054pt0
. We can choose two such vectors unless
\omega=0
. Dividing
\mathbf{e}_{1}
by a scalar, we have that
\omega(\mathbf{e}_{1},\mathbf{e}_{n+1})=1
. Then, on the plane
{\bf P}_{1}
spanned by
\mathbf{e}_{1},\mathbf{e}_{n+1}
the matrix of
\omega
is

\left(\begin{matrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{matrix}\right)\,.

Let
{\bf E}_{1}
the
\omega
–orthogonal complement of
{\bf P}_{1}
in
{\bf E}
, that is:

{\bf E}_{1}=\{{\bf a}\in{\bf E}\,|\,\omega({\bf a},{\bf a}_{1})=0,\forall{\bf a}_{1}\in{\bf P}_{1}\}\ .

Observe that
{\bf E}_{1}\cap{\bf P}_{1}=\{0\}
and
{\bf E}_{1}+{\bf P}_{1}={\bf E}
. If
\mathbf{a}\in{\bf E}
, we have

\mathbf{a}-\omega({\bf a},{\bf e}_{n+1}){\bf e}_{1}+\omega({\bf a},{\bf e}_{1}){\bf e}_{n+1}\in{\bf E}_{1}\ .

Then,
{\bf E}_{1}\oplus{\bf P}_{1}={\bf E}
and we can repeat the process on
{\bf E}_{1}
, with dimension less that
2n
, and we have finished the proof.

The result on the expression of
\omega
as
\displaystyle\omega=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}^{i}\wedge\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}^{i+n}
is immediate.

Remark 2.3

•

The non degeneracy condition is equivalent to say that
\omega^{n}=\omega\overbrace{\wedge\ldots\wedge}^{n}\omega\equiv\wedge^{n}\omega
is a volume form on
{\bf E}
.

•

If we take out the non degeneracy condition, then the mapping
\omega^{\flat}
is not one–to–one. In this case, we need to add the basis
\mathbf{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{h}
of
\ker\omega^{\flat}
to the given basis for the decomposition of
{\bf E}
. Then the corresponding matrix is,

\left(\begin{matrix}0_{n}&I_{n}&0_{n\times h}\\
-I_{n}&0_{n}&0_{n\times h}\\
0_{h\times n}&0_{h\times n}&0_{h}\end{matrix}\right)

being
2n+h
the dimension of
{\bf E}
and denoting by
0_{n\times h}
the zero matrix of
n
rows and
h
columns. We say that
2n
is the rank of
\omega
.

2.1.2
Subspaces of a symplectic linear space

Associated to a linear symplectic structure we have a notion of orthogonality in parallel to the ideas related to the Euclidean scalar product, but the results are very different. In the next lines, we develop these ideas that are necessary to understand our later description of Lagrangian and Hamiltonian systems.

Let
({\bf E},\omega)
be a
2n
-dimensional symplectic vector space and
{\bf F}\subset{\bf E}
a linear subspace. The
\omega
–orthogonal complement
of
{\bf F}
is defined as

{\bf F}^{\bot}=\{{\bf u}\in{\bf E}\ |\ \omega({\bf u},{\bf u}^{\prime})=0\,,\,\mathrm{for\,\,every}\,\,{\bf u}^{\prime}\in{\bf F}\}.

Observe that, in general,
{\bf F}\cap{\bf F}^{\bot}=\hskip-9.95845pt/\hskip 5.69054pt0
(for example, if
{\bf F}=\mathrm{span}\,\{{\bf u}\}
, then
{\bf F}\subset{\bf F}^{\bot}
).

Definition 2.4

Let
{\bf F}
be a subspace of a linear symplectic
2n
–dimensional vector space
({\bf E},\omega)
.

1.

{\bf F}
is
isotropic
if
{\bf F}\subset{\bf F}^{\bot}
; that is,
\omega({\bf u},{\bf u}^{\prime})=0
, for every
{\bf u},{\bf u}^{\prime}\in{\bf F}
.

2.

{\bf F}
is
coisotropic
if
{\bf F}\supset{\bf F}^{\bot}
; that is, if

\omega({\bf u},{\bf u}^{\prime})=0
, for every
{\bf u}^{\prime}\in{\bf F}
. Then
{\bf u}\in{\bf F}
.

3.

{\bf F}
is
Lagrangian
if
{\bf F}
is isotropic and there exists an isotropic complement
{\bf F}^{\prime}
; that is, an isotropic subspace
{\bf F}^{\prime}\subset{\bf E}
such that
{\bf F}\oplus{\bf F}^{\prime}={\bf E}
.

4.

{\bf F}
is
symplectic
if
\omega
restricted to
{\bf F}
is non degenerated; that is,
({\bf F},\omega_{\bf F}=\left.\omega\right|_{\bf F})
is a symplectic vector space.

These notions have several associated properties that we resume in the following two propositions, with some proofs and indications.

Proposition 2.5

Let
({\bf E},\omega)
be a symplectic vector space and

{\bf F},{\bf G}
subspaces of
{\bf E}
.

1.

If
{\bf F}\subset{\bf G}
, then
{\bf F}^{\bot}\supset{\bf G}^{\bot}
.

2.

{\bf F}^{\bot}\cap{\bf G}^{\bot}=({\bf F}+{\bf G})^{\bot}
.

3.

\dim{\bf E}=\dim{\bf F}+\dim{\bf F}^{\bot}
.

4.

{\bf F}={\bf F}^{\bot\bot}
.

5.

({\bf F}\cap{\bf G})^{\bot}={\bf F}^{\bot}+{\bf G}^{\bot}
.

(
Proof
)

3
.

Consider the linear map
\omega^{\flat}\colon\textbf{E}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\textbf{E}^{*}
.
Observe that, if
{\bf u}\in{\bf F}
, then
\omega^{\flat}({\bf u})\in\textbf{E}^{*}

annihilates the subspace
{\bf F}^{\bot}
. Hence, the restricted linear map

\omega_{\bf F}^{\flat}\colon\textbf{F}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\textbf{E}^{*}
induces another one

\hat{\omega}_{\bf F}^{\flat}\colon\textbf{F}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\left(\textbf{E}/{\bf F}^{\bot}\right)^{*}
.
This last map is injective, but not necessarily onto. Thus, we have

\dim{\bf F}\leq\dim\left(\textbf{E}/{\bf F}^{\bot}\right)^{*}=\dim\left(\textbf{E}/{\bf F}^{\bot}\right)=\dim\textbf{E}-\dim{\bf F}\ .

Conversely, consider the map

{\bf F}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\omega^{\flat}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\textbf{E}^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\bf F}^{*}
,
where
j
is the restriction from
E
to
{\bf F}
.
Let
\bar{\omega}_{\bf F}^{\flat}=\omega^{\flat}\circ j
and observe that

\ker\bar{\omega}_{\bf F}^{\flat}={\bf F}^{\bot}
. Hence,

\dim{\bf F}=\dim{\bf F}^{*}\geq\dim(\mathrm{img}\,\bar{\omega}_{\bf F}^{\flat})=\dim\textbf{E}-\dim{\bf F}^{*}=\dim\textbf{E}-\dim{\bf F}\ .

From both expressions, we obtain the result we wanted.

4
.

It is clear that
{\bf F}\subset{\bf F}^{\bot\bot}
.
If we apply the previous result to
{\bf F}
and to
{\bf F}^{\bot}
we have

\dim{\bf E}=\dim{\bf F}+\dim{\bf F}^{\bot}=\dim{\bf F}^{\bot}+\dim{\bf F}^{\bot\bot}\ ;

that is,
\dim{\bf F}=\dim{\bf F}^{\bot\bot}
, hence
{\bf F}={\bf F}^{\bot\bot}
as we wanted.

5
.

From 2. and 4. we have

({\bf F}\cap{\bf G})^{\bot}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 4.}}{{=}}({\bf F}^{\bot\bot}\cap{\bf G}^{\bot\bot})^{\bot}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2.}}{{=}}({\bf F}^{\bot}\cap{\bf G}^{\bot})^{\bot\bot}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 4.}}{{=}}{\bf F}^{\bot}\cap{\bf G}^{\bot}\ .

And the next proposition gives alternative definitions for a subspace to be Lagrangian.

Proposition 2.6

Let
({\bf E},\omega)
be a symplectic vector space
({\bf E},\omega)
and
{\bf F}
a subspace of
{\bf E}
. The following statements are equivalent:

1.

{\bf F}
is Lagrangian.

2.

{\bf F}={\bf F}^{\bot}
.

3.

{\bf F}
is isotropic and
\dim{\bf F}=\frac{1}{2}\dim{\bf E}
.

(
Proof
)
(1.
\Longrightarrow
2).
As
{\bf F}
is Lagrangian it is isotropic, hence
{\bf F}\subset{\bf F}^{\bot}
, and there exists another isotropic subspace
{\bf F}^{\prime}
with
{\bf F}\oplus{\bf F}^{\prime}={\bf E}
.

Suppose now that
{\bf u}\in{\bf F}^{\bot}
and put
{\bf u}={\bf a}+{\bf b}
with
{\bf a}\in{\bf F}
,
{\bf b}\in{\bf F}^{\prime}
. We prove that
{\bf b}=0
. In fact,
{\bf b}\in{\bf F}^{\prime\bot}
since
{\bf F}^{\prime}
is isotropic. We also have that
{\bf b}={\bf u}-{\bf a}\in{\bf F}^{\bot}
since
{\bf u},{\bf a}\in{\bf F}
and
{\bf F}
is isotropic. Then

{\bf b}\in{\bf F}^{\prime\bot}\cap{\bf F}^{\bot}=({\bf F}^{\prime}+{\bf F})^{\bot}={\bf E}^{\bot}=\{0\}\ ,

because
\omega
is non–degenerate. Then we have that
{\bf u}={\bf a}\in{\bf F}
and

{\bf F}^{\bot}\subset{\bf F}
.

(2.
\Longrightarrow
3).
The hypothesis and the item 3 of the above proposition give the result.

(3.
\Longrightarrow
2).
From 3 and the above proposition we obtain that
\dim{\bf F}=\dim{\bf F}^{\bot}
and being
{\bf F}
isotropic, we have that
{\bf F}={\bf F}^{\bot}
; that is,
the statement 2.

(2.
\Longrightarrow
1).
We have
{\bf F}={\bf F}^{\bot}
, hence
{\bf F}
is isotropic. We need to construct a subspace
{\bf F}^{\prime}
.
Let
{\bf a}_{1}\notin{\bf F}
and
{\bf F}_{1}=\mathrm{span}\{{\bf a}_{1}\}
. Then

{\bf F}\cap{\bf F}_{1}=\{0\}
, hence

{\bf F}^{\bot}+{\bf F}_{1}^{\bot}={\bf F}\oplus{\bf F}_{1}^{\bot}={\bf E}
, that is
{\bf F}\oplus{\bf F}_{1}^{\bot}={\bf E}
by the above proposition.
Now let
{\bf a}_{2}\notin{\bf F}+{\bf F}_{1}
,
{\bf a}_{2}\in{\bf F}_{1}^{\bot}
. We have two alternatives:

1.

This vector
{\bf a}_{2}
does not exist. In this case
{\bf F}_{1}^{\bot}\subset{\bf F}+{\bf F}_{1}
, hence
{\bf F}+{\bf F}_{1}={\bf E}
and

{\bf F}_{1}^{\bot}=(\mathrm{span}\{{\bf a}_{1}\})^{\bot}\supset\mathrm{span}\{{\bf a}_{1}\}={\bf F}_{1}\ ,

then
{\bf F}_{1}
is isotropic and we can take
{\bf F}^{\prime}={\bf F}_{1}
.

2.

There exists such
{\bf a}_{2}
. Let

{\bf F}_{2}={\bf F}_{1}+\mathrm{span}\{{\bf a}_{2}\}
, following the same procedure as above, we have that

{\bf F}\cap{\bf F}_{2}=\{0\}
, hence

{\bf F}^{\bot}+{\bf F}_{2}^{\bot}={\bf F}\oplus{\bf F}_{2}^{\bot}={\bf E}
, that is
{\bf F}\oplus{\bf F}_{2}^{\bot}={\bf E}
. But

{\bf F}_{2}^{\bot}=(\mathrm{span}\{{\bf a}_{1},{\bf a}_{2}\})^{\bot}\supset\mathrm{span}\{{\bf a}_{1},{\bf a}_{2}\}={\bf F}_{2}\ ,

hence
{\bf F}_{2}
is isotropic and we can take
{\bf F}^{\prime}={\bf F}_{2}
.

Inductively we can continue and, as
\dim{\bf E}
is finite, we arrive to some
{\bf F}_{k}
such that
{\bf F}^{\prime}={\bf F}_{k}
and we have finished the proof.

This last proposition says that a Lagrangian subspace is a
maximal isotropic subspace
.

2.1.3
Symplectic and presymplectic manifolds

Bearing in mind the results of the above section, first we define:

Definition 2.7

(a)

Let
M
be a differentiable manifold.
A
symplectic form

2
2

2

The word
symplectic
comes from the Greek word
“
\sigma\iota\mu\pi\lambda\epsilon\kappa\tau\iota\kappa o
” which means “what unites”.
It was introduced by H. Weyl, who substituted
the Latin root of the term “complex”,
to refer to a structure of the group

{\bf Sp}(n,\mathbb{C})
.
Although the structure of the symplectic manifolds
had been implicitly considered before,
it is not until the decade of 1950 when the symplectic geometry appears
as a differentiated branch of Differential Geometry, being
A. Lichnerowicz the first in introducing the term

symplectic manifold
.

in
M
is a differential
2
-form

\Omega\in{\Omega}^{2}(M)
such that:

1.

It is closed:
{\rm d}\,\Omega=0
, (we write
\Omega\in Z^{2}(M)
).

2.

It is non-degenerated at every point of
M
; that is,
\Omega_{{\rm p}}
is a linear symplectic structure in
{\rm T}_{\rm p}M
for every
{\rm p}\in M
.

If the form
\Omega
is closed but degenerated it is said to be a

presymplectic form
, and if
\Omega
is nondegenerated but not closed
it is an
almost-symplectic form
.

(b)

A
symplectic
(resp.
presymplectic
)
manifold

is a pair
(M,\Omega)
where
M
is a differentiable manifold
and
\Omega
is a symplectic (resp. presymplectic) form.

If the symplectic (resp. presymplectic) form is exact;
that is, there exists

\Theta\in{\Omega}^{1}(M)
such that
{\rm d}\Theta=\Omega
,
then
\Omega
is an
exact symplectic
(resp.
exact presymplectic
)

form
, and
(M,\Omega)
is an

exact symplectic
(resp.
exact presymplectic
)
manifold
.
The form
\Theta
is called a
symplectic
(resp.
presymplectic
)
potential
.

Remark 2.8

•

As a consequence of the second condition of the definition, a non-degenerated differential
2
-form
can only be defined in manifolds of even dimensión,
thus we write
\dim M=2n
.

•

As, since
Poincaré’s Lemma
, every closed form is locally exact,
if
\Omega
is a symplectic or a presymplectic form, for every point

{\rm p}\in M
, there is an open neighbourhood

U\subset M
,
{\rm p}\in U
, and
\vartheta\in{\Omega}^{1}(U)
such that

\Omega\mid_{U}={\rm d}\vartheta
.
Every
1
-form
\vartheta
satisfying this condition is called a

local symplectic (
or
presymplectic) potential
.

Observe that if
\vartheta\in{\Omega}^{1}(U)

and
\vartheta^{\prime}\in{\Omega}^{1}(U^{\prime})
are two different symplectic
(or presymplectic) potentials, then
\vartheta^{\prime}=\vartheta+{\rm d}f

in
U\cap U^{\prime}
, for some
f\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(U\cap U^{\prime})
.

•

In the case of a presymplectic form, the dimension of
\ker\omega_{\rm p}^{\flat}
, for
{\rm p}\in M
, may depend on the chosen point
{\rm p}\in M
. Usually, we demand that this dimension does not depend on the point and say that the presymplectic form is
regular
.

The following theorem describes the local structure of the symplectic manifolds
[
110
]
.

Theorem 2.9

(Darboux)
Let
(M,\Omega)
be a
2n
-dimensional symplectic manifold.
For every point
{\rm p}\in M
there exists an open neighbourhood

U\subset M
,
{\rm p}\in U
,
which is the domain of a local chart
(U;x^{i},y_{i})_{i=1\ldots n}
,
such that
\Omega
has the expression

\Omega\mid_{U}={\rm d}x^{i}\wedge{\rm d}y_{i}\ .

These local charts are called
symplectic charts

and their coordinates are the
canonical coordinates
or

Darboux coordinates
of the symplectic manifold in this chart.

(
Proof
)
(This proof is taken from
[
1
]
. For other different proofs, see for example

[
44
,
108
,
143
]
).
The proof is organized in several parts:

1.

As this is a local result, we can suppose that
M
is
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
and
{\rm p}={\bf 0}
.

2.

Let
\Omega
be a symplectic form in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
and
\Omega_{0}=\Omega({\rm p})
the constant symplectic form in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
equal to
\Omega
at
{\rm p}
.

It is enough to prove that there exists a neighbourhood
U
of
{\rm p}
, and a diffeomorphism
\phi:U\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>U
such that
\phi^{*}\Omega_{0}=\Omega
on
U
. This is true since, by the above Lemma, we can choose a global coordinate system in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
such that the symplectic form
\Omega_{0}
take the expression we need.

3.

Consider the 2–form
\omega_{t}\equiv\Omega+t(\Omega_{0}-\Omega)
in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
for
t\in[0,1]
. We have that

(a)

\omega_{0}=\Omega
,
\omega_{1}=\Omega_{0}
,
{\rm d}\,\omega_{t}=0
, for every
t\in[0,1]
.

(b)

\omega_{t}({\rm p})=\Omega({\rm p})+t(\Omega_{0}({\rm p})-\Omega({\rm p}))=\Omega({\rm p})=\Omega_{0}({\rm p})
is non–degenerated.

(c)

As the interval
[0,1]
is compact, there exists an open set
U_{0}\subset U
with
{\rm p}\in U_{0}
such that
\left.\omega_{t}\right|_{U_{0}}
is non–degenerate for every
t\in[0,1]
.

(d)

Being
\Omega_{0}-\Omega
closed, we can suppose that
U_{0}
is a ball with center in
{\rm p}
such that there exists
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(U_{0})
with
\Omega_{0}-\Omega={\rm d}\,\alpha
, by Poincaré Lemma.

4.

Let
X_{t}\in\mathfrak{X}(U_{0})
be defined by
\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{t})\omega_{t}=-\alpha
. This time-dependent vector field is well-defined because
\omega_{t}
is non–degenerated. Observe that
X_{t}({\rm p})=0
since
\alpha({\rm p})=0
.

5.

Let
F_{t,s}
be the time-dependent flux of
X_{t}
, satisfying
F_{t,s}\circ F_{s,r}=F_{t,r}
and
F_{t,t}=I
, and we can consider it is defined in
U_{0}
, or reduce it if necessary. Let
F_{t}=F_{t,0}
the associated diffeomorphic flux with
F_{t+h}=F_{t+h,t}\circ F_{t,0}
. Then, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\,t}F_{t}^{*}\omega_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{h\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 1.99306pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>0}\frac{F_{t+h}^{*}\omega_{t}-F_{t}^{*}\omega_{t}}{h}+F_{t}^{*}(\Omega_{0}-\Omega)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F_{t}^{*}(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{t})\omega_{t})+F_{t}^{*}(\Omega_{0}-\Omega)=F_{t}^{*}({\rm d}\,\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{t})\omega_{t}+\Omega_{0}-\Omega)=F_{t}^{*}(0)=0\,,

that is
F_{t}^{*}\omega_{t}
is constant, hence
F_{1}^{*}\omega_{1}=F_{0}^{*}\omega_{0}
. Then,

F_{1}^{*}\Omega_{0}=F_{1}^{*}\omega_{1}=F_{0}^{*}\omega_{0}=\Omega\,,

and
F_{1}
is the diffeomorphism transforming the constant symplectic form
\Omega_{0}
into
our given symplectic form

\Omega
.

Remark 2.10

For presymplectic manifolds there is a similar result (see, for instance,
[
108
,
143
]
).

Finally, as a straightforward consequence of the definition, we have:

Proposition 2.11

Every symplectic manifold is an
oriented manifold
.

(
Proof
)
In fact, using the symplectic form we can define the volume form

\displaystyle\Omega^{n}:=\bigwedge^{n}\Omega\in{\Omega}^{2n}(M)
,
which is called the
Liouville volume form
on
M
.

Some examples of symplectic manifolds
:

1.

The cotangent bundle
{\rm T}^{*}Q
of a manifold
Q
is an example of symplectic manifold which, moreover, is the canonical model of these kinds of manifolds
(see Theorem
3.16
in the Appendix
A.1.2
for all the details). Here we do a short survey:
We have a canonical 1-form
\Theta\in\Omega^{1}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
which, in natural coordinates
(q^{i},p_{i})
of
{\rm T}^{*}Q
, is

\Theta=p_{i}{\rm d}q^{i}
. Then
\Omega=-{\rm d}\Theta={\rm d}q^{i}\wedge{\rm d}p_{i}
is a symplectic form.
Observe that the natural coordinates of
{\rm T}^{*}Q
are Darboux coordinates for
\Omega
.

2.

\mathbb{R}^{2n}
with the usual Cartesian coordinate system,
(x^{1},\ldots,x^{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})
, and the 2-form
\Omega={\rm d}x^{i}\wedge{\rm d}y_{i}
is a symplectic manifold.

3.

The 2-sphere
S^{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
as a Riemannian submanifold with the induced area 2-form is a symplectic manifold.

4.

Another relevant example which will be used later is given by the following:

Proposition 2.12

Let
(M_{1},\Omega_{1})
,
(M_{2},\Omega_{2})
be symplectic manifolds
with
\dim M_{1}=\dim M_{2}
,
and
\pi_{j}\colon M_{1}\times M_{2}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M_{j}
,
j=1,2
be the natural projections.
Then the product manifold
(M_{1}\times M_{2},\Omega_{12}=\pi_{1}^{*}\Omega_{1}-\pi_{2}^{*}\Omega_{2})

is a symplectic manifold.

2.1.4
Submanifolds of a symplectic manifold

As in the case of linear symplectic structures, in a symplectic manifold there exist some interesting kinds of submanifolds. They are associated to the idea of orthogonality in the tangent space at every point with respect to the induced linear symplectic structure at this point. Lagrangian submanifolds play an important role in the study of dynamics of Hamiltonian systems.

Definition 2.13

Let
(M,\Omega)
be a symplectic manifold and
j:L\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M
an immersion.

1.

L
is an
isotropic immersed submanifold
of
(M,\Omega)
if

{\rm T}_{\rm p}j({\rm T}_{\rm p}L)\subset{\rm T}_{j(\rm p)}M
is an isotropic subspace of

({\rm T}_{j(\rm p)}M,\Omega_{j(\rm p)})
as a linear symplectic space, for every
{\rm p}\in L
;

2.

L
is a
coisotropic immersed submanifold
of
(M,\Omega)
if

{\rm T}_{\rm p}j({\rm T}_{\rm p}L)\subset{\rm T}_{j(\rm p)}M
is a coisotropic subspace of

({\rm T}_{j(\rm p)}M,\Omega_{j(\rm p)})
as a linear symplectic space, for every
{\rm p}\in L
;

3.

L
is a
symplectic immersed submanifold
of
(M,\Omega)
if

{\rm T}_{\rm p}j({\rm T}_{\rm p}L)\subset{\rm T}_{j(\rm p)}M
is a symplectic subspace of

({\rm T}_{j(\rm p)}M,\Omega_{j(\rm p)})
as a linear symplectic space, for every
{\rm p}\in L
;

This same terminology is used for subbundles of
{\rm T}M
over submanifolds of
M
.

Definition 2.14

Let
(M,\Omega)
be a symplectic manifold and
L\subset M
a submanifold. We say that
L
is a
Lagrangian submanifold
if it is isotropic and
\dim L=(1/2)\dim M
.

Remark 2.15

•

A submanifold

j\colon L\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M
is isotropic if, and only if,
j^{*}\Omega=0
.

•

From the above study about linear Lagrangian subspaces, we have that, if
L\subset M
is Lagrangian, then
\dim L=(1/2)\dim M
and
({\rm T}_{\rm p}L)^{\bot}={\rm T}_{\rm p}L
.

Examples of Lagrangian submanifolds
:

1.

We know that
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
with elements denoted by
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
and coordinates
(x,y)=(x^{i},y_{j})
has a natural symplectic form given by
\omega={\rm d}x^{i}\wedge{\rm d}y_{i}
. Examples of Lagrangian submanifolds are the following:

L_{1}=\{(x,y)|x=0\}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptL_{2}=\{(x,y)|y=0\}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptL_{3}=\{(x,y)|x=y\}\ .

2.

Taking the cotangent bundle of a manifold
Q
, we have a symplectic manifold,
({\rm T}^{*}Q,\omega)
. Then Lagrangian submanifolds are the fibers of the bundle, that is
q=\mathrm{constant}
, or the section zero, that is the manifold
Q
as a submanifold of
{\rm T}^{*}Q
.

3.

In the symplectic manifold
({\rm T}^{*}Q,\omega)
, let
\alpha:Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}^{*}Q
be a differential form and
N=\{(q,p)\in{\rm T}^{*}Q\ |\ p=\alpha(q)\}
the graph of
\alpha
. Then
N
is a Lagrangian submanifold of
{\rm T}^{*}Q
if, and only if,
\alpha
is a closed form. In fact, we have

{\rm d}\alpha={\rm d}(\alpha^{*}\theta)=\alpha^{*}{\rm d}\theta=-\alpha^{*}{\rm d}\omega\,,

by the properties defining the canonical forms in
{\rm T}^{*}Q
. You can see the corresponding section in the coming chapters for a detailed account of all this.

4.

As a particular case, given a function
f:Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}
, the graph of
{\rm d}f
is a Lagrangian submanifold of
{\rm T}^{*}Q
.

2.1.5
Canonical isomorphism. Hamiltonian vector fields

The fact that a symplectic form is non-degenerated
has important consequences. One of the main ones is the following:
every differential form
\Omega\in{\Omega}^{k}(M)

defines a linear map

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\flat_{\Omega}&\colon&{\rm T}M&\longrightarrow&\wedge^{k-1}{\rm T}^{*}M\\
&&({\rm p},X_{\rm p})&\mapsto&({\rm p},\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\rm p})\Omega_{\rm p})\end{array}\ ,

and its natural extension (which we denote with the same notation)

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\flat_{\Omega}&\colon&\mathfrak{X}(M)&\longrightarrow&{\Omega}^{k-1}(M)\\
&&X&\mapsto&\mathop{i}\nolimits(X)\Omega\end{array}\ .

The inverse of this isomorphism is denoted
\sharp_{\Omega}:=\flat_{\Omega}^{-1}
.

Given a differentiable manifold
M

and a form
\Omega\in{\Omega}^{2}(M)
;
it is obvious that
\Omega
is non-degenerated (symplectic) if, and only if,

\flat_{\Omega}
is an isomorphism between
{\rm T}M
and
{\rm T}^{*}M
. Then:

Definition 2.16

If
(M,\Omega)
is a symplectic manifold, the map

\flat_{\Omega}
is called the
canonical isomorphism
induced by

\Omega
.

Given a symplectic manifold
(M,\Omega)
,
every function
f\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
has associated a unique
vector field
X_{f}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
by means of the map

\sharp_{\Omega}\circ{\rm d}\colon{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)\mathrel{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits^{{\rm d}}}{\Omega}^{1}(M)\mathrel{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits^{\sharp_{\Omega}}}\mathfrak{X}(M)\ ;

that is, defined as
X_{f}:=\sharp_{\Omega}({\rm d}f)

or, what is equivalent, given implicitly by

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f})\Omega:={\rm d}f\ .

(2.1)

Remark 2.17

Observe that the map
\sharp_{\Omega}\circ{\rm d}
is not surjective.
This means that, although the canonical isomorphism allows us to
associate to every vector field
X
a differential
1
-form

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X)\Omega
,
it is not always possible to associate a function since,
in order to do this, the form would be exact necessarily;
but that form is not even closed, in general.
Neither is the map
\sharp_{\Omega}\circ{\rm d}
injective,
since functions differing in an additive constant have
the same vector field associated by this map.

Bearing in mind this comment, we define:

Definition 2.18

Let
(M,\Omega)
be a symplectic manifold. A vector field
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
is a

(global) Hamiltonian vector field
if

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X)\Omega
is an exact form.
In this case, the function
f\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)

such that
\mathop{i}\nolimits(X)\Omega={\rm d}f

is called a
(global) Hamiltonian function

of the vector field
X
.

The set of global Hamiltonian vector fields in
M
is denoted
\mathfrak{X}_{H}(M)
.

Observe that, taking into account the comment before Equation
(
2.1
), every function
f\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)

is a Hamiltonian function of a global Hamiltonian vector field
X_{f}
.

Nevertheless, the requirement in this definition is too restrictive
and, for the physical interest, it is sufficient to demand that:

Definition 2.19

Let
(M,\Omega)
be a symplectic manifold. A vector field

X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
is a
local Hamiltonian vector field
if

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X)\Omega
is a closed form.

In this case, for every point
{\rm p}\in M
, Poincaré’s Lemma assures
the existence of a neighbourhood
U\subset M
,
{\rm p}\in U
, and a function

f\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(U)
such that
\mathop{i}\nolimits(X)\Omega={\rm d}f
, in
U
.
This function is called a
local Hamiltonian function

of the vector field
X
, in
U
.

The set of local Hamiltonian vector fields in
M
is denoted
\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M)
.

Remark 2.20

•

Obviously
\mathfrak{X}_{H}(M)\subset\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M)
.
Thus, all we state for local Hamiltonian vector fields
holds also for global Hamiltonian vector fields.

•

The above definitions are also valid for presymplectic manifolds.
The difference is that, in this case, the map
\flat_{\Omega}

is not an isomorphism because it is not injective, thus not exhaustive, and, hence,
not every function in the manifold is associated to a Hamiltonian vector field.

Remember that a curve
c\colon I\subseteq\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M
is an
integral curve

of a vector field
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
if
\dot{c}(t)=(X\circ c)(t)
, for
t\in I
;
where
\dot{c}(t)
denotes de
derivative
of
c
at
t

(i.e, the
tangent vector
of the curve at
c(t)
).
Let
\widetilde{c}\colon I\subseteq\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}M
the canonical lift of
c

to the tangent bundle
{\rm T}M
; that is,
\widetilde{c}(t)=(c(t),\dot{c}(t))
,
for
t\in I
(see Definition
A.10
).
Then, bearing in mind these definitions and equation (
2.11
),
it is immediate to prove that:

Theorem 2.21

A vector field
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
in a symplectic manifold
(M,\Omega)
, is the (local) Hamiltonian vector field corresponding to the function
f\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
, that is
X=X_{f}
, if, and only if,
the integral curves
c\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M
of
X
are the solutions to the equation

\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{c})(\Omega\circ c)={\rm d}f\circ c\ ;

(2.2)

Equation (
2.2
) is the
Hamilton equation
for the
integral curves of
X
.

Remember that equation (
2.2
) is a straightforward consequence of equation (
2.1
),
of the definitions of integral curve of a vector field, and of the contraction

\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{c})(\Omega\circ c)

(see Remark
A.11
).

Local expressions
:
If
(U;x^{i},y_{i})
is a symplectic chart, we have that

\displaystyle X_{f}\mid_{U}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x^{i}}}+B_{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{y_{i}}}\ ,

\displaystyle{\rm d}f\mid_{U}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{x^{i}}}\,{\rm d}x^{i}+\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{y_{i}}}\,{\rm d}y_{i}\ ,

then, for
X_{f}
, the solution to equation (
2.1
), we have that

0=(\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f})\Omega-{\rm d}f)\mid_{U}=\left(-B_{i}-\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{x^{i}}}\right){\rm d}x^{i}+\left(A^{i}-\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{y_{i}}}\right){\rm d}y_{i}\ ;

that is,

A^{i}=\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{y_{i}}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptB_{i}=-\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{x^{i}}}\ ,

(2.3)

and then

X_{f}\mid_{U}=\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{y_{i}}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x^{i}}}-\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{x^{i}}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{y_{i}}}\ ,

(2.4)

therefore, the integral curves
c(t)=(x^{i}(t),y_{i}(t))
of
X_{f}
are the solutions to the system of
first-order differential equations

\frac{dy_{i}}{dt}=-\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{x^{i}}}(c(t))\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dx^{i}}{dt}=\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{y_{i}}}(c(t))\ .

(2.5)

Equations (
2.3
) and (
2.5
) are the local expression of equations
(
2.1
) and (
2.2
) respectively, and are called the
Hamilton equations

of the (local) Hamiltonian vector field
X_{f}
and its integral curves, respectively.

An important result which is later used is:

Lemma 2.22

Let
(M,\Omega)
be a symplectic manifold.
For every point
{\rm p}\in M
, there exist vector fields
X_{j}\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M)
,
j=1,\ldots,2n
,
such that
\{X_{j}({\rm p})\}
is a basis of
{\rm T}_{\rm p}M
.

(
Proof
)
It is immediate using symplectic charts,
since the local coordinate vector fields

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x^{i}}},\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{y_{i}}}

are locally Hamiltonian vector fields associated to the Hamiltonian functions

y_{i}
and
-x^{i}
, respectively.

This means that local Hamiltonian vector field expand locally the tangent bundle of
M
.

2.1.6
Invariant forms

The properties of Hamiltonian vector field are closely related with the properties
of the symplectic form.
Originally, this relationship was established by studying the so-called
integral invariants
of mechanics
[
79
,
150
,
165
]
.
Next, we explore this relation.
First, we introduce the following concept:

Definition 2.23

Let
M
be a differentiable manifold and
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
.
A form
\beta\in{\Omega}^{p}(M)
is an
absolute invariant form

for
X
if
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X)\beta=0
.

Remark 2.24

Reminding the interpretation of the Lie derivative,
to be an absolute invariant form for
X
means that

\beta
is invariant along the integral curves of
X
,
and, if
F_{t}
denotes the flux of the vector field
X
,
this is equivalent to demand that
F_{t}^{*}\beta=\beta
for every
t
.

Now we can state the following result, which is usually taken
as an alternative definition of Hamiltonian vector field:

Theorem 2.25

Let
(M,\Omega)
be a symplectic (resp. presymplectic) manifold.
The vector field
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
is a
local Hamiltonian vector field if, and only if,

\Omega
is an absolute invariant form for
X
.

(
Proof
)
As
\Omega
is a closed form, we have that

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X)\Omega=\mathop{i}\nolimits(X){\rm d}\Omega+{\rm d}\mathop{i}\nolimits(X)\Omega={\rm d}\mathop{i}\nolimits(X)\Omega=0\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \mathop{i}\nolimits(X)\Omega\in Z^{1}(M)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ X\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M)\ .

Remark 2.26

This result relates the Hamiltonian vector fields with the fact that
\Omega

is closed, although it is less precise than Definition
2.19
,
since it does not allow us to distinguish the global Hamiltonian vector fields inside the set of the local Hamiltonian ones.

From this theorem we deduce:

Theorem 2.27

(Liouville)
:
Let
(M,\Omega)
be a symplectic manifold
and
\Omega^{n}
the Liouville volume form in
M
.
Then
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X)\Omega^{n}=0
, for every
X\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M)
.

(
Proof
)
It is immediate since, as the Lie derivative is a derivation,

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X)\Omega^{n}=n\,(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X)\Omega)\wedge\overbrace{\Omega\wedge\ldots\wedge\Omega}^{(n-1\ times)}=0\ .

Proposition 2.28

Let
(M,\Omega)
be a symplectic (resp. presymplectic) manifold.
The set
\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M)
is closed for the Lie bracket of vector fields
and it is a real Lie algebra.

(
Proof
)
We have to prove that
[X,Y]\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M)
, for every
X,Y\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M)
.
Taking into account the relation

\mathop{i}\nolimits([X,Y])\Omega=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X)\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\Omega-\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X)\Omega

and Theorem
2.25
, we have that, if
\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\Omega|_{U}={\rm d}f
, on an open set
U
, we have

\mathop{i}\nolimits([X,Y])\Omega=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X)\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\Omega|_{U}=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X){\rm d}f={\rm d}\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X)f\ ,

that is, in
U
, the local Hamiltonian function for
[X,Y]
is
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X)f
.

Linearity, skew symmetry and the Jacobi identity of the Lie bracket
complete the proof.

So, given a symplectic manifold

(M,\Omega)
, we have proven that the symplectic and the Liouville volume forms are invariant
by the local Hamiltonian vector fields; that is,
by the groups of local diffeomorphisms generated by their fluxes.
Actually, there is a more general property stating that some geometrical structures,
such as those defined by a volume or a symplectic
form on a differentiable manifold,
are determined by their automorphism groups
(i.e.; the groups of volume-preserving and symplectic diffeomorphisms), as it was shown by
A. Banyaga

[
16
,
17
,
18
]
.

Now we can try to characterize all the elements in

{\Omega}^{k}(M)
with the above property.
The answer to this problem was established by

Lee Hwa Chung

[
243
]
, who studied the uniqueness
of the
integral invariant forms
by local transformations generated
by the flux of the local Hamiltonian vector fields.
Next, we state the geometrical version of this theorem and prove a partial result of it

3
3

3

The original proof of this theorem is local, using Darboux coordinates.
.

Theorem 2.29

(Lee Hwa Chung)
.
Let
(M,\Omega)
be a symplectic manifold
and
\alpha\in{\Omega}^{k}(M)
an absolute invariant form for
every
X\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M)
. Then:

1.

If
k
is odd, that is
k=2r-1
with
r\in\mathbb{N}
, then

\alpha=0
.

2.

If
k
is even, that is
k=2r
with
r\in\mathbb{N}
, then

\displaystyle\alpha=c\,\overbrace{\Omega\wedge\ldots\wedge\Omega}^{(r\ times)}\equiv c\,\bigwedge^{r}\Omega
,
where
c\in\mathbb{R}
.

(
Proof
)
We prove the statement for the case
k\leq 2
, which is the only we need later
(for the proof of the general case, see
[
252
]
).

As
\alpha
is invariant under the action of every
X\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M)
, we have that

0=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X)\alpha={\rm d}\mathop{i}\nolimits(X)\alpha+\mathop{i}\nolimits(X){\rm d}\alpha\hskip 10.00002pt\Longleftrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt{\rm d}\mathop{i}\nolimits(X)\alpha=-\mathop{i}\nolimits(X){\rm d}\alpha\ .

(2.6)

Consider now
X,X^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M)
and
{\rm p}\in M
; there exist

U\subset M
,
{\rm p}\in U
, and
f,g\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(U)
such that

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X)\Omega|_{U}={\rm d}f
and
\mathop{i}\nolimits(X^{\prime})\Omega|_{U}={\rm d}g

(from now on we write
X|_{U}\equiv X_{f}
y

X^{\prime}|_{U}\equiv X_{g}
).
The vector field
X_{h}
given in
U
as
X_{h}|_{U}=fX_{g}+gX_{f}
, is locally Hamiltonian and, in
U
, its local Hamiltonian function is
h=fg\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(U)
, since:

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{h})\Omega|_{U}=\mathop{i}\nolimits(fX_{g}+gX_{f})\Omega=f\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{g})\Omega+g\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f})\Omega=f{\rm d}g+g{\rm d}f\equiv{\rm d}h\ .

Thus we have

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{h})\alpha|_{U}=f\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{g})\alpha+g\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f})\alpha\ ,

and taking the exterior differential

{\rm d}\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{h})\alpha|_{U}={\rm d}f\wedge\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{g})\alpha+f{\rm d}\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{g})\alpha+{\rm d}g\wedge\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f})\alpha+g{\rm d}\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f})\alpha\ .

But, having in mind (
2.6
),

{\rm d}\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{h})\alpha=-\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f}h){\rm d}\alpha|_{U}=-f\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{g}){\rm d}\alpha-g\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f}){\rm d}\alpha=f{\rm d}\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{g})\alpha+g{\rm d}\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f})\alpha\ ,

and comparing the last two equations, we conclude that

({\rm d}f\wedge\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{g})\alpha+{\rm d}g\wedge\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f})\alpha)|_{U}=0\ .

(2.7)

Putting now
X_{f}=X_{g}
in this expression,
that is,
f=g
, we obtain that, for every
f\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(U)
,

{\rm d}f\wedge\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f})\alpha|_{U}=0\ .

Now, we have two options:

1.

If
k=1
, then
\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f})\alpha\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
,
and this last equality leads to
\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f})\alpha=0
,
for every
X_{f}\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M)
.
Taking into account that, by Lemma
2.22
,
local Hamiltonian vector fields span locally
{\rm T}M
,
we obtain that
\mathop{i}\nolimits(X)\alpha|_{U}=0
,
for every
X\in\mathfrak{X}(U)
, and this implies necessarily that

\alpha|_{U}=0
(for every
U
) and then
\alpha=0
.

2.

If
k=2
, we have to conclude:

•

either
\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f})\alpha=0
,

•

or
\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f})\alpha|_{U}=\eta_{X_{f}}{\rm d}f
,
where
\eta_{X_{f}}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(U)
.

In the first case, reasoning as in the above item,
we conclude that
\alpha=0
.
In the second case, going to the expression (
2.7
),
we obtain

({\rm d}f\wedge{\rm d}g\,\eta_{X_{g}}+{\rm d}g\wedge{\rm d}f\,\eta_{X_{f}})|_{U}=0\ ;

that is

({\rm d}f\wedge{\rm d}g)(\eta_{X_{g}}-\eta_{X_{f}})|_{U}=0\ ,

for every
f,g\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(U)
. Then it must be

\eta_{X_{f}}=\eta_{X_{g}}\equiv\eta
;
that is, the function
\eta
does not depend on the
local Hamiltonian vector field.

Therefore, for every
X_{f}\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M)
, we have that

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f})\alpha|_{U}=\eta\,{\rm d}f
, with
\eta\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
, then

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f})\alpha|_{U}=\eta\,{\rm d}f=\eta\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f})\Omega=\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f})(\eta\Omega)\ ;

but, taking into account Lemma
2.22
,
this equality leads to

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X)(\alpha-\eta\Omega)|_{U}=0\ ,

for every
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
, and we conclude that

\alpha=\eta\,\Omega
.
Finally, as
\alpha
is invariant for every local
Hamiltonian vector field, we have that, for every
Y\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M)
,

0=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\alpha=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)(\eta\Omega)=(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\eta)\Omega+\eta\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\Omega=(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\eta)\Omega\ ,

then
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\eta=0
and, by Lemma
2.22
, this result holds for every
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
;
therefore
\eta=c
(constant) and thus

\alpha=c\,\Omega
.

Remark 2.30

•

So that, the absolute invariant forms for every
local Hamiltonian vector field are multiple
of exterior products of the symplectic form and hence
they are of degree even necessarily. The above proof is for 2-forms only.

•

A similar result can be proved also for presymplectic manifolds
[
181
]

(See also
[
152
]
for another interesting generalization of this theorem).

2.1.7
Poisson brackets

The symplectic form allows us to introduce
certain well known operations in Analytical Mechanics in a natural way.

Definition 2.31

Let
(M,\Omega)
be a symplectic manifold.
The
Lagrange bracket
of two vector fields
X,Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)

is the bilinear map

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}(\ ,\ )&\colon&\mathfrak{X}(M)\times\mathfrak{X}(M)&\longrightarrow&{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)\\
&&X,Y&\mapsto&(X,Y)\end{array}

defined by

(X,Y):=\Omega(X,Y):=\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\mathop{i}\nolimits(X)\Omega\ .

Remark 2.32

•

This bracket is not an internal operation in
\mathfrak{X}(M)
, as is the
Lie bracket
,
whose result is another vector field.

•

From the skew symmetry of
\Omega
we deduce immediately that the Lagrange bracket is also skew symmetric; that is,

(X,Y)=-(Y,X)
.

Taking into account (
2.1
), from this concept we obtain:

Definition 2.33

Let
(M,\Omega)
be a symplectic manifold.
The
Poisson bracket
of two functions
f,g\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)

is the Lagrange bracket of their associated Hamiltonian vector fields,
that is, the bilinear map

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\{\ ,\ \}&\colon&{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)\times{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)&\longrightarrow&{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)\\
&&f,g&\mapsto&\{f,g\}\end{array}

defined by

\{f,g\}:=\Omega(X_{f},X_{g}):=\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{g})\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f})\Omega\ .

Local expressions
:
If
(U;x^{i},y_{i})
is a symplectic chart and

X\mid_{U}=A^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x^{i}}}+B_{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{y_{i}}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptY\mid_{U}=C^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x^{i}}}+D_{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{y_{i}}}\ ,

then

(X,Y)\mid_{U}=-B_{i}C^{i}+A^{i}D_{i}\ .

Furthermore,

\{f,g\}\mid_{U}=\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{x^{i}}}\frac{\partial{g}}{\partial{y_{i}}}-\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{y_{i}}}\frac{\partial{g}}{\partial{x^{i}}}\ .

In particular, for the canonical coordinates
x^{i},y_{i}
we have that

\{x^{i},x^{j}\}=0\ ,\ \{y_{i},y_{j}\}=0\ ,\ \{x^{i},y_{j}\}=\delta^{i}_{j}\ .

The main properties of the Poisson bracket are collected in the following:

Proposition 2.34

Let
(M,\Omega)
be a symplectic manifold and
\{\,,\,\}
the associate Poisson bracket. Then

1.

\{f,g\}=-\{g,f\}
(
skew symmetry
).

2.

\{f,\{g,h\}\}+\{g,\{h,f\}\}+\{h,\{f,g\}\}=0
(
Jacobi identity
).

3.

\{f,g\}=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{g})f=-\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{f})g

4.

X_{\{f,g\}}=[X_{g},X_{f}]
.

(
Proof
)

1.

Immediate from the definition.

2.

It is a consequence of
\Omega
being closed.

3.

Bearing in mind the Cartan formula for the Lie derivative:

\{f,g\}=\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{g})\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f})\Omega=\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{g}){\rm d}f=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{g})f\ .

In an analogous way, we have that

\{f,g\}=-\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{f})g
.

4.

The statement is equivalent to
\mathop{i}\nolimits([X_{g},X_{f}])\Omega={\rm d}\{f,g\}
,
and remembering Proposition
2.28
we obtain

\mathop{i}\nolimits([X_{g},X_{f}])\Omega=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{g})\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f})\Omega=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{g}){\rm d}f={\rm d}\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{g})f={\rm d}\{f,g\}\ .

(2.8)

Remark 2.35

•

The first two properties establish that

{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
with the Poisson bracket is a real Lie algebra.

•

The third property allows us to give a geometric interpretation
of the Poisson bracket between two functions: it measures the variation of one of them along
the integral curves of the Hamiltonian vector field associated to the other.

•

The fourth property tells us that the map
{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathfrak{X}(M)
given by
f\mapsto X_{f}
, is a Lie algebra
(anti)–homomorphism between
({\rm C}^{\infty}(M),\{\,\,,\,\})
and
(\mathfrak{X}(M),[\,\,,\,])
.

Using again the canonical isomorphism,
and taking into account (
2.8
),
we can establish the following generalization:

Definition 2.36

Let
(M,\Omega)
be a symplectic manifold.
The
Poisson bracket
of two
1
-forms

\alpha,\beta\in{\Omega}^{1}(M)

is the bilinear map

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\{\ ,\ \}&\colon&{\Omega}^{1}(M)\times{\Omega}^{1}(M)&\longrightarrow&{\Omega}^{1}(M)\\
&&\alpha,\beta&\mapsto&\{\alpha,\beta\}\end{array}\ ,

defined by

\{\alpha,\beta\}:=\mathop{i}\nolimits([X_{\alpha},X_{\beta}])\Omega\ ,

where
X_{\alpha}=\sharp_{\Omega}(\alpha)

and
X_{\beta}=\sharp_{\Omega}(\beta)
.

It is evident that:

Proposition 2.37

Let
(M,\Omega)
be a symplectic manifold. Then,
for every
f,g\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
,

{\rm d}\{f,g\}=-\{{\rm d}f,{\rm d}g\}\ .

The properties of the Poisson bracket of
1
-forms
are obviously analogous to those of the Poisson bracket of functions.

2.1.8
Canonical transformations and symplectomorphisms

In the previous section we have seen how the properties
of the symplectic structure allow us to introduce the concept of
local Hamiltonian vector field and how the
integral curves of these fields are obtained
as solutions to the Hamilton equations.
We will see also that this kind of vector fields is suitable
to describe dynamical systems.
This means that there is a deep relation between the dynamics
of physical systems and the geometric properties of
their phase spaces.

In this way, from a dynamical perspective,
it is reasonable to suppose that the more relevant transformations
among dynamical systems are those preserving the dynamical equations
which, in our case, means geometrically
that they transform Hamiltonian vector fields into Hamiltonian vector fields.
Consequently, we define:

Definition 2.38

Let
(M_{1},\Omega_{1})
and
(M_{2},\Omega_{2})
be
symplectic manifolds and
\Phi:M_{1}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M_{2}
a diffeomorphism.
We say that

\Phi
is a
canonical transformation

if it maps local Hamiltonian vector fields into
local Hamiltonian vector fields biunivocally; that is,

\Phi_{*}(\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M_{1}))=\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M_{2})
.

Concerning the geometrical aspects,
the more interesting transformations between symplectic manifolds
are the following:

Definition 2.39

Let
(M_{1},\Omega_{1})
and
(M_{2},\Omega_{2})
be
symplectic manifolds and
\Phi:M_{1}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M_{2}
a diffeomorphism.
We say that
\Phi
is a
symplectomorphism

(or also a
symplectic transformation
)
if it preserves their symplectic structures;
that is,
\Phi^{*}\Omega_{2}=\Omega_{1}
.

As Hamiltonian vector fields are defined using the symplectic form,
we can expect that there is some relation between both kinds of transformations.
In fact, Lee Hwa Chung’s Theorem allows us to prove that
both concepts are essentially the same:

Theorem 2.40

Let
(M_{1},\Omega_{1})
and
(M_{2},\Omega_{2})
be
symplectic manifolds
and
\Phi\in{\rm Diff}\,(M_{1},M_{2})
.
The necessary and sufficient condition for
\Phi

to be a canonical transformation is that

\Phi^{*}\Omega_{2}=c\Omega_{1}
, with
c\in{\bf R}
.

(
Proof
) Suppose that
\Phi
is a canonical transformation. For every

X_{1}\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M_{1})
, we have that

X_{2}=\Phi_{*}X_{1}\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M_{2})
and,
as we know from Theorem
2.25
,

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{2})\Omega_{2}=0
, hence

0=\Phi^{*}(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{2})\Omega_{2})=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(\Phi_{*}^{-1}X_{2})(\Phi^{*}\Omega_{2})=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{1})(\Phi^{*}\Omega_{2})\ .

But this means that
\Phi^{*}\Omega_{2}
is invariant by any element of
\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M_{1})
and, from Lee Hwa Chung’s Theorem,
we conclude that

\Phi^{*}\Omega_{2}=c\Omega_{1}
,
c\in{\bf R}
and
\Phi
is a symplectomorphism.

Conversely, suppose that
\Phi
is a symplectomorphism,
\Phi^{*}\Omega_{2}=c\Omega_{1}
. Given
X_{1}\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M_{1})
we have that
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{1})\Omega_{1}=0
. Then

0=\Phi^{*^{-1}}(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{1})\Omega_{1})=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(\Phi_{*}X_{1})(\Phi^{*^{-1}}\Omega_{1})=\frac{1}{c}\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(\Phi_{*}X_{1})\Omega_{2}\ ;

hence, by Theorem
2.25
,
\Phi_{*}X_{1}\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M_{2})
, that is
\Omega_{2}
is invariant by every element of
\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M_{2})
,
and
\Phi
is a canonical transformation.

Remark 2.41

•

The constant
c
that appears in this last theorem
is called the
valence
of the canonical transformation.
It is usual to consider transformations with
c=1

(that is, symplectomorphisms),
and they are called
univalent
or
restricted canonical transformations
.
Another terminology is also used, calling canonical transformations
to those with valence
c=1
, and then call

generalized canonical transformations
to the rest
[
313
]
.

•

Observe that a diffeomorphism is an
univalent canonical transformation

if, and only if, it is a
symplectomorphism
.

•

All these definitions and properties are also valid for
presymplectic manifolds. In this case we talk about

presymplectomorphisms

(the study of this case is done in
[
28
,
63
,
64
]
).

Another fundamental result is:

Proposition 2.42

Let
(M,\Omega)
be a symplectic (resp. presymplectic) manifold.
A vector field
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
is a local Hamiltonian vector field
if, and only if, its flux is a group of
local symplectomorphisms (resp. local presymplectomorphisms).

(
Proof
) Let
F_{t}
be the flux of a vector field
X
. We know that
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X)\Omega=0
is equivalent to
F_{t}^{*}\Omega=\Omega
, hence the result follows directly.

Finally, it is easy to prove that:

Proposition 2.43

The set of canonical transformations of a symplectic (resp. presymplectic) manifold

(M,\Omega)
, with the operation of composition, is a group.

The group of the symplectomorphisms
of a symplectic manifold is denoted by
{\bf Sp}(M,\Omega)
,
and it has a crucial relevance in the study of
symmetries
of dynamical systems.

2.1.9
Characterization of canonical transformations

The last theorem has some important corollaries, which give
alternative characterizations for a transformation to be canonical
(or a symplectomorphism).
The most important of them uses the Poisson bracket of functions.
Previously, we have to specify how the Hamiltonian functions associated to
Hamiltonian vector fields are transformed under
these kinds of transformations.

Proposition 2.44

Let
(M_{1},\Omega_{1})
and
(M_{2},\Omega_{2})
be
symplectic manifolds
and
\Phi\in{\rm Diff}\,(M_{1},M_{2})

a canonical transformation of valence
c
.
If
X_{1}\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M_{1})
, let
X_{2}:=\Phi_{*}X_{1}\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M_{2})
,
and
h_{1}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(U_{1})
and
h_{2}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(U_{2})

local Hamiltonian functions of
X_{1}
and
X_{2}

on
U_{1}\subset M_{1}
and
U_{2}:=\Phi(U_{1})\subset M_{2}
,
respectively. Then

ch_{1}=\Phi^{*}h_{2}+k\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptk\in{\bf R}\ .

(
Proof
)
Following the previous theorem, we have that

\displaystyle{\rm d}(\Phi^{*^{-1}}h_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Phi^{*^{-1}}{\rm d}h_{1}=\Phi^{*^{-1}}(\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{1})\Omega_{1})\mid_{U_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(\Phi_{*}X_{1})(\Phi^{*^{-1}}\Omega_{1})\mid_{\Phi(U_{1})}=\frac{1}{c}\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{2})\Omega_{2}\mid_{U_{2}}=\frac{1}{c}{\rm d}h_{2}={\rm d}\Big(\frac{1}{c}h_{2}\Big)\,,

that is

{\rm d}(ch_{1})={\rm d}(\Phi^{*}h_{2})
, and the result follows.

Bearing this in mind, we state:

Theorem 2.45

Let
(M_{1},\Omega_{1})
and
(M_{2},\Omega_{2})
be
symplectic manifolds. A diffeomorphism

\Phi:M_{1}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M_{2}
is a canonical transformation of valence
c

if, and only if

\Phi^{*}\{f_{2},g_{2}\}=\frac{1}{c}\{\Phi^{*}f_{2},\Phi^{*}g_{2}\}\ .

for every
f_{2},g_{2}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M_{2})
,

(
Proof
)
Let
f_{2},g_{2}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M_{2})
and

X_{f_{2}},X_{g_{2}}\in\mathfrak{X}_{h}(M_{2})
be the corresponding Hamiltonian vector fields.

If
\Phi
is a canonical transformation, then
\Phi_{*}^{-1}X_{g_{2}}\in\mathfrak{X}_{h}(M_{1})

and, by the above proposition we know that

\mathop{i}\nolimits(\Phi_{*}^{-1}X_{g_{2}})\Omega_{1}={\rm d}(\frac{1}{c}\Phi^{*}g_{2})
;
that is,
\Phi_{*}^{-1}X_{g_{2}}=X_{\frac{1}{c}\Phi^{*}g_{2}}
,
and then

\Phi^{*}\{f_{2},g_{2}\}=\Phi^{*}(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{g_{2}})f_{2})=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(\Phi_{*}^{-1}X_{g_{2}})\Phi^{*}f_{2}=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{\frac{1}{c}\Phi^{*}g_{2}})\Phi^{*}f_{2}=\frac{1}{c}\{\Phi^{*}f_{2},\Phi^{*}g_{2}\}\ .

Conversely, if the condición holds, first we have that,
given
f_{2},g_{2}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M_{2})
,

\Phi^{*}\{f_{2},g_{2}\}=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(\Phi_{*}^{-1}X_{g_{2}})\Phi^{*}f_{2}\ ,

and, furthermore,

\frac{1}{c}\{\Phi^{*}f_{2},\Phi^{*}g_{2}\}=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{\frac{1}{c}\Phi^{*}g_{2}})\Phi^{*}f_{2}\ ;

hence, combining these two last equalities,

\Phi_{*}^{-1}X_{g_{2}}=X_{\frac{1}{c}\Phi^{*}g_{2}}\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M_{1})\ ;\ \forall X_{g_{2}}\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M_{2})\ .

Therefore,
\Phi
is a canonical transformation
