with valence
c
as a consequence of Lee Hwa Chung’s Theorem.

Remark 2.46

This result states that a transformation is canonical
if, and only if,
the Poisson bracket is invariant, up to a multiplicative constant, by its action.
Really, this is an alternative way to say that
the symplectic structure is invariant by the transformation.

From the local point of view, if
\Phi:M_{1}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M_{2}
is a canonical transformation with valence
c=1
, and we have Darboux coordinates

(x^{i},y_{i})
in
M_{1}
and
(\tilde{x}^{i},\tilde{y}_{i})
in
M_{2}
, we have

\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}\{\Phi^{*}\tilde{x}^{i},\Phi^{*}\tilde{x}^{j}\}&=&\Phi^{*}\{\tilde{x}^{i},\tilde{x}^{j}\}\ ,\\
\{\Phi^{*}\tilde{y}_{i},\Phi^{*}\tilde{y}_{j}\}&=&\Phi^{*}\{\tilde{y}_{i},\tilde{y}_{j}\}\ ,\\
\{\Phi^{*}\tilde{y}_{i},\Phi^{*}\tilde{y}_{j}\}&=&\Phi^{*}\{\tilde{y}_{i},\tilde{y}_{j}\}\,,\end{array}

that is:
(\Phi^{*}\tilde{x}^{i},\Phi^{*}\tilde{y}_{i})
is a symplectic coordinate system in
M_{1}
if, and only if,
(\tilde{x}^{i},\tilde{y}_{i})
is a symplectic coordinate system in
M_{2}
.

2.1.10
Generating functions of canonical transformations

Another characterization of canonical transformations
is by means of the so called
generating functions
.

Proposition 2.47

Let
(M_{1},\Omega_{1})
and
(M_{2},\Omega_{2})
be
symplectic manifolds and
\Phi\in{\rm Diff}\,(M_{1},M_{2})
.
Let
U_{1}\subset M_{1}
and
U_{2}:=\Phi(U_{1})\subset M_{2}
, and

\Theta_{i}\in{\Omega}^{1}(U_{i})
such that

\Omega_{i}\mid_{U_{i}}={\rm d}\Theta_{i}
, (
i=1,2
).
Then
\Phi
is
a canonical transformation, with valence
c
, if, and only if,
there exists a function
F_{1}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(U_{1})
such that

(\Phi^{*}\Theta_{2}-c\Theta_{1}-{\rm d}F_{1})\mid_{U_{1}}=0\ ,

or, equivalently, that there exists a function
F_{2}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(U_{2})

such that

(\Phi^{*^{-1}}\Theta_{1}-\frac{1}{c}\Theta_{2}-{\rm d}F_{2})\mid_{U_{2}}=0\ .

These functions
F_{1},F_{2}
are called
(Poincaré) generating functions

of the canonical transformation, and the relation between them is

F_{1}=c\Phi^{*}F_{2}+k
,
k\in{\bf R}
.

(
Proof
)
From Theorem
2.40
,

\Phi
is a canonical transformation if, and only if,

0=(\Phi^{*}\Omega_{2}-c\Omega_{1})\mid_{U_{1}}={\rm d}(\Phi^{*}\Theta_{2}-\Theta_{1})\ ,

then Poincaré’s Lemma leads to the result.

The statement concerning
F_{2}
is obtained in an analogous way,
and comparing both results we arrive to the relation between these functions.

In classical texts of Mechanics (see, for example,
[
165
,
237
,
180
]
) a more general concept
of generating function, which include the above ones, is studied.
In the geometrical context, they are introduced as follows

[
1
]

(without loss of generality, we will restrict ourselves to the case
c=1
;
that is, symplectomorphisms or univalent canonical transformations).

First, taking into account Proposition
2.12
, we have that:

Proposition 2.48

Let
(M_{1},\Omega_{1})
,
(M_{2},\Omega_{2})
be symplectic manifolds
with
\dim M_{1}=\dim M_{2}
,

\Phi\colon M_{1}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M_{2}
a diffeomorphism, and

{\jmath}\colon graph\,\Phi\hookrightarrow M_{1}\times M_{2}
the natural embedding.

The map
\Phi
is a symplectomorphism if, and only if,

graph\,\Phi
is a Lagrangian submanifold of the symplectic manifold

(M_{1}\times M_{2},\Omega_{12}:=\pi_{1}^{*}\Omega_{1}-\pi_{2}^{*}\Omega_{2})
.

(
Proof
)
Remember that
Lagrangian submanifolds
of a symplectic manifold

({\cal M},\Omega)
are
maximal isotropic submanifolds

{\jmath}\colon S\hookrightarrow{\cal M}
or, equivalently,
such that
S
verify that
{\rm dim}\,S=\frac{1}{2}{\rm dim}\,{\cal M}

and
{\jmath}^{*}\Omega=0
.
Obviously
{\rm dim}(graph\,\Phi)=\frac{1}{2}{\rm dim}\,(M_{1}\times M_{2})
,
and as
\Phi^{*}\Omega_{2}=\Omega_{1}
,
the other condition holds trivially.

If
\Omega_{j}=-{\rm d}\theta_{j}
,
j=1,2
,
and
\Theta_{j}
are local symplectic potentials for
\Omega_{j}
;
being
graph\,\Phi
a Lagrangian submanifold we have

0=\jmath^{*}(\pi_{1}^{*}\Omega_{1}-\pi_{2}^{*}\Omega_{2})={\rm d}\jmath^{*}(\pi_{2}^{*}\Theta_{2}-\pi_{1}^{*}\Theta_{1})

(2.9)

which, given a point in
graph\,\Phi
, is locally equivalent to

\jmath^{*}(\pi_{2}^{*}\Theta_{2}-\pi_{1}^{*}\Theta_{1})|_{W}=-{\rm d}{\cal S}\ .

(2.10)

where
{\cal S}
is a function defined in
an open neighbourhood of the given point,
W\subset graph\,\Phi
.
This function depends on the choice of
\Theta_{1}
and
\Theta_{2}
.

Definition 2.49

{\cal S}
is called a

Weinstein generating function
of the Lagrangian submanifold
graph\,\Phi

and hence of the symplectomorphism
\Phi
.

If
(U_{1};x^{i},y_{i})
,
(U_{2};\tilde{x}^{i},\tilde{y}_{i})
are Darboux charts such that

W\subset U_{1}\times U_{2}
, local coordinates in
W
can be chosen in several ways.
This leads to six different possible choices for
{\cal S}
.
Thus, for instance, if
(W;x^{i},\tilde{x}^{i})
is a chart,
then (
2.10
) gives the symplectomorphism explicitly as

\tilde{y}_{i}{\rm d}\tilde{x}^{i}-y_{i}{\rm d}x^{i}=-{\rm d}{\cal S}(x,\tilde{x})\hskip 10.00002pt\Longleftrightarrow\ \hskip 10.00002pt\tilde{y}_{i}=-\frac{\partial{{\cal S}}}{\partial{\tilde{x}^{i}}}(x,\tilde{x})\ ,\ y_{i}=\frac{\partial{{\cal S}}}{\partial{x^{i}}}(x,\tilde{x})\ .

Of course, the
Poincaré generating functions
are two particular choices
of
Weinstein generating functions
.

2.2
Hamiltonian dynamical systems

Once the foundations of symplectic geometry are established,
we are ready to use it for describing the autonomous Hamiltonian dynamical systems.
As we will see in the next chapter, this geometric formulation includes, as particular cases,
the Lagrangian and the canonical Hamiltonian formalisms of variational dynamical systems;
that is, those which are described by Lagrangian functions.

2.2.1
Hamiltonian systems

Some of the authors who developed geometric mechanics chose an axiomatic manner in their exposition
(see, for instance,
[
327
]
).
Following their ideas, first, we state the postulates
for the geometric study of autonomous Hamiltonian dynamical systems.

The first postulate concerns the physical states:

Postulate 2.50

(First Postulate of Hamiltonian mechanics)
:
The state space, or phase space, of a dynamical system
is a differentiable manifold
M
endowed with a closed form

\Omega\in Z^{2}(M)
such that:

•

If
\Omega
is nondegenerate; that is
symplectic
,
the system is
regular
and the dimension of
M

is twice the number of degrees of freedom of the system.
In this case, every point of this manifold represents a physical state of the system.

•

If
\Omega
is degenerate, that is
presymplectic
,
the system is
singular
(and the manifold
M

is not even-dimensional necessarily).

The second Postulate refers to the
observables
,
that is, the
physical magnitudes
:

Postulate 2.51

(Second Postulate of Hamiltonian mechanics)
:
The observables or physical magnitudes of a dynamical system
are functions of
{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
.

The result of the measure of an observable
is the value that the function which represents it takes
at a point in the phase space
M

(that is, in a given state, according to the first postulate).

If, according to the Postulate
2.50
,
a (symplectic or presymplectic) manifold
(M,\Omega)

constitutes the phase space of a dynamical system,
there is a very natural way of introducing the dynamics.
Thus, we state:

Postulate 2.52

(Third Postulate of Hamiltonian mechanics)
:
The dynamics of a dynamical system
is given by a closed
1
-form
\alpha\in Z^{1}(M)

which is called the
Hamiltonian
1
-form
of the system

4
4

4

In some cases, the 2-form
\Omega
can also contain dynamical information
as it happens, for instance, in the Lagrangian formalism of the Lagrangian systems
(see Chapter
3
).
.

And, finally, the dynamical equations are stated in:

Postulate 2.53

(Fourth Postulate of Hamiltonian mechanics)
:
The dynamical trajectories of the system are the integral curves
of a vector field
X_{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
, if it exists, associated with the form
\alpha

by the map
\flat_{\Omega}
; that is,
of the vector field solution to the equation

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\alpha})\Omega=\alpha\ .

(2.11)

Then, the integral curves
c\colon I\subseteq\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M
of
X_{\alpha}
are solutions to the equation

\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{c})(\Omega\circ c)=\alpha\circ c\ .

(2.12)

The vector field
X_{\alpha}
is called a (local or global)
Hamiltonian vector field

of the Hamiltonian system, and equations (
2.11
) and (
2.12
) are the
Hamilton equation
for
X_{\alpha}

and its integral curves.

These equations can be obtained from a variational principle called
the
minimal action Principle of Hamilton–Jacobi

5
5

5

As we will see in Section
3.6.4
.
.

Then, we define:

Definition 2.54

1.

A
regular
or
symplectic Hamiltonian dynamical system

is a triple
(M,\Omega,\alpha)
,
where
(M,\Omega)
is a symplectic manifold
and
\alpha\in Z^{1}(M)
is the Hamiltonian
1
-form of the system.
If
(M,\Omega)
is a presymplectic manifold, then
(M,\Omega,\alpha)

is said to be a
singular
or
presymplectic Hamiltonian dynamical system
.

2.

By Poincaré’s Lemma, for every
m\in M
,
there exists
U\subset M
, with
m\in U
,
and
h\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(U)
, such that
\alpha\mid_{U}={\rm d}h
,
which is called a
local Hamiltonian function
of the system,
and the above mentioned triple is said to be a

local Hamiltonian system
.

If
\alpha
is an exact form, then there exist

h\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
such that
\alpha={\rm d}h
,
which is called a
global Hamiltonian function
of the system,
and the triple
(M,\Omega,h)
sis said to be a

global Hamiltonian system
.

2.2.2
Hamilton equations

Given a Hamiltonian dynamical system
(M,\Omega,\alpha)
,
the
Hamiltonian problem
posed by the system
consists in finding a vector field
X_{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)

verifying equations (
2.11
).

For the case of regular systems, we have

Proposition 2.55

If
(M,\Omega,\alpha)
is a regular Hamiltonian system,
then there exists a unique vector field
X_{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
which is a solution to equation (
2.11
)

(
Proof
)
If the system is regular,
\flat_{\Omega}
is the canonical isomorphism of
the symplectic manifold
(M,\Omega)
and
the existence and uniqueness of
X_{\alpha}
is assured.

Remark 2.56

If
(M,\Omega,\alpha)
is a presymplectic Hamiltonian system,
the Hamilton equations are not necessarily compatible everywhere on
M
and we need to look for the maximal subset of
M
where there exists solution.
This subset is obtained by a procedure called the
constraint algorithm
which, in the most favourable cases,
leads to find a
final constraint submanifold

P_{f}\hookrightarrow M
, where there are Hamiltonian vector fields
X_{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
,
tangent to
P_{f}
, which are
solutions to the Hamilton equations on
P_{f}
; although
these vector fields solution are not necessarily unique.
Functions vanishing on the final constraint submanifold are called
constraints
.

Singular (presymplectic) Hamiltonian systems and the corresponding constraint algorithms
have been widely studied in the literature. The interested reader can
see, for instance,
[
60
,
63
,
149
,
186
,
196
,
212
,
219
,
258
,
280
,
324
]

and others papers cited therein. Apart from some examples in the problems,
in this work we only work with regular Hamiltonian systems.

Local expressions
:
If
(M,\Omega,\alpha)
is a regular Hamiltonian system, in a symplectic chart
(U;x^{i},y_{i})
of
M
with
\alpha={\rm d}h
,
if
\displaystyle X|_{U}=f^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x^{i}}}+g_{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{y_{i}}}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)

then, according to (
2.4
), we have that

X_{\alpha}\mid_{U}=\frac{\partial{h}}{\partial{y_{i}}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x^{i}}}-\frac{\partial{h}}{\partial{x^{i}}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{y_{i}}}\ ,

and the integral curves
c(t)=(x^{i}(t),y_{i}(t))
of
X_{\alpha}
are the solution to the Hamilton equations (
2.2
) whose local expression is

\frac{dx^{i}}{dt}=\frac{\partial{h}}{\partial{y_{i}}}(c(t))\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dy_{i}}{dt}=-\frac{\partial{h}}{\partial{x^{i}}}(c(t))\ .

Remark 2.57

•

In a Hamiltonian dynamical system,
the physical observable represented by the Hamiltonian function
is associated with the energy of the system.

•

This presentation of dynamical systems
is called
Hamiltonian formalism of Mechanics

because the dynamical trajectories
are given by the integral curves of a Hamiltonian vector field.

2.3
Symmetries of regular Hamiltonian systems

In this section we introduce the ideas of
constants of motion
, or
conserved quantities
, and the
symmetries
of a dynamical system and the relationship between both notions. Noether’s theorem will be proved and actions of Lie group on Hamiltonian systems together with reduction theory will be also introduced and developed.

2.3.1
Preliminaries

The adequate way of doing this study
is by means of the theory of
actions of Lie groups
on
symplectic and presymplectic manifolds
(see, for instance,
[
1
,
108
,
156
,
177
,
207
,
249
,
260
,
290
]
).
Within this theory, the associated concept of
momentum map
plays a crucial role
in order to introduce conserved quantities and the subsequent

Marsden–Weinstein reduction theorem
.
Although this theorem was initially stated for regular autonomous Hamiltonian systems,
the Marsden–Weinstein technique has been applied and generalized to many different situations.
For instance, for time-dependent regular Hamiltonian systems
(with regular values of the momentum map)
in the framework of cosymplectic manifolds
[
4
]
and
for autonomous and nonautonomous Hamiltonian systems
with singular values of the momentum map
[
10
,
140
,
320
]
,
also for singular systems in the autonomous (presymplectic)
case
[
51
,
156
]
and in the nonautonomous case
[
136
,
210
,
220
]
.
Further generalizations are reduction on Poisson and Jacobi manifolds
[
61
,
193
,
253
,
264
,
284
,
285
]
,
on Lie algebroids
[
76
,
77
]
,
on cotangent bundles of Lie groups
[
268
]
,
Lagrangian reduction
[
86
,
269
]
, Euler-Poincaré reduction
[
80
,
85
]
,
Routh reduction for regular and singular Lagrangians
[
82
,
239
]
,
reduction of nonholonomic systems
[
27
,
36
,
55
,
257
]
,
reduction of optimal control systems
[
35
,
271
,
334
,
341
]
,
and in the context of Dirac structures and implicit Hamiltonian systems

[
31
,
274
]
.
Finally, although symmetries and conservation laws in field theories
have been studied in several geometric frameworks (see, for instance,

[
133
,
183
,
205
,
206
,
261
,
312
]

and the references quoted therein),
the problem of reduction by symmetries of classical field theories
has been solved only for particular situations such as Lagrangian and Poisson
reduction
[
83
]
, Euler-Poincaré reduction in principal fiber bundles

[
81
,
84
]
,
reduction in multisymplectic and poly(co)symplectic manifolds
[
144
,
146
,
158
,
262
]
,
and for discrete field theories
[
343
]
.
Of course, this list of references is far from being complete.

To develop our study we follow approximately the historical order:
we begin by introducing the basic notions about conserved quantities
and symmetries, paying particular attention to the case of

Noether symmetries
and
Noether’s theorem
. The primitive ideas were the constants of motion, the linear momentum and the angular momentum, and their relation with symmetries as global transformation of the space of positions of a system, and after that the infinitesimal symmetries and the Noether theorem. The modern approach comes from the action of Lie groups on the space of states of the system and the reduction theory, hence we review this theory, the notions of comomentum and momentum maps and the subsequent
Marsden–Weinstein reduction theorem
.
We consider only the case of regular dynamical systems
(although some results can be generalized to the singular case).

Throughout this section
(M,\Omega,\alpha)
will be
a regular Hamiltonian dynamical system and
\alpha={\rm d}h
, where the Hamiltonian function
h
is locally or globally defined.
Usually we will write
(M,\Omega,h)
.
Then,
X_{h}\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M)
denotes the dynamical vector field
solution to the system.

2.3.2
Conserved quantities (constants of motion)

Let
(M,\Omega,h)
be a regular Hamiltonian system and
X_{h}\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M)
the dynamical vector field.

The dynamical evolution of an observable which is represented
by a function
f\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
,
is the variation of this function along the integral curves

c(t)=(q^{i}(t),y_{i}(t))
of the vector field
X_{h}
; that is, is given by

\frac{d(f\circ c)(t)}{dt}=\left(\left(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{h})f\right)\circ c\right)(t)=\left(X_{h}(f)\circ c\right)(t)\ ,

and, bearing in mind the definition of Poisson bracket, it can be written as

\frac{d(f\circ c)}{dt}=\{f,h\}\circ c\ .

Or, in a symplectic chart
(U;x^{i},y_{i})
of
M
,

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{h})f)\mid_{U}=X_{h}(f)\mid_{U}=\frac{\partial{h}}{\partial{y_{i}}}\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{x^{i}}}-\frac{\partial{h}}{\partial{x^{i}}}\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{y_{i}}}\ .

Then, we define:

Definition 2.58

A function
f\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
is a
conserved quantity

or a
constant of motion
if

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{h})f=0\ ;

that is, it is invariant by the dynamical vector field.

To say that
f\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
is a conserved quantity of the
system
(M,\Omega,h)
means the following:
if
{\rm p}\in M
and
X_{h}
is the Hamiltonian vector field of the system,
let
c\colon(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M
be the integral curve of
X_{h}

with initial condition
c(0)={\rm p}
.
Then, if
f
is a conserved quantity, we have that

f(c(t))=f({\rm p})
, for every
t\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
;
that is, the image of
c
by
f
is contained in
S_{\rm p}=\{{\rm q}\in M\ \mid\ f({\rm q})=f({\rm p})\}
,
the level surface of
f
passing through
{\rm p}
.

One of the fundamental properties of the autonomous Hamiltonian dynamical systems
is the
conservation of the Hamiltonian
. Remember that for the physical systems, the Hamiltonian is the energy of the system.
Now, we can state this result in a geometric way:

Proposition 2.59

(Conservation of energy)
:
Let
(M,\Omega,h)
be a Hamiltonian system.
The (local or global) Hamiltonian function
h
is a conserved quantity.

(
Proof
)
It is immediate since, as
\Omega
is skew symmetric, we have

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{h})h=\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{h}){\rm d}h=\Omega(X_{h},X_{h})=0\ .

Remark 2.60

We have seen how the fundamental properties
of the symplectic form are essential to describe
geometrically physical systems:
the
non degeneracy
allows us to assure the existence (and uniqueness)
of the dynamical Hamiltonian vector field, and hence, to determine the dynamical evolution
of the system, and from skew symmetry and the fact to be closed
we obtain the conservation of the energy.

2.3.3
Dynamical symmetries

When talking about symmetries, it is usual to refer to the idea that
“a symmetry of a dynamical system lets invariant the solutions
to the differential equations describing the dynamics of the system”.
In this way we define:

Definition 2.61

A
dynamical symmetry
of the Hamiltonian dynamical system
(M,\Omega,h)

is a diffeomorphism
\Phi\colon M\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M
satisfying that,

\Phi_{*}X_{h}=X_{h}\ ;

that is, the dynamical vector field
X_{h}
is invariant by
\Phi
.

Observe that if
\Phi
is a dynamical symmetry, then so is
\Phi^{-1}
.

Let
c\colon(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M
be an integral curve of
X_{h}
, that is
\dot{c}=X_{h}\circ c
, then
\dot{c}=X_{h}\circ c=\Phi_{*}X_{h}\circ c
. If
X_{h}
is invariant by
\Phi
we have:

X_{h}\circ(\Phi\circ c)=\Phi_{*}X_{h}\circ\Phi\circ c={\rm T}\Phi\circ X_{h}\circ\Phi^{-1}\circ\Phi\circ c={\rm T}\Phi\circ X_{h}\circ\circ c={\rm T}\Phi\circ\dot{c}=\dot{\overline{\Phi\circ c}}\ ;

hence
\Phi
transforms integral curves of
X_{h}
into integral curves of
X_{h}
. The converse is also true; that is: if
\Phi
transforms integral curves of
X_{h}
into integral curves of
X_{h}
then
\Phi
lets invariant the vector field
X_{h}
. This is another equivalent version of a dynamical symmetry.

If the diffeomorphism
\Phi
is
locally generated by a vector field, by means of
the local group of diffeomorphisms generated by its flux,
then, the above definition leads to the infinitesimal version of symmetries:

Definition 2.62

An
infinitesimal dynamical symmetry
of a Hamiltonian system

(M,\Omega,h)
is a vector field
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
such that
the local diffeomorphisms generated by its flux
are dynamical symmetries of the system; that is,

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)X_{h}=[Y,X_{h}]=0\ .

(2.13)

Recall that this definition is equivalent to say that the flux of
Y
is made of dynamical symmetries of
X_{h}
. Thus, if
\Phi_{t}
is the flux of
Y
and
c
is an integral curve of
X_{h}
, the
\Phi_{t}\circ c
is also an integral curve of
X_{h}
.

Remark 2.63

In the above definition, sometimes the condition (
2.13
) is relaxed by setting

[Y,X_{h}]=gX_{h}\ ,\ g\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)\ ,

(2.14)

(and the original condition is recovered taking
g=0
).
This allows us to consider also as symmetries
the reparametrizations of the integral curves of the
dynamical vector field.

The infinitesimal dynamical symmetries of
(M,\Omega,h)
have a natural structure of real Lie algebra. Clearly they are closed by real linear combinations and we have the following proposition:

Proposition 2.64

If
Y_{1},Y_{2}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
are infinitesimal dynamical symmetries,
then
[Y_{1},Y_{2}]
is an infinitesimal dynamical symmetry.

(
Proof
)
Using the Jacobi identity, we have

[[Y_{1},Y_{2}],X_{h}]=[Y_{2},[X_{h},Y_{1}]]+[Y_{1},[Y_{2},X_{h}]]=0\ .

A first result relating symmetries with conserved quantities is:

Proposition 2.65

1.

Let
\Phi\colon M\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M
be a dynamical symmetry of
(M,\Omega,h)
. If
f\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)

is a constant of motion of the system,
then
\Phi^{*}f
is also a conserved quantity.

2.

Let
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
be an infinitesimal dynamical symmetry of
(M,\Omega,h)
. If
f\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
is a constant of motion of the system,
then
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)f
is also a conserved quantity.

(
Proof
)

1.

Directly we have

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{h})(\Phi^{*}f)=\Phi^{*}(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(\Phi_{*}X_{h})f)=0\ .

2.

The same proof holds taking the flux
\Phi_{t}
of
Y
as a dynamical symmetry.
Alternatively,

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{h})\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{h}){\rm d}\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)f=\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{h})\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y){\rm d}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{h}){\rm d}f-\mathop{i}\nolimits([Y,X_{h}]){\rm d}f=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{h})f=0\ .

2.3.4
Noether symmetries. Noether Theorem

In the above study of symmetries of the Hamiltonian system
(M,\Omega,\alpha={\rm d}h)
we have not used the specific structure of the system. In fact, the only element we have considered is the dynamical vector field
X_{h}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
. No mention have been made to the symplectic structure of
M
,
the Hamiltonian function
h
, or the Hamiltonian form
\alpha
. We have studied symmetries of the dynamical vector field independently of its origin.

If we consider the other elements defining the system, we arrive to different types of dynamical symmetries depending on whether they leave invariant the geometric structure;
that is, the symplectic form or the dynamical elements
(i.e., the Hamiltonian function).
For a more complete analysis of all these kinds of symmetries
and how they generate conserved quantities, see, for instance,
[
87
,
88
,
310
,
314
,
315
]
.

The following kind of symmetries has a special relevance as
generators of constants of motion:

Definition 2.66

A diffeomorphism
\Phi\in{\rm Diff}\,(M)
is a
Noether symmetry

of the Hamiltonian system
(M,\Omega,h)
if:

1.

\Phi
is a symplectomorphism in
M
: that is,
\Phi^{*}\Omega=\Omega
.

2.

\Phi
lets the dynamics invariant ; that is,
\Phi^{*}h=h
.

Remark 2.67

The second condition can also be expressed in the form

\Phi^{*}h=h+c
(with
c\in\mathbb{R}
).
This means that it transforms a Hamiltonian function into another
Hamiltonian function of the same dynamical vector field
X_{h}
.
More generically, if
\alpha={\rm d}h
is the Hamiltonian
1
-form, this is equivalent to say that
\Phi^{*}\alpha=\alpha
.

Definition 2.68

A vector field
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
is an
infinitesimal Noether symmetry

of the system if the local diffeomorphisms generated by the flux of
Y

are dynamical symmetries of the system; that is,

1.

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\Omega=0
; that is,
Y\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M)
.

2.

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)h=0
.

Remark 2.69

In the infinitesimal case, as every infinitesimal Noether symmetry

Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
is a locally Hamiltonian vector field, by the first item of the definition,
we have that, for every
{\rm p}\in M
, there exists an open set
U_{\rm p}\ni p

and
f_{Y}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(U_{\rm p})
such that
\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\Omega={\rm d}f_{Y}
, in
U_{\rm p}
. The local Hamiltonian
f
is unique, up to the sum of constant functions.

A first relevant result is:

Proposition 2.70

1.

Every Noether symmetry of
(M,\Omega,h)
is a dynamical symmetry.

2.

Every infinitesimal Noether symmetry of
(M,\Omega,h)
is an infinitesimal dynamical symmetry.

(
Proof
)

1.

Let
\Phi\in{\rm Diff}\,(M)
be a Noether symmetry. If
X_{h}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
is a solution to the dynamical equations, then

0=\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{h})\Omega-{\rm d}h
, and as

\Phi^{*}\Omega=\Omega
and
\Phi^{*}h=h
, we have

0=\Phi^{*}(\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{h})\Omega-{\rm d}h)=\mathop{i}\nolimits(\Phi_{*}^{-1}X_{h})\Phi^{*}\Omega-\Phi^{*}{\rm d}h=\mathop{i}\nolimits(\Phi_{*}^{-1}X_{h})\Omega-{\rm d}h=\mathop{i}\nolimits(\Phi_{*}^{-1}X_{h})\Omega-{\rm d}h\ ;

but as the system regular, the vector field solution is unique,
then
\Phi_{*}^{-1}X_{h}=X_{h}
, and the result holds.

2.

If
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
is an infinitesimal Noether symmetry, then

Y\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M)
and
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)h~=~0
, hence

\mathop{i}\nolimits([Y,X_{h}])\Omega=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{h})\Omega-\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{h})\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\Omega=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y){\rm d}h={\rm d}\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)h=0\ ,

but being
\Omega
nondegenerated, this implies that

[Y,X_{h}]=0
, and therefore
Y
is an infinitesimal dynamical symmetry.

Clearly, the set of infinitesimal Noether symmetries is a real vector space. Moreover, it is easy to prove that it is real Lie algebra:

Proposition 2.71

If
Y_{1},Y_{2}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
are infinitesimal Noether symmetries,
then
[Y_{1},Y_{2}]
is also an infinitesimal Noether symmetry.

(
Proof
)
First we have that

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits([Y_{1},Y_{2}])\Omega=0
, since
[Y_{1},Y_{2}]\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M)
, because
Y_{1},Y_{2}\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M)
.
Furthermore,

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits([Y_{1},Y_{2}])h

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits([Y_{1},Y_{2}]){\rm d}h=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y_{1})\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y_{2}){\rm d}h-\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y_{2})\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y_{1}){\rm d}h

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y_{1})\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y_{2})h-\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y_{2}){\rm d}\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y_{1})h=0\ .

In addition, we get:

Proposition 2.72

Let
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
be an infinitesimal Noether symmetry of
(M,\Omega,h)
.
For every
{\rm p}\in M
, there exists an open set
U_{\rm p}\ni{\rm p}
such that,
if
\vartheta\in{\Omega}^{1}(U_{\rm p})
is a symplectic potential of
\Omega

in
U_{\rm p}
, that is
{\rm d}\vartheta=\Omega
, then:

1.

There exists
\zeta_{Y}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(U_{\rm p})
verifying that

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\vartheta={\rm d}\zeta_{Y}
.

2.

If
f_{Y}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(U_{\rm p})
is a local Hamiltonian function of
Y
, then
in
U_{\rm p}
, we have that,

f_{Y}=\zeta_{Y}-\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\vartheta\hskip 20.00003pt\mbox{\it(up to the sum of a constant function)}\ .

(2.15)

(
Proof
)

1.

Observe that
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\vartheta
is a closed form in
U_{\rm p}
since

{\rm d}(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\vartheta)=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y){\rm d}\vartheta=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\Omega=0\ ,

Then, by Poincaré’ s Lemma,
there exists
\zeta_{Y}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(U_{\rm p})

such that
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\vartheta={\rm d}\zeta_{Y}
, in an open subset of
U_{\rm p}
and, reducing the initial domain if necessary, we can suppose it is in
U_{\rm p}
.

2.

If
\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\Omega={\rm d}f_{Y}
in
U_{\rm p}
, we obtain that

{\rm d}\zeta_{Y}=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\vartheta={\rm d}\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\vartheta+\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y){\rm d}\vartheta={\rm d}\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\vartheta+\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\Omega={\rm d}\{\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\vartheta+f_{Y}\}\ ,

and the result holds.

Finally, the fundamental result related with infinitesimal Noether-type symmetries
is the classical
Noether’s Theorem
, whose geometric Hamiltonian version is the following:

Theorem 2.73

(Noether)
.
If
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
is an infinitesimal Noether symmetry of a Hamiltonian system
(M,\Omega,h)
, then
its Hamiltonian function
f_{Y}

is a conserved quantity; that is,

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{h})f_{Y}=0\ .

(
Proof
)
In fact, as
\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\Omega={\rm d}f_{Y}
we have

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{h})f_{Y}=\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{h}){\rm d}f_{Y}=\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{h})\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\Omega=-\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{h})\Omega=-\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y){\rm d}h=-\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)h=0\ .

Noethers’s Theorem is very relevant, since it gives a way to associate
a constant of motion to every Noether symmetry.
The converse statement of Noether’s theorem also holds, and it
allows associating a (Noether) symmetry to every conserved quantity:

Theorem 2.74

(Inverse Noether)
:

For every conserved quantity
f\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
, its Hamiltonian vector field

Y_{f}\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M)
is an infinitesimal Noether symmetry.

(
Proof
)
As
Y_{f}\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M)
, then
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y_{f})\Omega=0
. Furthermore, as
f

is a conserved quantity, then
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{h})f=0
and therefore

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y_{f})h=\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y_{f}){\rm d}h=\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y_{f})\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{h})\Omega=-\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{h})\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y_{f})\Omega=-\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{h}){\rm d}f=-\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{h})f=0\ .

In general, for symmetries which are not of Noether-type,
there is not a so direct way of obtaining constants of motion,
except in some particular cases like in the following:

Theorem 2.75

If
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
is an infinitesimal dynamical symmetry such that

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)h\not=0
, then the function

f=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)h
is a conserved quantity.

(
Proof
)
We have that

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{h})f=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{h})\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)h=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits([X_{h},Y])h+\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{h})h=0\ .

For other kinds of symmetries, the way to obtain conserved quantities is, in general,
more complicated (see, for instance,
[
6
,
74
,
104
,
254
]
, and
[
310
]

and the references quoted therein,
where a complete classification of the symmetries of Hamiltonian systems is done).

2.3.5
Actions of Lie groups on symplectic manifolds

In this section, we analyze the transformations
(i.e., diffeomorphisms) of a dynamical system
which are generated by Lie groups.
In fact, a family of diffeomorphisms in a differentiable manifold
endowed with the operation of composition
has the structure of group and when the
variations of these transformations are considered, the set of
these variations has a smooth differentiable structure.
In this case, the corresponding group of transformations is a Lie group.
(For more information about actions of Lie groups, see,
for instance,
[
1
,
11
,
91
,
108
,
177
,
207
,
249
,
260
,
290
,
327
,
357
]

6
6

6

We would also like to highlight the work of
J.A. Lázaro-Camí
,
who compiled the main results presented in Sections 2.3.5, 2.3.6, and 2.3.7 in an unpublished note.
.
See also the appendix
A.2
for a review about Lie groups).

Definition 2.76

Let
G
be a Lie group and
M
a differentiable manifold.
A
left-action
of
G
on
M
is a map

\phi\colon G\times M\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M

verifying the following properties:

(i)

\phi(g_{1}g_{2},{\rm p})=\phi(g_{1},\phi(g_{2},{\rm p}))
;
for
g_{1},g_{2}\in G
and
{\rm p}\in M
.

(ii)

If
e\in G
denotes the neutral element, then

\phi(e,{\rm p})={\rm p}
.

A
right action
is defined in an analogous way,
changing the operation law in
G
by the opposite.
In any case,
M
is said to be a

left
G
-manifold
(resp., a
right
G
-manifold
).

Since, for every
g\in G
and
p\in M
, the map
\Phi_{g}\colon{\rm p}\mapsto\phi(g,{\rm p})
is a diffeomorphism in
M
, we have that a left-action of
G
in
M

is also a homomorphism

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\Phi&\colon&G&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&{\rm Diff}(M)\\
&&g&\mapsto&\Phi_{g}\end{array}\ .

From now on, we only consider left-actions.

Definition 2.77

let
G
be a Lie group,
M
a differentiable manifold,

\Phi
an action of
G
on
M
, and
{\rm p}\in M
.

1.

The
isotropy group
of
p
(with respect to
\Phi
) is the subgroup of
G

G_{\rm p}:=\{g\in G\ |\ \Phi_{g}({\rm p})={\rm p}\}\ .

2.

The
orbit
of
{\rm p}
(with respect to
\Phi
) is the set

{\cal O}_{\rm p}:=\{{\rm p}^{\prime}\in M\ |\ {\rm p}^{\prime}=\Phi_{g}({\rm p})\ ,\ \mbox{\rm for every $g\in G$}\}

3.

The action is
effective
or
faithful

if
\displaystyle\bigcap_{{\rm p}\in M}G_{\rm p}=\{e\}

or, what is equivalent, if

\Phi_{g}={\rm Id}_{M}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ g=e

(that is,
\Phi
is injective).

4.

The action is
free
if the following map, defined for a fixed
{\rm p}\in M
,

\begin{array}[]{ccc}G&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&M\\
g&\mapsto&\Phi_{g}({\rm p})\end{array}

is injective, for every
{\rm p}\in M

(this means that there are no invariant points in
M
under the action).

5.

The action is
transitive
if, for every
{\rm p}_{1},{\rm p}_{2}\in M
,
there exists
g\in G
such that
\Phi_{g}({\rm p}_{1})={\rm p}_{2}
or, what is the equivalent, if
{\cal O}_{\rm p}=M
, for every
{\rm p}\in M

(that is,
\Phi
has only one orbit).
In this case,
M
is said to be a
homogeneous
G
-space
.

6.

The action is
proper
if the anti-image of every compact set by the map

(g,{\rm p})\mapsto(\Phi_{g}({\rm p}),{\rm p})
, for every
g\in G
and
{\rm p}\in M
,
is also a compact set.

Definition 2.78

Let
M_{1},M_{2}
be differentiable manifolds,

G
a Lie group and

\Phi_{1}\colon G\times M_{1}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M_{1}
,
\Phi_{2}\colon G\times M_{2}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M_{2}

actions of
G
on
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
.
A map
F\colon M_{1}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M_{2}
is
equivariant

with respect to these actions if, for every
g\in G
, the following diagram commutes

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}&M_{1}&\mathrel{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits^{\Phi_{1_{g}}}}&M_{1}&\\
F&\Big\downarrow&&\Big\downarrow&F\\
&M_{2}&\mathrel{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits^{\Phi_{2_{g}}}}&M_{2}&\end{array}

Remember that the
Lie algebra
of a Lie group
G
is

{\bf g}:={\rm T}_{e}G
or, equivalently, the set of

left-invariant vector fields
of
\mathfrak{X}(G)

(that is, the set of vector fields which are invariant
by the action induced on
\mathfrak{X}(G)
by the left-action
\Phi
of
G
on
G
).
Then, every action of
G
on
M
induces a Lie algebra-homomorphism

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\xi&\colon&{\bf g}&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&\mathfrak{X}(M)\\
&&X&\mapsto&\xi_{X}\end{array}

defined as follows: the uniparametric subgroup of
G

generated by
X
determines another
uniparametric subgroup of transformations in
M

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\sigma_{t}&\colon&M&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&M\\
&&{\rm p}&\mapsto&\Phi_{\alpha(-t)}({\rm p}):=\Phi_{{\rm exp}(-tX_{e})}({\rm p})\end{array}\ ,

where
{\rm exp}\colon{\rm T}_{e}G\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>G
denotes the

exponential map
of the group. Then, the map

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\sigma&\colon&\mathbb{R}\times M&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&M\\
&&(t,{\rm p})&\mapsto&\sigma_{t}({\rm p})\end{array}

is the flux of some vector field
\xi_{X}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
;
that is, for
{\rm p}\in M
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\sigma(t,{\rm p})
is the integral curve of
\xi_{X}

passing through
{\rm p}
. This vector field is given by

\xi_{X}({\rm p})=\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\sigma(t,{\rm p})=\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\sigma_{t}({\rm p})=\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\Phi_{{\rm exp}(-tX_{e})}({\rm p})\Big|_{t=0}\ .

Hence, for every
f\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
, we have that

(\xi_{X}(f))({\rm p})=\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}f(\Phi_{{\rm exp}(-tX_{e})}({\rm p}))\Big|_{t=0}\ .

Definition 2.79

For
X\in{\bf g}
, the vector field
\xi_{X}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
is the

infinitesimal generator
or
fundamental vector field

of the action
\Phi
associated to
X
.

Definition 2.80

Let
\Phi
be an action of
G
on
M
.

1.

A
k
-form
\vartheta\in{\Omega}^{k}(M)
is
G
-invariant
by
\Phi

if
\Phi_{g}^{*}\vartheta=\vartheta
, for every
g\in G
;
or what is equivalent, if

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(\xi_{Z})\vartheta=0
, for every
Z\in{\bf g}
.

2.

A vector field
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
is
G
-invariant
by
\Phi

if
\Phi_{g_{*}}Z=Z
, for every
g\in G
;
or what is equivalent, if

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(\xi_{X})Z=0
, for every
X\in{\bf g}
.

Proposition 2.81

Let
\psi\colon G_{1}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>G_{2}
be a Lie group homomorphism.
Then the following diagram commutes:

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}&G_{1}&\mathrel{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits^{\psi}}&G_{2}&\\
{\rm exp}&\Big\uparrow&&\Big\uparrow&{\rm exp}\\
&{\rm T}_{e_{1}}G_{1}\simeq{\bf g}_{1}&\mathrel{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits^{\psi_{*}}}&{\rm T}_{e_{2}}G_{2}\simeq{\bf g}_{2}&\end{array}

that is,

\psi({\rm exp}(tX_{e_{1}}))={\rm exp}(t(\psi_{*}X)_{e_{2}})
.

(
Proof
)
Immediate observing that
t\mapsto{\rm exp}(t(\psi_{*}X)_{e_{2}})
is the only uniparametric subgroup of
G_{2}

whose tangent vector field at
t=0
is
(\psi_{*}X)_{e_{2}}
.

A very relevant type of group actions are the following:

Definition 2.82

Let
G
be a Lie group,
(M,\Omega)
a symplectic manifold, and

\Phi\colon G\times M\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M
an action of
G
on
M
.
We say that
\Phi
is a
symplectic action
of
G
on
M

(also that
G

acts symplectically
on
M
by
\Phi
)
if
\Phi_{g}
is a symplectomorphism, for every
g\in G
;
that is,
\Phi_{g}^{*}\Omega=\Omega
.
Then
M
is said to be a
symplectic
G
-space
.

According to this definition, if
G
acts symplectically on
M
by
\Phi
,
then the fundamental vector field
\xi_{X}
associated to
X
by
\Phi

is a locally Hamiltonian vector field and, conversely,
if
\xi_{X}\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M)
, for every
X\in{\bf g}
, then

\Phi
is a symplectic action of
G
on
M
. Therefore:

Proposition 2.83

Let
\xi\colon{\bf g}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathfrak{X}(M)
be the map such that

\xi(X):=\xi_{X}
, for
X\in{\bf g}
.
Then,
\Phi
is a symplectic action of
G
on
M
if, and only if,

{\rm Im}\,\xi\subseteq\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M)
.

This means that

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(\xi_{X})\Omega=0
, for every
X\in{\bf g}
,
(that is,
\mathop{i}\nolimits(\xi_{X})\Omega
is a closed form).

Definition 2.84

Let
G
be a Lie group,
(M,\Omega)
a symplectic manifold and

\Phi\colon G\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M
a symplectic action of
G
on
M
.
We say that
\Phi
is a
strongly symplectic action
of
G
on
M

if
\xi_{X}\in\mathfrak{X}_{h}(M)
, for every
X\in{\bf g}
;
or, what is equivalent,
\mathop{i}\nolimits(\xi_{X})\Omega
is an exact form.

In this case
M
is said to be a

strongly symplectic
G
-space
and

\Phi
is a
Hamiltonian action
of
G
on
M
.
Otherwise, it is a
locally Hamiltonian action
of
G
on
M
.

In order to discuss the obstruction for a symplectic action to be strongly symplectic,
let
{\bf g}_{h}:=\{X\in{\bf g}\ \mid\ \xi(X)=\xi_{X}\in\mathfrak{X}_{H}(M)\}

and consider the following sequences of Lie algebras:

\begin{array}[]{ccccccccc}0&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&{\bf g}_{h}&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&{\bf g}&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern &&\Big\downarrow\ \xi&&\Big\downarrow\ \xi&&\Big\downarrow\ \widetilde{\xi}&&\\
0&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern
