2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&0\end{array}\ ,

(2.16)

where
H^{1}(M)
denotes the
first de Rham’s cohomology group
of
M
,
and
\widetilde{\xi}
is a Lie algebra homomorphism which makes the diagram commutative.
Then, the image of
g
by
\xi
is in
{\cal X}_{h}(M)

(that is, the action is strongly symplectic) if, and only if,

\widetilde{\xi}=0
.

There are two specially relevant cases
for which every symplectic action is strongly symplectic:
when
M
is simply connected
(then every closed form is exact and,
therefore,
{\cal X}_{lh}(M)={\cal X}_{h}(M)
) and
if
G
is semisimple (then
{\bf g}=[{\bf g},{\bf g}]

and
[{\cal X}_{lh}(M),{\cal X}_{lh}(M)]\subset{\cal X}_{h}(M)
).

As a particular case, we have:

Definition 2.85

Let
G
be a Lie group,
(M,\Omega)
an exact symplectic manifold
(that is,
\Omega={\rm d}\Theta
for some
\Theta\in{\Omega}^{1}(M)
)
and
\Phi\colon G\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M
an action of
G
to
M
.

\Phi
is said to be an

exact action
of
G
on
M

if,
\Phi_{g}^{*}\Theta=\Theta
, for every
g\in G
.

Of course, every exact action is strongly symplectic.

2.3.6
Comomentum and momentum maps

Next, we introduce new geometric elements
which are very relevant in the theory of symplectic group actions.
First, following
[
327
]
we define:

Definition 2.86

Let
G
a Lie group which acts symplectically on a symplectic manifold
(M,\Omega)
.
A
comomentum map
associated to this action
is every Lie algebra linear application (if it exists)

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}{\rm j}^{*}&\colon&{\bf g}&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)\\
&&X&\mapsto&f_{X}:={\rm j}^{*}(X)\end{array}\ ,

such that the following diagram commutes

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 11.40273pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 52.53468pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 113.702pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\bf g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 78.1557pt\raise-9.02812pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.99155pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\rm j}^{*}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 72.0908pt\raise-22.79222pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 119.57698pt\raise-17.95642pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\xi}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 119.57698pt\raise-22.79222pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 184.29977pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-11.40273pt\raise-30.73668pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\>\lx@xy@svgnested{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 35.40273pt\raise-30.73668pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 35.40273pt\raise-30.73668pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 75.12335pt\raise-36.84778pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\sharp_{\Omega}\circ{\rm d}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 99.66664pt\raise-30.73668pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 99.66664pt\raise-30.73668pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathfrak{X}_{lh}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 163.48732pt\raise-30.73668pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 163.48732pt\raise-30.73668pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H^{1}(M)\>\lx@xy@svgnested{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>0}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ ,

or, what is equivalent,

\mathop{i}\nolimits(\xi_{X})\Omega={\rm d}f_{X}\ .

The obstruction to the existence of comomentum maps
is given in the following:

Proposition 2.87

Let
G
be a Lie group which acts symplectically on
(M,\Omega)
.
A comomentum map associated to this action exists
if, and only if, the map
\widetilde{\xi}\colon{\bf g}/{\bf g}_{h}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>H^{1}(M)
in (
2.16
) reduces to be
\widetilde{\xi}=0
.

(
Proof
)
By definition, if a comomentum map exists, then
\sharp_{\Omega}~\circ~{\rm d}~\circ~{\rm j}^{*}=\xi
.
But, as
{\rm Im}\ \xi\subset{\rm Im}\ \sharp_{\Omega}
,
this implies that
\widetilde{\xi}=0
.

Conversely, if
\widetilde{\xi}=0
, then
{\rm Im}\ \xi:=\xi({\bf g})\subset{\cal X}_{h}(M)
and, for all
X\in{\bf g}
, there exists

f_{X}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
such that
\mathop{i}\nolimits(\xi_{X})\Omega={\rm d}f_{X}
.

Observe that this is also the obstruction
for a symplectic action to be strongly symplectic.
Hence:

Proposition 2.88

Let
G
be a Lie group which acts symplectically on
(M,\Omega)
.
A comomentum map associated to this action exists
if, and only if, the action is strongly symplectic.

Remark 2.89

If a comomentum map exists, it is not unique.
In fact, if
f_{C}\colon{\bf g}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}

is a continuous linear function, then
f_{C}\in{\bf g}^{*}

and then
f_{C}(X)=ctn.
, for every
X\in{\bf g}
.
Therefore, if
{\rm j}^{*}
is a comomentum map,
so is
{\rm j}^{{}^{\prime}*}={\rm j}^{*}+f_{C}
.

Furthermore, if a comomentum map exists, it is not a
Lie algebra homomorphism necessarily since,
for
X,Y\in{\bf g}
, we have that

\sharp_{\Omega}\circ{\rm d}\{f_{X},f_{Y}\}=\sharp_{\Omega}\mathop{i}\nolimits([\xi_{X},\xi_{Y}])\Omega=-\sharp_{\Omega}\mathop{i}\nolimits(\xi_{[X,Y]})\Omega=-\sharp_{\Omega}{\rm d}f_{[X,Y]}

therefrom

{\rm d}\{f_{X},f_{Y}\}=-{\rm d}f_{[X,Y]}
and then

\{f_{X},f_{Y}\}=-f_{[X,Y]}+\sigma(X,Y)

where
\sigma\colon{\bf g}\times{\bf g}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}

is a skew symmetric bilinear function
whose existence measures the obstruction for the
comomentum map to be a Lie algebra homomorphism.
This leads to state:

Definition 2.90

Let
G
be a Lie group which acts symplectically on
(M,\Omega)
.
The action is said to be a
Poissonian action

or also a
strongly Hamiltonian action
if

(i)

There exists a comomentum map for this action.

(ii)

The comomentum map is a Lie algebra (anti)homomorphism.

Then the triple
(M,\Omega,{\rm j}^{*})
is called a

Hamiltonian
G
-space
.

As a particular case, we have that:

Proposition 2.91

If
(M,\Omega)
is an exact symplectic manifold
and the action of
G
on
M
is exact, then it is Poissonian.

(
Proof
)
Let
\Omega={\rm d}\Theta
.
Defining
f_{X}:=-\Theta(\xi_{X})
, then

{\rm j}^{*}\colon X\mapsto f_{X}
is a comomentum map and

f_{[X,Y]}=-\Theta(\xi_{[X,Y]})=\Theta([\xi_{X},\xi_{Y}])=\mathop{i}\nolimits([\xi_{X},\xi_{Y}])\Theta=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(\xi_{X})\mathop{i}\nolimits(\xi_{Y})\Theta=-\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(\xi_{X})f_{Y}=-\{f_{X},f_{Y}\}\ .

Definition 2.92

[
327
]
.
Let
G
be a Lie group which acts
in a strongly symplectic way on a symplectic manifold
(M,\Omega)
.
A
momentum map
associated to this action
is the dual map of a comomentum map; that is, a map

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}{\rm J}&\colon&M&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&{\bf g}^{*}\\
&&{\rm p}&\mapsto&\mu\end{array}

such that, for every
X\in{\bf g}
and for
{\rm p}\in M
,

\langle X,{\rm J}({\rm p})\rangle:={\rm j}^{*}(X)({\rm p})=f_{X}({\rm p})\ .

(Observe that the map

\mu:={\rm J}({\rm p})\colon{\bf g}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}
such that

X\mapsto f_{X}({\rm p})
, for
X\in{\bf g}
,
is, in fact, an element of
{\bf g}^{*}
).

Obviously, the obstruction to the existence of momentum maps is
the same that for the comomentum maps.
In particular, as an immediate corollary of Proposition

2.91
we have:

Proposition 2.93

If
(M,\Omega)
is an exact symplectic manifold
and the action of
G
on
M
is exact,
then a momentum map exists. It is given by

\langle X,{\rm J}({\rm p})\rangle:=-\Theta(\xi_{X})({\rm p})
,
for every
X\in{\bf g}
, for
{\rm p}\in M
.

Remark 2.94

As a more general result,
if
G
is a connected Lie group and
\Phi
a strongly symplectic action
of
G
on
(M,\Omega)
, then the action is Poissonian
if, and only if,
the momentum maps associated to this action
are
Ad^{*}
-equivariant, that is,
for every
g\in G
, we have that
Ad^{*}\circ{\rm J}=\Phi_{g}\circ{\rm J}
;
where
Ad^{*}\colon{\bf g}^{*}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\bf g}^{*}
is the
coadjoint action
of
G
.
(See
[
327
]
for the proof).

2.3.7
Reduction by symmetries

Finally, we introduce the concept of

group of symmetries
of a Hamiltonian system,
its relation with the momentum map,
and give the main insights about the theory of reduction by symmetries.

Definition 2.95

Let
G
be a Lie group,
(M,\Omega,h)
a Hamiltonian system,
and
\Phi\colon G\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M
an action of
G
on
M
.
We say that
G
is a
symmetry group
of the Hamiltonian system if

(i)

\Phi
is a symplectic action.

(ii)

For every
g\in G
, we have
\Phi_{g}^{*}h=h
.

The diffeomorphism
\Phi_{g}
, for every
g\in G
, is called a

symmetry
of the Hamiltonian system.

From this definition, it is obvious that

G
is a symmetry group of a Hamiltonian system
(M,\Omega,h)

if, and only if,
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(\xi_{X})h=0
, for every
X\in{\bf g}
.

In this context, the Noether Theorem is stated as follows::

Theorem 2.96

(
Noether’s theorem
).
Let
G
be a symmetry group which acts in a strongly symplectic way
on the Hamiltonian system
(M,\Omega,h)
.
Then, for every
X\in{\bf g}
, the functions
f_{X}

are constants of motion; that is,
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{h})f_{X}=0
.

(
Proof
)
We have that
\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{h})\Omega={\rm d}h
, then

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{h})f_{X}=\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{h}){\rm d}f_{X}=\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{h})\mathop{i}\nolimits(\xi_{X})\Omega=-\mathop{i}\nolimits(\xi_{X})\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{h})\Omega=-\mathop{i}\nolimits(\xi_{X}){\rm d}h=-\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(\xi_{X})h=0\ .

One of the most important features on the study of
Hamiltonian systems with symmetry is the so-called

reduction theory
.
Now we are going to state the main results on this topic
(see, for instance,
[
107
,
263
,
265
,
266
,
267
,
292
,
293
,
294
,
358
]
for deeper explanations and details).

First, we have to introduce the following concept:

Definition 2.97

Let
G
be a Lie group and a strongly symplectic action
of
G
on
(M,\Omega)
.
Let
{\rm J}
be a momentum map associated to this action. Then,

\mu\in{\bf g}^{*}
is a

weakly regular value
of
{\rm J}
if:

(i)

{\rm J}^{-1}(\mu)
is a submanifold of
M
.

(ii)

For every
{\rm p}\in{\rm J}^{-1}(\mu)
,

{\rm T}_{\rm p}({\rm J}^{-1}(\mu))=\ker{\rm T}_{\rm p}{\rm J}
.

If
{\rm T}_{\rm p}{\rm J}
is surjective, then
\mu
is said to be a
regular value
.
(Of course, every regular value is weakly regular).

Then we have:

Theorem 2.98

Let
G
be a Lie group acting on a symplectic manifold

(M,\Omega)
such that the action is
Poissonian, free and proper.
Let
\mu
be a weakly regular value of the
momentum map associated to this action,
and let
G_{\mu}
be the isotropy group of

{\rm J}^{-1}(\mu)
for the action of
G
on
M
.
Then:

1.

The submanifold

{\rm J}^{-1}(\mu)
is stable under the action of
G_{\mu}
and
so the quotient
J^{-1}(\mu)/G_{\mu}
is well-defined
(it is called the

orbit space
of
{\rm J}^{-1}(\mu)
).

2.

(Marsden–Weinstein’s theorem)
.
Denote by
\pi\colon{\rm J}^{-1}(\mu)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm J}^{-1}(\mu)/G_{\mu}

the canonical projection and by

\iota\colon{\rm J}^{-1}(\mu)\hookrightarrow M
the embedding.
Then,
{\rm J}^{-1}(\mu)/G_{\mu}
is a differentiable manifold
which is endowed with a (unique) symplectic structure

\widehat{\Omega}
such that

\iota^{*}\Omega=\pi^{*}\widehat{\Omega}
.

3.

If
G
is a symmetry group of the
Hamiltonian system
(M,\Omega,h)
,
then
({\rm J}^{-1}(\mu)/G_{\mu},\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{h})

is a Hamiltonian system, where

\iota^{*}h=\pi^{*}\widehat{h}
.

(
Proof
)

1.

In fact, if
g\in G_{\mu}
, for all
{\rm p}\in{\rm J}^{-1}(\mu)
we have

\Phi_{g}({\rm p})=\Phi_{g}({\rm J}^{-1}(\mu))={\rm J}^{-1}(g\mu))={\rm J}^{-1}(\mu)\ ,

then
{\rm J}^{-1}(\mu)
is invariant under the action of
G_{\mu}

and the quotient is well-defined.

2.

We omit he proof of this statement because it is long and heavy.
It can be found in the above-mentioned references.

3.

It is evident, since
h
is
G
-invariant
(and hence it is also
G_{\mu}
-invariant),
therefore it projects onto

{\rm J}^{-1}(\mu)/G_{\mu}
.

Chapter 3
Symplectic mechanics (II): Autonomous Lagrangian dynamical systems

The aim of this chapter is to state a geometrical formulation
for a particular kind of dynamical systems: those which are described by
Lagrangian functions and that, consequently, are
variational
.
These systems admit a description as Hamiltonian dynamical systems,
but they present relevant particular characteristics.
This formulation gives rise to two formalisms: the so-called
Lagrangian formalism

and its associated
canonical Hamiltonian formalism
.
These kinds of systems include, as particular cases, a wide range of mechanical systems
which are very relevant in physics: the
conservative Newtonian systems
,
which are those whose configuration space is endowed with a metric
(they are studied in Chapter
5
).

There are several alternative ways to state the geometric description for these kinds of systems.
The geometric framework where the theory is developed is the tangent bundle of a manifold,
{\rm T}Q
,
which represents the phase space of the system.
A selected function on it, the
Lagrangian

{\cal L}
,
is a depository of the physical information of the system.
Then, a first formulation consists in using
{\cal L}
to define the so-called

Legendre transformation
which connects
{\rm T}Q
and
{\rm T}^{*}Q

and, if this map is a (local) diffeomorphism, use it to translate the Hamiltonian description previously stated in
{\rm T}^{*}Q
to
{\rm T}Q
(see, for instance,
[
1
]
).

The second and more general approach consists in using the canonical structures of the tangent bundle
to construct, starting from
{\cal L}
, the geometrical objects needed to establish the dynamics of the system.
This is the so-called
Klein
or
Cartan formulation
of the Lagrangian formalism, which was stated in the classical references

[
164
,
178
,
200
,
201
,
227
]
and developed later by many other authors

[
58
,
103
,
105
,
108
,
138
,
316
]
.
It has the advantage that is more direct and
does not need any previously established Hamiltonian formalism
(in fact, the existence of this Hamiltonian formalism is not
assured for some singular Lagrangian systems).
This is the procedure that we follow in this exposition.

Another elegant alternative was developed in
[
338
,
339
]
,
using the concept of
special symplectic manifold
and of

Lagrangian submanifold
, and obtaining both the Lagrangian and Hamiltonian formalisms
for Lagrangian systems and their equivalence.
Finally, another unified Lagrangian-Hamiltonian formalism for these systems
is given in
[
322
]
.
These two approaches have subsequently been generalized
and applied in many physical contexts.

The structure of the chapter is as follows:
first, we introduce the mathematical framework which is needed
to state the Lagrangian formalism and its associated canonical Hamiltonian formalism;
that is, the canonical geometric structures of the tangent and the cotangent bundles of a manifold.
Next, we develop the Lagrangian formalism for
dynamical Lagrangian systems

and its associated
canonical Hamiltonian formalism
,
their equivalence, and the
Hamilton–Jacobi theory

for the Hamiltonian formalism.
Furthermore, we describe the
Skinner-Rusk formalism,
which is
a nice formulation that unifies both the Lagrangian and the Hamiltonian formalism.
We also discuss the symmetries and conserved quantities in both formalisms,
paying special attention to those which are canonical lifts of diffeomorphisms and vector fields (which are called
natural
),
and studying the equivalence of Lagrangians in this context.
Finally, we study the variational formulation introducing the
Hamilton
and the
Hamilton–Jacobi variational principles

for the Lagrangian and the Hamiltonian formalisms, respectively.
Some relevant physical systems, the harmonic oscillator and the Kepler problem, are analyzed using this geometric treatment.

3.1
Geometric structures of the tangent and cotangent bundles

(See
[
103
,
105
,
108
,
138
,
164
,
178
,
200
,
201
,
227
,
316
]
).

The tangent bundle of a manifold
Q
, denoted as
{\rm T}Q
(whose construction and main characteristics are reviewed in the appendix
A.1
)
is endowed with three canonical geometric structures:
the
vertical subbundle
, the
vertical endomorphism
and the
Liouville vector field
.
Furthermore, there are some characteristic vector fields in
{\rm T}Q
,
those whose integral curves are obtained as solutions to

second-order differential equations
in
Q
.
Similarly, the
cotangent bundle
of
Q
, denoted as
{\rm T}^{*}Q

is endowed with some canonical differential forms.
Next, we present and discuss all these topics in detail.

Along this section,
Q
is a differentiable manifold with
\dim\,Q=n
.

3.1.1
The vertical subbundle. Vertical lift

Considering the canonical projection
\tau_{Q}\colon{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
,
and its tangent map

.

Definition 3.1

Let
(q,u)\in{\rm T}Q
and
{\rm V}_{(q,u)}(\tau_{Q}):=\ker{\rm T}_{(q,u)}\tau_{Q}
.
The

of
{\rm T}{\rm T}Q
is the vector bundle (of rank
n
)

{\rm V}(\tau_{Q})\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q
, where

{\rm V}(\tau_{Q}):=\bigcup_{(q,u)\in{\rm T}Q}{\rm V}_{(q,u)}(\tau_{Q})\ .

The sections of this bundle
{\rm V}(\tau_{Q})\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q
are called
vertical vector fields
,
and the set of all these vector fields is denoted by

\mathfrak{X}^{V(\tau_{Q})}({\rm T}Q)
.

It is immediate to prove that, in natural coordinates of
{\rm T}Q
,
the expression of these vector fields is

\displaystyle f^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}
; that is,
\mathfrak{X}^{V(\tau_{Q})}({\rm T}Q)

is locally generated by the set
\displaystyle\left\{\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\right\}
.

Another interpretation of the fibers of
{\rm V}(\tau_{Q})
is the following:
for every
q\in Q
, consider the
n
-dimensional vector space
{\rm T}_{q}Q
,
as a differentiable manifold and the natural immersion

\begin{array}[]{cccc}j_{q}\colon&{\rm T}_{q}Q&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&{\rm T}Q\\
&u&\mapsto&(q,u)\end{array}\ .

Observe that
\tau_{Q}\circ j_{x}
is the constant map equal to
x
.
If
u\in{\rm T}_{q}Q
, we have that

{\rm T}_{u}j_{q}\colon{\rm T}_{u}{\rm T}_{q}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}_{(q,u)}{\rm T}Q\ ;

but, as
\tau_{Q}\circ j_{q}
is a constant map,
then
{\rm T}_{u}(\tau_{Q}\circ j_{q})={\rm T}_{(q,u)}\tau_{Q}\circ{\rm T}_{u}j_{q}=0
.
This is equivalent to say that

{\rm Im}\,{\rm T}_{u}j_{q}\subseteq\ker{\rm T}_{(q,u)}\tau_{Q}={\rm V}_{(q,u)}(\tau_{Q})

and, as
j_{q}
is an immersion,

\dim\,{\rm Im}\,{\rm T}_{u}j_{q}=\dim\,({\rm T}_{u}{\rm T}_{q}Q=n=\dim\,{\rm V}_{(q,u)}(\tau_{Q})\ ,

hence
{\rm Im}\,{\rm T}_{u}j_{q}={\rm V}_{(q,u)}(\tau_{Q})
,
(since both of them have the same dimension and the first one is a subset of the second one).

As a consequence of the above discussion,
{\rm V}_{(q,u)}(\tau_{Q})

is identified naturally with
{\rm T}_{u}{\rm T}_{q}Q
,
through the isomorphism induced by
{\rm T}_{u}j_{q}
onto its image.
Furthermore, as
{\rm T}_{q}Q
is a vector space,
if
u\in{\rm T}_{q}Q
, we have that
{\rm T}_{q}Q

is canonically identified with
{\rm T}_{u}({\rm T}_{q}Q)
by means of the directional derivative

1
1

1

Remember that, if
F
is an
n
-dimensional real vector space and

and

is given as follows:

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle{\rm T}_{u}F

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle{\rm D}_{v}(u)\ ,

where

denotes the directional derivative with respect to the vector

at the point

;
that is, if

is any differentiable function, then

({\rm D}_{v}(u))f\equiv{\rm D}_{v}f(u):=\lim_{t\mapsto 0}\frac{f(u+tv)-f(u)}{t}\ .

If

are coordinates in

, then

\displaystyle({\rm D}_{v}(u))f=\lambda^{i}\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{x^{i}}}\Big|_{u}
;
hence

\displaystyle{\rm D}_{v}(u)=\lambda^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x^{i}}}\Big|_{u}
,
and the identification is immediate.

.
Thus,

can also be identified with

.
In other words,

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}{\rm V}(\tau_{Q})&\simeq&\tau_{Q}^{*}({\rm T}Q)&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&{\rm T}Q\\

\displaystyle{\rm T}_{q}Q

\displaystyle{\rm T}_{u}{\rm T}_{q}Q\simeq{\rm V}_{(q,u)}(\tau_{Q})\subset{\rm T}_{(q,u)}{\rm T}Q

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\mapsto

Definition 3.2

The vector

is called the

of

to the point

,
and the map

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\lambda_{q}^{(q,u)}&\colon&{\rm T}_{q}Q&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&{\rm T}_{(q,u)}{\rm T}Q\\

.

In natural coordinates, if
\displaystyle v=\lambda^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\Big|_{(q,u)}
,
