.

The vertical lift is extended in a natural way to the vector fields of
\mathfrak{X}(Q)
and the mapping
\lambda^{V}:\mathfrak{X}(Q)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathfrak{X}^{V(\tau_{Q})}({\rm T}Q)
,
is
{\rm C}^{\infty}(Q)
-lineal.

In natural coordinates, if
\displaystyle X=f^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}
,
then its vertical lift is

\displaystyle\lambda^{V}(X)\equiv X^{V}=\tau^{*}_{Q}f^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}
.

3.1.2
The canonical (or vertical) endomorphism

Definition 3.3

Let
(q,u)\in{\rm T}Q
. The map

\begin{array}[]{cccc}J_{(q,u)}\colon&{\rm T}_{(q,u)}{\rm T}Q&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&{\rm T}_{(q,u)}{\rm T}Q\\
&Y&\mapsto&{\rm D}_{({\rm T}_{(q,u)}\tau_{Q})Y}(q,u)=\lambda_{q}^{(q,u)}({\rm T}_{(q,u)}\tau_{Q}(Y))\end{array}

is called the
canonical
or
vertical endomorphism
(at
(q,u)
).

Observe that
J_{(q,u)}Y
is the vertical lift of
({\rm T}_{(q,u)}\tau_{Q})Y

to the point
(q,u)
(that is,
J_{(q,u)}
consists in projecting
to
{\rm T}_{q}Q
and to lift vertically).

It is clear that the image of
J_{(q,u)}
is in

{\rm V}_{(q,u)}(\tau_{Q})
and, as
{\rm T}_{(q,u)}\tau_{Q}
is a surjective map,
it coincides with
{\rm V}_{(q,u)}(\tau_{Q})=\ker\,J_{(q,u)}
.
Therefore:

Proposition 3.4

The canonical endomorphism has the following properties:
for
(q,u)\in{\rm T}_{q}Q
,

1.

{\rm Im}\,J_{(q,u)}={\rm V}_{(q,u)}(\tau_{Q})=\ker\,J_{(q,u)}
.

2.

(J_{(q,u)})^{2}=0
.

The action of
J
is extended in a natural way to vector fields:

J\colon\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathfrak{X}^{V(\tau_{Q})}({\rm T}Q)\subset\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)\ ,

and to differential forms:

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}J^{*}&\colon&{\Omega}^{k}({\rm T}Q)&\longrightarrow&{\Omega}^{k}({\rm T}Q)\\
&&\alpha&\mapsto&\mathop{i}\nolimits(J)\alpha\end{array}\ ,

where
J^{*}\alpha=\mathop{i}\nolimits(J)\alpha
is defined as

[\mathop{i}\nolimits(J)\alpha]({X}_{1},\ldots,{X}_{k}):=\alpha(J(X_{1}),\ldots,J(X_{i}),\ldots,J(X_{k}))\ .

In particular, if
k=1
, then we have that

\mathop{i}\nolimits(J)\alpha=\alpha\circ J
, which is a

1
-form verifying that

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X)(\mathop{i}\nolimits(J)\alpha)=0
, for every
X\in\mathfrak{X}^{V(\tau_{Q})}({\rm T}Q)
.
These kinds of differential forms are called
\tau_{Q}
-semibasic forms
.

Local expressions
:
If
(q^{i},v^{i})
are natural coordinates in
{\rm T}Q
, we have

J_{(q,u)}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\Big|_{(q,u)}={\rm D}_{\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\Big|_{q}}(q,u)=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\Big|_{(q,u)}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptJ_{(q,u)}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\Big|_{(q,u)}={\rm D}_{0}(q,u)=0\ ;

then, the local expression of
J_{(q,u)}
is

J_{(q,u)}={\rm d}q^{i}\Big|_{(q,u)}\otimes\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\Big|_{(q,u)}\ ,

and, by extension,

J={\rm d}q^{i}\otimes\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\ .

Observe that
J
is a tensor field of type
(1,1)
in
{\rm T}Q
.

3.1.3
The Liouville vector field

Definition 3.5

Let
q\in Q
and
(q,v)\in{\rm T}Q
.
Consider the vertical lift of the vector
v\in{\rm T}_{q}Q

to the point
(q,v)
; that is,
{\rm D}_{v}(q,v)
.
This operation allows us to construct a vertical vector field

\Delta\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
, which is called the
Liouville vector field
,
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\Delta\colon&{\rm T}Q&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&{\rm T}{\rm T}Q\\
&(q,v)&\mapsto&((q,v),{\rm D}_{v}(q,v))\end{array}

Local expression
:
In local coordinates
(q^{i},v^{i})
en
{\rm T}Q
;
let
f\colon{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}
be a function and
(q,v)\in{\rm T}Q
;
then we have that

\Delta_{(q,v)}f={\rm D}_{v}(q,v)f=\lim_{t\mapsto 0}\frac{f(q,v+tv)-f(q,v)}{t}\ ;

therefore

\displaystyle\Delta_{(q,v)}q^{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{t\mapsto 0}\frac{q^{i}(q,v+tv)-q^{i}(q,v)}{t}=\lim_{t\mapsto 0}\frac{q^{i}(q)-q^{i}(q)}{t}=0\ ,

\displaystyle\Delta_{(q,v)}v^{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{t\mapsto 0}\frac{v^{i}(q,v+tv)-v^{i}(q,v)}{t}=\lim_{t\mapsto 0}\frac{(v+tv)(q^{i})-v(q^{i})}{t}=v(q^{i})=v^{i}(q,v)\ ,

and hence, the local expression of
\Delta
is

\Delta=v^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\ .

Bearing in mind that the fibers
{\rm T}_{q}Q
of the
tangent bundle are vector spaces, and that

\Delta_{(q,v)}=v\in{\rm T}_{q}Q
, for every
(q,v)\in{\rm T}Q
,
it is usual to say that
\Delta
is the vector field that generates
the dilatations along the fibers of
{\rm T}Q
.
Another way to see this interpretation is considering the local
expression of
\Delta
and its associated system of differential equations

\frac{dq^{i}}{dt}=0\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dv^{i}}{dt}=v^{i}\ ,

whose general solution is

q^{i}(t)=A^{i}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptv^{i}(t)=B^{i}e^{t}\hskip 10.00002pt;\hskip 10.00002pt(A^{i},B^{i}\ ctns.)\ .

Thus, the flux of
\Delta
is

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}F^{\Delta}&\colon&\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q&\longrightarrow&{\rm T}Q\\
&&(t,q^{i},v^{i})&\mapsto&(q^{i},v^{i}e^{t})\end{array}\ .

Hence, the elements of the uniparametric local group of diffeomorphisms generated by
\Delta
are

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}F^{\Delta}_{t}&\colon&{\rm T}Q&\longrightarrow&{\rm T}Q\\
&&(q^{i},v^{i})&\mapsto&(q^{i},v^{i}e^{t})\ ;\end{array}

that is, they are homothetics on the fibers with positive reason.

3.1.4
Holonomic curves. Second Order Differential Equations

A curve in
{\rm T}Q
is not necessarily the canonical lift
of a curve in the base manifold
Q

2
2

2

See the Appendix
A.1.1
.
Then we define:

Definition 3.6

A curve
\sigma\colon(a,b)\subseteq\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q
is

holonomic
if there exists
\gamma\colon(a,b)\subseteq\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q

such that
\sigma=\widetilde{\gamma}
.

In natural coordinates,
\sigma(t)=(q^{i}(t),v^{i}(t))
,
is a canonical lift if, and only if,

v^{i}(t)=\dot{q}^{i}(t)
.

Definition 3.7

Let
\sigma\colon(a,b)\subseteq\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q
a curve
in
{\rm T}Q
. Then
\tau_{Q}\circ\sigma\colon(a,b)\subseteq\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q

is a curve in
Q
, which is called the

curve in the base manifold
associated with
\sigma
.

Using this, we can characterize holonomic curves as follows:

Proposition 3.8

A curve
\sigma\colon(a,b)\subseteq\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q
is
holonomic if, and only if,

\widetilde{(\tau_{Q}\circ\sigma)}=\sigma
.

(
Proof
)
(
\Longrightarrow
)  If
\sigma
is holonomic then there exists
\gamma\colon(a,b)\subseteq\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q

such that
\sigma=\widetilde{\gamma}
; therefore

\tau_{Q}\circ\sigma=\tau_{Q}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}=\gamma\hskip 10.00002pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\widetilde{(\tau_{Q}\circ\sigma)}=\widetilde{\gamma}=\sigma\ .

(
\Longleftarrow
)  The converse is immediate.

Finally, we have the following fundamental result:

Proposition 3.9

1.

Let
\varphi\colon Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
be a diffeomorphism and

{\rm T}\varphi\colon{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q
its canonical lift to
{\rm T}Q
. Then

({\rm T}\varphi)^{*}J=J\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt({\rm T}\varphi)_{*}\Delta=\Delta\ .

2.

Let
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
and
Z^{C}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
its canonical lift to
{\rm T}Q
.
Then the canonical endomorphism
J
and the Liouville vector field
\Delta

are invariant by the uniparametric group of local diffeomorphisms generated by
Z^{C}
.

(
Proof
)

•

The result for
J
is a straightforward consequence of the local expressions
of
J
and
{\rm T}\varphi
.

The result for
\Delta
is a straightforward consequence of the property

{\rm T}\varphi\circ F_{t}^{\Delta}=F_{t}^{\Delta}\circ{\rm T}\varphi
,
where
F_{t}^{\Delta}
is an element uniparametric group of local diffeomorphisms generated by
\Delta
.

•

It is immediate from the above result, taking the
uniparametric group of local diffeomorphisms generated by
Z
and
Z^{C}
.

This means that the canonical lifts of diffeomorphisms and vector fields
to the tangent bundle preserve the canonical structures of this bundle.

Definition 3.10

A vector field
X\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
is a

Second Order Differential Equation (
sode
)

or also a
holonomic vector field

3
3

3

It is also said that
X
satisfies the

second-order condition
.

if its integral curves are holonomic.

The local interpretation of this definition is as follows:

Proposition 3.11

The necessary and sufficient condition for a vector field

X\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
to be a
sode
is that its local expression
in any natural system of coordinates in
{\rm T}Q
is

X=v^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}+g^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\ ;

(3.1)

that is,

X_{(q,v)}=(v^{i},g^{i}(q,v))
, for every
(q,v)\in{\rm T}Q
.

(
Proof
)
(
\Longrightarrow
) In natural coordinates, the general expression of
X\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
is

\displaystyle X=f^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}+g^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}
.
Let
\sigma\colon(a,b)\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q

be an integral curve of
X
, with
\sigma(t)=(q^{i}(t),v^{i}(t))
; then

\frac{dq^{i}}{dt}=(f^{i}\circ\sigma)(t)=f^{i}(q^{j}(t),v^{j}(t))\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dv^{i}}{dt}=(g^{i}\circ\sigma)(t)=g^{i}(q^{j}(t),v^{j}(t))\ .

(3.2)

Furthermore, if
X
is a
sode
, by definition there exists

\gamma\colon(a,b)\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
, with
\gamma(t)=(q^{i}(t))
,
such that
\sigma=\widetilde{\gamma}
; that is,

\sigma(t)=(q^{i}(t),\dot{q}^{i}(t))
.
Therefore, we have that
\dot{q}^{i}(t)=v^{i}(t)
, for
i=1,\ldots,n
;
and hence, going to the first group of equations (
3.2
), we obtain that

v^{i}(t)=\frac{dq^{i}}{dt}=f^{i}(q^{j}(t),v^{j}(t))\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptt\in(a,b)\ ,

and the result follows.

(
\Longleftarrow
) If
\displaystyle X=v^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}+g^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}
, then its integral curves

\sigma\colon(a,b)\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q
, with
\sigma(t)=(q^{i}(t),v^{i}(t))
,
are determined by the system

\frac{dq^{i}}{dt}=(v^{i}\circ\sigma)(t)=v^{i}(t)\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dv^{i}}{dt}=(g^{i}\circ\sigma)(t)=g^{i}(q^{j}(t),v^{j}(t))\ ;

that is,
\sigma(t)=(q^{i}(t),\dot{q}^{i}(t))
.
Therefore, they are holonomic curves and
X
is a
sode
.

Remark 3.12

Observe that, with the above conditions,
the second group of equations (
3.2
) is written as

\frac{d^{2}q^{i}}{dt^{2}}=f^{i}(q^{j},\dot{q}^{j})\ ,

which is a system of second order ordinary differential equations,
whose solution completely determines the integral curves of
X
.
This fact justifies the name
sode
for this kind of vector fields.

From this result, we obtain the following intrinsic characterization:

Proposition 3.13

The necessary and sufficient condition for
X\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
to be a
sode
is that

J(X)=\Delta\ .

(
Proof
)
In natural coordinates, the general expression of
X\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
is

\displaystyle X=f^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}+g^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}
; then

J(X)=f^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}=v^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\equiv\Delta\hskip 10.00002pt\Longleftrightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptf^{i}=v^{i}\ ,

and the result follows from the above proposition.

There is another intrinsic characterization as follows:
the bundle
{\rm T}{\rm T}Q
has two natural projections

\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\rm T}{\rm T}Q&\mathrel{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits^{{\rm T}\tau_{Q}}}&{\rm T}Q\\
\tau_{{\rm T}Q}\downarrow&&\downarrow\tau_{Q}\\
{\rm T}Q&\mathrel{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits^{\tau_{Q}}}&Q\end{array}\ .

Taking natural coordinates in the bundles
{\rm T}Q
and

{\rm T}{\rm T}Q
, and reminding the expression (
A.1
),
for every
((q,v),Y_{(q,v)})\equiv(q^{i},v^{i};u^{i},w^{i})\in{\rm T}{\rm T}Q
,
we have that

\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}\tau_{{\rm T}Q}((q,v),Y_{(q,v)})&=&\tau_{{\rm T}Q}(q^{i},v^{i};u^{i},w^{i})&=&(q^{i},v^{i})&&\\
{\rm T}\tau_{Q}((q,v),Y_{(q,v)})&=&\tau_{{\rm T}Q}(q^{i},v^{i};u^{i},w^{i})&=&(\tau_{Q}(q^{i},v^{i}),{\rm T}_{(q^{i},v^{i})}\tau_{Q}(u^{i},w^{i}))&=&(q^{i},u^{i})\ .\end{array}

By definition,
X\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
is a section of the projection

\tau_{{\rm T}Q}
; that is, a map
X\colon{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}{\rm T}Q

such that
\tau_{{\rm T}Q}\circ X={\rm Id}_{{\rm T}Q}
. Then:

Proposition 3.14

The necessary and sufficient condition for a vector field

X\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
to be a
sode
is that
X
is a section
of the projection
{\rm T}\tau_{Q}
; that is, a map

X\colon{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}{\rm T}Q
such that

{\rm T}\tau_{Q}\circ X={\rm Id}_{{\rm T}Q}

(
Proof
)
In natural coordinates, the general expression of

X\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
is

\displaystyle X=f^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}+g^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}
;
that is, for every

(q,v)\equiv(q^{i},v^{i})\in{\rm T}Q
, it is a map

X(q,v)=X(q^{i},v^{i})=(q^{i},v^{i};f^{i}(q,v),g^{i}(q,v))\ .

Therefore

({\rm T}\tau_{Q}\circ X)(q,v)=({\rm T}\tau_{Q}\circ X)(q^{i},v^{i})={\rm T}\tau_{Q}(q^{i},v^{i};f^{i}(q,v),g^{i}(q,v))=(q^{i},f^{i}(q,v))\ ;

and, as
{\rm Id}_{{\rm T}Q}(q,v)=(q^{i},v^{i})
, we have that

({\rm T}\tau_{Q}\circ X)(q,v)={\rm Id}_{{\rm T}Q}(q,v)\hskip 10.00002pt\Longleftrightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptf^{i}=v^{i}\ ,

and the result follows.

Remark 3.15

Summarizing, we have proved that the following assertions are equivalent:

1.

A vector field
X\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
is a
sode
.

2.

The integral curves of
X
are canonical lifts of curves in
Q
.

3.

The expression of
X
in a system of natural coordinates in
{\rm T}Q
,
is (
3.1
).

4.

J(X)=\Delta
.

5.

{\rm T}\tau_{Q}\circ X={\rm Id}_{{\rm T}Q}
.

3.1.5
Canonical forms in the cotangent bundle

An essential characteristic of the
cotangent bundle is the following:

Theorem 3.16

The cotangent bundle
{\rm T}^{*}Q
is endowed with a canonical
2
-form
which is closed and nondegenerate and, hence, it
is a
symplectic manifold
.

(
Proof
)
As we know,

{\rm T}^{*}Q:=\{{\rm p}=(q,\xi)\ \mid\ q\in Q\ ,\ \xi\in{\rm T}^{*}_{q}Q\}
.
As
{\rm T}^{*}Q
is a differentiable manifold,
we can consider its tangent bundle
{\rm T}{\rm T}^{*}Q

and the tangent structure induced by

\pi_{Q}\colon{\rm T}^{*}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
,
which must be understood as a fiber map

{\rm T}_{(q,\xi)}\pi_{Q}\colon{\rm T}_{(q,\xi)}{\rm T}^{*}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}_{q}Q
.
Now, we prove the theorem constructively using the fiber projection.

1.

We construct the differential
1
-form

\Theta\in{\Omega}^{1}({\rm T}^{*}Q)

as follows: for every
{\rm p}=(q,\xi)\in{\rm T}^{*}Q
and

X_{\rm p}\in{\rm T}_{\rm p}{\rm T}^{*}Q
,

\Theta_{\rm p}(X_{\rm p}):=\xi({\rm T}_{\rm p}\pi_{Q}(X_{\rm p}))\ .

Its expression in a natural chart of coordinates of
{\rm T}^{*}Q

is obtained in the following way:
as we have seen in proposition
A.13
of the Appendix
A.1.2
,

\xi=p_{i}({\rm p})\,{\rm d}q^{i}\mid_{q}
;
furthermore, every
X_{\rm p}\in{\rm T}_{\rm p}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
has the local expression

\displaystyle X_{\rm p}=A^{j}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\Big|_{\rm p}+B_{j}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{j}}}\Big|_{\rm p}
,
and the general expression of
\Theta_{\rm p}
is

\Theta_{\rm p}=a_{i}\,{\rm d}q^{i}\mid_{\rm p}+b^{i}\,{\rm d}p_{i}\mid_{\rm p}
, then

\Theta_{\rm p}(X_{\rm p})=a_{i}A^{i}+b^{i}B_{i}\ ;

but, from the definition,

\displaystyle\Theta_{\rm p}(X_{\rm p})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\xi({\rm T}_{\rm p}\pi_{Q}(X_{\rm p}))=(p_{i}(m){\rm d}q^{i}\mid_{q})\left[{\rm T}_{\rm p}\pi_{Q}\Big(A^{j}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\Big|_{\rm p}+B_{j}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{j}}}\Big|_{\rm p}\Big)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(p_{i}({\rm p}){\rm d}q^{i}\mid_{x})\Big(A^{j}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\Big|_{q}\Big)=p_{i}({\rm p})A^{j}\delta^{i}_{j}=p_{i}({\rm p})A^{i}\ ,

and as this holds for every
X_{\rm p}
(that is, for every
A^{i},B_{i}
),
from the above expressions we obtain that

a_{i}=p_{i}(m)
and
b^{i}=0
; that is, the final local expression of
\Theta
is

\Theta=p_{i}\,{\rm d}q^{i}\ .

2.

We define the differential
2
-form

\Omega:=-{\rm d}\Theta\ ,

whose local expression in a natural chart is

\Omega={\rm d}q^{i}\wedge{\rm d}p_{i}\ .

Obviously
\Omega
is closed (because it is exact)
and nondegenerate (as we can see from its local expression in coordinates).

Then, cotangent bundles of manifolds are the canonical models of symplectic manifolds, since they carry a canonical symplectic form.
Furthermore, their natural coordinates are also the Darboux coordinates
for this canonical symplectic form.

Definition 3.17

The forms
\Theta\in{\Omega}^{1}({\rm T}^{*}Q)

and
\Omega\in{\Omega}^{2}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
are the

1
and
2
canonical forms
of the cotangent bundle

4
4

4

The
1
-form
\Theta
is also called the
tautological form
of
{\rm T}^{*}Q
.
.

From Proposition
2.11
we have that:

Proposition 3.18

The cotangent bundle of a differentiable manifold is an

oriented
manifold.

The canonical lift of a vector field in
Q
to the cotangent bundle
{\rm T}^{*}Q

5
5

5

See the Appendix
A.1.2
.

can be characterized using the canonical forms. First we define:

Definition 3.19

Every vector field
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)

induces a function
F_{Z}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
defined as

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}F_{Z}&\colon&{\rm T}^{*}Q&\longrightarrow&\mathbb{R}\\
&&{\rm p}\equiv(q,\xi)&\mapsto&\xi(Z_{q})\end{array}

Taking into account the definition of the canonical 1-form

\Theta
of
{\rm T}^{*}Q
and Proposition
A.18

we have that, for every
{\rm p}\equiv(q,\xi)\in{\rm T}^{*}Q
,

F_{Z}({\rm p})\equiv F_{Z}(q,\xi)=\xi(Z_{q})=\xi({\rm T}\pi_{Q}(Z^{*}_{\rm p}))=\Theta_{\rm p}(Z^{*}_{\rm p})\ ,

and thus we have proved that:

Proposition 3.20

If
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
, then
F_{Z}=\Theta(Z^{*})
.

Bearing this in mind, we obtain that:

Proposition 3.21

If
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
, then the canonical lift of
Z
to
{\rm T}^{*}Q

is the only vector field
Z^{*}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
such that

\mathop{i}\nolimits(Z^{*})\Omega={\rm d}F_{Z}

6
6

6

That is, with the terminology of Section
2.1.5
,

Z^{*}
is the (global) Hamiltonian vector field associated with the function
F_{Z}
.
.

(
Proof
)
In fact, using the Cartan formula and taking into account Proposition
7
,

\mathop{i}\nolimits(Z^{*})\Omega=-\mathop{i}\nolimits(Z^{*}){\rm d}\Theta={\rm d}\mathop{i}\nolimits(Z^{*})\Theta-\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Z^{*})\Theta={\rm d}(\Theta(Z^{*}))={\rm d}F_{Z}

since, according to Proposition
7
,
as the local uniparametric groups of diffeomorphisms of
Z^{*}

are canonical lifts,
they let the canonical forms of
{\rm T}^{*}Q
invariant, and then

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Z^{*})\Theta=0
.

Local expression
:
Bearing in mind this characterization, it is easy to obtain the local expression
of
Z^{*}
in a chart of canonical coordinates

(U;q^{i},p_{i})
of
{\rm T}^{*}Q
. So, if

\displaystyle Z|_{\pi_{Q}(U)}=f^{i}(q)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}
, we have that
F_{Z}(q^{i},p_{i})=p_{i}f^{i}(q^{j})
, and then

Z^{*}|_{U}=f^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}-p_{j}\frac{\partial{f^{j}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{i}}}\ .

(3.3)

Another relevant property is:

Proposition 3.22

If
\varphi\colon Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
is a diffeomorphism,
then
({\rm T}^{*}\varphi)^{*}\Theta=\Theta
and,
as a consequence,

({\rm T}^{*}\varphi)^{*}\Omega=~\Omega

7
7

7

With the terminology introduced in Section
2.1.8
,
we say that
{\rm T}^{*}\varphi
is a
simplectomorphism
.

(
Proof
)
For every
(q,\xi)\in{\rm T}^{*}Q
and

V\in{\rm T}_{(q,\xi)}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
,
using the definition of
\Theta
and the first property of Proposition
A.16
, we obtain

\displaystyle\big(({\rm T}^{*}\varphi)^{*}\Theta\big)_{(\varphi(q),\xi)}(V)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Theta_{(q,{\rm T}^{*}\varphi(\xi))}\big({\rm T}_{(\varphi(q),\xi)}({\rm T}^{*}\varphi)(V)\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\varphi_{q}^{*}\xi)\Big({\rm T}_{(q,{\rm T}^{*}\varphi(\xi))}\pi_{Q}\big({\rm T}_{(\varphi(q),\xi)}({\rm T}^{*}\varphi)(V)\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\varphi_{q}^{*}\xi)\big({\rm T}_{(\varphi(q),\xi)}(\pi_{Q}\circ{\rm T}^{*}\varphi)(V)\big)=\xi\Big({\rm T}_{\varphi(q)}\varphi\big({\rm T}_{(\varphi(q),\xi)}(\pi_{Q}\circ{\rm T}^{*}\varphi)(V)\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\xi\Big({\rm T}_{(\varphi(q),\xi)}\big(\varphi\circ\pi_{Q}\circ{\rm T}^{*}\varphi)(V)\big)\Big)=\xi\big({\rm T}_{(\varphi(q),\xi)}\pi_{Q}(V)\big)=\Theta_{(\varphi(q),\xi)}(V)\,.

The result for
\Omega
is immediate from here.

Finally, from Proposition
7
we obtain:

Proposition 3.23

If
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
, then

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Z^{*})\Theta=0\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Z^{*})\Omega=0\ .

(
Proof
)
To prove this, it suffices to take the local uniparametric groups of diffeomorphisms
of
Z
and their canonical lifts.

3.1.6
Fiber derivative of a function

Let
F\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)
.
Given
q\in Q
, we consider the function
F_{q}\colon{\rm T}_{q}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}
,
which is the restriction of
F
to the fiber
{\rm T}_{q}Q
.
If
(q,u)\in{\rm T}Q
, the differential of
F_{q}
at this point
is an element of
{\rm T}_{q}^{*}Q
; so
{\rm D}_{(q,u)}F_{q}\in{\rm T}^{*}_{q}Q
. Then:

Definition 3.24

The
fiber derivative
of
F
is the map

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}{\cal F}F&\colon&{\rm T}Q&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&{\rm T}^{*}Q\\
&&(q,u)&\mapsto&(q,{\rm D}F_{q}(u))\end{array}\ .

Observe that
\pi_{Q}\circ{\cal F}F=\tau_{Q}
;
that is,
{\cal F}F
preserve the fibers.

Local expressions
:
Consider a natural chart
(U;q^{i},v^{i})
of
{\rm T}Q

and the corresponding canonical chart
({\cal F}F(U);q^{i},p_{i})
in
{\rm T}^{*}Q
.
Observe that a basis of
{\rm T}_{q}Q
is

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\Big|_{q}\right\}
,
and the coordinates in
{\rm T}_{q}Q
are
(v^{1},\ldots,v^{n})
.
Then, the Jacobian matrix of
{\rm D}_{(q,u)}F_{q}
is

\left(\frac{\partial{F}}{\partial{v^{1}}}\Big|_{(q,u)}\ldots\frac{\partial{F}}{\partial{v^{n}}}\Big|_{(q,u)}\right)\ ,

and if
\displaystyle v=\lambda^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\Big|_{q}\in{\rm T}_{q}Q
, then

({\rm D}_{(q,u)}F_{q})(v)=\left(\frac{\partial{F}}{\partial{v^{1}}}\Big|_{(q,u)}\ldots\frac{\partial{F}}{\partial{v^{n}}}\Big|_{(q,u)}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}\lambda^{1}\\
\vdots\\
\lambda^{n}\end{matrix}\right)\ ,

and therefore

{\cal F}F(x,u)=\left(q,\frac{\partial{F}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\Big|_{(q,u)}{\rm d}q^{i}\Big|_{q}\right)\ ;

that is, the expression in coordinates of
{\cal F}F
is

q^{i}\circ{\cal F}F=q^{i}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptp_{i}\circ{\cal F}F=\frac{\partial{F}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\ .

3.2
Lagrangian formalism for Lagrangian dynamical systems

Next we introduce the so-called
Lagrangian formalism
of the
Lagrangian dynamical systems.
(See

[
58
,
103
,
105
,
108
,
138
,
164
,
178
,
200
,
201
,
227
,
316
]

as general references).

First, we state what are these kinds of systems.

3.2.1
Lagrangian dynamical systems

The physical and geometrical foundations of this formulation are the following:

Postulate 3.25

(First Postulate of Lagrangian mechanics)
:
In a physical Lagrangian system, the
n
possible degrees of freedom of the system
are described by the domain of variation of a set of
n

generalized coordinates

8
8

8

If we have a mechanical system, the generalized coordinates
correspond to “kinematic” degrees of freedom (position coordinates). But if we have a more generic physical system,
such as an electric or a thermodynamical system,
then the generalized coordinates correspond to other kinds of physical magnitudes
(for instance, electric charge, temperature, etc.).

,
which determine locally the
configuration space
of the system.
The
states
of the system are locally described by means of
generalized coordinates and their corresponding
generalized velocities.

Geometrically, this means that
the configuration space
Q
of a dynamical system
with
n
degrees of freedom is a
n
-dimensional differentiable manifold,
and that
the phase space is the tangent bundle
{\rm T}Q
of the configuration manifold
Q
,
which is called the

state
or
phase space
of
positions-velocities
of the system.

Postulate 3.26

(Second Postulate of Lagrangian mechanics)
:
The observables or physical magnitudes of a
dynamical system are functions of
{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)
.

The result of a measure of an observable is the value that the function
which represents the observable takes at a point of the phase space
{\rm T}Q

(that is, in some state, following the first postulate).

Postulate 3.27

(Third Postulate of Lagrangian mechanics)
:
There is a function
{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)
, called
Lagrangian function
,
which carries the dynamical information of the system.

As we will see,
from this function and using the geometrical structures of the
tangent bundle, we can construct a differential form

\Omega_{\cal L}\in{\Omega}^{2}({\rm T}Q)
and the so-called

Lagrangian Energy function

E_{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)
,
and we can set the dynamical equations of the system
(Postulate
3.34
).

Taking all of this into account, we define:

Definition 3.28

A
Lagrangian dynamical system
is a pair
({\rm T}Q,{\cal L})
,
where
Q
is a manifold representing the configuration space of
a physical system and
{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)

is the Lagrangian function of the system.

Remark 3.29

It is important to point out that the dynamical systems which are
described in this way are
autonomous
; that is,
independent of time

(a geometrical description of
nonautonomous dynamical systems

is presented in Chapter
4
).
They are also
first-order
systems,
which are those whose Lagrangian function
depends locally on the coordinates of position and velocity,
in contrast with those for which the dependence is also on the

generalized accelerations
or, in general,
higher-order time-derivatives of the generalized positions.
(For the geometrical description of
higher-order
systems,
see, for instance,
[
42
,
53
,
71
,
139
,
167
,
197
,
198
,
231
,
296
,
297
,
298
]
).

In the next section we explain how, using the canonical structures of the
tangent bundle introduced in Section
3.1
and starting from the Lagrangian function,
we can construct the dynamical-geometric structures which allow us to
set the dynamical equations in an intrinsic way.
In this way, we will complete the set of Postulates of the Lagrangian formalism.

3.2.2
Geometric structures induced by the dynamics

Given a Lagrangian function
{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)
and
using the canonical structures of the tangent bundle
(the vertical endomorphism

J\colon\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathfrak{X}^{V(\tau_{Q})}({\rm T}Q)

and the Liouville vector field
\Delta\in\mathfrak{X}^{V(\tau_{Q})}({\rm T}Q)
)
we can define the following elements:

Definition 3.30

The
Cartan
or
Lagrangian
1
and
2
-forms
associated with
{\cal L}
are

\displaystyle\Theta_{\cal L}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(J){\rm d}{\cal L}={\rm d}{\cal L}\circ J\in{\Omega}^{1}({\rm T}Q)\ ,

\displaystyle\Omega_{\cal L}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle-{\rm d}\Theta_{\cal L}\in{\Omega}^{2}({\rm T}Q)\ .

The
Lagrangian energy
associated with
{\cal L}
is the function

E_{\cal L}:=\Delta({\cal L})-{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)\ .

The function
A_{\cal L}:=\Delta({\cal L})\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)
is sometimes referred to as the
Lagrangian action function
associated with
{\cal L}
.

Remark 3.31

•

The physical observable represented by the Lagrangian energy
is the total energy of the system, and this justifies this terminology.

•

There are Lagrangian functions which, being different, give the same
Lagrangian form
\Omega_{{\cal L}}
and the same Lagrangian energy
E_{{\cal L}}
.
They are called
gauge-equivalent Lagrangians
(and they are studied in Section
3.5.3
).

•

It is important to point out that, for an arbitrary function

{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)
, the form
\Omega_{\cal L}

has not constant rank in
{\rm T}Q
necessarily.
The Lagrangian
{\cal L}
is said to be
geometrically admissible

when this rank is constant.
The theory we are developing concerns Lagrangian systems of this kind

9
9

9

When this is not the case,
there exists a more general framework which consists in using
Poisson manifolds
as phase states for the formalism (see, for instance,
[
249
]
).

.

Local expressions
:
Consider a natural chart
(U;q^{i},v^{i})
in
{\rm T}Q
.
Remember that the local expression of the vertical endomorphism and
the Liouville vector field on this chart are

\displaystyle J={\rm d}q^{i}\otimes\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}
and

\displaystyle\Delta=v^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}
. Then,
given a Lagrangian function
{\cal L}={\cal L}(q^{i},v^{i})
,
the local expressions of the action and the Lagrangian energy associated with the Lagrangian
{\cal L}
are

A_{\cal L}=v^{i}\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptE_{\cal L}=v^{i}\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}-{\cal L}\ .

The more general expression for a
1
-form in
{\rm T}Q
is

\Theta_{\cal L}=a_{i}(q^{j},v^{j}){\rm d}q^{i}+b_{i}(q^{j},v^{j}){\rm d}v^{i}\ ,

then, for an arbitrary
Y\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
,

Y=A^{i}(q^{j},v^{j})\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}+B^{i}(q^{j},v^{j})\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\ ,

we have that

\Theta_{\cal L}(Y)=a_{i}A^{i}+b_{i}B^{i}

and furthermore, by definition,

\Theta_{\cal L}(Y):=({\rm d}{\cal L}\circ J)(Y)=\left(\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}{\rm d}q^{i}+\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}{\rm d}v^{i}\right)\left(A^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\right)=A^{i}\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\ ;

therefore, from both expressions we obtain that

\displaystyle a_{i}=\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}
and
b_{i}=0
; that is

\Theta_{\cal L}=\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\,{\rm d}q^{i}\ ,

and, as a consequence,

\Omega_{\cal L}=\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial q^{j}\partial v^{i}}\,{\rm d}q^{i}\wedge{\rm d}q^{j}+\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{j}\partial v^{i}}\,{\rm d}q^{i}\wedge{\rm d}v^{j}\ .

It is important to remark that, for an arbitrary function

{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)
, although the Lagrangian
2
-form
\Omega_{\cal L}

is always closed, it is not necessarily non-degenerated
(since, as it is seen from the above local expression, its rank
is determined by the second derivatives with respect to the velocities of the function
{\cal L}
). Then:

Definition 3.32

{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)
is a
regular Lagrangian function

(and
({\rm T}Q,{\cal L})
is a
regular Lagrangian system
)
if the Lagrangian
2
-form
\Omega_{\cal L}
is nondegenerated.
Otherwise,
{\cal L}
is a
singular Lagrangian function

(and
({\rm T}Q,{\cal L})
is said to be a
singular Lagrangian system
)

10
10

10

In this exposition, we are mainly interested in
regular Lagrangian systems.
.

The non degeneracy of the Lagrangian
2
-form
can be characterized locally as follows:

Proposition 3.33

Let
({\rm T}Q,{\cal L})
be a Lagrangian system. Then

{\cal L}
is a regular Lagrangian if, and only if,
in a natural chart
(U;q^{i},v^{i})
of
{\rm T}Q
, the Hessian matrix

\displaystyle W=\left(\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{j}\partial v^{i}}\right)

is regular at every point of
U
.

(
Proof
)
We have to prove that
\Omega_{\cal L}
is nondegenerated if, and only if,
this condition holds. Then, it suffices to take into account that

\Omega_{\cal L}\in{\Omega}^{2}({\rm T}Q)

is non degenerated if, and only if,
(\Omega_{\cal L})^{\ n}

is a volume form in
{\rm T}Q
. Therefore,

\Omega_{\cal L}^{\ n}=n!\ {\rm det}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{j}\partial v^{i}}\right)^{n}{\rm d}q^{1}\wedge\ldots{\rm d}q^{n}\wedge{\rm d}v^{1}\wedge\ldots{\rm d}v^{n}\ ,

which is a non-vanishing form at every point when

\displaystyle{\rm det}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{j}\partial v^{i}}\right)\not=0

everywhere.

3.2.3
Lagrangian dynamical equations.
Euler–Lagrange equations

The dynamical equation in the Lagrangian formalism is stated in the following:

Postulate 3.34

(Fourth Postulate of Lagrangian mechanics)
:
The dynamical trajectories of a Lagrangian system

({\rm T}Q,{\cal L})
are the integral curves
of a vector field
X_{\cal L}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
such that:

1.

X_{\cal L}
is a solution to the equation

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\cal L})\Omega_{\cal L}={\rm d}E_{\cal L}\ .

(3.4)

2.

X_{\cal L}
is a
sode
; that is,

J(X_{\cal L})=\Delta\ .

(3.5)

Equation (
3.4
) is called the

Lagrangian equation for vector fields,
and
a vector field
X_{\cal L}
solution to (
3.4
) (if it exists) is a

Lagrangian dynamical vector field
.
If, in addition, the condition (
3.5
) holds, then

X_{\cal L}
it is called an
Euler–Lagrange vector field
of the system,

Bearing in mind the definition of integral curve and equation (
2.11
),
it is immediate to prove that:

Theorem 3.35

The vector field
X_{\cal L}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
is a Lagrangian vector field for a
Lagrangian system
({\rm T}Q,{\cal L})
if, and only if,
the integral curves
c\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M
of
X_{\cal L}
are the solutions to the equations

\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{c})(\Omega_{\cal L}\circ c)={\rm d}E_{\cal L}\circ c\ ,

(3.6)

Equation (
3.6
) is the
Lagrangian equation
for the
integral curves of
X_{\cal L}
.
If, in addition,
X_{\cal L}
is a
sode
, then
c
is a holonomic curve
and (
3.6
) is the

Euler–Lagrange equation
for the integral curves of
X_{\cal L}
.

Remark 3.36

It is usual to require that the Lagrangian dynamical equations are
obtained from a variational principle (as we will see
in Section
3.6
) and
the necessary condition for it is that the integral curves
of the dynamical vector field
X_{\cal L}
must be canonical lifts of
curves in the base
Q
of the bundle
{\rm T}Q
which represents the
Lagrangian phase space.
This is why
X_{\cal L}
is asked to be a
sode
.

Definition 3.37

Given a Lagrangian dynamical system
({\rm T}Q,{\cal L})
,
the
Lagrangian problem
posed by the system
consists in finding a vector field
X_{\cal L}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)

verifying the conditions (
3.4
) and (
3.5
).

Local expressions
:
Consider a natural chart
(U;q^{i},v^{i})
of
{\rm T}Q
.
Bearing in mind the local expressions of the several geometric elements
appearing in the dynamical equations we obtain that, if

\displaystyle X_{\cal L}=A^{i}(q^{j},v^{j})\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}+B^{i}(q^{j},v^{j})\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}
,
equation (
3.4
), written in coordinates, is:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{j}\partial v^{i}}B^{i}-\left(\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial q^{j}\partial v^{i}}+\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{j}\partial q^{i}}\right)A^{i}+\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{i}\partial q^{j}}v^{i}-\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{j}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\ ,

(3.7)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{j}\partial v^{i}}(A^{i}-v^{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\ .

(3.8)

As we know, condition (
3.5
) is locally equivalent to demand that

A^{i}=v^{i}
. Furthermore, the integral curves

\sigma\colon I\subseteq\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q
of
X_{\cal L}

are canonical lifts of curves

\gamma\colon I\subseteq\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
; therefore, if

\gamma(t)=(q^{i}(t))
, then
\sigma(t)=(q^{i}(t),\dot{q}^{i}(t))
and

A^{i}=v^{i}=\frac{dq^{i}}{dt}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptB^{i}=\frac{d^{2}q^{i}}{dt^{2}}\ ,

and the combination of these expressions with equations (
3.7
) and (
3.8
) leads to the
equation of the integral curves which is

\left(\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{j}\partial v^{i}}\circ\sigma\right)\frac{d^{2}q^{i}}{dt^{2}}=-\left(\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{j}\partial q^{i}}\circ\sigma\right)\frac{dq^{i}}{dt}+\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\circ\sigma\ ,

and can be written as

W_{ji}(q^{k}(t),\dot{q}^{k}(t))\frac{d^{2}q^{i}}{dt^{2}}=F_{j}(q^{k}(t),\dot{q}^{k}(t))\ ,

or also in an equivalent form as

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{j}}}\circ\sigma\right)-\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\circ\sigma=0\ .

(3.9)

This is the classical coordinate expression of the Euler–Lagrange equation (
3.6
).

Therefore, for the case of regular Lagrangians we have the following result:

Theorem 3.38

Let
({\rm T}Q,{\cal L})
be a regular Lagrangian system.
Then, there exists a unique vector field
X_{\cal L}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)

which is the solution to the Lagrangian equation
(
3.4
) and it is a
sode
.

(
Proof
)
The existence and uniqueness are a straightforward consequence
of the fact that
\Omega_{\cal L}
is non-degenerated.

Furthermore, if the Lagrangian is regular, the Hessian matrix

H_{\cal L}
is regular and equation (
3.8
) leads to

A^{i}=v^{i}
, and thus the vector field
X_{\cal L}
is a
sode
.
(Observe that, in this case, all the coefficients
B^{i}
are determined
by equations (
3.7
)).

Assuming that the Lagrangian
{\cal L}
is regular, from (
3.7
) and (
3.8
), we can obtain the local expression of the Lagrangian dynamical vector field:

X_{\cal L}=v^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}+W^{ik}\left(\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}-v^{j}\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial q^{j}\partial v^{i}}\right)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{k}}}\,,

(3.10)

where
W^{ik}
is the inverse matrix of the partial Hessian matrix of
{\cal L}
; that is,

W^{ik}W_{ji}=\delta^{k}_{j}
.

Remark 3.39

•

For Lagrangian systems, the triple

({\rm T}Q,\Omega_{{\cal L}},{\rm d}E_{{\cal L}})
is a Hamiltonian system
which is regular or singular depending on the regularity of the Lagrangian
{\cal L}
.
Thus, the Lagrangian formalism of these kinds of systems is a Hamiltonian formalism
with certain additional characteristics.

•

If the Lagrangian system is singular, then
({\rm T}Q,\Omega_{{\cal L}},{\rm d}E_{{\cal L}})

is a presymplectic Hamiltonian system and then equation (
3.4
)
is not necessarily compatible everywhere on
{\rm T}Q

and, even in the case that it has solution,
it is not unique and it is not necessarily a
sode
.
In fact, if
X_{\cal L}^{0}
is a solution, then
X_{\cal L}^{0}+Z
, with

Z\in\ker\,\Omega_{\cal L}
, is also a solution.
Then, in order to obtain the Euler–Lagrange equations (
3.6
),
the condition
J(X_{\cal L})=\Delta
must be added to the above Lagrangian equations.
In general, solutions
X_{\cal L}
could exist only in some submanifold
S_{f}\hookrightarrow{\rm T}Q
,
and a suitable
constraint algorithm
must be implemented in order to find
this
final constraint submanifold

S_{f}
(if it exists) where there are

sode
vector fields
X_{\cal L}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
,
tangent to
S_{f}
, which are (not necessarily unique) solutions to the Lagrangian equations on
S_{f}
.
All these problems have been also widely studied
(see, for instance,
[
28
,
29
,
72
,
73
,
184
,
185
,
223
,
281
]
).

3.3
Canonical Hamiltonian formalism for Lagrangian systems

The
canonical Hamiltonian formalism
of Mechanics was
initially introduced by
Hamilton, Lagrange, Poisson,
Ostrogadsky
and
Donkin
, among others, and constitutes the dual
formulation of the Lagrangian formalism.

3.3.1
Legendre map

The construction of the canonical Hamiltonian formalism is based on the introduction of a map:
the
fiber derivative
of the Lagrangian function:

Definition 3.40

Let
({\rm T}Q,{\cal L})
be a Lagrangian dynamical system.
The
Legendre map
associated with this system
is the fiber derivative of
{\cal L}
; that is the map

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}{\cal F}{\cal L}&\colon&{\rm T}Q&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&{\rm T}^{*}Q\\
&&(q,u)&\mapsto&(q,{\rm D}{\cal L}_{q}(u))\end{array}\ .

Local expression
:
Given a natural chart
(U;q^{i},v^{i})
in
{\rm T}Q

and the corresponding canonical coordinates

({\cal F}{\cal L}(U);q^{i},p_{i})
in
{\rm T}^{*}Q
,
the local expression of
{\cal F}{\cal L}
is

q^{i}\circ{\cal F}{\cal L}=q^{i}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptp_{i}\circ{\cal F}{\cal L}=\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\ ,

the coordinates
p_{i}
are called the

generalized momenta
associated with the generalized coordinates
q^{i}
.

A relevant characteristic of the Legendre map is given by the following:

Theorem 3.41

If
\Theta
and
\Omega
are the canonical forms in
{\rm T}^{*}Q
,
then
{\cal F}{\cal L}^{*}\Theta=\Theta_{\cal L}
and
{\cal F}{\cal L}^{*}\Omega=\Omega_{\cal L}
.

(
Proof
)
The local expression of
\Theta
is
\Theta=p_{i}{\rm d}q^{i}
; therefore

{\cal F}{\cal L}^{*}\Theta=({\cal F}{\cal L}^{*}p_{i}){\rm d}(q^{i}\circ{\cal F}{\cal L})=\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}{\rm d}q^{i}=\Theta_{\cal L}\ ,

and, bearing in mind that
{\cal F}{\cal L}^{*}
commutes with the exterior differential,
we obtain the result for
\Omega
.

Working also with local coordinates, it is immediate to prove that:

Proposition 3.42

{\cal L}
is a regular Lagrangian if, and only if,
{\cal F}{\cal L}
is a local diffeomorphism.

(
Proof
)
As
{\cal L}
is
{\rm C}^{\infty}
, the necessary and sufficient condition for

{\cal F}{\cal L}
to be a local diffeomorphism is that, for all
{\rm p}\in{\rm T}Q
,
the differential of
{\cal L}
at
{\rm p}
,
{\rm D}_{\rm p}{\cal F}{\cal L}
, is an isomorphism.
Then it suffices to analyze the local expression
of the Jacobian matrix of
{\cal F}{\cal L}
at
{\rm p}
, which is

{\cal H}_{{\cal F}{\cal L}}({\rm p})=\left(\begin{matrix}({\rm Id})_{n\times n}&(0)_{n\times n}\\
\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial q^{i}j\partial v^{i}}({\rm p})\Big)&\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{j}\partial v^{i}}({\rm p})\Big)\end{matrix}\right)\ ;

therefore,
{\rm det}\,{\cal H}_{{\cal F}{\cal L}}({\rm p})\not=0

if, and only if,
{\rm det}\,\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{i}\partial v^{j}}({\rm p})\Big)\not=0
.

This leads to:

Definition 3.43

{\cal L}
is a
hyperregular Lagrangian
if

{\cal F}{\cal L}
is a (global) diffeomorphism.

Regarding the singular Lagrangians, the most interesting ones are:

Definition 3.44

{\cal L}
is an
almost-regular Lagrangian
if:

1.

{\cal F}{\cal L}({\rm T}Q)\equiv P
is a closed submanifold of
{\rm T}^{*}Q
.

2.

{\cal F}{\cal L}
is a submersion onto its image.

3.

For every
{\rm p}\in{\rm T}Q
, the fibers
{\cal F}{\cal L}^{-1}({\cal F}{\cal L}({\rm p}))
are connected submanifolds of
{\rm T}Q
.

3.3.2
Canonical Hamiltonian formalism. Equivalence with the Lagrangian formalism

We study essentially the case of hiperregular systems.
Nevertheless, all the results hold also for the case de
regular systems in general,
changing
{\rm T}^{*}Q
by
{\cal F}{\cal L}({\rm T}Q)\subset{\rm T}^{*}Q

(or locally, at least).
First, as
{\cal F}{\cal L}
is a diffeomorphism we have:

Proposition 3.45

Let
({\rm T}Q,{\cal L})
be a hiperregular Lagrangian system.
Then there exists a unique function
{\rm h}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
such that

{\cal F}{\cal L}^{*}{\rm h}=E_{\cal L}
,
which is called the
Hamiltonian function
associated with the system

({\rm T}Q,{\cal L})
,
and the triple
({\rm T}^{*}Q,\Omega,{\rm h})
is the

canonical Hamiltonian system
associated with
({\rm T}Q,{\cal L})
.

As
E_{\cal L}
represents the energy
of the system in the Lagrangian formalism, the function
{\rm h}

represents the same observable in the canonical Hamiltonian formalism.

Therefore, we have the regular Hamiltonian system

({\rm T}^{*}Q,\Omega,{\rm d}{\rm h})
, where
\Omega
is the canonical
2
-form in

{\rm T}^{*}Q

fulfilling the Postulates and results established in Section
2.2
.
In particular, the Hamiltonian equations for vector fields and their
integral curves read

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\rm h})\Omega={\rm d}{\rm h}\hskip 10.00002pt

,

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002ptX_{\rm h}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}^{*}Q)\ .

(3.11)

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{c})(\Omega\circ c))={\rm d}{\rm h}\circ c\hskip 10.00002pt

,

\displaystyle c\colon I\subseteq\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}^{*}Q\ ;

(3.12)

Their local expressions in a natural chart of coordinates

(U;q^{i},p_{i})
in
{\rm T}^{*}Q
are the following:
if
\displaystyle X_{\rm h}|_{U}=f^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}+g_{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{i}}}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
,
then,

f^{i}=\frac{\partial{h}}{\partial{p_{i}}}\hskip 10.00002pt;\hskip 10.00002ptg_{i}=-\frac{\partial{h}}{\partial{q^{i}}}

and its integral curves
c(t)=(q^{i}(t),p_{i}(t))
solution to (
3.12
) are
the solutions to the system of first-order differential equations

\frac{dq^{i}}{dt}=\frac{\partial{h}}{\partial{p_{i}}}(c(t))\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dp_{i}}{dt}=-\frac{\partial{h}}{\partial{q^{i}}}(c(t))\ .

(3.13)

The relation between the Lagrangian and the canonical Hamiltonian formalisms of a (hyper)regular Lagrangian system is stated in the following:

Theorem 3.46

(Equivalence Theorem)

Let
({\rm T}Q,{\cal L})
be a (hyper)regular Lagrangian system.

1.

If
X_{\cal L}
is the Lagrangian vector field
solution to equations (
3.4
) and (
3.5
),
then there exists a unique vector field

{\cal F}{\cal L}_{*}X_{\cal L}\equiv X_{\rm h}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}^{*}Q)

which is the solution to equations (
3.11
).

Conversely, if
X_{\rm h}
is the Hamiltonian vector field
solution to equation (
3.11
),
then there exists a unique vector field

{\cal F}{\cal L}_{*}^{-1}X_{\rm h}\equiv X_{\cal L}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)

which is the solution to equations (
3.4
) and (
3.5
).

2.

Equivalently, if
\gamma\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q

is a curve and its canonical lift

\widetilde{\gamma}\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q
is a
solution to equation (
3.6
),
then
\zeta={\cal F}{\cal L}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}
is a curve
solution to equation (
3.12
).

Conversely, if
\zeta\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}^{*}Q

is a curve solution to equation (
3.12
),
then
\widetilde{\gamma}=\widetilde{\pi_{Q}\circ\zeta}\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q

is a curve solution to equation (
3.6
).

(
Proof
)

1.

For the first item we have that

0=\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\cal L})\Omega_{\cal L}-{\rm d}E_{\cal L}=\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\cal L})({\cal F}{\cal L}^{*}\Omega)-{\rm d}({\cal F}{\cal L}^{*}{\rm h})={\cal F}{\cal L}^{*}[\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\rm h})\Omega-{\rm d}{\rm h}]

and as
{\cal F}{\cal L}
is a diffeomorphism, this is equivalent to equation (
3.11
).
The proof that
X_{\cal L}
is a
sode
is a consequence of the regularity.

2.

For the second item, if
\widetilde{\gamma}

is a solution to equations (
3.6
)
then, by Theorem
3.35
, it is an integral curve of the Euler–Lagrange vector field
X_{\cal L}
,
and this means that

\dot{\widetilde{\gamma}}=X_{\cal L}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}
.
Then, as
\zeta={\cal F}{\cal L}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}
and
{\cal F}{\cal L}_{*}X_{\cal L}=X_{\rm h}
, we have that

\dot{\zeta}={\rm T}{\cal F}{\cal L}\circ\dot{\widetilde{\gamma}}={\rm T}{\cal F}{\cal L}\circ X_{\cal L}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}=X_{\rm h}\circ{\cal F}{\cal L}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}=X_{\rm h}\circ\zeta\ ;

then
\zeta
is an integral curve of the Hamiltonian vector field
X_{\rm h}

and, by Theorem
2.21
, it is a solution to equation (
3.12
)

Conversely, from a solution
\zeta
to the equation (
3.12
),
we construct the curve
\gamma=\pi_{Q}\circ\zeta
and its
canonical lift
\widetilde{\gamma}
. Then, reasoning as above, we conclude that
\widetilde{\gamma}
is a solution to (
3.6
).

The following (commutative) diagram summarizes the situation

\textstyle{{\rm T}{\rm T}Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm T}\mathcal{FL}}

\textstyle{{\rm T}{\rm T}^{*}Q}

\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\zeta}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\widetilde{\gamma}}}

\textstyle{{\rm T}Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{X_{\cal L}}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{FL}}

\scriptstyle{X_{\rm h}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{Q}}

\textstyle{Q}

Remark 3.47

If
({\rm T}Q,{\cal L})
is an almost-regular Lagrangian system
and
j_{0}\colon P\hookrightarrow{\rm T}^{*}Q
is the
natural embedding of
P\equiv{\cal F}{\cal L}({\rm T}Q)
in
{\rm T}^{*}Q
,
then it can be proved that there exists
{\rm h}_{0}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(P)

such that
{\cal F}{\cal L}_{0}^{*}{\rm h}_{0}=E_{\cal L}
, where

{\cal F}{\cal L}_{0}\colon{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>P
is defined by
{\cal F}{\cal L}=j_{0}\circ{\cal F}{\cal L}_{0}
.
This function is the
canonical Hamiltonian function
of the system,
and has the same physical interpretation than
{\rm h}
.
Now, taking
\Omega_{0}=j_{0}^{*}\Omega
, the triple

(P,\Omega_{0},{\rm d}{\rm h}_{0})
is in this case the
canonical Hamiltonian system

associated with
({\rm T}Q,{\cal L})
, which is the equivalent to
({\rm T}^{*}Q,\Omega,{\rm d}{\rm h})
in the regular case, and is a presymplectic Hamiltonian system, in general.
In particular, the equation equivalent to (
3.11
) is

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{{\rm h}_{0}})\Omega_{0}={\rm d}{\rm h}_{0}\hskip 10.00002pt;\hskip 10.00002ptX_{{\rm h}_{0}}\in\mathfrak{X}(P)\ ,

which, if
\Omega_{0}
is a presymplectic form,
is incompatible in general and, in the most interesting cases,
X_{{\rm h}_{0}}
exists only in
some submanifold
P_{f}\hookrightarrow P
, and is tangent to it.
Moreover, the solution is not unique, since if
X_{{\rm h}_{0}}
is a solution, then

X_{{\rm h}_{0}}+Z
, for every
Z\in\ker\,\Omega_{0}
, is also a solution.

Details on the construction of the canonical Hamiltonian formalism for
almost-regular Lagrangians and a deeper study on the equivalence of
both, the Lagrangian and Hamiltonian formalisms, for this case
can be found, for instance, in

[
28
,
70
,
72
,
73
,
184
,
195
,
223
,
322
]
).

3.3.3
Discussion and comparison

As a summary of the results in this section,
we state the essential characteristics of the Lagrangian and canonical Hamiltonian
formalisms of the Lagrangian dynamical systems.

In the Lagrangian formalism we have that:

1.

Local description:

(a)

To describe locally the
states
of the system we use
the
generalized coordinates

(q^{i})
(
i=1,\ldots,n
),
representing the degrees of freedom of the system, and
the
generalized velocities

(v^{i})
corresponding to each generalized coordinate.

(b)

The dynamical information of the system is given by the

Lagrangian function
of the system,
{\cal L}(q^{i},v^{i})
.

(c)

The dynamical evolution of the system is described by the
Euler–Lagrange equations
(
3.9
),
which are a second-order system of
n
differential equations for the functions
q^{i}(t)
.

(d)

The dynamical system is
regular
if

\displaystyle{\rm det}\left(\frac{\partial{{}^{2}{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}\partial v^{j}}}(q,v)\right)\not=0

in all the points
(q,v)
.
In this case, given a set of initial conditions
(that is, a state of the system), the local solution to the equations is unique.

2.

In the geometrical description, the above characteristics are translated as:

(a)

The phase space of the system is the tangent bundle

{\rm T}Q
of the manifold
Q
which constitutes the configuration space of the system.

(b)

The Lagrangian function
{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)

contains the dynamical information of the system.

(c)

Starting from this function and using the geometric structures
of the tangent bundle (the
canonical endomorphism
and the

Liouville vector field
), one can construct
the
Lagrange
2
-form

\Omega_{\cal L}

and the
Lagrangian energy
of the system,
E_{{\cal L}}
,
and write the Lagrangian dynamical equations which are

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{{\cal L}})\Omega_{{\cal L}}={\rm d}E_{{\cal L}}
,
together with the second-order condition
J(X_{\cal L})=\Delta
.
The integral curves of
X_{\cal L}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
are the
dynamical trajectories of the system.

(d)

The dynamical system is
regular
if

\Omega_{{\cal L}}
is a non-degenerated form.
In this case, the vector field
X_{\cal L}
is unique and necessarily a
sode
.

Concerning to the canonical Hamiltonian formalism, we have that:

1.

Local description:

(a)

The states of the system are now described
by generalized coordinates of position
(q^{i})

and their corresponding generalized momenta
(p_{i})
.

(b)

The dynamical information of the system is given by the

Hamiltonian function
of the system,
{\rm h}(q^{i},p_{i})
.

(c)

The dynamical evolution of the system is obtained from the

Hamilton equations
(
3.13
),
which are a first-order system of
2n
differential equations.

(d)

The dynamical system is
regular
if
{\cal F}{\cal L}
is a (local) diffeomorphism.

In this case, given a set of initial conditions
(that is, a state of the system), the solution to the equations is unique.

2.

In the geometrical description, the above characteristics are translated as::

(a)

The phase space of the system is
{\cal F}{\cal L}({\rm T}Q)
,
and is, whether the cotangent bundle

{\rm T}^{*}Q
of the manifold
Q
which constitutes the
configuration space of the system, or an open set
U\subset{\rm T}^{*}Q
,
or a submanifold
P\hookrightarrow{\rm T}^{*}Q

(depending on the regularity of
{\cal L}
).

(b)

The dynamical information is given by
{\rm h}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
,
(resp.
{\rm h}_{0}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(P)
): the
Hamiltonian function
.

(c)

The Hamiltonian dynamical equations are obtained
using this function and the canónical
2
-form
of the cotangent bundle
\Omega
, and they are

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\rm h})\Omega={\rm d}{\rm h}
(resp.
\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{{\rm h}_{0}})\Omega_{0}={\rm d}{\rm h}_{0}
).
The integral curves of the vector field solution are the
dynamical trajectories of the system.

(d)

The dynamical systems is
regular
if

{\cal F}{\cal L}
is a (local) diffeomorphism.

The canonical Hamiltonian formalism is especially interesting by the following facts:

•

Locally, it is manifest the asymmetry between the sets of variables
q^{i}

and
v^{i}
in the Lagrangian dynamical equations.
This does not happen in the dynamical equations of the
Hamiltonian formalism, with the coordinates
q^{i}
and
p_{i}
.

•

Geometrically,
in the Lagrangian formalism, the Lagrange 2-form and the
Lagrangian energy, which appear in the dynamical equations,
are obtained from the Lagrangian function and the canonical structures of the tangent bundle.
This means that the dynamical information is present in both the
Lagrangian 2-form and the Lagrangian energy.

On the contrary, in the canonical Hamiltonian formalism,
the corresponding geometric elements appearing
in the dynamical equations are the canonical
2
-form
of the cotangent bundle and the Hamiltonian function;
the first of them contains only the geometric information
and the second one only the dynamical information.

•

Finally, the characteristics of the canonical Hamiltonian formalism
