are suitable in order to make the quantization of the physical system and,
in particular, to implement the so-called
geometric quantization procedure

(see, for instance,
[
32
,
157
,
166
,
182
,
208
,
226
,
319
,
325
,
327
,
340
,
363
]
).

In conclusion, a
Lagrangian dynamical system

can be thought as a triple
({\rm T}Q,\Omega_{{\cal L}},E_{{\cal L}})

in the Lagrangian formalism, or
as a triple
({\rm T}^{*}Q,\Omega,{\rm h})
(or
(P,\Omega_{0},{\rm h}_{0})
),
in the canonical Hamiltonian formalism.
In both cases, the phase spaces are differential manifolds
endowed with closed differential 2-forms (symplectic or presymplectic,
depending on the regularity of the system).

3.3.4
Geometric Hamilton–Jacobi theory

One of the most relevant features of the study of Hamiltonian systems is the

Hamilton–Jacobi theory
, which gives a way to integrate
Hamilton equations (and systems of first-order ordinary differential equations in general).
The classical theory is based on using canonical transformations

[
11
,
222
,
165
,
237
,
313
]
and is also extended to singular systems
[
151
]

or higher-order dynamics
[
98
]
.
The geometric description of the standard theory and other geometric descriptions (for autonomous Hamiltonian systems)
is well-established in several texts, as
[
1
,
11
,
222
,
30
,
249
,
259
]
.
Following the ideas stated in
[
1
,
232
]
,
the Hamilton–Jacobi theory has been formulated, recently, in a new more general geometric perspective
both for the Lagrangian and the Hamiltonian formalisms,
for autonomous and nonautonomous mechanical systems
[
65
]
.
This framework has been extended to many other situations; namely,
to singular Lagrangian and singular Hamiltonian systems
[
134
,
135
,
244
]
,
holonomic and nonholonomic mechanics

[
26
,
66
,
129
,
221
,
244
,
287
,
289
]
,
higher-order dynamical systems
[
95
,
96
]
,
control theory
[
22
,
355
]
,
systems described using Poisson manifolds
[
135
,
203
]

and Lie algebroids
[
14
,
245
]
,
dissipative systems described using contact manifolds
[
119
,
120
,
204
]
,
discretization of dynamical systems
[
23
,
288
]
,
first-order classical field theories
[
48
,
128
,
132
,
137
,
142
]

and higher-order field theories
[
349
,
351
]
,
partial differential equations in general
[
350
,
352
]
,
and other geometric applications and generalizations
[
21
,
67
,
202
,
356
]
.
(A review on this approach and some of its applications is given in
[
311
]
).

Next, we review the foundations of this geometric framework of the Hamilton–Jacobi theory
for the Hamiltonian formalisms of autonomous dynamical systems.

Let
({\rm T}^{*}Q,\omega,{\rm h})
be a Hamiltonian system and
X_{\rm h}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}^{*}Q)

the Hamiltonian vector field.

Definition 3.48

The
generalized Hamiltonian Hamilton–Jacobi problem

consists in finding a vector field
X\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
and
a
1
-form
\alpha\in{\Omega}^{1}(Q)

such that, if
\gamma\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
is an integral curve
of
X
, then
\alpha\circ\gamma\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}^{*}Q

is an integral curve of
X_{\rm h}
; that is,
if
X\circ\gamma=\dot{\gamma}
, then

\dot{\overline{\alpha\circ\gamma}}=X_{\rm h}\circ(\alpha\circ\gamma)
.
The pair
(X,\alpha)
is said to be a
solution to the generalized Hamiltonian Hamilton–Jacobi problem
.

We have the following diagram:

\textstyle{{\rm T}Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm T}\alpha}

\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\rm T}({\rm T}^{*}Q)}
\scriptstyle{{\rm T}\pi_{Q}}

\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{X}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}

\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\rm T}^{*}Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{Q}}
\scriptstyle{X_{\rm h}}

Solutions to the generalized Hamiltonian Hamilton–Jacobi problem are characterized as follows:

Theorem 3.49

The following statements are equivalent:

1.

The pair
(X,\alpha)
is a solution to the generalized Hamiltonian Hamilton–Jacobi problem.

2.

The vector fields
X
and
X_{\rm h}
are
\alpha
-related; that is,

X_{\rm h}\circ\alpha={\rm T}\alpha\circ X
.

Therefore

X={\rm T}\pi_{Q}\circ X_{\rm h}\circ\alpha
and, as a consequence,
the integral curves of
X_{\rm h}
with initial conditions
in
\mathrm{Im}\,\alpha
project onto the integral curves of

X
.

X
is called the
vector field associated with the form
\alpha
.

3.

The submanifold
{\rm Im}\,\alpha
of
{\rm T}^{*}Q

is invariant by
X_{\rm h}

(that is,
X_{\rm h}
is tangent to
{\rm Im}\,\alpha
).

4.

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X){\rm d}\alpha=-{\rm d}(\alpha^{*}H)
.

(
Proof
)
The equivalence between 1 and 2 is as follows:
If
(X,\alpha)
satisfies the condition in Definition
3.48
then,
for every integral curve of
X
,

\gamma\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
, by definition
X\circ\gamma=\dot{\gamma}
,
then

X_{\rm h}\circ\alpha\circ\gamma=\dot{\overline{\alpha\circ\gamma}}=T\alpha\circ\dot{\gamma}={\rm T}\alpha\circ X\circ\gamma\ ;

and hence
X_{\rm h}\circ\alpha={\rm T}\alpha\circ X
.
The proof of the converse is immediate.

From here, composing both members of this equality with
{\rm T}\pi_{Q}

and taking into account that
\pi_{Q}\circ\alpha={\rm Id}_{Q}
,
we obtain that
X={\rm T}\pi_{Q}\circ X_{\rm h}\circ\alpha
.
As a consequence,
we conclude that the
\pi_{Q}
-projection of the integral curves of
X_{\rm h}

on
{\rm Im}\,\alpha
are the integral curves of
X
.

For the equivalence between 2 and 3 we have that,
if
X_{\rm h}\circ\alpha={\rm T}\alpha\circ X
, then

X_{\rm h}(\alpha({\rm q}))={\rm T}\alpha(X({\rm q}))
, for every
{\rm q}\in Q

and therefore
X_{\rm h}
is tangent to
{\rm Im}\,\alpha
.
Conversely, if
{\rm Im}\,\alpha
is invariant by
X_{\rm h}
then

X_{\rm h}(\alpha({\rm q}))\in{\rm T}_{\alpha({\rm q})}{\rm Im}\,\alpha
,
which implies that there exists
{\rm u}\in{\rm T}_{\rm q}Q
such that

X_{\rm h}(\alpha({\rm q}))={\rm T}_{\rm q}\alpha({\rm u})
. Defining

X
by
{\rm T}_{\rm q}\alpha(X_{\rm q})=X_{\rm h}(\alpha({\rm q}))
,
we have that
X
is differentiable, since
X={\rm T}\pi_{Q}\circ X_{\rm h}\circ\alpha
,
and thus
X
is a vector field in
Q
satisfying that

X_{\rm h}\circ\alpha={\rm T}\alpha\circ X
, and then
\alpha
is a solution to the
generalized Hamilton–Jacobi problem.

If
(X,\alpha)
is a solution to the generalized Hamilton–Jacobi problem, by item 2 we have that
X={\rm T}\pi_{Q}\circ X_{\rm h}\circ\alpha

and

Finally, the proof of the equivalence with 4 is the following:
from the Hamiltonian equation (
3.11
) we obtain that

\alpha^{*}\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\rm h})\omega=\alpha^{*}{\rm d}{\rm h}={\rm d}(\alpha^{*}{\rm h})\ .

As
\Theta
is the canonical form of
{\rm T}^{*}Q
, then

\alpha^{*}\Theta=\alpha
, and

\alpha^{*}\Omega=-\alpha^{*}{\rm d}\Theta=-{\rm d}(\alpha^{*}\Theta)=-{\rm d}\alpha\ ,

(3.14)

and, as
X
and
X_{\rm h}
are
\alpha
-related, we have

\alpha^{*}\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\rm h})\omega=\mathop{i}\nolimits(X)\alpha^{*}\Omega=-\mathop{i}\nolimits(X){\rm d}\alpha\ ,

which yields condition 4.
For the converse, first define

Z_{\rm h}=X_{\rm h}\circ\alpha-{\rm T}\alpha\circ X\colon Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
,
which is a vector field along
\alpha
;
then we have to prove that
Z_{\rm h}=0
.
First, we have that
Z_{\rm h}
is
\pi_{Q}
-vertical since,
as
\pi_{Q}\circ\alpha={\rm Id}_{Q}
,

\displaystyle{\rm T}\pi_{Q}\circ Z_{\rm h}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm T}\pi_{Q}\circ(X_{\rm h}\circ\alpha-{\rm T}\alpha\circ X)={\rm T}\pi_{Q}\circ(X_{\rm h}\circ\alpha-{\rm T}\alpha\circ{\rm T}\pi_{Q}\circ X_{\rm h}\circ\alpha)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm T}\pi_{Q}\circ X_{\rm h}\circ\alpha-{\rm T}\pi_{Q}\circ X_{\rm h}\circ\alpha=0\ .

From equation (
3.11
)
and the hypothesis, as
\alpha^{*}\Omega=-{\rm d}\alpha
, we obtain

\displaystyle\alpha^{*}\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\rm h})\Omega=\alpha^{*}{\rm d}{\rm h}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(\alpha^{*}{\rm h})\ ,

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(X)\alpha^{*}\Omega=-\mathop{i}\nolimits(X){\rm d}\alpha

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm d}(\alpha^{*}{\rm h})\ ,

hence
\alpha^{*}\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\rm h})\Omega-\mathop{i}\nolimits(X)\alpha^{*}\Omega=0
.
Therefore, for every
{\rm q}\in Q
and
Y_{\rm q}\in{\rm T}_{\rm q}Q
, we have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\alpha^{*}\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\rm h})\Omega-\mathop{i}\nolimits(X)\alpha^{*}\Omega)_{\rm q}(Y_{\rm q})=\Omega_{\alpha({\rm q})}(X_{\rm h}(\alpha({\rm q})),{\rm T}_{\rm q}\alpha(Y_{\rm q}))-\Omega_{\alpha({\rm q})}({\rm T}_{\rm q}\alpha(X_{\rm q}),{\rm T}_{\rm q}\alpha(Y_{\rm q}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Omega_{\alpha({\rm q})}(Z_{\rm h}({\rm q}),{\rm T}_{\rm q}\alpha(Y_{\rm q}))\ .

Furthermore, as
{\rm V}(\pi_{Q})
is a Lagrangian distribution in

({\rm T}^{*}Q,\Omega)
, for every
\pi_{Q}
-vertical vector field
V\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}^{*}Q)

we have that

\Omega_{\alpha({\rm q})}(Z_{\rm h}({\rm q}),V(\alpha({\rm q})))=0
;
but
{\rm T}_{\alpha({\rm q})}{\rm T}^{*}Q={\rm T}_{\alpha({\rm q})}({\rm Im}\,\alpha)\oplus{\rm V}_{\alpha({\rm q})}(\pi_{Q})
,
and thus we have proved that

\Omega_{\alpha({\rm q})}(Z_{\rm h}({\rm q}),Z(\alpha({\rm q})))=0\ ;

for every
Z\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
. Then we conclude that
Z_{\rm h}=0
,
since
\Omega
is non-degenerate;
or what is equivalent,

X
and
X_{\rm h}
are
\alpha
-related, and thus

(X,\alpha)
is a solution to the generalized Hamilton–Jacobi problem

To solve the generalized Hamilton–Jacobi problem is, in general, a very difficult task.
Then, it is usual to state a less general version of the problem
which leads to the standard version of the Hamilton–Jacobi problem:

Definition 3.50

The
Hamiltonian Hamilton–Jacobi problem

consists in finding a closed
1
-form
\alpha\in{\Omega}^{1}({\rm T}^{*}Q)

which is a solution to the generalized Hamiltonian Hamilton–Jacobi problem.
This form
\alpha
is said to be a
solution to the Hamiltonian Hamilton–Jacobi problem
.

As
{\rm d}\alpha=0
, for every point in
Q
,
there exists a function
S
in a neighbourhood
U\subset Q

such that
\alpha={\rm d}S
.
Then, we say that
S
is a
local generating function
of the solution
\alpha
.

Theorem 3.51

The following statements are equivalent:

1.

The form
\alpha\in{\Omega}^{1}(Q)
is a solution to the Hamiltonian Hamilton–Jacobi problem.

2.

\mathrm{Im}\,\alpha
is a Lagrangian submanifold of

{\rm T}^{*}Q
, which is invariant by
X_{\rm h}
,
and
S
is a local generating function of this Lagrangian submanifold.

3.

The condition
{\rm d}(\alpha^{*}{\rm h})=0
holds
or, what is equivalent, the function

{\rm h}\circ{\rm d}S\colon Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}
is locally constant.

(
Proof
)
These statements are consequences of Theorem
3.49
and Definition
3.50
.
In fact, if
\alpha
is a solution to the Hamilton–Jacobi problem,
as a consequence of (
3.14
),

d\alpha=0
is equivalent to
\alpha^{*}\Omega=0
. Then

{\rm Im}\,\alpha
is a Lagrangian submanifold of
({\rm T}^{*}Q,\Omega)
,
which is contained in a level set of

{\rm h}
, because the condition
\mathop{i}\nolimits(X){\rm d}\alpha=-{\rm d}(\alpha^{*}{\rm h})

implies that
d(\alpha^{*}{\rm h})=0
.
Notice that
\dim\,{\rm Im}\,\alpha=n
and,
if
j_{\alpha}\colon{\rm Im}\,\alpha\hookrightarrow{\rm T}^{*}Q

is the natural embedding, we have that
j_{\alpha}^{*}\Omega=0
.

In natural coordinates of
{\rm T}^{*}Q
, condition (3) is the classical form of the
Hamiltonian Hamilton–Jacobi equation:

H\left(q^{i},\frac{\partial{S}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\right)=E\ (ctn.)\ .

(3.15)

Until now, we have only considered particular solutions
\alpha

to the (generalized) Hamilton–Jacobi problem, which are given by particular solutions
to the partial differential equation (
3.15
).
Nevertheless, we are also interested in the general solution. Then:

Definition 3.52

Let
\Lambda\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
A family of solutions
\{\alpha_{\lambda};\lambda\in\Lambda\}

(which depends on
n
parameters

\lambda\equiv(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n})\in\Lambda
)
is a
complete solution
to the Hamilton–Jacobi problem
if the map

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\Phi&\colon&Q\times\Lambda&\longrightarrow&{\rm T}^{*}Q\\
&&(q,\lambda)&\mapsto&\alpha_{\lambda}(q)\end{array}

is a local diffeomorphism.

Remark 3.53

•

Given a complete solution
\{\alpha_{\lambda};\lambda\in\Lambda\}
,
since
{\rm d}\alpha_{\lambda}=0
, for every
\lambda\in\Lambda
,
there is a family of functions
\{S_{\lambda}\}
defined in
open sets
U_{\lambda}\subset Q
such that
\alpha_{\lambda}={\rm d}S_{\lambda}
.
Therefore, we have a function

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}{\cal S}&\colon&\bigcap U_{\lambda}\times\Lambda\subset Q\times\Lambda&\longrightarrow&\mathbb{R}\\
&&(q,\lambda)&\mapsto&S_{\lambda}(q)\end{array}

which is locally defined, and is called a
local generating function
of the
complete solution
\{\alpha_{\lambda};\lambda\in\Lambda\}
.

•

Every complete solution defines a Lagrangian foliation in
{\rm T}^{*}Q

which is transverse to the fibers,
and such that
X_{\rm h}
is tangent to the leaves.
This foliation is locally defined by a family of functions which are the components of a map

F\colon{\rm T}^{*}Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi^{-1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}Q\times\Lambda{\longrightarrow}\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Furthermore, these functions are a set of constants of motion of
X_{\rm h}
.

Conversely, from a set
n
first integrals
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}

of
X_{\rm h}
in involution, such that
{\rm d}f_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge{\rm d}f_{n}\not=0
;
we can define a
\pi
-transversal Lagrangian foliation of
{\rm T}^{*}Q

taking
f_{i}=\lambda_{i}
, with
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
,
and in this way we obtain a local complete solution
\{\alpha_{\lambda},\lambda\in\Lambda\}
.
Then, from equations
f_{i}=\lambda_{i}
, we can locally isolate

p_{i}=p_{i}(q,\lambda)
, replace them in the expression of
X_{\rm h}

and finally project to the basis, then obtaining the family of vector fields

\{X_{\lambda}\}
associated with the local complete solution.
From a complete solution
\{\alpha_{\lambda};\lambda\in\Lambda\}
,
all the integral curves of
X_{\rm h}
are obtained starting from
the integral curves of the vector fields
\{X_{\lambda}\}

associated to this complete solution.

•

This geometric framework for the Hamilton–Jacobi theory
can be also stated in a very natural way for the Lagrangian formalism
(see
[
65
]
).

The “classical” Hamilton–Jacobi problem for a (regular) Hamiltonian system

({\rm T}^{*}Q,\Omega,{\rm h})
consists in
obtaining a canonical transformation which leads
the system to equilibrium
[
11
,
222
,
237
,
313
]
.
This transformation is given by a generating function, which is
the solution to the
Hamilton–Jacobi equation
.
From a geometric point of view, this canonical transformation
is associated with a foliation in the phase space of the system,
{\rm T}^{*}Q
,
which has the following properties:
it is invariant by the dynamics, transverse to the canonical projection of the cotangent bundle,
and is Lagrangian with respect to the
canonical symplectic structure of
{\rm T}^{*}Q
.
Then, the restriction of the Hamiltonian vector field
X_{\rm h}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
to
each leaf
S_{\lambda}
of this foliation projects onto a vector field
X_{\lambda}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
,
and the integral curves of
X_{\rm h}
and
X_{\lambda}
are one-to-one related.
In this way, all the dynamical trajectories are recovered from the integral curves
of all these vector fields
\{X_{\lambda}\}
.
Thus, the geometric Hamilton–Jacobi problem consists in finding
this foliation and the vector fields
\{X_{\lambda}\}
.

Bearing this in mind, the relation between the classical and the geometric Hamilton–Jacobi theories
is established through the equivalence of complete solutions
and canonical transformations (see
[
311
,
352
]
).

Theorem 3.54

A complete solution
\{\alpha_{\lambda};\lambda\in\Lambda\}

to the Hamilton–Jacobi problem provides a canonical transformation

\Phi\colon{\rm T}^{*}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}^{*}Q
leading the system to equilibrium,
and conversely.

(
Proof
)
Let
\{\alpha_{\lambda};\lambda\in\Lambda\}
be a complete solution, and
let
{\cal S}
be a generating function of it
in a neighbourhood of every point of
{\rm T}^{*}Q
.
As
{\cal S}={\cal S}(q^{i},\lambda^{i})
, the set
\lambda^{i}
can be identified
with a subset of coordinates
\lambda^{i}\equiv\tilde{q}^{i}
in
{\rm T}^{*}Q\times{\rm T}^{*}Q
,
and therefore
{\cal S}={\cal S}(q^{i},\tilde{q}^{i})
can be thought as
the local expression of a generating function
of a local canonical transformation
\Phi
, and hence
of an open set
W
of the Lagrangian submanifold

graph\,\Phi\hookrightarrow{\rm T}^{*}Q\times{\rm T}^{*}Q
.
When this construction is done in every chart, we obtain the transformation
\Phi

and the submanifold
graph\,\Phi
.
Finally, since (
3.15
) holds for every particular solution
S_{\lambda}
, we have that

E={\rm h}\left(q^{i}(\tilde{q},\tilde{p}),\frac{\partial{{\cal S}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}(q(\tilde{q},\tilde{p}),\tilde{q})\right)={\rm h}(\tilde{q}^{i},\tilde{p}_{i})\ .

Conversely, starting from a canonical transformation
\Phi
and a
generating function
{\cal S}={\cal S}(q^{i},\tilde{q}^{i})
;
if we take
\tilde{q}\equiv(\tilde{q}^{i})=(\lambda^{i})\equiv\lambda
, we obtain
a family of functions
\{S_{\lambda}\}
and then we get a
local complete solution
\{\alpha_{\lambda}={\rm d}S_{\lambda};\lambda\in\Lambda\}

to the Hamiltonian Hamilton–Jacobi problem.
Doing this construction in every chart, we have the complete solution.
This means that, on each local chart of
{\rm T}^{*}Q
,
fixing the coordinates
\tilde{q}^{i}=\lambda^{i}
of a point,
we obtain a local submanifold of
{\rm T}^{*}Q
whose image by
\Phi^{-1}
is
the image of a local section
\alpha_{\lambda}\colon Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}^{*}Q

which constitutes a particular solution to the Hamiltonian Hamilton–Jacobi problem.

3.4
Skinner-Rusk unified Lagrangian-Hamiltonian formalism

In their seminal articles of 1983
[
322
,
323
]
,

R. Skinner
and
R. Rusk
proposed a new geometric framework
in order to unify the Lagrangian and the Hamiltonian formalisms
of first-order autonomous mechanical systems
into a single one formulation.
This is a simpler and elegant framework
which is particularly suitable for the treatment of singular systems.
Later, this nice formalism was generalized to many other types of physical systems;
such as nonautonomous dynamical systems
[
25
,
52
,
194
]
,
vakonomic and nonholonomic mechanics
[
100
]

control systems
[
24
,
94
]
,
higher-order mechanics
[
296
,
297
]
,
dissipative systems (first and higher-order contact mechanics)
[
113
,
114
]
,
and first-order and higher-order classical field theories

[
49
,
56
,
126
,
153
,
299
,
305
,
306
,
348
]
.

In this section, we describe the main features of this so-called
Skinner-Rusk

or
unified Lagrangian-Hamiltonian formalism
for autonomous dynamical systems.

3.4.1
Unified bundle. Unified formalism

This formalism is developed in the following bundle:

Definition 3.55

The
unified bundle
or
Pontryagin bundle

is
{\cal W}={\rm T}Q\times_{Q}{\rm T}^{*}Q

and has natural projections

\varrho_{1}\colon{\cal W}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q\ ,\ \varrho_{2}\colon{\cal W}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}^{*}Q\ ,\ \varrho_{0}\colon{\cal W}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q\ ,

Natural coordinates in
{\cal W}
are
(q^{i},v^{i},p_{i})
.

Definition 3.56

A curve
c\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\cal W}

is
holonomic
in
{\cal W}
if

\varrho_{1}\circ c\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q
is holonomic.

A vector field
\Gamma\in\mathfrak{X}({\cal W})
is a
holonomic vector field
in
{\cal W}

if its integral curves are holonomic in
{\cal W}
.

The coordinate expressions of holonomic curves and vector fields in
{\cal W}
are the following

\displaystyle c(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(q^{i}(t),\frac{dq^{i}}{dt}(t),p_{i}(t),\Big)\ ,

\displaystyle\Gamma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}+F^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}+G_{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{i}}}\ .

Definition 3.57

The unified bundle
{\cal W}
is endowed with the following canonical structures:

1.

The
coupling function
is the
map
{\cal C}\colon{\cal W}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}
defined by

\begin{array}[]{rclcc}{\cal C}&\colon&{\rm T}Q\times_{Q}{\rm T}^{*}Q&\longrightarrow&\mathbb{R}\\
&&(q,v_{q},\xi_{q})=(q^{i},v^{i},p_{i})&\longmapsto&\langle v_{q}\mid\xi_{q}\ \rangle=v^{i}p_{i}\end{array}\ .

2.

If
\Theta\in{\Omega}^{1}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
and
\Omega=-{\rm d}\Theta\in{\Omega}^{2}({\rm T}^{*}Q)

are the canonical forms in
{\rm T}^{*}Q
,
the
canonical forms
in
{\cal W}
are

\Theta_{\cal W}:=\varrho_{2}^{*}\,\Theta\in{\Omega}^{1}({\cal W})\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\Omega_{\cal W}:=-{\rm d}\Theta_{\cal W}=\varrho_{2}^{*}\,\Omega\in{\Omega}^{2}({\cal W})\ .

And using the coupling function, we introduce:

Definition 3.58

Given a Lagrangian function
{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)
, if

{\mathfrak{L}}=\varrho_{1}^{*}{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\cal W})
, the
Hamiltonian function
is defined as

{\cal H}:={\cal C}-{\mathfrak{L}}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\cal W})\ .

The coordinate expressions of these elements are

\Theta_{\cal W}=p_{i}{\rm d}q^{i}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\Omega_{\cal W}={\rm d}q^{i}\wedge{\rm d}p_{i}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt{\cal H}=v^{i}p_{i}-{\mathfrak{L}}(q^{i},v^{i})\ .

The triple
({\cal W},\Omega_{\cal W},{\cal H})

is a presymplectic Hamiltonian system since

\displaystyle\ker\Omega_{\cal W}=\left\langle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\right\rangle
.
Then, the
dynamical problem
for this system consists in finding
X_{\cal H}\in\mathfrak{X}({\cal W})

which is a solution to the Hamiltonian equations

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\cal H})\Omega_{\cal W}={\rm d}{\cal H}\ ,

(3.16)

and then the integral curves
c\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\cal W}

of
X_{\cal H}
are solutions to the equations

\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{c})(\Omega_{\cal W}\circ c)={\rm d}{\cal H}\circ c\ .

(3.17)

As
({\cal W},\Omega_{\cal W},{\cal H})
is a presymplectic Hamiltonian system,
these equations are not compatible in
{\cal W}
.
In fact, for an arbitrary vector field in
\mathfrak{X}({\cal W})
,

X_{\cal H}=f^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}+F^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}+G_{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{i}}}\ ,

equations (
3.16
) give

f^{i}=v^{i}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptG_{i}=\displaystyle\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptp_{i}=\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\ .

(3.18)

•

The first equations assure that
X_{\cal H}
is a holonomic vector field in
{\cal W}

(regardless of the regularity of the Lagrangian function).

•

The second equations allow us to determine the component functions
G_{i}
.

•

The third equations are compatibility conditions;
that is,
compatibility constraints
defining a submanifold

{\cal W}_{0}\hookrightarrow{\cal W}

where vector fields
X_{\cal H}
solution to (
3.16
) are defined.
Observe that these constraints give the Legendre map and hence
{\cal W}_{0}={\rm graph}({{\cal F}{\cal L}})
.

Thus, we have that

X_{\cal H}|_{{\cal W}_{0}}=v^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}+F^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}+\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{i}}}\ ,

where the functions
F^{i}
are still undetermined.
Nevertheless, the constraint algorithm for presymplectic systems continues by demanding that

X_{\cal H}
is tangent to
{\cal W}_{0}
; that is, we have

\displaystyle{X_{\cal H}\Big(p_{i}-\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\Big)}\Big|_{{}_{{\cal W}_{0}}}=0,

which gives the equations for the remaining coefficients
F^{i}
,

\frac{\partial^{2}L}{\partial v^{i}\partial v^{j}}F^{j}+\ \frac{\partial^{2}L}{\partial q^{j}\partial v^{i}}v^{j}-\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}=0\hskip 10.00002pt(\text{on }\mathcal{W}_{0})\ .

(3.19)

If
{\cal L}
is regular, these equations are compatible and define a unique vector field
X_{\cal H}
solution to (
3.16
) on
{\cal W}_{0}
,
and the last system of equations give the dynamical trajectories.
If
{\cal L}
is singular, equations (
3.19
) can be compatible or not.
and, eventually, new compatibility constraints can appear
that define a new submanifold
{\cal W}_{1}\hookrightarrow{\cal W}_{0}
.
In that case, the constraint algorithm continues by demanding the tangency of solutions to the new constraint submanifold
{\cal W}_{1}
and so on.
In the most favorable cases,
there is a submanifold
{\cal W}_{f}\hookrightarrow{\cal W}_{0}
(it could be
{\cal W}_{f}={\cal W}_{0}
)
such that there exist holonomic vector fields
X_{\cal H}\in\mathfrak{X}({\cal W})
defined on
{\cal W}_{0}
and tangent to
{\cal W}_{f}
,
which are solutions to equations (
3.16
)
at support on
{\cal W}_{f}
.

3.4.2
Recovering the Lagrangian and Hamiltonian formalisms

Next, we study the equivalence of the unified formalism
with the Lagrangian and the Hamiltonian formalisms.
We restrict our analysis to the hyperregular case
(the regular case is the same, at least locally).

Denoting by
\jmath_{0}\colon{\cal W}_{0}\hookrightarrow{\cal W}

the natural embedding, we have that

(\varrho_{1}\circ\jmath_{0})({\cal W}_{0})={\rm T}Q\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt(\varrho_{2}\circ\jmath_{0})({\cal W}_{0})={\rm T}^{*}Q\ ,

and being
{\cal W}_{0}
the graph of the Legendre map,
the restricted projection
\varrho_{1}\circ\jmath_{0}

is a diffeomorphism between

{\cal W}_{0}
and
{\rm T}Q
.
The following diagram summarizes the situation:

\textstyle{{\cal W}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varrho_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\varrho_{2}}

\textstyle{{\cal W}_{0}={\rm graph}\,({\cal F}{\cal L})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varrho_{1}\circ\jmath_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\varrho_{2}\circ\jmath_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\jmath_{0}}

\textstyle{{\rm T}Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\cal F}{\cal L}}

\textstyle{{\rm T}^{*}Q}

Therefore, functions, differential forms, and vector fields in
{\cal W}
tangent to
{\cal W}_{0}

can be restricted to
{\cal W}_{0}
, and then
they can be translated to the Lagrangian side
by using this diffeomorphism, and to the Hamiltonian side
using the Legendre map and the projection
\varrho_{2}
.

In particular, if
c(t)=(q^{i}(t),v^{i}(t),p_{i}(t))
is a solution to equation (
3.16
)
(or, what is equivalent,
c(t)
is an integral curve of the vector field

\,X_{\cal H}
solution to the dynamical equations (
3.16
))
then equation (
3.19
) leads to

\frac{d}{dt}\Big(\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\circ c\Big)=\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\circ c\ ,

(3.20)

and from equations (
3.18
) we obtain

\frac{dq^{i}}{dt}=v^{i}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dp_{i}}{dt}=\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\circ c=-\frac{\partial{{\cal H}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\circ c\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptp_{i}=\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\circ c\ .

(3.21)

From the first group of equations (
3.21
), together with (
3.20
),
we recover the Euler–Lagrange equations for the curves
c_{\cal L}(t)=(q^{i}(t),v^{i}(t))
.
Furthermore, bearing in mind the local expression of
{\cal H}
,
we have that
\displaystyle\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}=-\frac{\partial{{\cal H}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}
and hence
the second group of equations (
3.21
) reads

\frac{dp_{i}}{dt}=-\frac{\partial{{\cal H}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\circ c

and, using again the local expression of
{\cal H}
and the first group of equations (
3.21
), we get

\frac{\partial{{\cal H}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\circ c=v^{i}=\frac{dq^{i}}{dt}\ ;

finally, using the third group of equations (
3.18
) (that is, the Legendre map)
we have that
{\cal H}={\cal F}{\cal L}^{*}{\rm h}
, and these last equations become the
Hamilton equations

for the curves
c_{\rm h}(t)=(q^{i}(t),p_{i}(t))
.

In this way, for the dynamical trajectories, we can state.

Theorem 3.59

Every curve
c\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\cal W}
,
taking values in
{\cal W}_{0}
can be viewed as

c=(c_{\cal L},c_{\rm h})
, where

c_{\cal L}=\varrho_{1}\circ c\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q

and
c_{\rm h}={\cal F}{\cal L}\circ c_{\cal L}\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}^{*}Q
.

If
c\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\cal W}
,
with
{\rm Im}\,c\subset{\cal W}_{0}
,
is a curve fulfilling equation (
3.17
), then

c_{\cal L}
is the lift to

{\rm T}Q
of the projected curve

c_{o}=\varrho_{0}\circ c\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q

(that is,
c_{\cal L}
is a holonomic curve),
and it is a solution to equation (
3.6
),
where
E_{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)
is such that
{\cal H}=\varrho_{1}^{*}E_{\cal L}
.
Moreover, the curve

c_{\rm h}=\varrho_{2}\circ c={\cal F}{\cal L}\circ c_{\cal L}

is a solution to equation (
3.12
),
where
{\rm h}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
is such that

{\cal H}=\varrho_{1}^{*}{\rm h}
.

Conversely, if
c_{o}\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
is a curve such that

\widetilde{c}_{o}\equiv c_{\cal L}
is a solution to equation (
3.6
), then the curve

c=(c_{\cal L},{\cal F}{\cal L}\circ c_{\cal L})

is a solution to equation (
3.17
)
and
{\cal F}{\cal L}\circ c_{\cal L}

is a solution to equation (
3.12
)
.

Now, the curves
c\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\cal W}
which are
solution to equation (
3.17
)
are the integral curves of a holonomic vector field
X_{\cal H}\in\mathfrak{X}({\cal W})

which is the solution to (
3.16
),
the curves
c_{\cal L}\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q

are the integral curves of the holonomic vector field
X_{\cal L}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
which is the
solution to (
3.4
), and the curves
c_{\rm h}\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}^{*}Q

are the integral curves of the vector field
X_{\rm h}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}^{*}Q)

which is the solution to (
3.11
).
Then, as a corollary of the above theorem,
for the dynamical vector fields we have:

Theorem 3.60

Let
X_{\cal H}\in\mathfrak{X}({\cal W})
be the solution to equations (
3.16
) (on
{\cal W}_{0}
),
which is tangent to
{\cal W}_{0}
. Then:

The vector field
X_{\cal L}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
, defined by

X_{\cal L}\circ\varrho_{1}={\rm T}\varrho_{1}\circ X_{\cal H}
,
is the solution to equations (
3.4
) and (
3.5
),
where
E_{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)
is such that
{\cal H}=\varrho_{1}^{*}E_{\cal L}
.

The vector field
X_{\rm h}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
, defined by

X_{\rm h}\circ\varrho_{2}={\rm T}\varrho_{2}\circ X_{\cal H}
,
is the solution to equations (
3.11
),
where
{\rm h}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
is such that

{\cal H}=\varrho_{1}^{*}{\rm h}
.
Furthermore
{\cal F}{\cal L}_{*}X_{\cal L}=X_{\rm h}
.

Summarizing, the main features of the unified formalism are:

•

It assures holonomy (even in the non-regular case).

•

It provides the Legendre map.

•

It gives the Euler–Lagrange and the Hamilton equations.

3.5
Symmetries of regular Lagrangian systems

For Lagrangian dynamical systems,
the phase space of the system is either

M={\rm T}Q
in the Lagrangian formalism, or
M={\rm T}^{*}Q
,
in the canonical Hamiltonian formalism.
In these cases, there exist distinguished symplectic potentials:
the Lagrangian
1
-form
\Theta_{\cal L}\in{\Omega}^{1}({\rm T}Q)
and the
canonical
1
-form
\Theta\in{\Omega}^{1}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
.
Furthermore, the symmetries of the
dynamical systems use to be canonical lifts
of diffeomorphisms or vector fields on the base manifold
Q
.
All of this lead to introduce new kinds of symmetries in that cases,
whose properties are studied next.

3.5.1
Symmetries in the canonical Hamiltonian formalism

Consider a (regular) canonical Hamiltonian system
({\rm T}^{*}Q,\Omega,{\rm h})
,
and let
X_{\rm h}\in\mathfrak{X}_{lh}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
be the Hamiltonian vector field of the system.

Obviously, all we have stated for Hamiltonian dynamical systems
in Section
2.3
holds for this particular case.
Nevertheless, new kinds of symmetry can be introduced for this situation.
Thus, in addition to the previous symmetries already defined,
we can consider the following particular cases:

Definition 3.61

A dynamical symmetry
\Phi\in{\rm Diff}\,({\rm T}^{*}Q)
of the canonical Hamiltonian system
is a
natural dynamical symmetry
if there exists
\varphi\in{\rm Diff}\,(Q)

such that
\Phi={\rm T}^{*}\varphi
(that is,
\Phi
is the canonical lift
of a diffeomorphism in
Q
).

Definition 3.62

An infinitesimal dynamical symmetry
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
of the canonical Hamiltonian system
is a
natural infinitesimal dynamical symmetry
if there exists
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)

such that
Y=Z^{*}
(that is,
Y
is the canonical lift of a vector field in
Q

11
11

11

The terminology “natural symmetries” refers both to the diffeomorphism
\varphi

or the vector field
Z
.
).

Remember that
({\rm T}^{*}Q,\Omega)
is an exact
symplectic manifold and a symplectic potential of
\Omega

is the canonical
1
-form
\Theta\in{\Omega}^{1}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
.
Furthermore, for every
\varphi\in{\rm Diff}\,(Q)
,
we have that
({\rm T}^{*}\varphi)^{*}\Theta=\Theta
, and hence

({\rm T}^{*}\varphi)^{*}\Omega=\Omega
.
In the same way, for every
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
,
we have that
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Z^{*})\Theta=0
, and then
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Z^{*})\Omega=0
;
therefore, if
Z^{*}
is an infinitesimal natural dynamical symmetry,
then
Z^{*}\in\mathfrak{X}_{H}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
and, as we saw in Proposition
6
,
the global Hamiltonian function of
Z^{*}
is
f_{Z}=\mathop{i}\nolimits(Z^{*})\Theta
(up to constants).
This leads to introduce the following particular type of Noether symmetries
for the canonical Hamiltonian system
({\rm T}^{*}Q,\Omega,{\rm h})
:

Definition 3.63

A diffeomorphism
\Phi\in{\rm Diff}\,({\rm T}^{*}Q)
is a

natural Noether symmetry
if:

1.

There exists a diffeomorphism
\varphi\in{\rm Diff}\,(Q)
such that

\Phi={\rm T}^{*}\varphi
.

2.

\Phi^{*}{\rm h}=({\rm T}^{*}\varphi)^{*}{\rm h}={\rm h}
.

Definition 3.64

A vector field
Y\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
is an

infinitesimal natural Noether symmetry
if:

1.

There exists a vector field
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
such that
Y=Z^{*}
.

2.

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y){\rm h}=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Z^{*}){\rm h}=0
.

Other particular cases of Noether symmetries in this formalism are:

Definition 3.65

A Noether symmetry is
exact
if
\Phi^{*}\Theta=\Theta
.

Definition 3.66

An infinitesimal Noether symmetry is
exact
if
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\Theta=0
.

For infinitesimal exact Noether symmetries,
we have that their local Hamiltonian functions can be expressed as

f_{Y}=\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\Theta
(see Proposition
2.72
) .

Obviously, every (infinitesimal) natural Noether symmetry is a
(infinitesimal) natural dynamical symmetry.
Moreover, as every canonical lift preserves the canonical forms

\Theta\in{\Omega}^{1}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
and
\Omega\in{\Omega}^{2}({\rm T}^{*}Q)

(Propositions
7
and
3.23
), we have that:

Proposition 3.67

Every (infinitesimal) natural Noether symmetry is an
(infinitesimal) exact Noether symmetry.

At this point, it is possible to state the Noether Theorem as in Theorem
2.73
.
In particular, for infinitesimal exact Noether symmetries,
the associated conserved quantities are

f_{Y}=\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\Theta
(up to constants).

Summarizing, the following table recovers the relations among
the several types
of symmetries of the canonical Hamiltonian systems:

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{\rm Natural Noether symmetries}\end{array}\right\}&\subset&\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{\rm Natural dynamical symmetries}\end{array}\right\}\\
\cap&&\\
\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{\rm Exact Noether symmetries}\end{array}\right\}&&\cap\\
\cap&&\\
\{\mbox{\rm Noether symmetries}\}&\subset&\{\mbox{\rm Dynamical symmetries}\}\end{array}

3.5.2
Lagrangian formalism: Lagrangian symmetries and Noether’s Theorem

Let
({\rm T}Q,\Omega_{\cal L},E_{\cal L})
be a regular Lagrangian systems
and
X_{\cal L}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
the Euler–Lagrange vector field solution of the system.

Also in this situation all the concepts and results about symmetries
established in Section
2.3
are true and,
in this way, we can also introduce the concepts of

(infinitesimal) Lagrangian dynamical symmetry
,

(infinitesimal) Lagrangian Noether symmetry

and
(infinitesimal) exact Lagrangian Noether symmetry
,
and their properties and relations, including Noether’s Theorem.
But, in addition, the study of symmetries en the Lagrangian formalism
presents some nuances that should be highlighted.

First, we define:

Definition 3.68

A diffeomorphism
\Phi\colon{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q
is a

natural Lagrangian dynamical symmetry
if:

1.

There exists a diffeomorphism
\varphi\colon Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
such that
\Phi={\rm T}\varphi
.

2.

\Phi_{*}X_{\cal L}=X_{\cal L}
.

Definition 3.69

A vector field
Y\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
is an

infinitesimal natural Lagrangian dynamical symmetry
if

1.

There exists
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
such that
Y=Z^{C}
.

2.

[Y,X_{\cal L}]=[Z^{C},X_{\cal L}]=0

12
12

12

Or, more generically,

[Y,X_{\cal L}]=[Z^{C},X_{\cal L}]=gX_{\cal L}
, for some function
g\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)
).
.

As in the canonical Hamiltonian formalism,
the canonical lifts of diffeomorphisms and vector fields
preserve the canonical structures of
{\rm T}Q
(Proposition
3.9
).
A consequence of this is the following:

Proposition 3.70

1.

Let
\varphi\colon Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
be a diffeomorphism and

\Phi={\rm T}\varphi
its canonical lift to
{\rm T}Q
. Then

\Phi^{*}\Theta_{\cal L}=\Theta_{\Phi^{*}{\cal L}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\Phi^{*}\Omega_{\cal L}=\Omega_{\Phi^{*}{\cal L}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\Phi^{*}E_{\cal L}=E_{\Phi^{*}{\cal L}}\ .

2.

Let
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
and its canonical lift
Z^{C}
to
{\rm T}Q
. Then

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Z^{C})\Theta_{\cal L}=0\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Z^{C})\Omega_{\cal L}=0\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Z^{C})E_{\cal L}=0\ .

(
Proof
)
It is a straightforward consequence of Proposition
3.9
and the
definitions of
\Theta_{\cal L}
,
\Omega_{\cal L}
, and
E_{\cal L}
.
In fact:

1.

For
\Phi={\rm T}\varphi
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Phi^{*}\Theta_{\cal L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Phi^{*}({\rm d}{\cal L}\circ J)={\rm d}(\Phi^{*}{\cal L})\circ J)=\Theta_{\Phi^{*}{\cal L}}\ ,

\displaystyle\Phi^{*}\Omega_{\cal L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Phi^{*}(-{\rm d}\Theta_{\cal L})=-{\rm d}\Phi^{*}\Theta_{\cal L}=\Omega_{\Phi^{*}{\cal L}}\ ,

\displaystyle\Phi^{*}E_{\cal L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Phi^{*}(\Delta({\cal L})-{\cal L})=\Delta(\Phi^{*}{\cal L})-\Phi^{*}{\cal L}=E_{\Phi^{*}{\cal L}}\ .

2.

They are proved using the uniparametric groups of diffeomorphisms generated by
the fluxes of
Z
and
Z^{C}
, and the above item.

Nevertheless, the Lagrangian forms
\Theta_{\cal L}
and
\Omega_{\cal L}

are not canonical structures of
{\rm T}Q
,
since they depend on the choice of a Lagrangian function
{\cal L}

and hence they are not invariant by these lifts, necessarily.
Thus, for Lagrangian Noether symmetries, we can state the following definitions:

Definition 3.71

A diffeomorphism
\Phi\colon{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q
is a

natural Lagrangian Noether symmetry

if there exist a diffeomorphism
\varphi\colon Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
such that

\Phi={\rm T}\varphi
and it satisfies:

1.

\Phi^{*}\Omega_{\cal L}=({\rm T}\varphi)^{*}\Omega_{\cal L}=\Omega_{\cal L}
.

2.

\Phi^{*}E_{\cal L}=({\rm T}\varphi)^{*}E_{\cal L}=E_{\cal L}+c
(
c\in\mathbb{R}
)

13
13

13

It is usual to write simply that
\Phi^{*}E_{\cal L}=E_{\cal L}
.

.

Definition 3.72

A vector field
Y\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
is an

infinitesimal natural Lagrangian Noether symmetry

if there exists
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
such that
Y=Z^{C}
and it satisfies:

1.

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\Omega_{\cal L}=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Z^{C})\Omega_{\cal L}=0
.

2.

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)E_{\cal L}=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Z^{C})E_{\cal L}=0
.

Obviously, every (infinitesimal) natural Lagrangian Noether symmetry
is a (infinitesimal) natural Lagrangian dynamical symmetry.

Finally, as a particular case, we have:

Definition 3.73

A Lagrangian Noether symmetry is
exact
if
\Phi^{*}\Theta_{\cal L}=\Theta_{\cal L}
.

Definition 3.74

An infinitesimal Lagrangian Noether symmetry is
exact

if
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\Theta_{\cal L}=0
.

In these circumstances, it is possible to state the Lagrangian geometric version
of the Noether Theorem.
First, observe that, if
Y\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
is a
infinitesimal natural Lagrangian Noether symmetry, then
Proposition
2.72
holds for these kinds of symmetries.
Thus, for every
{\rm p}\in{\rm T}Q
, there exists an open set
U_{\rm p}\ni{\rm p}
and

f_{Y}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(U_{\rm p})
, which is unique up to the sum constant functions, such that

\mathop{i}\nolimits(Z^{C})\Omega_{\cal L}={\rm d}f_{Y}\hskip 20.00003pt\mbox{\rm(in $U_{\rm p}$)}\ .

(3.22)

Furthermore, there exists
\zeta_{Y}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(U_{\rm p})
, defined as

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Z^{C})\Theta_{\cal L}={\rm d}\zeta_{Y}
, in
U_{\rm p}
, and such that

\displaystyle f_{Y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\zeta_{Y}-\mathop{i}\nolimits(Z^{C})\Theta_{\cal L}=\zeta_{Y}-\Theta_{\cal L}(Z^{C})=\zeta_{Y}-{\rm d}{\cal L}\circ J(Z^{C})

(3.23)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\zeta_{Y}-{\rm d}{\cal L}(Z^{V})=\zeta_{Y}-\mathop{i}\nolimits(Z^{V}){\rm d}{\cal L}=\zeta_{Y}-Z^{V}({\cal L})\ ,

(up to the sum of constant functions in
U_{\rm p}
). Then:

Theorem 3.75

(Lagrangian Noether):

If
Y=Z^{C}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
(with
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
)
is an infinitesimal natural Lagrangian Noether symmetry,
then
f_{Y}=\zeta_{Y}-Z^{V}({\cal L})

is a conserved quantity; that is,
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{\cal L})f_{Y}=0
.

(
Proof
)
The proof is the same as in Theorem
2.73
, taking into account (
3.22
) and (
3.23
).

If the infinitesimal Noether symmetry is exact, then
Y=Z^{C}
and
we can take
f_{Y}=\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\Theta_{\cal L}=Z^{V}({\cal L})
.

3.5.3
Equivalent Lagrangians and Noether’s Theorem

It is evident that, if
\Phi\in{\rm Diff}({\rm T}Q)
(resp.
Y\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
)
is a canonical lift of a diffeomorphism (resp. of a
vector field) of
Q
to
{\rm T}Q
which, in addition,
lets the Lagrangian function of the system invariant,
then the symplectic form
\Omega_{{\cal L}}
, the
Lagrangian energy
E_{{\cal L}}
and hence the Euler–Lagrange vector field

X_{\cal L}
(that is, the Euler–Lagrange equations) are also invariant by
\Phi
.
All of this assures that the conditions of Definitions
3.71
and
3.72
hold.
Nevertheless, this requirement is too strong
because there are Lagrangian functions that, being different,
give the same form
\Omega_{{\cal L}}
and the same
Euler–Lagrange equations. This leads to the following:

Definition 3.76

Two Lagrangian functions
{\cal L}_{1},{\cal L}_{2}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)

are
equivalent
if

\Omega_{{\cal L}_{1}}=\Omega_{{\cal L}_{2}}\hskip 10.00002pt{\rm and}\hskip 10.00002ptX_{{\cal L}_{1}}=X_{{\cal L}_{2}}\ .

For regular Lagrangians this definition is equivalent to the following:

Proposition 3.77

Two regular Lagrangians
{\cal L}_{1},{\cal L}_{2}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)

are equivalent if

\Omega_{{\cal L}_{1}}=\Omega_{{\cal L}_{2}}\hskip 10.00002pt{\rm and}\hskip 10.00002ptE_{{\cal L}_{1}}=E_{{\cal L}_{2}}+c\ \mbox{\rm( $c\in\mathbb{R}$)}\ .

(
Proof
)
We must prove that, if
\Omega_{{\cal L}_{1}}=\Omega_{{\cal L}_{2}}
,
then
X_{{\cal L}_{1}}=X_{{\cal L}_{2}}
is equivalent to

E_{{\cal L}_{1}}=E_{{\cal L}_{2}}+c
.

If
X_{{\cal L}_{1}}=X_{{\cal L}_{2}}
, then

0=\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{{\cal L}_{1}})\Omega_{{\cal L}_{1}}-{\rm d}E_{{\cal L}_{1}}=\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{{\cal L}_{2}})\Omega_{{\cal L}_{2}}-{\rm d}E_{{\cal L}_{1}}\ ,

which implies that
{\rm d}E_{{\cal L}_{1}}={\rm d}E_{{\cal L}_{2}}
and, hence,
E_{{\cal L}_{1}}=E_{{\cal L}_{2}}+c
.

Conversely, if
E_{{\cal L}_{1}}=E_{{\cal L}_{2}}+c
, then

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{{\cal L}_{1}})\Omega_{{\cal L}_{1}}={\rm d}E_{{\cal L}_{1}}={\rm d}E_{{\cal L}_{2}}=\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{{\cal L}_{2}})\Omega_{{\cal L}_{2}}\ ,

and, as
\Omega_{{\cal L}_{1}}=\Omega_{{\cal L}_{2}}
,
this implies that
X_{{\cal L}_{1}}=X_{{\cal L}_{2}}
, since

{{\cal L}_{1}}
and
{{\cal L}_{2}}
are regular Lagrangians and the solution is unique.

Next, we specify how equivalent Lagrangians are (see also
[
1
]
).
First we find the form of Lagrangian functions leading to vanishing Cartan forms:

Proposition 3.78

A Lagrangian
{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)
satisfies that

\Omega_{\cal L}=0
if, and only if, there exists a closed
1
-form

\alpha\in{\Omega}^{1}(Q)
in
Q
and a function

f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(Q)
, such that

{\cal L}=\widehat{\alpha}+\tau_{Q}^{*}f
(up to a constant),
where
\widehat{\alpha}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)
is the function defined by

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\widehat{\alpha}&\colon&{\rm T}Q&\longrightarrow&\mathbb{R}\\
&&(q,v)&\mapsto&\alpha_{q}(v)\end{array}\ .

(
Proof
)
Let
\Omega_{\cal L}=-{\rm d}\Theta_{\cal L}
; then
\Theta_{\cal L}={\rm d}{\cal L}\circ J

is a closed and semibasic form in
{\rm T}Q
and, as a consequence,
it is a basic form. Then, there exists
\alpha\in{\Omega}^{1}(Q)
such that

{\rm d}{\cal L}\circ J=\tau_{Q}^{*}\alpha\ .

(3.24)

Moreover, since
0={\rm d}\Theta_{\cal L}={\rm d}(\tau_{Q}^{*}\alpha)=\tau_{Q}^{*}({\rm d}\alpha)
,
then
{\rm d}\alpha=0
; that is,
\alpha
is a closed
1
-form in
Q
.
Furthermore, a simple computation in local coordinates shows that

{\rm d}\widehat{\alpha}\circ J=\tau_{Q}^{*}\alpha
, and from (
3.24
) we have that

{\rm d}\widehat{\alpha}\circ J=\tau_{Q}^{*}\alpha={\rm d}{\cal L}\circ J\ .

Then
{\rm d}({\cal L}-\widehat{\alpha})\circ J=0
, and therefore, the
1
-form

{\rm d}({\cal L}-\widehat{\alpha})
is closed and semi-basic.
As a consequence,
{\rm d}({\cal L}-\widehat{\alpha})
is a basic
1
-form;
that is, there exists
f\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(Q)
such that

{\rm d}({\cal L}-\widehat{\alpha})=\tau_{Q}^{*}{\rm d}f={\rm d}(\tau_{Q}^{*}f)\ ,

and thus

{\cal L}=\widehat{\alpha}+\tau_{Q}^{*}f
(up to a constant).

Conversely, let us suppose that
{\cal L}=\widehat{\alpha}+\tau_{Q}^{*}f

(up to a constant). We have

\Theta_{\cal L}={\rm d}{\cal L}\circ J={\rm d}(\widehat{\alpha}+\tau_{Q}^{*}f)\circ J={\rm d}\widehat{\alpha}\circ J=\tau_{Q}^{*}\alpha\ ,

since
{\rm d}\tau_{Q}^{*}f
vanishes on the vertical vector fields.
As
\alpha
is closed,
{\rm d}\alpha=0
and we obtain

\Omega_{\cal L}=-{\rm d}\Theta_{\cal L}=-{\rm d}(\tau_{Q}^{*}\alpha)=-\tau_{Q}^{*}({\rm d}\alpha)=0\ .

In a local chart of natural coordinates of
{\rm T}Q
,
the local expression of a closed
1
-form
\alpha
is

\alpha=\alpha_{i}{\rm d}q^{i}=\frac{\partial{g}}{\partial{q^{i}}}{\rm d}q^{i}\ ,

for some local function
g
;
then the local expression of the function
\widehat{\alpha}
is

\widehat{\alpha}=\frac{\partial{g}}{\partial{q^{i}}}v^{i}\ .

Now, from this result we obtain the explicit characterization of the equivalent Lagrangians:

Proposition 3.79

Two regular Lagrangians
{\cal L}_{1},{\cal L}_{2}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)
are
equivalent if, and only if,
{\cal L}_{1}={\cal L}_{2}+\widehat{\alpha}
(up to a constant).

(
Proof
)
Suppose that
{\cal L}_{1},{\cal L}_{2}
are equivalent.
As
\Omega_{{\cal L}_{1}}=\Omega_{{\cal L}_{1}}
, then

\Omega_{{\cal L}_{1}-{\cal L}_{2}}=0
. Therefore, by Proposition
3.78
,
there exist
\alpha\in Z^{1}(Q)
and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(Q)
such that

{\cal L}_{1}-{\cal L}_{2}=\widehat{\alpha}+\tau_{Q}^{*}f
(up to a constant).
From Proposition
3.77
we know that

E_{{\cal L}_{1}}=E_{{\cal L}_{2}}
, (up to a constant), or equivalently,

E_{{\cal L}_{1}}-E_{{\cal L}_{2}}=0
(up to a constant). Then

\begin{array}[]{lcl}0&=&E_{{\cal L}_{1}}-E_{{\cal L}_{2}}=\Delta({\cal L}_{1})-{\cal L}_{1}-\Delta({\cal L}_{2})+{\cal L}_{2}=\Delta({\cal L}_{1}-{\cal L}_{2})-({\cal L}_{1}-{\cal L}_{2})\\
&=&\Delta(\widehat{\alpha}+\tau_{Q}^{*}f)-({\cal L}_{1}-{\cal L}_{2})=\widehat{\alpha}-({\cal L}_{1}-{\cal L}_{2})\quad\makebox{(up to a constant)}\ .\end{array}

Conversely, suppose that
{\cal L}_{1}={\cal L}_{2}+\widehat{\alpha}
(up to a constant).
First, a simple computation gives

\Omega_{{\cal L}_{2}}-\Omega_{{\cal L}_{1}}={\rm d}(\Theta_{{\cal L}_{1}}-\Theta_{{\cal L}_{2}})={\rm d}({\rm d}({\cal L}_{1}-{\cal L}_{2})\circ J)={\rm d}({\rm d}\widehat{\alpha}\circ J)={\rm d}(\tau_{Q}^{*}\alpha)=\tau_{Q}^{*}({\rm d}\alpha)=0\ .

Thus
\Omega_{{\cal L}_{1}}=\Omega_{{\cal L}_{2}}
. Furthermore,
as
\Delta(\widehat{\alpha})=\widehat{\alpha}
, we have

E_{{\cal L}_{1}}=\Delta({\cal L}_{1})-{\cal L}_{1}=\Delta({\cal L}_{2}+\widehat{\alpha})-({\cal L}_{2}+\widehat{\alpha})=E_{{\cal L}_{2}}+\widehat{\alpha}-\widehat{\alpha}=E_{{\cal L}_{2}}\hskip 10.00002pt\makebox{\rm(up to a constant)}\ .

As
\Omega_{{\cal L}_{1}}=\Omega_{{\cal L}_{2}}
and

E_{{\cal L}_{1}}=E_{{\cal L}_{2}}
(up to a constant),
then
{\cal L}_{1}
and
{\cal L}_{2}
are equivalent (Proposition
3.77
).

Taking this into account, one can define:

Definition 3.80

A
symmetry of the Lagrangian
is
a diffeomorphism
\Phi\colon{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q
such that

{\cal L}
and
\Phi^{*}{\cal L}
are equivalent Lagrangians; that is,

\Phi^{*}{\cal L}={\cal L}+\widehat{\alpha}
(up to constants),
where
\widehat{\alpha}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)
is the function defined in Proposition
3.78
.

Definition 3.81

An
infinitesimal symmetry of the Lagrangian
is
a vector field
Y\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
such that the uniparametric groups of diffeomorphisms
generated by its flux are symmetries of the Lagrangian; that is,

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y){\cal L}=\widehat{\alpha}
.

A special case of these kinds of symmetries are:

Definition 3.82

A
strict symmetry of the Lagrangian
is a diffeomorphism

\Phi\colon{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q
such that
\Phi^{*}{\cal L}={\cal L}
.

Definition 3.83

An
infinitesimal strict symmetry of the Lagrangian
is
a vector field
Y\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
such that the uniparametric groups of diffeomorphisms
generated by its flux are strict symmetries of the Lagrangian; that is,

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y){\cal L}=0
.

And, in particular, we define:

Definition 3.84

A (strict) symmetry of the Lagrangian
\Phi\colon{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q
is
said to be
natural
if there exists a diffeomorphism
\varphi\colon Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
such that

\Phi={\rm T}\varphi
.

Definition 3.85

An infinitesimal (strict) symmetry of the Lagrangian
Y\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
is
said to be
natural
if there
exists a vector field
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
such that
Y=Z^{C}
.

Remark 3.86

A symmetry of the Lagrangian
\Phi\colon{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q

is not necessarily a Lagrangian Noether symmetry since, in general,

\Phi^{*}\Omega_{\cal L}\not=\Omega_{\Phi^{*}{\cal L}}
and

\Phi^{*}E_{\cal L}\not=E_{\Phi^{*}{\cal L}}
,
as a simple calculation in coordinates shows.
In addition, it is not a Lagrangian dynamical symmetry.
Nevertheless, the following relation holds:

Proposition 3.87

A diffeomorphism
\Phi\colon T^{1}_{k}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>T^{1}_{k}Q
is a natural Lagrangian Noether symmetry
if, and only if, it is a natural symmetry of the Lagrangian.

(
Proof
)
If
\Phi={\rm T}\varphi
, for some diffeomorphism
\varphi\colon Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
,
according to Lemma (
3.70
) we have that

\Phi^{*}\Omega_{\cal L}=\Omega_{\Phi^{*}{\cal L}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\Phi^{*}E_{\cal L}=E_{\Phi^{*}{\cal L}}\ ,

and then

\left.\begin{array}[]{cccc}\Phi^{*}\Omega_{\cal L}&=&\Omega_{\cal L}&\\
\Phi^{*}E_{\cal L}&=&E_{\cal L}&\mbox{\rm(up to constants)}\end{array}\right\}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \left\{\begin{array}[]{cccc}\Omega_{\Phi^{*}{\cal L}}&=&\Omega_{\cal L}&\\
E_{\Phi^{*}{\cal L}}&=&E_{\cal L}&\mbox{\rm(up to constants)}\end{array}\right.\ ;

that is,
\Phi
is a natural Lagrangian Noether symmetry
if, and only if,
{\cal L}
and
\Phi^{*}{\cal L}
are equivalent Lagrangians and hence

\Phi
is a natural symmetry of the Lagrangian.

This result holds also for infinitesimal symmetries,
as can be proved taking the flux of the vector fields that generate them.
Thus, we have the following immediate corollary:

Proposition 3.88

A vector field
Y\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
is an infinitesimal natural Lagrangian Noether symmetry
if, and only if, it is an infinitesimal natural symmetry of the Lagrangian.

Finally, a version of Noether’s Theorem
for infinitesimal natural strict symmetries of the Lagrangian can be established as follows:

Theorem 3.89

(Classical Noether for Lagrangian systems
).
Let
Y=Z^{C}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
, with
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
,
be an infinitesimal natural strict symmetry of the Lagrangian.
Then
\widetilde{f}=Z^{V}({\cal L})
is a conserved quantity; that is,
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X_{\cal L})\widetilde{f}=0
.

(
Proof
)
As every infinitesimal natural strict symmetries of the Lagrangian
is a natural symmetry of the Lagrangian and then,
it is a natural Noether Lagrangian symmetry, according to the above proposition;
then the result is a straightforward consequence of Theorem
3.75
,
since
\zeta_{Y}=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\Theta_{\cal L}=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Z^{C})\Theta_{\cal L}=0
.

The following table summarizes the relation among the different kinds
of symmetries in the Lagrangian formalism of Lagrangian systems:

\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{\rm Strict}\\
\mbox{\rm Lagrangian}\\
\mbox{\rm symmetries}\end{array}\right\}&\supset&\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{\rm Strict}\\
\mbox{\rm natural}\\
\mbox{\rm Lagrangian}\\
\mbox{\rm symmetries}\end{array}\right\}&&&&\\
\cap&&\cap&&&&\\
\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{\rm symmetries}\\
\mbox{\rm of the}\\
\mbox{\rm Lagrangian}\end{array}\right\}&\supset&\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{\rm Natural}\\
\mbox{\rm symmetries}\\
\mbox{\rm of the}\\
\mbox{\rm Lagrangian}\end{array}\right\}&=&\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{\rm Natural}\\
\mbox{\rm Lagrangian}\\
\mbox{\rm Noether}\\
\mbox{\rm symmetries}\\
\end{array}\right\}&\subset&\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{\rm Natural}\\
\mbox{\rm Lagrangian}\\
\mbox{\rm dynamical}\\
\mbox{\rm symmetries}\end{array}\right\}\\
&&&&\cap&&\\
&&&&\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{\rm Exact}\\
\mbox{\rm Lagrangian}\\
\mbox{\rm Noether}\\
\mbox{\rm symmetries}\end{array}\right\}&&\cap\\
&&&&\cap&&\\
&&&&\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{\rm Lagrangian}\\
\mbox{\rm Noether}\\
\mbox{\rm symmetries}\end{array}\right\}&\subset&\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{\rm Lagrangian}\\
\mbox{\rm dynamical}\\
\mbox{\rm symmetries}\end{array}\right\}\end{array}

3.6
