Variational formulation for Lagrangian systems

One of the main characteristics of the Lagrangian systems is that
they are variational; that is, their dynamical equations can be obtained
from a variational principle, and the same happens for Hamiltonian systems

[
5
,
33
,
34
,
92
,
147
,
159
,
173
,
216
,
234
,
236
,
246
,
279
,
286
]
.
Next, we present the variational formulation of Lagrangian systems.
In order to study this topic, we need to enlarge the phase space to include
the time coordinate, and so we have to consider the trivial bundle

\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
(or
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q
).

3.6.1
Functional associated to a Lagrangian function

Let
({\rm T}Q,{\cal L})
be a Lagrangian dynamical system.
We can associate to the Lagrangian function a functional defined on a suitable space of curves
which are taken to be possible trajectories of the system.

To get it, consider the manifolds

\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
,
\mathbb{R}\times Q
, and
\mathbb{R}

with the canonical projections

\rho\colon\mathbb{R}\times Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\ ,\ \tau_{(1,0)}=id\times\tau_{Q}\colon\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times Q\ ,\ \tau_{1}=\rho\circ\tau_{(1,0)}\colon\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\ .

The canonical projections
\rho
and
\tau_{1}
define a global coordinate in
\mathbb{R}

which is denoted
t
, and is physically identified with “time”.
Then we can take the natural volume element
{\rm d}t
in
\mathbb{R}
and its natural lift to

\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
, which is also denoted by
{\rm d}t
.

Now, let
\gamma\colon[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
be a curve,
and let
\widetilde{\gamma}\colon[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q
be
its canonical lift of
\gamma
from
Q
to the tangent bundle
{\rm T}Q
.
We denote
\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}\colon[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times Q

the curve given by
\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}(t)=(t,\gamma(t))
,
and by
\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}\colon[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q

the curve
\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}(t)=(t,\widetilde{\gamma}(t))
.

Now, let
\alpha
be a
1
-form in
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
.
As
\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}^{*}\alpha
is a
1
-form in
\mathbb{R}
,
we can define

\int_{\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}}\alpha=\int_{a}^{b}\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}^{*}\alpha\ .

Finally, given a (time-dependent) Lagrangian function

{\cal L}\colon\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}
, consider the
1
-form

{\cal L}\,{\rm d}t\in{\Omega}^{1}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)
.
Then we define

Definition 3.90

For every curve
\gamma\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
, the

action
of
{\cal L}
along
\gamma
is the functional

{\mathbf{L}}(\gamma):=\int_{\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}}{\cal L}{\rm d}t\ .

Remark 3.91

Usually we take curves

\gamma\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
defined on a closed interval

I=[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}
, but we can extend the domain to the whole real numbers defining
\gamma
out of the interval as a constant function taking permanently the values on the two extreme points of the interval. Observe that, in this case, the extension may not be differentiable at the extreme points, but this is not a problem for the existence of the integral

14
14

14

From now on it is only necessary that the curve is of class
C^{2}
.
.

If the Lagrangian function is time-independent,
it is enough to extend it from
{\rm T}Q
to
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
.

3.6.2
Hamilton variational principle

The
variational problem
consists in optimizing the above functional;
that is, to choose the curves for which the functional takes the extremal value.
As this is a very complicated problem we restrict ourselves to look for the local extremal points of the functional;
so we look for curves
\gamma
such that the value of

L(\gamma)
does not change for small variations of the curve,
but in first approximation. Next we are going to precise these ideas.

Definition 3.92

Let
\gamma\colon[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
be a curve with

\gamma(a)=q_{0}
,
\gamma(b)=q_{1}
.
A
variation
of
\gamma
is a map

\mu\colon(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\times[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q

such that

1.

\mu(0,t)=\gamma(t)
,
t\in[a,b]
.

2.

\mu(s,a)=q_{0}
,
\mu(s,b)=q_{1}
, for
s\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
.

We denote
\mu_{s}
the curve defined by

\mu_{s}\colon[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
, with
\mu_{s}(t)=\mu(s,t)
.

Observe that, given one of these curves
\mu_{s}
, we can lift it to
{\rm T}Q
and
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
in the usual way.
We denote these lifts as
\widetilde{\mu}_{s}
and
\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}_{s}
, which are variations of
\widetilde{\gamma}
and
\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}
respectively.

There is a natural way to construct different variations of that type.
In fact, given
X\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
with

X(q_{0})=0
,
X(q_{1})=0
; if
F_{s}
is a local flux for
X
,
a variation of the curve
\gamma
is obtained by

\mu(s,t)=(F_{s}\circ\gamma)(t)=F_{s}(\gamma(t))\ .

(3.25)

We know that
F_{s}\colon Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q

is a diffeomorphism, and the corresponding lift

{\rm T}F_{s}\colon{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q
is the local flux corresponding to the canonical lift
X^{C}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
of
X
to
{\rm T}Q
.
In the same way that (
3.25
) is a variation of
\gamma
,
we have that

\widetilde{\mu}(s,t)=({\rm T}F_{s}\circ\widetilde{\gamma})(t)={\rm T}F_{s}(\widetilde{\gamma}(t))

is a variation of
\widetilde{\gamma}
.
Observe that
X^{C}
is naturally a vector field in
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
acting as the identity on the component
\mathbb{R}
.
It is vertical with respect to the projection
\tau_{1}\colon\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}
.

Given
\gamma
and
F_{s}
as above, the following properties hold:

1.

\mu_{s}(t)=(F_{s}\circ\gamma)(t)=F_{s}(\gamma(t))

2.

\widetilde{\mu}_{s}(t)=({\rm T}F_{s}\circ\widetilde{\gamma})(t)={\rm T}F_{s}(\widetilde{\gamma}(t))

3.

{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}_{s}(t)=(F_{s}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$})(t)=F_{s}(\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$})(t))=(t,F_{s}(\gamma(t))

4.

\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}_{s}(t)=({\rm T}F_{s}\circ\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}})(t)={\rm T}F_{s}(\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}(t))=(t,{\rm T}F_{s}(\widetilde{\gamma}(t))

These kinds of variations obtained from specific vector fields on the manifold
Q
are sufficient to obtain the local extreme of the functional
{\mathbf{L}}(\gamma)
as we are going to see.

Definition 3.93

A curve
\gamma
is a
local extreme
for the functional
{\mathbf{L}}

if for every variation
\mu_{s}=F_{s}\circ\gamma
, with
F_{s}
corresponding to a vector field
X
as above, the following condition holds:

\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\int_{\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}_{s}}{\cal L}{\rm d}t=0\ .

This is the so-called
Hamilton variational principle
.

To obtain a more useful way to manage with this definition, first we need the following results:

Proposition 3.94

Let
X\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
and
\mu_{s}
be the corresponding variation of a curve
\gamma
. Then

1.

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\int_{\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}_{s}}{\cal L}{\rm d}t=\int_{\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}}(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X^{C}){\cal L})\,{\rm d}t
.

2.

If
\alpha\in{\Omega}^{1}({\rm T}Q)
, then
\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\int_{\widetilde{\mu}_{s}}\alpha=\int_{\widetilde{\gamma}}\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X^{C})\alpha
.

(
Proof
)
Taking into account that

\widetilde{\mu}_{s}=\widetilde{F}_{s}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}
and
\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}_{s}=({\rm T}F_{s}\circ\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}})
,
we have:

1.

First,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\int_{\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}_{s}}{\cal L}{\rm d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{s\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 1.99306pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>0}\frac{1}{s}\left(\int_{\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}_{s}}{\cal L}{\rm d}t-\int_{\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}_{0}}{\cal L}{\rm d}t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{s\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 1.99306pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>0}\frac{1}{s}\left(\int_{a}^{b}\widetilde{\widehat{\mu}}_{s}^{*}({\cal L}\,{\rm d}t)-\int_{a}^{b}\widetilde{\widehat{\mu}}_{0}^{*}({\cal L}\,{\rm d}t)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}\lim_{s\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 1.99306pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>0}\frac{\widetilde{\widehat{\mu}}_{s}^{*}{\cal L}-\widetilde{\widehat{\mu}}_{0}^{*}{\cal L}}{s}{\rm d}t=\int_{a}^{b}\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}^{*}\lim_{s\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 1.99306pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>0}\frac{{\rm T}F_{s}^{*}{\cal L}-{\rm T}F_{0}^{*}{\cal L}}{s}{\rm d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}^{*}(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X^{C}){\cal L}){\rm d}t=\int_{\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}}(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X^{C}){\cal L}){\rm d}t\ ,

because

\widetilde{\mu}_{s}^{*}\alpha=(\widetilde{\gamma}^{*}\circ{\rm T}F_{s}^{*})\alpha=\widetilde{\gamma}^{*}({\rm T}F_{s}^{*}\alpha)
.

2.

Second,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\int_{\widetilde{\mu}_{s}}\alpha

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{s\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 1.99306pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>0}\frac{1}{s}\left(\int_{\widetilde{\mu}_{s}}\alpha-\int_{\widetilde{\mu}_{0}}\alpha\right)=\lim_{s\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 1.99306pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>0}\frac{1}{s}\left(\int_{a}^{b}\widetilde{\mu}_{s}^{*}\alpha-\int_{a}^{b}\widetilde{\mu}_{0}\alpha\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}\lim_{s\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 1.99306pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>0}\frac{\widetilde{\mu}_{s}^{*}\alpha-\widetilde{\mu}_{0}^{*}\alpha}{s}=\int_{a}^{b}\widetilde{\gamma}^{*}\lim_{s\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 1.99306pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>0}\frac{{\rm T}\widetilde{F}_{s}^{*}\alpha-{\rm T}\widetilde{F}_{0}^{*}\alpha}{s}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}\widetilde{\gamma}^{*}\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X^{C})\alpha=\int_{\widetilde{\gamma}}\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X^{C})\alpha\ .

Hence, the problem is to seek for the equation fulfilled by the curves

\gamma\colon[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q

satisfying that
\gamma(a)=q_{0}
,
\gamma(b)=q_{1}
,
and such that

\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\int_{\widetilde{\mu}_{s}}{\cal L}{\rm d}t=\int_{\widetilde{\gamma}}\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X^{C}){\rm d}t=0\ ,

for every variation
\mu_{s}=F_{s}\circ\gamma
of
\gamma
.
Then, we need the following:

Lemma 3.95

For every curve
\gamma\colon[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
we have

\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}^{*}(A_{\cal L}\,{\rm d}t)=\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}^{*}({\cal L}\,{\rm d}t)+\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}^{*}(E_{\cal L}\,{\rm d}t)=\widetilde{\gamma}^{*}\Theta_{\cal L}\ .

(
Proof
)
If
t_{0}\in[a,b]
, let
(q^{i},v^{i})
be
a canonical local coordinate system in a neighbourhood of

\widetilde{\gamma}(t_{0})
.
On the one hand,

\displaystyle\big(\widetilde{\gamma}^{*}(A_{\cal L}\,{\rm d}t)\big)_{t_{0}}\left(\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\gamma}^{*}A_{\cal L})(t_{0})=A_{\cal L}(\widetilde{\gamma}(t_{0}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(v^{i}\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\right)(\widetilde{\gamma}(t_{0}))=\left(v^{i}\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\right)(\gamma(t_{0}),\dot{\gamma}(t_{0}))=\dot{\gamma}(t_{0})\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\Big|_{\widetilde{\gamma}(t_{0})}\ ,

and, on the other hand,

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\gamma}^{*}\Theta_{\cal L})_{t_{0}}\left(\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\Theta_{\cal L})_{t_{0}}\left({\rm T}_{t_{0}}\widetilde{\gamma}\left(\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t_{0}}\right)\right)=({\rm d}{\cal L}\circ J)_{\widetilde{\gamma}(t_{0})}\left({\rm T}_{t_{0}}\widetilde{\gamma}\left(\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t_{0}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\rm d}{\cal L}\circ J)_{\widetilde{\gamma}(t_{0})}\left(\dot{\gamma}^{i}(t_{0})\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\Big|_{\widetilde{\gamma}(t_{0})}+\ddot{\gamma}^{i}(t_{0})\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\Big|_{\widetilde{\gamma}(t_{0})}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\rm d}{\cal L})_{\widetilde{\gamma}(t_{0})}\left(\dot{\gamma}^{i}(t_{0})\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\Big|_{\widetilde{\gamma}(t_{0})}\right)=\dot{\gamma}^{i}(t_{0})\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\Big|_{\widetilde{\gamma}(t_{0})}\ .

and the result follows.

3.6.3
Euler–Lagrange equations

Now we can obtain the equation for the curve
\gamma
solution to the variational problem.

Theorem 3.96

The curves
\gamma
which are local extremes for the functional
{\mathbf{L}}

are the solutions to the Euler–Lagrange equations.

(
Proof
)
We have that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\int_{\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}_{s}}({\cal L}{\rm d}t)=\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\int_{a}^{b}\widetilde{\mu}_{s}^{*}{\cal L}{\rm d}t=\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\int_{a}^{b}\widetilde{\mu}_{s}^{*}\Theta_{\cal L}-\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}_{s}^{*}E_{\cal L}{\rm d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\int_{\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}_{s}}\Theta_{\cal L}-E_{\cal L}{\rm d}t=\int_{\widetilde{\gamma}}\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X^{C})\Theta_{\cal L}-\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X^{C})(E_{\cal L})\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}}{\rm d}\mathop{i}\nolimits(X^{C})\Theta_{\cal L}+\mathop{i}\nolimits(X^{C}){\rm d}\Theta_{\cal L}-\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X^{C})(E_{\cal L}){\rm d}t\ .

But, by Stokes Theorem, being

X(q_{0})=0=X(q_{1})
and
\Theta_{\cal L}
a semibasic form, we have that

\displaystyle\int_{\widetilde{\gamma}}{\rm d}\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{X}){\rm d}\Theta_{\cal L}=0
; hence

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\widetilde{\gamma}}\mathop{i}\nolimits(X^{C}){\rm d}\Theta_{\cal L}-\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X^{C})(E_{\cal L}){\rm d}t=\int_{a}^{b}\widetilde{\gamma}^{*}(\mathop{i}\nolimits(X^{C}){\rm d}\Theta_{\cal L})-\widetilde{\gamma}^{*}(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X^{C})(E_{\cal L})){\rm d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}\left[{\rm d}\Theta_{\cal L}(X^{C},({\rm T}_{t}\widetilde{\gamma})\frac{d}{dt})-(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X^{C})(E_{\cal L}))(\widetilde{\gamma}(t)\right]{\rm d}t\ ,

because

\left(\widetilde{\gamma}^{*}(\mathop{i}\nolimits(X^{C}){\rm d}\Theta_{\cal L}\right)\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t}=(\mathop{i}\nolimits(X^{C}){\rm d}\Theta_{\cal L})\left({\rm T}_{t}\widetilde{\gamma}\frac{d}{dt}\right)=(d\Theta_{\cal L})_{\widetilde{\gamma}(t)}\left(X^{C},{\rm T}_{t}\gamma(\frac{d}{dt})\right)\ ,

and we have

0=\int_{a}^{b}\mathop{i}\nolimits(X^{C}_{\widetilde{\gamma}(t)}\left[-\mathop{i}\nolimits\left({\rm T}_{t}\widetilde{\gamma}\frac{d}{dt}\right){\rm d}\Theta_{\cal L}-{\rm d}E_{\cal L}\right]{\rm d}t\ .

But
X^{C}
is an arbitrary vector field; then, observing that

\displaystyle{\rm T}_{t}\widetilde{\gamma}\left(\frac{d}{dt}\right)=(\widetilde{\gamma}(t),\dot{\widetilde{\gamma}}(t))
,
we have

-\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{\gamma}(t),\dot{\widetilde{\gamma}}(t)){\rm d}\Theta_{\cal L}={\rm d}E_{\cal L}\ ,

and, as
-{\rm d}\Theta_{\cal L}=\Omega_{\cal L}
, we obtain that

\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{\gamma}(t),\dot{\widetilde{\gamma}}(t))\Omega_{\cal L}={\rm d}E_{\cal L}\ .

(Recall that

(\widetilde{\gamma}(t),\dot{\widetilde{\gamma}}(t))

is the tangent vector to the curve
\widetilde{\gamma}
at the point
\gamma(t)
).

If we consider that the curves
\widetilde{\gamma}(t)

which are the solutions to the problem are the integral curves of a vector field

X_{\cal L}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)
, observing that
the curve
\widetilde{\gamma}
is a natural lift
to
{\rm T}Q
of the curve
\gamma
in
Q
, then this vector field must verify the following conditions:

1.

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\cal L})\Omega_{\cal L}={\rm d}E_{\cal L}
.

2.

X_{\cal L}
is a
sode
(
J(X_{\cal L})=\Delta
).

and, as we know, these are the geometrical expression of the Euler–Lagrange equations.

As a final remark, observe that we have said that the vector field
X^{C}
is arbitrary, and this is not exact. The real arbitrary vector field is
X
instead of
X^{C}
. But the 1-form

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits\left({\rm T}\widetilde{\gamma}\Big(\frac{d}{dt}\Big)\right){\rm d}\Theta_{\cal L}+{\rm d}E_{\cal L}

is semibasic and, hence, when we apply it to
X^{C}
the obtained value depends only on
X
and not on its canonical lift

X^{C}
from
Q
to
{\rm T}Q
.

3.6.4
Hamilton–Jacobi variational principle. Relation with the canonical Hamiltonian formalism

When we studied the Hamiltonian formalism in the previous Sections,
we commented about the possibility of obtaining the dynamical equations from a variational principle.
Following the discussion on the equivalence between the Lagrangian and the Hamiltonian formalisms,
we can establish the relation between the corresponding variational formalisms.

We have studied above the variational approach to the Lagrangian formalism, then using the corresponding geometrical and dynamical elements we can relate it with the Hamiltonian one.
Thus, let
{\cal F}{\cal L}\colon{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}^{*}Q
be the Legendre map associated to the Lagrangian
{\cal L}
.
If
\gamma\colon[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
is a curve we have

\displaystyle{\mathbf{L}}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}}{\cal L}{\rm d}t=\int_{\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}}\Theta_{\cal L}-E_{\cal L}\,{\rm d}t=\int_{\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}}{\cal F}{\cal L}^{*}\Theta-{\cal F}{\cal L}^{*}{\rm h}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}}{\cal F}{\cal L}^{*}(\Theta-{\rm h}\,{\rm d}t)=\int_{{\cal F}{\cal L}\circ\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}}\Theta-{\rm h}\,{\rm d}t\ ;

where we have extended the Legendre transformation
{\cal F}{\cal L}\colon{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}^{*}Q
(with the same notation) to

{\cal F}{\cal L}\colon\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q
,
as the identity on
\mathbb{R}

(see Definition
4.22
).
Note that, if
\gamma
is a solution to the Hamilton variational problem, that is of the Lagrangian formalism, then

{\cal F}{\cal L}\circ\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}
is the solution to the following variational problem:

Definition 3.97

The
Hamilton-Jacobi variational principle
consists in
finding the curves
\zeta\colon[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}^{*}Q
,
with fixed extremes, such that they are extremal for the functional

{\mathbf{H}}(\zeta):=\int_{\zeta}\Theta-{\rm h}{\rm d}t\ .

By a similar computation as above,
although something simpler because there are no canonical lifts,
we obtain that

Theorem 3.98

The curves
\zeta
which are local extremes for the functional
{\mathbf{H}}

are the solutions to the Hamilton equations.

(
Proof
)
In fact, let
\zeta\colon[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}^{*}Q
a curve with

\zeta(a)=A,\zeta(b)=B
, and
Z\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
with
Z(A)=0,Z(B)=0
.
Let
F_{s}
the flux of
Z
and consider the variation
\eta_{s}=F_{s}\circ\zeta
.
We have

{\mathbf{H}}(F_{s}\circ\zeta)=\int_{a}^{b}(F_{s}\circ\zeta)^{*}\Theta-({\rm h}\circ F_{s}\circ\zeta){\rm d}t=\int_{a}^{b}\zeta^{*}(F_{s}^{*}\Theta)-\zeta^{*}(F_{s}^{*}{\rm h}){\rm d}t\ .

Hence:

\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}{\mathbf{H}}(F_{s}\circ\zeta)=\int_{a}^{b}\zeta^{*}(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Z)\Theta)-\zeta^{*}(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Z){\rm h}){\rm d}t=\int_{\zeta}\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Z)\Theta-(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Z){\rm h}){\rm d}t\ .

And following the same lines as in Theorem
3.96
,
we conclude that the tangent vector to the curve
\zeta
satisfies the Hamilton equations

\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{\zeta})\Omega={\rm d}{\rm h}(\zeta(t))\,,

with
\Omega=-{\rm d}\Theta
, the symplectic form of the cotangent bundle.
If we suppose that the curve is an integral curve of a vector field
X_{\rm h}
then

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\rm h})\Omega={\rm d}{\rm h}\,.

Remark 3.99

The Hamilton–Jacobi variational principle can be stated for every
Hamiltonian system
(M,\Omega,\alpha)
in general, taking a symplectic potential

\theta\in{\Omega}^{1}(M)
, such that
\Omega=-{\rm d}\theta
,
and a (local) Hamiltonian function
h\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
,
such that
\alpha={\rm d}h
.

Summarizing,
in the texts of classical mechanics, all the above results are collected
stating the so-called:

Minimal action principles

Given a Lagrangian system
({\rm T}Q,{\cal L})
and the corresponding associated canonical
Hamiltonian system
({\rm T}^{*}Q,\Omega,{\rm h})
, we have:

Hamilton’s Principle of minimal action
:
The dynamics of the Lagrangian system

({\rm T}Q,{\cal L})
is given by the curves

\gamma\colon[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
,
with fixed extremes, such that they minimize the functional

{\mathbf{L}}(\gamma):=\int_{\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}}{\cal L}{\rm d}t\ .

Hamilton–Jacobi principle of minimal action
:
The dynamics of the Hamiltonian system

({\rm T}^{*}Q,\Omega,{\rm d}{\rm h})
is given by the curves

\zeta\colon[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}^{*}Q
,
with fixed extremes, such that they minimize the functional

{\mathbf{H}}(\zeta):=\int_{\zeta}\Theta-{\rm h}\,{\rm d}t\ .

3.7
Examples

In this last section, we study two of the most typical and relevant mechanical systems
using the symplectic Lagrangian, the Hamiltonian, and the unified formalisms.

3.7.1
Harmonic oscillator

The classical harmonic oscillator is a mechanical system
made of a point-particle with mass
m

moving in
\mathbb{R}
, submitted to a recuperative force (
Hook’s law
).
The configuration bundle is
Q=\mathbb{R}
, with coordinate
(q)
.

Lagrangian formalism

The Lagrangian formalism takes place in
{\rm T}Q\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2}
, with coordinates

(q,v)
, and the Lagrangian function is

{\cal L}=\frac{1}{2}(mv^{2}-kq^{2})\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptk\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\ .

The Lagrangian elements are

E_{\cal L}=\frac{1}{2}(mv^{2}+kq^{2})\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\Theta_{\cal L}=mv{\rm d}q\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\Omega_{\cal L}=m\,{\rm d}q\wedge{\rm d}v\ ,

and the Lagrangian is regular.
For
\displaystyle X_{\cal L}=f\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q}}+g\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v}}
, equation (
3.4
) gives

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\cal L})\Omega_{\cal L}=m\,(f\,{\rm d}v-g\,{\rm d}q)=mv\,{\rm d}v+kq\,{\rm d}q={\rm d}E_{\cal L}\ ,

which leads to

f=v\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptmg=-kq\ .

So the Euler–Lagrange vector field is

X_{\cal L}=v\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q}}-\frac{k}{m}q\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v}}\ ,

and its integral curves
(q(t),v(t))
are the solutions to

\frac{dq}{dt}=v\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptm\frac{dv}{dt}=-kq\hskip 10.00002pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptm\frac{d^{2}q}{dt^{2}}=-kq\ ,

which is the Euler–Lagrange equation for the system.

Hamiltonian formalism

For the Hamiltonian formalism,
{\rm T}^{*}Q\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
with coordinates
(q,p)
. First, the Legendre transformation is

{\cal F}{\cal L}^{*}q=q\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt{\cal F}{\cal L}^{*}p=mv\ ,

which is a diffeomorphism (the Lagrangian is hyperregular).
The canonical Hamiltonian function is

{\rm h}=\frac{p^{2}}{2m}+kq^{2}\ .

As
\Omega={\rm d}q\wedge{\rm d}p
,
for
\displaystyle X_{\rm h}=F\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q}}+G\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p}}
,
equation (
3.11
) gives

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\rm h})\Omega=F\,{\rm d}p-G\,{\rm d}q-=\frac{p}{m}\,{\rm d}p+kq\,{\rm d}q={\rm d}{\rm h}\ ,

which leads to

F=\frac{p}{m}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptG=-kq\ .

So the Hamiltonian vector field is

X_{\rm h}=\frac{p}{m}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q}}-kq\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p}}\ ,

and its integral curves
(q(t),p(t))
are the solutions to

m\frac{dq}{dt}=p\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dp}{dt}=-kq\ ,

which are the Hamilton equations for the system.

Observe that, using the Legendre map, the Hamilton and
the Euler–Lagrange equations of the system are, in fact, equivalent.
Obviously, we have that
{\cal F}{\cal L}_{*}X_{\cal L}=X_{\rm h}
.

Unified Lagrangian–Hamiltonian formalism

Consider the unified bundle
{\cal W}={\rm T}Q\times_{Q}{\rm T}^{*}Q\simeq\mathbb{R}^{3}

with coordinates
(q,v,p)
. On it, we have the canonical presymplectic form

\Omega_{\cal W}={\rm d}q\wedge{\rm d}p

and the Hamiltonian function

{\cal H}=pv-\frac{1}{2}(mv^{2}-kq^{2})\ .

For
\displaystyle X_{\cal H}=f\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q}}+g\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v}}+G\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p}}
,
equation (
3.16
) gives

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\cal H})\Omega_{\cal W}=f\,{\rm d}p-G\,{\rm d}q=kq\,{\rm d}q+(p-mv)\,{\rm d}v+v\,{\rm d}p={\rm d}{\cal H}\ ,

which leads to

f=v\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptG=-kq\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptp=mv\ .

The last equation is a constraint which defines the submanifold

{\cal W}_{0}\hookrightarrow{\cal W}
and gives the Legendre map.
Therefore, the Hamiltonian vector field is

X_{\cal H}|_{{\cal W}_{0}}=v\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q}}+g\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v}}-kq\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p}}\ .

Then, the tangency condition leads to

X_{\cal H}(p-mv)=-kq-gm=0\ \Longleftrightarrow\ g=-\frac{kq}{m}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{\rm(on ${\cal W}_{0}$)}\

and then

X_{\cal H}|_{{\cal W}_{0}}=v\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q}}-\frac{kq}{m}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v}}-kq\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p}}\ .

Its integral curves
(q(t),v(t),p(t))
are the solutions to

\frac{dq}{dt}=v\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptm\frac{dv}{dt}=-kq\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dp}{dt}=-kq\ .

Te first two equations are equivalent to

m\frac{d^{2}q}{dt^{2}}=-kq\ ,

which is the Euler–Lagrange equation of the system.
Furthermore, using the constraint
p=mv
(the Legendre map),
the first and third equations are

\frac{dq}{dt}=\frac{p}{m}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dp}{dt}=-kq\ ;

which are the Hamilton equations for the system.

3.7.2
Central forces: the Kepler problem

The
Kepler problem
consists in studying
the motion of a particle of mass
m

under the action of
Newtonian central forces
.
It is well-known that the motion of such a particle is on a plane and hence

Q=\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We take polar coordinates
(r,\phi)
in the plane
(with origin at the center of the force).

Lagrangian formalism

The Lagrangian formalism takes place in
{\rm T}Q\simeq\mathbb{R}^{4}
, with local coordinates

(r,\phi,v_{r},v_{\phi})
. The Lagrangian function is

{\cal L}=\frac{1}{2}m(v_{r}^{2}+r^{2}v_{\phi}^{2})-\frac{K}{r}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptK\not=0\ ;

therefore

\displaystyle E_{\cal L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}m(v_{r}^{2}+r^{2}v_{\phi}^{2})+\frac{K}{r}\ ,

\displaystyle\Theta_{\cal L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m(v_{r}\,{\rm d}r+r^{2}v_{\phi}\,{\rm d}\phi)\ ,

\displaystyle\Omega_{\cal L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m({\rm d}r\wedge{\rm d}v_{r}+r^{2}{\rm d}\phi\wedge{\rm d}v_{\phi}-2rv_{\phi}\,{\rm d}r\wedge{\rm d}\phi)\ ,

and the Lagrangian is regular.
For
\displaystyle{\rm X}_{\cal L}=f_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{r}}+f_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}+g_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{r}}}+g_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{\phi}}}
,
equation (
3.4
) gives

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\cal L})\Omega_{\cal L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m\,[f_{r}\,{\rm d}v_{r}+f_{\phi}r^{2}\,{\rm d}v_{\phi}-(g_{r}-2rv_{\phi}f_{\phi})\,{\rm d}r-(g_{\phi}r^{2}+2rv_{\phi}f_{r})\,{\rm d}\phi]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle mv_{r}\,{\rm d}v_{r}+mr^{2}v_{\phi}\,{\rm d}v_{\phi}+\Big(mrv_{\phi}^{2}-\frac{K}{r^{2}}\Big){\rm d}r={\rm d}E_{\cal L}\ ,

which leads to

f_{r}=v_{r}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptf_{\phi}=v_{\phi}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptmg_{r}=2mrv_{\phi}f_{\phi}-mrv_{\phi}^{2}+\frac{K}{r^{2}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptg_{\phi}=-\frac{2v_{\phi}f_{r}}{r}\ ,

and then the Euler–Lagrange vector field is

X_{\cal L}=v_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{r}}+v_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}+\Big(rv_{\phi}^{2}+\displaystyle\frac{K}{mr^{2}}\Big)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{r}}}-\frac{2v_{\phi}v_{r}}{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{\phi}}}\ .

Then, its integral curves
(r(t),\phi(t),v_{r}(t),v_{\phi}(t))
are the solutions to

\displaystyle\frac{dr}{dt}=v_{r}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{d\phi}{dt}=v_{\phi}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptm\frac{dv_{r}}{dt}=mrv_{\phi}^{2}+\frac{K}{r^{2}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dv_{\phi}}{dt}=-\frac{2v_{\phi}v_{r}}{r}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptm\frac{d^{2}r}{dt^{2}}=mr\Big(\frac{d\phi}{dt}\Big)^{2}+\frac{K}{r^{2}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{d^{2}\phi}{dt^{2}}=-\frac{2}{r}\,\frac{d\phi}{dt}\,\frac{dr}{dt}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{d}{dt}\Big(m\frac{dr}{dt}\Big)=mr\Big(\frac{d\phi}{dt}\Big)^{2}+\frac{K}{r^{2}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{d}{dt}\Big(mr^{2}\frac{d\phi}{dt}\Big)=0

\displaystyle\ ,

which are the Euler–Lagrange equations for this system.

There is a Lagrangian exact Noether symmetry
which is generated by the vector field
\displaystyle Y=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}
,
since

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\Theta_{\cal L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}\right)\left(m(v_{r}\,{\rm d}r+r^{2}v_{\phi}\,{\rm d}\phi)\right)

(3.26)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm d}\mathop{i}\nolimits\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}\right)\left(m(v_{r}\,{\rm d}r+r^{2}v_{\phi}\,{\rm d}\phi)\right)+\mathop{i}\nolimits\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}\right)\,{\rm d}\left(m(v_{r}\,{\rm d}r+r^{2}v_{\phi}\,{\rm d}\phi)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm d}(mr^{2}v_{\phi})+\mathop{i}\nolimits\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}\right)\left(m({\rm d}v_{r}\wedge{\rm d}r+r^{2}{\rm d}v_{\phi}\wedge{\rm d}\phi-2rv_{\phi}\,{\rm d}\phi\wedge{\rm d}r)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m(2rv_{\phi}{\rm d}r+r^{2}{\rm d}v_{\phi}-r^{2}{\rm d}v_{\phi}-2rv_{\phi}{\rm d}r)=0\ ,

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)E_{\cal L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}m(v_{r}^{2}+r^{2}v_{\phi}^{2})+\frac{K}{r}\right)=0\ ,

(3.27)

and hence its associated conserved quantity is

f_{Y}=\mathop{i}\nolimits\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}\right)\Theta_{\cal L}=mr^{2}v_{\phi}\ ;

that is, the angular momentum, as the last Euler–Lagrange equation shows.

Hamiltonian formalism

For the Hamiltonian formalism,
{\rm T}^{*}Q\simeq\mathbb{R}^{4}
,
with local coordinates
(r,\phi,p_{r},p_{\phi})
. First, the Legendre transformation is,

{\cal F}{\cal L}^{*}r=r\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt{\cal F}{\cal L}^{*}\phi=\phi\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt{\cal F}{\cal L}^{*}p_{r}=mv_{r}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt{\cal F}{\cal L}^{*}p_{\phi}=mr^{2}v_{\phi}\ ,

which is a diffeomorphism (the Lagrangian is hyperregular).
The canonical Hamiltonian function is

{\rm h}=\frac{p_{r}^{2}}{2m}+\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{2mr^{2}}+\frac{K}{r}\ .

As
\Omega={\rm d}r\wedge{\rm d}p_{r}+{\rm d}\phi\wedge{\rm d}p_{\phi}
,
for
\displaystyle X_{\rm h}=F_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{r}}+F_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}+G_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{r}}}+G_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{\phi}}}
,
equation (
3.11
) gives

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\rm h})\Omega=F_{r}\,{\rm d}p_{r}+F_{\phi}\,{\rm d}p_{\phi}-G_{r}\,{\rm d}r-G_{\phi}\,{\rm d}\phi=\frac{p_{r}}{m}\,{\rm d}p_{r}+\frac{p_{\phi}}{mr^{2}}\,{\rm d}p_{\phi}-\left(\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{mr^{3}}+\frac{K}{r^{2}}\right)\,{\rm d}r={\rm d}{\rm h}\ ,

which leads to

F_{r}=\frac{p_{r}}{m}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptF_{\phi}=\frac{p_{\phi}}{mr^{2}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptG_{r}=\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{mr^{3}}+\frac{K}{r^{2}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptG_{\phi}=0\ .

Then, the Hamiltonian vector field is

X_{\rm h}=\frac{p_{r}}{m}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{r}}+\frac{p_{\phi}}{mr^{2}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}+\left(\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{mr^{3}}+\frac{K}{r^{2}}\right)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{r}}}\ ,

and its integral curves
(r(t),\phi(t),p_{r}(t),p_{\phi}(t))
are the solutions to

m\frac{dr}{dt}=p_{r}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptmr^{2}\frac{d\phi}{dt}=p_{\phi}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dp_{r}}{dt}=\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{mr^{3}}+\frac{K}{r^{2}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dp_{\phi}}{dt}=0\ ,

which are the Hamilton equations for this system.

As in the above example, using the Legendre map
one can check that the Hamilton and
the Euler–Lagrange equations of the system are, in fact, equivalent.
Obviously
{\cal F}{\cal L}_{*}X_{\cal L}=X_{\rm h}
.

The Hamiltonian exact Noether symmetry is again the
vector field
\displaystyle Y=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}
, since

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\Theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}\right)\left(p_{r}\,{\rm d}r+p_{\phi}\,{\rm d}\phi\right)=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm d}\mathop{i}\nolimits\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}\right)\left(p_{r}\,{\rm d}r+p_{\phi}\,{\rm d}\phi\right)+\mathop{i}\nolimits\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}\right)\,{\rm d}\left(p_{r}\,{\rm d}r+p_{\phi}\,{\rm d}\phi\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm d}p_{\phi}-{\rm d}p_{\phi}=0\ ,

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y){\rm h}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}\right)\left(\frac{p_{r}^{2}}{2m}+\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{2mr^{2}}+\frac{K}{r}\right)=0\ ,

and, as the last Hamilton equation shows,
its associated conserved quantity is again the angular momentum

f_{Y}=\mathop{i}\nolimits\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}\right)\Theta=p_{\phi}\ .

This Hamiltonian system is also a good and simple example to show how the geometric method of reduction by symmetries proceeds.
The symmetry group
G
is the group of rotations on the orbit plane.
The Lie algebra
{\bf g}
is spanned by the vector field

\displaystyle\xi\equiv\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}
and hence
{\bf g}^{*}=\{{\rm d}\phi\}
.
Thus, the set of fundamental vector fields
\tilde{\bf g}
is generated by
the vector field
\displaystyle\tilde{\xi}\equiv Y=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
.
The action
\Phi\colon G\times{\rm T}^{*}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}^{*}Q
is effective, free, and proper,
and is a strongly symplectic action on the symplectic manifold
({\rm T}^{*}Q,\Omega)
, since it is exact, as we have seen.
In this way, the momentum map is given by

({\rm J}(r,p_{r},\phi,p_{\phi}))\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}\right):=p_{\phi}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{\rm(for every $(r,p_{r},\phi,p_{\phi})\in{\rm T}^{*}Q$)}

and, for every weakly regular value

\mu=\mu_{0}{\rm d}\phi\in{\bf g}^{*}
, the level sets of this map are

{\rm J}^{-1}(\mu):=\{(r,p_{r},\phi,p_{\phi})\in{\rm T}^{*}Q\ |\ p_{\phi}=\mu_{0}\}\ ;

They are defined by the constraints
\zeta:=p_{\phi}-\mu_{0}
;
that is, the hypersurfaces of constant angular momentum in
{\rm T}^{*}Q
,
and hence
\displaystyle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}
is tangent to all of them.
On each one, we have the presymplectic Hamiltonian system

({\cal J}^{-1}(\mu),\Omega_{\mu},{\rm h}_{\mu})
, where

\Omega_{\mu}:=j_{\mu}^{*}\Omega={\rm d}p_{r}\wedge{\rm d}r\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt{\rm h}_{\mu}=\frac{p_{r}^{2}}{2m}+\frac{K}{r}\ ;

hence
\displaystyle\ker\Omega_{\mu}=\left\langle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}\right\rangle
.
In this case,
G_{\mu}=G
and, applying the Marsden–Weinstein reduction theorem,
this presymplectic system reduces to another symplectic one,

({\cal J}^{-1}(\mu)/G,\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\rm h})
,
where the local coordinate are
(r,p_{r})
, and

\widehat{\Omega}={\rm d}p_{r}\wedge{\rm d}r\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\widehat{\rm h}=\frac{p_{r}^{2}}{2m}+\frac{K}{r}\ .

The Hamiltonian equation
\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widehat{X})\widehat{\Omega}={\rm d}\widehat{\rm h}
,
with
\widehat{X}\in\mathfrak{X}({\cal J}^{-1}(\mu)/G)
,
gives the Hamiltonian vector field

\widehat{X}=\frac{p_{r}}{m}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{r}}+\left(\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{mr^{3}}+\frac{K}{r^{2}}\right)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{r}}}\ ,

whose integral curves
(r(t),p_{r}(t))
are the solutions to the Hamilton equations

\frac{dr}{dt}=\frac{p_{r}}{m}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dp_{r}}{dt}=\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{mr^{3}}+\frac{K}{r^{2}}\ ,

In order to obtain the complete set of Hamiltonian equations of the system,
first, remember that the Hamiltonian vector field
X_{\rm h}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
is tangent to the level sets
p_{\phi}=ctn.
,
and second that, by the Legendre map,
p_{\phi}=mr^{2}v_{\phi}
.
Therefore, for the integral curves of
X_{\rm h}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
, we have that

\frac{d\phi}{dt}=\frac{p_{\phi}}{mr^{2}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dp_{\phi}}{dt}=0\ .

Unified Lagrangian–Hamiltonian formalism

Consider the unified bundle
{\cal W}={\rm T}Q\times_{Q}{\rm T}^{*}Q\simeq\mathbb{R}^{6}

with coordinates
(r,\phi,v_{r},v_{\phi},p_{r},p_{\phi})
.
The canonical presymplectic form is

\Omega_{\cal W}={\rm d}r\wedge{\rm d}p_{r}+{\rm d}\phi\wedge{\rm d}p_{\phi}\ ,

and the Hamiltonian function is

{\cal H}=p_{r}v_{r}+p_{\phi}v_{\phi}-\frac{1}{2}m(v_{r}^{2}+r^{2}v_{\phi}^{2})+\frac{K}{r}\ .

For
\displaystyle X_{\cal H}=f_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{r}}+f_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}+g_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{r}}}+g_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{\phi}}}+G_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{r}}}+G_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{\phi}}}
,
equation (
3.16
) gives

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\cal H})\Omega_{\cal W}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{r}\,{\rm d}p_{r}+f_{\phi}\,{\rm d}p_{\phi}-G_{r}\,{\rm d}r-G_{\phi}\,{\rm d}\phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Big(\frac{K}{r^{2}}+mrv_{\phi}^{2}\Big){\rm d}r+(p_{r}-mv_{r})\,{\rm d}v_{r}+(p_{\phi}-mr^{2}v_{\phi})\,{\rm d}v_{\phi}+v_{r}\,{\rm d}p_{r}+v_{\phi}\,{\rm d}p_{\phi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm d}{\cal H}\ ,

which leads to

f_{r}=v_{r}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptf_{\phi}=v_{\phi}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptG_{r}=\frac{K}{r^{2}}+mrv_{\phi}^{2}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptG_{\phi}=0\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptp_{r}=mv_{r}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptp_{\phi}=mr^{2}v_{\phi}\ .

The last two equations are constraints defining the submanifold

{\cal W}_{0}\hookrightarrow{\cal W}
which give the Legendre map.
The Hamiltonian vector field is

X_{\cal H}|_{{\cal W}_{0}}=v_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{r}}+v_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}+g_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{r}}}+g_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{\phi}}}+\Big(\frac{K}{r^{2}}+mrv_{\phi}^{2}\Big)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{r}}}\ ,

and the tangency condition leads to

\displaystyle X_{\cal H}(p_{r}-mv_{r})=\frac{K}{r^{2}}+mrv_{\phi}^{2}-g_{r}m=0

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle g_{r}=\frac{K}{mr^{2}}+rv_{\phi}^{2}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{\rm(on ${\cal W}_{0}$)}\ ,

\displaystyle X_{\cal H}(p_{\phi}-mr^{2}v_{\phi})=-m(g_{\phi}r^{2}+2f_{r}rv_{\phi})=0

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle g_{\phi}=-\frac{2v_{r}v_{\phi}}{r}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{\rm(on ${\cal W}_{0}$)}\ ;

therefore

X_{\cal H}|_{{\cal W}_{0}}=v_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{r}}+v_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}+\Big(rv_{\phi}^{2}+\displaystyle\frac{K}{mr^{2}}\Big)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{r}}}-\frac{2v_{r}v_{\phi}}{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{\phi}}}+\left(\frac{K}{r^{2}}+mrv_{\phi}^{2}\right)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{r}}}\ ,

and its integral curves
(r(t),\phi(t),v_{r}(t),v_{\phi}(t),p_{r}(t),p_{\phi}(t))
are the solutions to

\frac{dr}{dt}=v_{r}\ ,\ \frac{d\phi}{dt}=v_{\phi}\ ,\ \frac{dv_{r}}{dt}=\frac{K}{mr^{2}}+rv_{\phi}^{2}\ ,\ \frac{dv_{\phi}}{dt}=-\frac{2v_{r}v_{\phi}}{r}\ ,\ \frac{dp_{r}}{dt}=\frac{K}{r^{2}}+mrv_{\phi}^{2}\ ,\ \frac{dp_{\phi}}{dt}=0\ .

Te first four equations are equivalent to

m\frac{d^{2}r}{dt^{2}}=mr\Big(\frac{d\phi}{dt}\Big)^{2}+\frac{K}{r^{2}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{d^{2}\phi}{dt^{2}}=-\frac{2}{r}\,\frac{d\phi}{dt}\,\frac{dr}{dt}\ ,

which are the Euler–Lagrange equation of the system.
Furthermore, using the constraints
p_{r}=mv_{r}

and
p_{\phi}=mr^{2}v_{\phi}
(that is, the Legendre map),
the first, second, fifth, and sixth equations are

\frac{dr}{dt}=\frac{p_{r}}{m}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{d\phi}{dt}=\frac{p_{\phi}}{mr^{2}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dp_{r}}{dt}=\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{mr^{3}}+\frac{K}{r^{2}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dp_{\phi}}{dt}=0\ ;

which are the Hamilton equations for the system.

Chapter 4
Cosymplectic mechanics: Nonautonomous dynamical systems

In the previous chapters, we have studied autonomous Hamiltonian and Lagrangian systems;
that is, dynamical systems described by Hamiltonian or Lagrangian functions
which are independent of time.
Now we are going to analyze the case of
nonautonomous
dynamical systems
, which are described by
time-dependent Hamiltonian or Lagrangian functions.

The geometrical description of nonautonomous Hamiltonian and Lagrangian systems
can be made using different approaches.
For instance, one can use the so-called
contact formalism

[
1
,
109
,
154
,
155
,
314
]
, or a generalization
of it, the
jet bundle formalism
, using
jet
and
fiber bundles

[
106
,
154
,
255
,
275
,
317
]
.
However, these kinds of systems can also be described as symplectic
Hamiltonian systems by means of the

extended formalisms

[
13
,
154
,
233
,
301
,
302
,
331
]
, as singular (presymplectic) dynamical systems

[
64
,
154
]
, or using the Lagrangian-Hamiltonian
unified formalism

[
25
,
101
]
.
Nevertheless, one of the most elegant and simpler geometric description
of time-dependent systems is the
cosymplectic formulation

[
54
,
89
]
, and this is where we focus our attention in this chapter.

As in the above chapters, first we state the geometrical foundations on which this formulation is based, which are the
cosymplectic manifolds
and their properties.
Next we introduce the concept of
cosymplectic Hamiltonian system

and we describe, in particular, the Lagrangian and Hamiltonian
formalisms of nonautonomous Lagrangian dynamical systems using this formulation.
Symmetries, conserved quantities and the theorem of Noether are also discussed in this context.
The chapter is completed with a brief presentation of two
other very common formulations of time-dependent mechanics:
the
contact
and the
extended symplectic formulations
,
and showing their equivalence with the cosymplectic picture.
Finally, some examples of the previous chapter are analyzed
for the case in which the Lagrangians are time-dependent.

4.1
Notions on cosymplectic geometry

First, we establish the basic foundations of
cosymplectic geometry
(see, for instance,
[
54
,
57
,
89
]
for details).

4.1.1
Cosymplectic manifolds

Definition 4.1

Let
M
be a differentiable manifold of dimension
2n+1
.
A
cosymplectic structure
on
M
is a couple

(\eta,\omega)
, where
\eta\in{\Omega}^{1}(M)
and
\omega\in{\Omega}^{2}(M)

are closed forms such that

\eta\wedge\omega^{n}
is a volume form.
Then,
(M,\eta,\omega)
is called a
cosymplectic manifold
.

If
\eta\wedge\omega^{n}
is not a volume form
and
{\rm dim}\,M
is arbitrary, then we say that
(\eta,\omega)

is a
precosymplectic structure
on
M
and

(M,\eta,\omega)
is a
precosymplectic manifold
.

The (pre)cosymplectic structure is said to be
exact
if

\omega={\rm d}\theta
, for some
\theta\in{\Omega}^{1}(M)
.

Proposition 4.2

If
(\eta,\omega)
is a cosymplectic structure on
M
,
then there exists a unique vector field

R\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
, called the
Reeb vector field
,
which is characterized by the conditions

\mathop{i}\nolimits(R)\eta=1\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits(R)\omega=0\ .

(4.1)

(
Proof
)
Observe that, by the second condition,
R\in\ker\,\omega
.
From the condition that
\eta\wedge\omega^{n}
is a volume form,
we have that
{\rm rank}\,\omega=2n
and hence

\ker\,\omega
is a
1
-dimensional
{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
-module.
Therefore, the first condition allows us to select one generator of this module.

The local structure of cosymplectic manifolds is given by the following extension of Darboux Theorem
[
138
,
143
]
:

Theorem 4.3

(Darboux)
Let
(M,\eta,\omega)
be a cosymplectic manifold.
Then, for every
{\rm p}\in M
, there exists an open neighborhood
U\subset M
,
{\rm p}\in U
,
which is the domain of a local chart of coordinates
(t,x^{i},y_{i})
,

1\leq i\leq n
, such that

\eta|_{U}={\rm d}t\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\omega|_{U}={\rm d}x^{i}\wedge{\rm d}y_{i}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptR|_{U}=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}\ .

These are called
Darboux
or
canonical coordinates

of the cosymplectic manifold.

(
Proof
)
The idea of the proof is the following:
in a cosymplectic manifold there is a symplectic foliation
which is made of the leaves of the distribution generated by
\ker\,\eta

(which are
2n
-dimensional submanifolds and, on each one of them, the restriction of
\omega
is a symplectic form
since it has maximal rank
2n
, by the condition of the volume form).
Then we take coordinates adapted to the foliation and,
on each leaf, apply the symplectic Darboux Theorem.
In this way we have local coordinates
(x^{i},y_{i},\tilde{z})

such that
\omega={\rm d}x^{i}\wedge{\rm d}y_{i}
.
Finally, we write
\eta
as a combination of all of these coordinates,

\eta=f(q,p,\tilde{z}){\rm d}\tilde{z}
,
with
f
a nonvanishing function, and then we can redefine the coordinate
z
.
In these coordinates, the Reeb vector field has the expression given by the theorem.

Remark 4.4

For precosymplectic manifolds, there is a similar result
[
138
]
.
In fact, if
(M,\eta,\omega)
is a precosymplectic manifold with
{\rm rank}\,\omega=2r<{\rm dim}\,M-1\equiv m-1
; then,
for every point on
M
, there exists a local chart
(U;t,x^{i},y_{i},z^{j})
, where
1\leq i\leq r
,
1\leq j\leq m-2r-1
, such that

\eta|_{U}={\rm d}t\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\omega|_{U}={\rm d}x^{i}\wedge{\rm d}y_{i}\ .

These local charts are the so-called
precosymplectic charts
,
and their coordinates are the
canonical coordinates
or

Darboux coordinates
of the precosymplectic manifold in this chart
[
143
]
.
In addition, for precosymplectic manifolds the solution to equations (
4.1
) is not unique
and Reeb vector fields are not uniquely defined.

Canonical model
:
The canonical model for cosymplectic manifolds is the following:
consider the manifold
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q
with canonical projections

\pi_{1}\colon\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\ ,\ \pi_{2}\colon\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q\rightarrow{\rm T}^{*}Q\ ,\ \pi_{0}\colon\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q\rightarrow Q\ ,\ \pi_{1,0}\colon\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times Q\ .

If
(q^{i})
are local coordinates on
U\subseteq Q
, the
induced local coordinates
(t,q^{i},p_{i})

on
\pi_{0}^{-1}(U)=\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}U
are given by

t({\rm t},\alpha_{q})={\rm t}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptq^{i}({\rm t},\alpha_{q})=x^{i}(q)\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptp_{i}({\rm t},\alpha_{q})=\alpha_{q}\left(\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial q^{i}}\Big|_{q}\right)\ ,

for
{\rm t}\in\mathbb{R}
,
q\in Q
and
\alpha_{q}\in{\rm T}_{q}^{*}Q
.
We define the differential forms on
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q
,

\eta=\pi_{1}^{*}{\rm d}t\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\theta=\pi_{2}^{*}\Theta\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\omega=\pi_{2}^{*}\Omega\,,

where
\Theta
and
\Omega
are the canonical forms on
{\rm T}^{*}Q
. In local coordinates we have

\eta={\rm d}t\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\theta=p_{i}{\rm d}q^{i}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\omega={\rm d}q^{i}\wedge{\rm d}p_{i}\,.

Hence
(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q,\eta,\omega)
is a
cosymplectic manifold, and the natural coordinates of
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q

are Darboux coordinates for this canonical cosymplectic structure.
Furthermore,
\displaystyle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}
is its Reeb vector field.

Almost-canonical cosymplectic manifolds
:
There is another kind of cosymplectic manifolds which are specially relevant:
those which are of the form
M=\mathbb{R}\times N
, where

(N,\Omega)
is a symplectic manifold.
Then, denoting by

\pi_{\mathbb{R}}\colon\mathbb{R}\times N\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\pi_{N}\colon\mathbb{R}\times N\rightarrow N

the canonical projections, we have the differential forms

\eta=\pi_{\mathbb{R}}^{*}{\rm d}t\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\omega=\pi_{M}^{*}\Omega\ .

The conditions given in Definition
4.1

are verified and hence
\mathbb{R}\times N
is a cosymplectic manifold.
From the Darboux Theorem
4.3
we have local coordinates
(t,x^{i},y_{i})
on
\mathbb{R}\times N
.
These kinds of
k
-cosymplectic manifolds are sometimes
called
almost-canonical
k
-cosymplectic manifolds
.
Observe that the standard model is a particular class of these kinds of
k
-cosymplectic manifolds,
where
N={\rm T}^{*}Q
.

Every cosymplectic manifold
(M,\eta,\omega)
is endowed with
the natural vector bundle isomorphism

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\flat_{(\eta,\omega)}\colon{\rm T}M&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&{\rm T}^{*}M\\
\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\cr({\rm p},X_{\rm p})&\mapsto&\big({\rm p},\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\rm p})\omega_{\rm p}+((\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\rm p})\eta_{\rm p})\eta_{\rm p}\big)\,.\end{array}

and its inverse
\sharp_{(\eta,\omega)}=\flat_{(\eta,\omega)}^{-1}\colon{\rm T}^{*}M\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}M
.
Their natural extensions are the
{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
-module isomorphisms
which are denoted with the same notation,

\begin{array}[]{rccl}\flat_{(\eta,\omega)}\colon&\mathfrak{X}(M)&\longrightarrow&{\Omega}^{1}(M)\\
&X&\longmapsto&\mathop{i}\nolimits(X)\omega+(\mathop{i}\nolimits(X)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\end{array}\ ,

and its inverse
\sharp_{(\eta,\omega)}=\flat_{(\eta,\omega)}^{-1}\colon{\Omega}^{1}(M)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathfrak{X}(M)
.
In particular, for the Reeb vector field, we have that

\flat_{(\eta,\omega)}(R)=\eta
.

4.1.2
Hamiltonian, gradient, and evolution vector fields

Using the natural
{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
-module isomorphism
\flat_{(\eta,\omega)}

introduced in the above section,
one can associate to every function
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)

some particular vector fields:

Definition 4.5

Let
(M,\eta,\omega)
be a cosymplectic manifold and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
.

The
Hamiltonian vector field
associated with
f

is the vector field
X_{f}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
defined by

\flat_{(\eta,\omega)}(X_{f}):={\rm d}f-R(f)\eta
.

The
gradient vector field
associated with
f

is the vector field
{\rm grad}\,f\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
defined by

\flat_{(\eta,\omega)}({\rm grad}\,f):={\rm d}f
.

The
evolution vector field
associated with
f

is the vector field
{\cal E}_{f}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
defined by

{\cal E}_{f}:=R+X_{f}
; or, equivalently

\flat_{(\eta,\omega)}({\cal E}_{f})={\rm d}f-(R(f)-1)\eta
.

These vector fields can be equivalently characterized as follows:

Proposition 4.6

The
Hamiltonian vector field
associated with
f

is determined by the equations:

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f})\eta=0\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f})\omega={\rm d}f-R(f)\eta\ .

(4.2)

The
gradient vector field
associated with
f
is determined by the equations:

\mathop{i}\nolimits({{\rm grad}\,f})\eta=R(f)\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits({{\rm grad}\,f})\omega={\rm d}f-R(f)\eta\ .

(4.3)

The
evolution vector field
associated with
f

is determined by the equations:

\mathop{i}\nolimits({\cal E}_{f})\eta=1\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits({\cal E}_{f})\omega={\rm d}f-R(f)\eta\ .

(4.4)

(
Proof
)
For every
f\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
,
if
X_{f}
is the Hamiltonian vector field associated with
f
,
using the definitions of the isomorphism
\flat_{(\eta,\omega)}
and of
X_{f}
, first we have that,

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f}){\rm d}\eta+(\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f})\eta)\eta=\flat_{(\eta,\omega)}(X_{f})={\rm d}f-R(f)\,\eta\ ,

(4.5)

and contracting both members with
R
and using (
4.1
), we get
