(\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f})\eta)\mathop{i}\nolimits(R)\eta=\mathop{i}\nolimits(R){\rm d}f-R(f)\,\mathop{i}\nolimits(R)\eta=R(f)-R(f)=0\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{f})\eta=0\ ,

since this holds for every
f
.
Now, going to (
4.5
), we obtain that

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{f}){\rm d}\eta={\rm d}f-R(f)\,\eta\ .

Repeating the same procedure for the gradient and the evolution vector fields associated with
f
,
we obtain the corresponding equations for
{\rm grad}\,f
and
{\cal E}_{f}
.

As in the above chapters, from these results it is immediate to obtain that:

Proposition 4.7

The equations for the integral curves
c\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M

of the Hamiltonian, gradient, and evolution vector fields associated with

f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
are, respectively:

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{c})(\eta\circ c)=0\

,

\displaystyle\ \mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{c})(\omega\circ c)=({\rm d}f-R(f)\eta)\circ c\ ,

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{c})(\eta\circ c))=R(f)\circ c\

,

\displaystyle\ \mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{c})(\omega\circ c))=({\rm d}f-R(f)\eta)\circ c\ ,

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{c})(\eta\circ c))=1\

,

\displaystyle\ \mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{c})(\omega\circ c))=({\rm d}f-R(f)\eta)\circ c\ .

(4.6)

Local expressions
:
In Darboux coordinates on
M
, we have that
\displaystyle R(f)=\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{t}}
; therefore

\displaystyle{\rm d}f-R(f)\eta=\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{x^{i}}}{\rm d}x^{i}+\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{y_{i}}}{\rm d}y_{i}
, and from (
4.2
), (
4.3
), and (
4.4
),
we obtain

1
1

1

Observe that the local expression of the Hamiltonian vector field in the cosymplectic formulation is the same as the Hamiltonian vector field in the symplectic formulation;
which justifies the terminology.
,

\displaystyle X_{f}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}-\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}\ ,

\displaystyle{\rm grad}\,f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial f}{\partial t}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}-\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}\ ,

\displaystyle{\cal E}_{f}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}-\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}\ .

(4.7)

Therefore, if
c(s)=(t(s),x^{i}(s),y_{i}(s))
is an integral curve of some of these vector fields, these expressions imply that
c(s)
should satisfy the following systems of differential equations,

\displaystyle\frac{dt}{ds}=0\

,

\displaystyle\ \frac{dx^{i}}{ds}=\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{i}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dy_{i}}{ds}=-\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{i}}\ ,

\displaystyle\frac{dt}{ds}=\frac{\partial f}{\partial t}\

,

\displaystyle\ \frac{dx^{i}}{ds}=\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{i}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dy_{i}}{ds}=-\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{i}}\ ,

\displaystyle\frac{dt}{ds}=1\

,

\displaystyle\ \frac{dx^{i}}{ds}=\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{i}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dy_{i}}{ds}=-\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{i}}\ ;

(4.8)

As in the symplectic case, a Poisson bracket can be defined now, using gradient vector fields. Indeed, it is immediate to prove that:

Proposition 4.8

Every cosymplectic manifold
(M,\eta,\omega)
is
a
Poisson manifold
, with the
Poisson bracket
defined by

\{f,q\}:=\omega({\rm grad}\,f,{\rm grad}\,g)\hskip 10.00002pt;\hskip 10.00002ptf,g\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)\ .

The expression of this Poisson bracket in Darboux coordinates is the usual one:

\{f,g\}=\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{i}}\frac{\partial g}{\partial x^{i}}-\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{i}}\frac{\partial g}{\partial y_{i}}\,.

(4.9)

As a consequence of these definitions and properties,
many of the results stated in Section
2.1

about Poisson brackets and canonical transformations for
symplectic manifolds can be extended also to this case.

4.2
Nonautonomous Hamiltonian dynamical systems

4.2.1
Nonautonomous Hamiltonian systems

Bearing in mind the above considerations and the set
of postulates for autonomous dynamical systems,
we can state the
following postulates for the geometric study of nonautonomous Hamiltonian dynamical systems.

Postulate 4.9

(First Postulate of nonautonomous Hamiltonian mechanics)
:
The phase space of a regular (resp. singular)
nonautonomous dynamical system
is a differentiable manifold
M
endowed with a cosymplectic
(resp. precosymplectic) structure
(\eta,\omega)
:

Postulate 4.10

(Second Postulate of nonautonomous Hamiltonian mechanics)
:
The observables or physical magnitudes of a nonautonomous dynamical system
are functions of
{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
.

Postulate 4.11

(Third Postulate of nonautonomous Hamiltonian mechanics)
:
The dynamics of a nonautonomous dynamical system
is given by a function
h\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)

(or, in general, a closed
1
-form
\alpha\in Z^{1}(M)
, such that
\alpha={\rm d}h
, locally)
which is called the
Hamiltonian function

(or the
Hamiltonian
1
-form
) of the system.
This function represents the energy of the system.

Postulate 4.12

(Fourth Postulate of nonautonomous Hamiltonian mechanics)
:
The dynamical trajectories of a nonautonomous dynamical system are the integral curves
of the evolution vector field
{\cal E}_{h}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
associated with
h
;
that is, of the vector field solution to equations (
4.4
).
Thus, they are the solutions to equations (
4.6
).

Then we define:

Definition 4.13

A
regular nonautonomous
or
cosymplectic Hamiltonian dynamical system

is a set
(M,\eta,\omega;h)
,
where
(M,\eta,\omega)
is a cosymplectic manifold
and
h\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
is the Hamiltonian function of the system.
If
(M,\eta,\omega)
is a precosymplectic manifold, then
(M,\eta,\omega;h)

is said to be a
singular nonautonomous
or
precosymplectic Hamiltonian dynamical system
.

Definition 4.14

Given a nonautonomous Hamiltonian dynamical system
(M,\eta,\omega;h)
,
the
Hamiltonian problem
posed by the system
consists in finding the evolution vector field
{\cal E}_{h}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)

associated with
h
(if it exists).

In addition, we have:

Proposition 4.15

If
(M,\eta,\omega;h)
is a regular nonautonomous Hamiltonian system,
then there exists a unique evolution vector field
{\cal E}_{h}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
;
that is, a unique vector field which is the solution to equations (
4.4
).

(
Proof
)
It is immediate because,
if the system is regular, the existence of the isomorphism
\sharp_{(\eta,\omega)}
is assured.

Remark 4.16

If
(M,\eta,\omega,h)
is a precosymplectic Hamiltonian system
equations (
4.4
) are not necessarily compatible everywhere on
M

and a suitable
constraint algorithm
must be implemented in order to find
a
final constraint submanifold

P_{f}\hookrightarrow M

(if it exists) where there are evolution vector fields
{\cal E}_{h}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
,
tangent to
P_{f}
which are solutions to equations (
4.4
) on
P_{f}

(they are not necessarily unique).
Singular nonautonomous systems and the
corresponding constraint algorithms are studied in
[
62
,
90
,
125
,
123
,
124
,
345
]
.

4.3
Nonautonomous Lagrangian dynamical systems

4.3.1
Geometric elements

In order to develop the cosymplectic Lagrangian formalism, first
we have to extend some canonical structures of

{\rm T}Q
to
{\rm T}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)
.
Notice that, as
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
is a product manifold, we can write

{\rm T}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)={\rm T}\mathbb{R}\oplus_{\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q}{\rm T}({\rm T}Q)

and this splitting extends in a natural way to vector fields.
Thus, any operation on tangent vectors to
{\rm T}Q

acts on tangent vectors to
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
.
In particular, the canonical endomorphism
J

of
{\rm T}({\rm T}Q)
yields a
canonical endomorphism

{\cal J}\colon{\rm T}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)
and,
similarly, the Liouville vector field on
{\rm T}Q

yields a
Liouville vector field

\Delta\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)
which is the Liouville vector field
of the vector bundle structure
\pi_{1,0}\colon\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times Q
.
In natural coordinates, their local expressions are

{\cal J}=\frac{\partial}{\partial v^{i}}\otimes{\rm d}q^{i}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\Delta=v^{i}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial v^{i}}\,.

Definition 4.17

Let
{\bf c}\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times Q
be a curve.
We can write
{\bf c}=(c_{1},c_{2})
, where

c_{1}\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
c_{2}\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow Q
.
The
lift
of
{\bf c}

to
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
is the curve

\widehat{\bf c}=(c_{1},\widetilde{c_{2}})\colon\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q\,,

where
\widetilde{c_{2}}
is the canonical lift of
c_{2}
to
{\rm T}Q
.
The curve
\widehat{\bf c}
is said to be
holonomic
on
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
.

A vector field
\Gamma\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)

is said to be a
sode
on
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
if its integral curves are holonomic.

As in the autonomous case, the last definition can be
equivalently expressed in terms of the canonical structures of

\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
, and so it is immediate to prove that:

Proposition 4.18

A vector field
\Gamma\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)

is a
sode
if, and only if,

{\cal J}(\Gamma)=\Delta
.

In coordinates, if

{\bf c}(t)=(s(t),q^{i}(t))
,
then

\widehat{\bf c}(t)=\left(s(t),q^{i}(t),\frac{dq^{i}}{dt}(t)\right)\ ,

and the local expression of a
sode
is

\Gamma=g\,\frac{\partial}{\partial s}+v^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial q^{i}}+f^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial v^{i}}\ ;

so, in coordinates a
sode
defines the following system of
differential equations

\frac{ds}{dt}=g(q,\dot{q},s)\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{d^{2}q^{i}}{dt^{2}}=f^{i}(q,\dot{q},s)\ .

4.3.2
Lagrangian formalism. Nonautonomous Lagrangian systems

The foundations of the Lagrangian formulation of
(first-order) nonautonomous dynamical system are analogous to those given
in Section
3.2.1
for autonomous Lagrangian systems;
and they can be stated as follows:

Postulate 4.19

(First Postulate of nonautonomous Lagrangian mechanics)
:
The configuration space of a system with
n
degrees of freedom
is
\mathbb{R}\times Q
, where
Q
is a
n
-dimensional differentiable manifold.
The phase space is the bundle
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
.

Postulate 4.20

(Second Postulate of nonautonomous Lagrangian mechanics)
:
The observables or physical magnitudes of the system are functions of
{\rm C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)
.

Postulate 4.21

(Third Postulate of nonautonomous Lagrangian mechanics)
:
There is a function
{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)
,
called the
Lagrangian function
,
which contains the dynamical information of the system.

Let
{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)
be a Lagrangian function.
As in the autonomous case, using the canonical structures in

\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
we can introduce the
Lagrangian forms

\theta_{\cal L}\in{\Omega}^{1}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)
and

\omega_{\cal L}\in{\Omega}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)
associated with
{\cal L}
,
which are defined as follows:

\theta_{\cal L}={\cal J}({\rm d}{\cal L})
and
\omega_{\cal L}=-{\rm d}\theta_{\cal L}
.
They have the local expressions

\theta_{\cal L}=\displaystyle\frac{\displaystyle\partial{\cal L}}{\displaystyle\partial v^{i}}\,{\rm d}q^{i}\ \hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\omega_{\cal L}={\rm d}q^{i}\wedge{\rm d}\left(\frac{\displaystyle\partial{\cal L}}{\displaystyle\partial v^{i}}\right)\ .

In the same way, we define the
energy Lagrangian function
associated with
{\cal L}
as

E_{\cal L}:=\Delta({\cal L})-{\cal L}
, whose local expression is

E_{\cal L}=v^{i}\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}-{\cal L}\ .

Definition 4.22

Given a Lagrangian

{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)
, the
Legendre map

associated with
{\cal L}
is the fiber derivative of
{\cal L}
,
considered as a function on the vector bundle

\pi_{0}\colon\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q\times\mathbb{R}
;
that is, the map

{\rm F}{\cal L}\colon\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q

given by

{\rm F}{\cal L}(t,q,v_{q})=\left(t,{\rm F}{\cal L}_{t}(q,v_{q})\right)\,,

where
{\cal L}_{t}\colon{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}
denotes the restriction of
{\cal L}
to each fiber of the bundle
\pi_{1}\colon\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}

(that is, the Lagrangian
{\cal L}
with
t
“freezed”).

In natural coordinates we have

t\circ{\rm F}{\cal L}=t\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptq^{i}\circ{\rm F}{\cal L}=q^{i}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptp_{i}\circ{\rm F}{\cal L}=\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\ .

Observe that, considering the canonical cosymplectic manifold

(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q,\eta,\omega)
, where
\omega=-{\rm d}\theta
,
then the Lagrangian forms can also be defined as

\theta_{\cal L}={\rm F}{\cal L}^{\;*}\theta\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\omega_{\cal L}={\rm F}{\cal L}^{\;*}\omega\ .

(4.10)

And, as in the autonomous case, we also have:

Definition 4.23

A Lagrangian function
{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)
is said
to be
regular
(resp.
hyperregular
) if the corresponding
Legendre map
{\rm F}{\cal L}
is a local (resp. global) diffeomorphism.
Elsewhere,
{\cal L}
is called a
singular
Lagrangian.

A singular Lagrangian function
{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)

is called
almost-regular
if
\mathcal{P}:={\rm F}{\cal L}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)
is
a closed submanifold of
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q
(we will denote
the natural embedding by
\jmath_{0}\colon\mathcal{P}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q
),

{\rm F}{\cal L}
is a submersion onto its image, and
the fibers
{\rm F}{\cal L}^{-1}(FL({\rm p}))
, for every
{\rm p}\in\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
, are
connected submanifolds of
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
.

Once again, as in the autonomous case, it is immediate to prove that
{\cal L}
is regular if, and only
if, the matrix
\left(\frac{\displaystyle\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\displaystyle\partial v^{i}\partial v^{j}}\right)
is regular everywhere.
Therefore, the following equivalences are also immediate:

Proposition 4.24

The following conditions are equivalent:

1.

The Lagrangian
{\cal L}
is regular.

2.

The Legendre map
{\rm F}{\cal L}
is a local diffeomorphism.

3.

The pair

({\rm d}t,\omega_{\cal L})
is a cosymplectic structure on
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
.

If
{\cal L}
is not regular, then
({\rm d}t,\omega_{\cal L})
is a precosymplectic structure on
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
.
Thus, if
{\cal L}
is a regular (resp. singular) Lagrangian,
we have that
(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q,{\rm d}t,\omega_{\cal L};E_{\cal L})
is a
cosymplectic (resp. precosymplectic) Hamiltonian system.
Then we define:

Definition 4.25

A
nonautonomous
or
pre)cosymplectic Lagrangian dynamical system

is a pair
(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q,{\cal L})
,
where
Q
is an
n
-dimensional manifold,
and
{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)
is the Lagrangian function of the system.

And now we can state:

Postulate 4.26

(Fourth Postulate of nonautonomous Lagrangian mechanics)
:
The dynamical trajectories of a nonautonomous Lagrangian system are the integral curves
of a vector field
\Gamma_{\cal L}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)
satisfying the conditions:

1.

\Gamma_{\cal L}
is the evolution vector field associated with
E_{\cal L}
;
that is, it is a solution to the equations

\mathop{i}\nolimits(\Gamma_{\cal L}){\rm d}t=1\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits(\Gamma_{\cal L})\omega_{\cal L}={\rm d}E_{\cal L}-R_{\cal L}(E_{\cal L}){\rm d}t\ .

(4.11)

2.

\Gamma_{\cal L}
is a
sode
:

{\cal J}(\Gamma_{\cal L})=\Delta
.

Therefore, these trajectories are the holonomic curves

{\bf c}\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q

which are the solutions to equations

\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{\bf c})({\rm d}t\circ{\bf c})=1\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{\bf c})(\omega_{\cal L}\circ{\bf c})=({\rm d}E_{\cal L}-R_{\cal L}(E_{\cal L}){\rm d}t)\circ{\bf c}\ .

(4.12)

Equations (
4.12
) are the

nonautonomous Euler–Lagrange equations for curves
.
Equations (
4.11
) are called the

nonautonomous Lagrangian equations for vector fields
and
a vector field solution to them (if it exists) is a

nonautonomous Lagrangian dynamical vector field
.
If, in addition,
\Gamma_{\cal L}
is a
sode
, then
it is called a
nonautonomous Euler–Lagrange vector field
of the system,

In this postulate,
R_{\cal L}
is a Reeb vector field of the structure

(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q,{\rm d}t,\omega_{\cal L})
, determined by the corresponding equations to (
4.1
) which are

\mathop{i}\nolimits(R_{\cal L}){\rm d}t=1\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits(R_{\cal L})\omega_{\cal L}=0\ .

Definition 4.27

Given a nonautonomous Lagrangian dynamical system
(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q,{\cal L})
,
the
Lagrangian problem
posed by the system
consists in finding a
sode
vector field
\Gamma_{\cal L}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)

solutions to (
4.11
).

Local expressions
:
Consider a natural chart
(U;t,q^{i},v^{i})
of
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
.
Bearing in mind the local expressions of the several geometric elements
appearing in the nonautonomous dynamical equations,
first we obtain that the Reeb vector field is given by

\displaystyle R_{\cal L}=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}+R^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\ ,

where the functions
R^{i}
are obtained from

\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial t\partial v^{j}}+\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{j}\partial v^{i}}R^{i}=0\ ,

and then we have that

R_{\cal L}(E_{\cal L})=-\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{t}}\ .

Therefore, if
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\cal L}=f\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}+A^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}+B^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}
,
equations (
4.11
), written in coordinates, lead to
f=1
and

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{j}\partial v^{i}}B^{i}+\left(\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial q^{j}\partial v^{i}}+\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{j}\partial q^{i}}\right)A^{i}+\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{i}\partial q^{j}}v^{i}+\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{j}\partial t}-\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\ ,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{j}\partial v^{i}}(A^{i}-v^{i})\ .

To demand that
\Gamma_{\cal L}
is a
sode
is locally equivalent to demand that

A^{i}=v^{i}
. Furthermore, the integral curves of
\Gamma_{\cal L}

are holonomic; that is, they are of the form
{\bf c}(t)=(t,q^{i}(t),\dot{q}^{i}(t))
and

A^{i}=v^{i}=\frac{dq^{i}}{dt}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptB^{i}=\frac{d^{2}q^{i}}{dt^{2}}\ ,

and the combination of these expressions with the above equations leads to the
equation of the integral curves of
\Gamma_{\cal L}
which is

\left(\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{j}\partial v^{i}}\circ{\bf c}\right)\frac{d^{2}q^{i}}{dt^{2}}+\left(\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{j}\partial q^{i}}\circ{\bf c}\right)\frac{dq^{i}}{dt}+\left(\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{j}\partial t}\circ{\bf c}\right)-\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\circ{\bf c}=0\ ,

or also in an equivalent form as in (
3.9
),
which is the classical coordinate expression of the Euler–Lagrange equations.

Remark 4.28

If the Lagrangian
{\cal L}
is singular (in particular almost-regular),
then the existence of solutions to equations (
4.11
)
is not assured except, perhaps, in a
submanifold of
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
. Furthermore,
when these solutions exist, they are not
sode
, in general.
Thus, in order to recover the Euler–Lagrange equations (
4.12
)
for the integral curves of
\Gamma_{\cal L}
,
the condition
{\cal J}(\Gamma_{\cal L})=\Delta
must be added to the
Lagrangian equations (
4.11
).
Then, as in the autonomous case, in general,
a constraint algorithm must be implemented in order to find
a submanifold
S\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
where the existence of

sode
vector fields solutions to the Lagrange equations on
S
,
and tangent to
S
, are assured.
Furthermore, Reeb vector fields
R_{\cal L}
are not uniquely defined by
equations (
4.1
) for this case; nevertheless
the dynamics and the constraint algorithm are independent
of the selected Reeb vector field
R_{\cal L}

[
90
]
.

4.3.3
Canonical Hamiltonian formalism

As in the autonomous case analyzed in Section
3.3.2
,
we study the case of hiperregular systems;
although all the results hold also for the case of
regular systems,
changing
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q
by
{\rm F}{\cal L}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)\subset\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q
.
First, as
{\rm F}{\cal L}
is a diffeomorphism we have:

Proposition 4.29

Let
(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q,{\cal L})
be a hiperregular nonautonomous Lagrangian system.
Then there exists a unique function
{\rm h}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q)
such that

{\rm F}{\cal L}^{*}{\rm h}=E_{\cal L}
,
which is the
Hamiltonian function
associated with the system

(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q,{\cal L})
.
The triple
(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q,{\rm d}t,\omega,{\rm h})
is the

canonical nonautonomous Hamiltonian system
associated with
(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q,{\cal L})
,
where
\omega\in{\Omega}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q)

is given in (
4.10
).

Therefore, we have the cosymplectic Hamiltonian system

(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q,{\rm d}t,\omega;{\rm h})
, fulfilling the postulates and results established in Section
4.2.1
.
and hence the Hamiltonian equations for vector fields

{\cal E}_{\rm h}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q)
and their
integral curves
{\bf c}\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q
read

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits({\cal E}_{\rm h}){\rm d}t=1\hskip 10.00002pt

,

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits({\cal E}_{\rm h})\omega={\rm d}{\rm h}-R({\rm h}){\rm d}t\ ,

(4.13)

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{\bf c})({\rm d}t\circ{\bf c})=1\

,

\displaystyle\ \mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{\bf c})(\omega\circ{\bf c})=({\rm d}{\rm h}-R({\rm h}){\rm d}t)\circ{\bf c}\ .

(4.14)

In canonical coordinates, the local expression
of the dynamical vector field
{\cal E}_{\rm h}
solution to equations
(
4.13
) is given by (
4.7
)
and the equations of its integral curves (
4.14
) are
equations (
4.8
), with
f={\rm h}
.
Then, since
\displaystyle\frac{dt}{ds}=1
implies
t(s)=s+const.

(
t
is an affine transformation of
s
), we deduce that

\frac{dx^{i}}{dt}=\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{i}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dy_{i}}{dt}=-\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{i}}\ ,

which are the
cosymplectic Hamilton equations
.

The relation between the Lagrangian and the canonical Hamiltonian formalisms of a (hyper)regular Lagrangian system is stated as in the autonomous case as follows:

Theorem 4.30

(Equivalence Theorem)

Let
(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q,{\cal L})
be a (hyper)regular Lagrangian system.

1.

If
\Gamma_{\cal L}
is the Lagrangian vector field
solution to equations (
4.11
),
then there exists a unique vector field

{\rm F}{\cal L}_{*}\Gamma_{\cal L}\equiv{\cal E}_{\rm h}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q)

which is the solution to equations (
4.13
).

Conversely, if
{\cal E}_{\rm h}
is the evolution vector field
solution to equation (
4.13
),
then there exists a unique vector field

{\rm F}{\cal L}_{*}^{-1}{\cal E}_{\rm h}\equiv\Gamma_{\cal L}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)

which is the solution to equations (
4.11
) and (
3.5
).

2.

Equivalently, if
\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times Q

is a curve and its canonical lift

\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
is a
solution to equation (
4.12
),
then
{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}={\rm F}{\cal L}\circ\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}
is a curve
solution to equation (
4.14
).

Conversely, if
{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q

is a curve solution to equation (
4.14
),
then
\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}=\widehat{\pi_{(1,0)}\circ{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}}\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q

is a curve solution to equation (
4.12
).

(
Proof
)
The proof follows the same pattern as in Theorem
3.46
.

Remark 4.31

If the Lagrangian is almost-regular, then there exists

{\rm h}_{0}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{P})
such that

{\rm F}{\cal L}_{0}^{*}{\rm h}_{0}=E_{\cal L}
, where

{\rm F}{\cal L}_{0}\colon\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{P}
is defined by

\jmath_{0}\circ{\rm F}{\cal L}_{0}={\rm FL}
.
Now, taking
\omega_{0}=\jmath_{0}^{*}\omega
, the set

(\mathcal{P},{\rm d}t,\omega_{0};{\rm h}_{0})
is a precosymplectic
Hamiltonian system which is called the
canonical Hamiltonian system

associated with
(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q,{\cal L})
.
In particular, the equations equivalent to (
4.13
) are

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{0}){\rm d}t=1\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{0})(\jmath_{0}^{*}\omega_{0})={\rm d}{\rm h}_{0}-R_{0}({\rm h}_{0}){\rm d}t\ .

where
X_{0}\in\mathfrak{X}({\cal P})
. The existence of such a vector field

X_{0}
solution to the above equations is not
assured except, perhaps, on a submanifold

\mathcal{P}_{f}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{P}

to which it is tangent. Moreover, the solution is not unique.
Furthermore, although Reeb vector fields
R
are not uniquely defined by
equations (
4.1
) for this case,
the dynamics and the constraint algorithm are independent
of the selected Reeb vector field
R_{0}
.
(Details on the construction of the canonical Hamiltonian formalism for
almost-regular nonautonomous Lagrangians and a deeper study on the constraint algorithms
and the equivalence between both formalisms
can be found, for instance, in

[
62
,
90
,
211
,
123
,
124
,
345
]
).

4.4
Unified Lagrangian-Hamiltonian formalism for nonautonomous systems

This section is devoted to explain the extension of the
Skinner-Rusk unified formalism, which was presented in
Section
3.4
, to the case of nonautonomous dynamical systems, using the cosymplectic setting
(see
[
25
,
52
,
194
]

for different but equivalent approaches).
This extended formalism is quite similar to the autonomous case.

4.4.1
Extended unified bundle. Unified nonautonomous formalism

Definition 4.32

Let
Q
be a
n
-dimensional differentiable manifold.
The
extended unified bundle
or
extended Pontryagin bundle
is

{\mathcal{M}}:=\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q\times_{Q}{\rm T}^{*}Q
, and has natural projections

\kappa_{1}\colon{\mathcal{M}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\kappa_{2}\colon{\mathcal{M}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\kappa_{0}\colon{\mathcal{M}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times Q

Its natural coordinates are
(t,q^{i},v^{i},p_{i})
.

Definition 4.33

A curve
{\bf c}\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\cal M}

is
holonomic
on
{\cal M}
if

\kappa_{1}\circ{\bf c}\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
is holonomic.

A vector field
\Gamma\in\mathfrak{X}({\cal M})
is a
holonomic vector field
on
{\cal M}

if its integral curves are holonomic on
{\cal M}
.

The coordinate expressions of holonomic curves and vector fields on
{\cal M}
are the following

\displaystyle{\bf c}(t)=\Big(s(t),q^{i}(t),\frac{dq^{i}}{dt}(t),p_{i}(t),\Big)\ ,

\displaystyle\Gamma=g\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{s}}+v^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}+F^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}+G_{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{i}}}\ .

The extended unified bundle
{\cal M}
is endowed with the following canonical structures:

Definition 4.34

1.

The
coupling function
on
{\mathcal{M}}

is the map
{\rm C}\colon{\mathcal{M}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}
defined by:

\begin{array}[]{ccccl}{\rm C}&:&{\mathcal{M}}=\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q\times_{Q}{\rm T}^{*}Q&\longrightarrow&\mathbb{R}\\
\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\cr&&(t,q,v_{q},\xi_{q})=(t,q^{i},v^{i},p_{i})&\mapsto&\langle v_{q},\xi_{q}\rangle=v^{i}p_{i}\end{array}\ .

2.

If
\theta\in{\Omega}^{1}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q)
and
\omega\in{\Omega}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q)

are the canonical forms on
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q
,
then the canonical forms on
{\mathcal{M}}
are

\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}:=\kappa_{2}^{*}\,\theta\in{\Omega}^{1}({\mathcal{M}})\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\Omega_{\mathcal{M}}:=\kappa_{2}^{*}\,\omega=-{\rm d}\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}\in{\Omega}^{2}({\mathcal{M}})\ ,

We denote also by
{\rm d}t
the pull-back to
{\mathcal{M}}
of the canonical
1
-form on
\mathbb{R}
.

Definition 4.35

Given a Lagrangian function
{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q\right)
,
if
{\mathfrak{L}}=\kappa_{1}^{*}{\cal L}
, the
Hamiltonian function
is defined as

{\rm H}={\rm C}-{\mathfrak{L}}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}})\ .

In coordinates, we have

\Theta_{\cal M}=p_{i}{\rm d}q^{i}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\Omega_{\cal M}={\rm d}q^{i}\wedge{\rm d}p_{i}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt{\rm H}=v^{i}p_{i}-{\mathfrak{L}}(t,q^{i},v^{i})\ .

The triple
({\cal M},{\rm d}t,\Omega_{\cal M})
is a precosymplectic manifold, since

\displaystyle\ker\Omega_{\cal M}=\left\langle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}},\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\right\rangle
,
where the Reeb vector field can be taken to be

\displaystyle R=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}
;
and hence

({\cal M},{\rm d}t,\Omega_{\cal M},{\rm H})
is a precosymplectic Hamiltonian system.
The
dynamical problem
for this system consists in finding
an evolution vector field
X_{\rm H}\in\mathfrak{X}({\cal M})
,
which is a solution to the equations

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\rm H}){\rm d}t=1\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\rm H})\Omega_{\cal M}={\rm d}{\rm H}-R({\rm H}){\rm d}t\ ,

(4.15)

and then the integral curves
{\bf c}\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\cal W}

of
X_{\rm H}
are solutions to the equations

\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{\bf c})({\rm d}t\circ{\bf c})=1\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{\bf c})(\Omega_{\cal W}\circ{\bf c}))=\left({\rm d}{\rm H}-R({\rm H}){\rm d}t\right)\circ{\bf c}\ .

(4.16)

But, as
({\cal M},\Omega_{\cal M},{\rm H})
is a precosymplectic Hamiltonian system,
these equations are not compatible on
{\cal M}
.
In fact, for an arbitrary vector field in
\mathfrak{X}({\cal M})
,

X_{\rm H}=g\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{s}}+f^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}+F^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}+G_{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{i}}}\ ,

equations (
4.15
) leads to

g=1\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptf^{i}=v^{i}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptG_{i}=\displaystyle\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptp_{i}=\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\ .

(4.17)

which give different kind of information:

•

The first equation fixes the evolution parameter
s=t
.

•

The second equations assure that
X_{\rm H}
is a holonomic vector field on
{\cal M}

(regardless the regularity of the Lagrangian function).

•

The third equations allow us to determine the component functions
G_{i}
.

•

The fourth equations are compatibility conditions;
that is,
compatibility constraints
defining a submanifold

{\cal M}_{0}\hookrightarrow{\cal M}

where vector fields
X_{\rm H}
solution to (
4.15
) are defined.
As in the autonomous case, these constraints give the Legendre map and
{\cal M}_{0}={\rm graph}({{\rm F}{\cal L}})
.

In this way, we have obtained

X_{\rm H}|_{{\cal M}_{0}}=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}+v^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}+F^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}+\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{i}}}\ ,

where the functions
F^{i}
are still undetermined.
Now, the constraint algorithm continues by demanding the tangency of

X_{\rm H}
to the submanifold
{\cal M}_{0}
; that is, we have
to impose that

\displaystyle{X_{\rm H}\Big(p_{i}-\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\Big)}\Big|_{{}_{{\cal M}_{0}}}=0
,
and then we obtain the equations for the functions
F^{i}
:

\frac{\partial^{2}L}{\partial v^{i}\partial v^{j}}F^{j}+\frac{\partial^{2}L}{\partial q^{j}\partial v^{i}}v^{j}+\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial t\partial v^{i}}-\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}=0\hskip 10.00002pt(\text{on }\mathcal{M}_{0})\,.

(4.18)

If
{\cal L}
is a regular Lagrangian, these equations are compatible, and they have a unique vector field
X_{\rm H}
which is the solution to (
4.15
) on
{\cal M}_{0}
,
and the last system of equations give the dynamical trajectories
(i.e., the solutions to equations (
4.16
)
on
{\cal M}_{0}
).
If
{\cal L}
is singular, equations (
4.18
) can be compatible or not
and, eventually, new compatibility constraints can appear,
defining a new submanifold
{\cal M}_{1}\hookrightarrow{\cal M}_{0}
.
Then, the constraint algorithm continues by demanding
the tangency of solutions to
{\cal M}_{1}
, and so on.
In the most favorable cases,
there is a submanifold
{\cal M}_{f}\hookrightarrow{\cal M}_{0}

such that there exist holonomic vector fields
X_{\rm H}\in\mathfrak{X}({\cal M})
defined on
{\cal M}_{0}
and tangent to
{\cal M}_{f}
,
which are solutions to equations (
4.15
)
at support on
{\cal M}_{f}
(they are not unique necessarily).

4.4.2
Recovering the nonautonomous Lagrangian and Hamiltonian formalisms

The analysis of the equivalence among the unified, the Lagrangian, and Hamiltonian formalisms is made again
for the hyperregular case (the regular case is locally the same.
See
[
25
]
for details about the singular case).

As in the autonomous case, denoting by

\jmath_{0}\colon\mathcal{M}_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}

the natural embedding, we have the projections

(\kappa_{1}\circ\jmath_{0})\colon\mathcal{M}_{0}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt(\kappa_{2}\circ\jmath_{0})\colon\mathcal{M}_{0}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q\ ,

where
\kappa_{1}\circ\jmath_{0}
is a diffeomorphism.
The diagram that summarizes the situation is

\textstyle{{\cal M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{2}}

\textstyle{{\cal M}_{0}={\rm graph}\,({\rm F}{\cal L})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{1}\circ\jmath_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{2}\circ\jmath_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\jmath_{0}}

\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm F}{\cal L}}

\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q}

Hence, functions, differential forms, and vector fields on
{\cal M}

tangent to
{\cal M}_{0}
can be restricted to
{\cal M}_{0}
, and then
they can be translated to the Lagrangian side
by using the diffeomorphism
\kappa_{1}\circ\jmath_{0}
,
and to the Hamiltonian side using the Legendre map and the projection

\kappa_{2}
.

Therefore, if
{\bf c}(t)=(t,q^{i}(t),v^{i}(t),p_{i}(t))
is a solution to equation (
4.15
),
and hence it is an integral curve of the vector field

\,X_{\rm H}
solution to equations (
4.15
),
then (
4.18
) leads to

\frac{d}{dt}\Big(\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\circ{\bf c}\Big)=\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\circ{\bf c}\ ,

(4.19)

and from equations (
4.17
) we obtain

\frac{dq^{i}}{dt}=v^{i}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dp_{i}}{dt}=\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\circ{\bf c}=-\frac{\partial{{\rm H}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\circ{\bf c}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptp_{i}=\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\circ{\bf c}\ .

(4.20)

From the first group of these equations, together with (
4.19
),
we recover the Euler–Lagrange equations for the curves

{\bf c}_{\cal L}(t)=(t,q^{i}(t),v^{i}(t))
.
In addition,
\displaystyle\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}=-\frac{\partial{{\rm H}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}
, and hence
the second group of equations (
4.20
) is

\frac{dp_{i}}{dt}=-\frac{\partial{{\rm H}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\circ{\bf c}

and, using the local expression of
{\rm H}
and the first group of equations (
4.20
), we get

\frac{\partial{{\rm H}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\circ{\bf c}=v^{i}=\frac{dq^{i}}{dt}\ ;

but, using the Legendre map (that is, the third group of equations (
4.17
))
we have that
{\rm H}={\rm F}{\cal L}^{*}{\rm h}
, and these last equations become the
Hamilton equations

for the curves
{\bf c}_{\rm h}(t)=(t,q^{i}(t),p_{i}(t))
.

Every curve
{\bf c}\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\cal M}
,
taking values on
{\cal M}_{0}
can be viewed as

{\bf c}=({\bf c}_{\cal L},{\bf c}_{\rm h})
, where

{\bf c}_{\cal L}=\kappa_{1}\circ{\bf c}\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q

and
{\bf c}_{\rm h}={\rm F}{\cal L}\circ{\bf c}_{\cal L}\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q
.
Thus, we have proved that:

Theorem 4.36

If
{\bf c}\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\cal M}
,
with
{\rm Im}\,{\bf c}\subset{\cal M}_{0}
,
is a curve fulfilling equations (
4.16
), then

{\bf c}_{\cal L}
is the lift to

\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
of the projected curve

{\bf c}_{o}=\kappa_{0}\circ{\bf c}\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times Q

(that is,
{\bf c}_{\cal L}
is a holonomic curve),
and it is a solution to equation (
4.12
),
where
E_{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)
is such that
{\rm H}=\kappa_{1}^{*}E_{\cal L}
.
Moreover, the curve

{\bf c}_{\rm h}=\kappa_{2}\circ{\bf c}={\rm F}{\cal L}\circ{\bf c}_{\cal L}

is a solution to equation (
4.14
),
where
{\rm h}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q)
is such that

{\rm H}=\kappa_{1}^{*}{\rm h}
.

Conversely, if
{\bf c}_{o}\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
is a curve such that

\widetilde{\bf c}_{o}\equiv{\bf c}_{\cal L}
is a solution to equation (
4.12
), then the curve

{\bf c}=({\bf c}_{\cal L},{\rm F}{\cal L}\circ{\bf c}_{\cal L})

is a solution to equation (
4.16
)
and
{\rm F}{\cal L}\circ{\bf c}_{\cal L}

is a solution to equation (
4.14
).

The curves
{\bf c}\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\cal M}
which are
solution to equation (
4.16
)
are the integral curves of the holonomic vector field
X_{\rm H}\in\mathfrak{X}({\cal M})

which is the solution to equations (
4.15
),
the curves
{\bf c}_{\cal L}\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q

are the integral curves of the holonomic vector field

\Gamma_{\cal L}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)
which is the
solution to equations (
4.11
), and the curves
{\bf c}_{\rm h}\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q

are the integral curves of the vector field
{\cal E}_{\rm h}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q)
which is the
solution to equations (
4.13
).
Therefore:

Theorem 4.37

Let
X_{\rm H}\in\mathfrak{X}({\cal M})
be the solution to equations (
4.15
) (on
{\cal M}_{0}
),
which is tangent to
{\cal M}_{0}
. Then:

The vector field
\Gamma_{\cal L}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)
, defined by

\Gamma_{\cal L}\circ\kappa_{1}={\rm T}\kappa_{1}\circ X_{\rm H}
,
is the
sode
solution to equations (
4.11
),
where
E_{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)
is such that
{\rm H}=\kappa_{1}^{*}E_{\cal L}
.

The vector field
{\cal E}_{\rm h}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q)
, defined by

{\cal E}_{\rm h}\circ\kappa_{2}={\rm T}\kappa_{2}\circ X_{\rm H}
,
is the solution to equations (
4.13
),
where
{\rm h}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q)
is such that

{\rm H}=\kappa_{1}^{*}{\rm h}
.
Furthermore
{\rm F}{\cal L}_{*}\Gamma_{\cal L}={\cal E}_{\rm h}
.

4.5
Symmetries of regular nonautonomous dynamical systems

We restrict the study of symmetries of regular nonautonomous dynamical system
to the case in which the phase space is an
almost-canonical cosymplectic manifold; i.e.,
\mathbb{R}\times M
,
with
M
a symplectic manifold, and, in particular, to the canonical case

M={\rm T}^{*}Q
.
Remember that, in this case, the natural projection

\pi_{\mathbb{R}}\colon\mathbb{R}\times M\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}
defines
a global canonical coordinate
t
.

This study is inspired in the ideas introduced mainly in
[
4
]
and follows
the scheme given in Section
2.3
.
(See also
[
314
]
for a very interesting description of symmetries in the Lagrangian formalism).

4.5.1
Symmetries of nonautonomous Hamiltonian systems. Noether Theorem

Let
(\mathbb{R}\times M,\eta,\omega;h)
be a regular nonautonomous (almost-canonical) Hamiltonian system, and
{\cal E}_{h}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}\times M)

its evolution vector field.

Definition 4.38

A function
f\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times M)
is a
conserved quantity

or a
constant of motion
if

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\cal E}_{h})f=0\ ;

Definition 4.39

A
dynamical symmetry
of the system
(\mathbb{R}\times M,\eta,\omega;h)

is a diffeomorphism
\Phi\colon\mathbb{R}\times M\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times M
satisfying that

\Phi_{*}{\cal E}_{h}={\cal E}_{h}\ .

Definition 4.40

An
infinitesimal dynamical symmetry
of the system
(\mathbb{R}\times M,\eta,\omega;h)

is a vector field
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
such that
the local diffeomorphisms generated by its flux
are dynamical symmetries of the system; that is,

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y){\cal E}_{h}=[Y,{\cal E}_{h}]=0\ .

It is immediate to check that the results obtained in Section
2.3.3

for the dynamical symmetries of autonomous Hamiltonian systems also hold in this case.

Definition 4.41

A
cosymplectic Noether symmetry
of the system
(\mathbb{R}\times M,\eta,\omega;h)
is a diffeomorphism

\Phi\colon\mathbb{R}\times M\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times M

satisfying the following conditions:

(a)
\Phi^{*}\omega=\omega
,  (b)
\Phi^{*}t=t
,  (c)
\Phi^{*}h=h
.

If the cosymplectic structure is exact, and
\omega=-{\rm d}\theta
,
then a cosymplectic Noether symmetry
is said to be
exact
if
\Phi^{*}\theta=\theta
.

Definition 4.42

An
infinitesimal cosymplectic Noether symmetry

of the system
(\mathbb{R}\times M,\eta,\omega;h)
is a vector field
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}\times M)
whose local flux are local cosymplectic Noether symmetries;
that is, it satisfies that:

(a)
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\omega=0
,  (b)
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)t=\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\eta=0
,  (c)
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)h=0
.

If the cosymplectic structure is exact,
an infinitesimal cosymplectic Noether symmetry
is said to be
exact
if
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\theta=0
.

For the Reeb vector field, we have the following property:

Proposition 4.43

1.

If
\Phi\in{\rm Diff}\,(\mathbb{R}\times M)
is a cosymplectic Noether symmetry, then
\Phi_{*}R=R
.

2.

If
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}\times M)

is an infinitesimal cosymplectic Noether symmetry,
then
[Y,R]=~0
.

(
Proof
)

1.

As
\Phi
is a cosymplectic Noether symmetry then

\Phi^{*}\omega=\omega
and
\Phi^{*}\eta=\eta
; then,
taking into account the conditions (
4.1
) that characterize the Reeb vector field, we have

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(\Phi_{*}R)\eta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(R)(\Phi^{-1})^{*}\eta=\mathop{i}\nolimits(R)\eta=1\ ,

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(\Phi_{*}R)\omega

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(R)(\Phi^{-1})^{*}\omega=\mathop{i}\nolimits(R)\omega=0\ ,

and the result is a consequence of the unicity of the Reeb vector field,
and so
\Phi_{*}R=R
.

2.

We have that

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits([Y,R])\omega=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\mathop{i}\nolimits(R)\omega-\mathop{i}\nolimits(R)\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\omega=0

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle[Y,R]\in\ker\,\omega\ ,

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits([Y,R])\eta=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\mathop{i}\nolimits(R)\eta-\mathop{i}\nolimits(R)\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\eta=0

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle[Y,R]\in\ker\,\eta\ ,

and then

[Y,R]\in\ker\,\omega\cap\ker\,\eta=\{0\}
(because
\eta\wedge\omega^{n}
is a volume form).

Remark 4.44

•

The condition
\Phi^{*}t=t
means that
cosymplectic Noether symmetries generate transformations along
the fibers of the projection

\pi_{\mathbb{R}}\colon\mathbb{R}\times M\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
; that
is, they leave the fibers of the projection

\pi_{\mathbb{R}}\colon\mathbb{R}\times M\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
invariant or,
what means the same thing,

\pi_{\mathbb{R}}\circ\Phi=\pi_{\mathbb{R}}
.

•

In the case of infinitesimal cosymplectic Noether symmetries, the
analogous condition is
\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y){\rm d}t=0
, which means that
Y
has
the local expression

\displaystyle Y=f^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}+g_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}
.
This means that
Y
is tangent to the fibers of the projection

\pi_{\mathbb{R}}
. Thus, these infinitesimal symmetries only generate
transformations along these fibers, or, what means the same thing,
the local flux of the generators
Y
leave the fibers of the projection

\pi_{\mathbb{R}}
invariant.
Furthermore, as a consequence of the above proposition,
and taking into account that
\displaystyle R=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}
,
in this local expression for
Y

the component functions
f^{i},g_{i}
do not depend on the coordinate
t
.

Proposition 4.45

1.

If
\Phi\in{\rm Diff}\,(\mathbb{R}\times M)
is a cosymplectic Noether symmetry, then it is a
dynamical symmetry.

2.

If
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}\times M)
is an infinitesimal cosymplectic Noether symmetry, then it is
an infinitesimal dynamical symmetry.

(
Proof
)

1.

As
\Phi
is a cosymplectic Noether symmetry, then

\Phi^{*}\omega=\omega
,
\Phi^{*}\eta=\eta
,
\Phi^{*}h=h
,
and
\Phi_{*}R=R
; therefore, from

0=\mathop{i}\nolimits({\cal E}_{h})\omega-{\rm d}h+(R(h))\eta
we obtain that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Phi^{*}[\mathop{i}\nolimits({\cal E}_{h})\omega-{\rm d}h+(R(h))\eta]=\mathop{i}\nolimits(\Phi_{*}^{-1}{\cal E}_{h})(\Phi^{*}\omega)-\Phi^{*}{\rm d}h+((\Phi_{*}^{-1}R)(\Phi^{*}h))(\Phi^{*}\eta)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(\Phi_{*}^{-1}{\cal E}_{h})\omega-{\rm d}h+(R(h))\eta\ ,

and from
0=\mathop{i}\nolimits({\cal E}_{h})\eta-1
we get

0=\Phi^{*}(\mathop{i}\nolimits({\cal E}_{h})\eta-1)=\mathop{i}\nolimits(\Phi_{*}^{-1}{\cal E}_{h})(\Phi^{*}\eta)-1=\mathop{i}\nolimits(\Phi_{*}^{-1}{\cal E}_{h})\eta-1\ ;

but as the system is regular, the evolution vector field solution is unique,
then
\Phi_{*}^{-1}{\cal E}_{h}={\cal E}_{h}
, and hence the result holds.

2.

The result for infinitesimal cosymplectic Noether symmetries follows
from the above item using the local uniparametric group of diffeomorphisms
associated to their fluxes
(or also, using the properties (
4.1
) that characterize the
Reeb vector field and reasoning as in the first item).

In addition, it is immediate to prove that, if
Y_{1},Y_{2}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}\times M)

are infinitesimal cosymplectic Noether symmetries, then so is
[Y_{1},Y_{2}]
.

As in the above chapters,
in order to establish Noether’s theorem for non-autonomous systems,
we will stick to the case of infinitesimal Noether symmetries:

Proposition 4.46

Let
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}\times M)
be an
infinitesimal cosymplectic Noether symmetry.
Then, for every
p\in\mathbb{R}\times M
, there is an open
neighbourhood
U\ni p
, such that:

1.

There exist
{\cal F}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(U)
,
which is unique up to a constant function, such that

\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\omega={\rm d}{\cal F}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{\rm(on $U$)}\;.

(4.21)

2.

There exist
\zeta\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(U)
, verifying that

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\theta={\rm d}\zeta
, on
U
; and then

{\cal F}=\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\theta-\zeta\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{\rm(up to a
constant function on $U$)}\

Then, for exact infinitesimal cosymplectic Noether
symmetries we have that

{\cal F}=\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\theta
(up to a constant function).

(
Proof
)
Recalling the conditions set out in Definition
4.42
:

1.

It is a consequence of the Poincaré Lemma and the condition

0=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\omega=\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y){\rm d}\omega+{\rm d}\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\omega={\rm d}\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\omega\ .

2.

We have that

{\rm d}\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\theta=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y){\rm d}\theta=-\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\omega=0

and hence
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\theta
are closed forms. Therefore, by the
Poincaré Lemma, there exists
\zeta\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(U)
, verifying
that
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\theta={\rm d}\zeta
, on
U
. Furthermore, as
(
4.21
) holds on
U
, we obtain that

{\rm d}\zeta=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\theta={\rm d}\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\theta+\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y){\rm d}\theta={\rm d}\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\theta-\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\omega={\rm d}\{\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\theta-{\cal F}\}

and the result holds.

Remark 4.47

Observe that, using Darboux coordinates on
\mathbb{R}\times M
,
the item 2 of Proposition
4.46
tells us that the conserved quantities
associated with infinitesimal cosymplectic Noether symmetries
do not depend on the coordinate
t

(since the generators of these symmetries,
the vector fields
Y
, neither depend on them).

Finally, the classical Noether’s Theorem can be stated
for these kinds of symmetries as follows:

Theorem 4.48

(Noether’s Theorem):

If
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}\times M)
is an infinitesimal cosymplectic Noether symmetry then,
for every
p\in\mathbb{R}\times M
, there is an open neighbourhood

U\ni p
such that the function
{\cal F}=\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\theta-\zeta

is a conserved quantity.

(
Proof
)
From (
4.21
) one obtains

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\cal E}_{h}){\cal F}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits({\cal E}_{h}){\rm d}{\cal F}=\mathop{i}\nolimits({\cal E}_{h})\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\omega=-\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\mathop{i}\nolimits({\cal E}_{h})\omega

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y){\rm d}h+\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)((R(h))\eta)=-\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)h+(R(h))\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\eta=0\ ,

because
Y
is an infinitesimal cosymplectic Noether symmetry.

As a final particular situation, consider now the case of the canonical model
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q
.
If
\varphi\colon Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
is a diffeomorphism,
we construct the diffeomorphism

\Phi:=({\rm{\rm Id}_{\mathbb{R}}},{\rm T}^{*}\varphi)\colon\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q
,
where
{\rm T}^{*}\varphi\colon{\rm T}^{*}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}^{*}Q

is the canonical lift of
\varphi
to
{\rm T}^{*}Q
.
Then
\Phi
is said to be the
canonical lift
of
\varphi
to
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q
.
Any transformation
\Phi
of this kind is called a
natural transformation
of
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q
.

In the same way, given a vector field
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)

we can define its
complete lift

to
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q
as the vector field

Y\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q)

whose local flux is the canonical lift of
the local flux of
Z
to
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q
;
that is,
Y=Z^{*}
,
where
Z^{*}
denotes the complete lift of
Z
to
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q
.
Any vector field
Y
of this kind is called a
natural infinitesimal transformation
of
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q
.

Then we define:

Definition 4.49

Let
(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q,\eta,\omega;h)
be a regular nonautonomous Hamiltonian system.

A symmetry (resp. cosymplectic Noether symmetry)
\Phi\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q)

is said to be
natural
if
\Phi
is a natural transformation of
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q
.

An infinitesimal symmetry (resp. infinitesimal cosymplectic Noether symmetry)
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q)

is said to be
natural
if
Y
is a natural infinitesimal transformation of
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q
.

Remark 4.50

The study of symmetries and conserved quantities in the Lagrangian formalism of nonautonomous dynamical systems
follows the same patterns as in the canonical Hamiltonian formalism, considering the system

(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q,\eta_{\cal L},\omega_{\cal L},E_{\cal L})

and and bearing in mind the development made in Section
3.5.2

(see also
[
102
,
314
]
).

4.6
Other geometrical settings for nonautonomous dynamical systems

As we have said in the beginning of this chapter, there are other ways to describe geometrically
time-dependent dynamical systems.
Next we briefly review two of the best known of them.

4.6.1
Contact formulation

(See
[
1
,
90
,
109
,
154
,
155
,
314
,
316
]
for more details).

The manifold where this formalism is developed is the same as in the cosymplectic case;
that is
\mathbb{R}\times M
, where
(M,\Omega)
is a symplectic manifold.
In particular, it is usual to consider the canonical case, where
M={\rm T}^{*}Q
(Hamiltonian formalism),
or
M={\rm T}Q
(Lagrangian formalism),
and
\mathbb{R}\times Q
is the configuration space of the system.

Thus, in the Lagrangian description of this contact formulation,
