the dynamics takes place on the manifold
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
.
Then, given an admissible time-dependent Lagrangian function

{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)

(then
{\rm rank}\,\omega_{\cal L}=2n
),
we define the so-called
Poincaré-Cartan’s forms
associated with
{\cal L}
as

\mbox{\boldmath$\Theta$}_{\cal L}:=\theta_{\cal L}-E_{\cal L}\,{\rm d}t\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{\boldmath$\Omega$}_{\cal L}:=-{\rm d}\Theta_{\cal L}=\omega_{\cal L}+{\rm d}E_{\cal L}\wedge{\rm d}t\ ;

The expression in coordinates of
\mbox{\boldmath$\Theta$}_{\cal L}
is

\mbox{\boldmath$\Theta$}_{\cal L}=\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}{\rm d}q^{i}-\Big(v^{i}\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}-{\cal L}\Big)\,{\rm d}t\ .

If
{\cal L}
is a regular Lagrangian, then the pair
(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q,\mbox{\boldmath$\Omega$}_{\cal L})

is said to be a
regular time-dependent Lagrangian system
.
The dynamical Lagrangian equations for a vector field

X_{\cal L}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)
are

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\cal L})\mbox{\boldmath$\Omega$}_{\cal L}=0\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\cal L}){\rm d}t=1\ ,

(4.22)

and the equations for the integral curves
{\bf c}\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
of
X_{\cal L}
are

\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{\bf c})(\mbox{\boldmath$\Omega$}_{\cal L}\circ{\bf c})=0\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{\bf c})({\rm d}t\circ{\bf c})=1\ .

These equations are compatible and determinate and,
in a local chart of coordinates on
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
, its unique solution is

X_{\cal L}=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}+v^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}+\Bigl(\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial{v^{i}}\partial{v^{j}}}\Bigr)^{-1}\Big(\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{j}}}-v^{k}\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{k}\partial v^{j}}\Big)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\ .

Notice that
X_{\cal L}
is a
sode
and hence
its integral curves are solutions to the Euler–Lagrange equations.
Moreover, we obtain the additional information

\frac{dE_{\cal L}}{dt}\circ{\bf c}=\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{t}}\circ{\bf c}\ ,

on every integral curve of
X_{\cal L}
,
{\bf c}\colon I\subseteq\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
.
This gives the non-conservation law of the energy.

In the Hamiltonian formalism, as in the cosymplectic formulation,
the dynamical information is given by the

time-dependent Hamiltonian function

{\rm h}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q)
,
which may be defined only locally.
Then, if
\omega=\pi_{2}^{*}\Omega
, where
\Omega
is the canonical
2
-form on
{\rm T}^{*}Q
,
we define the form (perhaps only locally if
{\rm h}

is a locally Hamiltonian function)

\mbox{\boldmath$\Omega$}_{\rm h}:=\omega+{\rm d}{\rm h}\wedge{\rm d}t\in Z^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q)\ ,

which is called the
Hamilton–Cartan
2
-form
associated with
{\rm h}
.
Observe that

\mbox{\boldmath$\Omega$}_{\rm h}=-{\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\Theta$}_{\rm h}
, with

\mbox{\boldmath$\Theta$}_{\rm h}=\theta-{\rm h}\,{\rm d}t
,
and
\theta=\pi_{2}^{*}\Theta
, where
\Theta
is the Liouville’s
1
-form on
{\rm T}^{*}Q
. Obviously
\omega=-{\rm d}\theta
.
Then, the pair
(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q,\mbox{\boldmath$\Omega$}_{\rm h})
is said to be a
time-dependent regular (locally) Hamiltonian system
.
Its dynamical equations for a vector field

X_{\rm h}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q)
are

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\rm h)}\mbox{\boldmath$\Omega$}_{\rm h}=0\hskip 10.00002pt;\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\rm h}){\rm d}t=1\ ,

and for the integral curves
{\bf c}\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q
of
X_{\rm h}
, the equations are

\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{\bf c})(\mbox{\boldmath$\Omega$}_{\rm h}\circ{\bf c})=0\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{\bf c})({\rm d}t\circ{\bf c}))=1\ .

This system of equations is compatible and determinate.
In fact, in a chart of canonical coordinates on
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q
,
the unique solution is the vector field

X_{\rm h}=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}+\frac{\partial{{\rm h}}}{\partial{p_{i}}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}-\frac{\partial{{\rm h}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{i}}}\ .

and, if
{\bf c}(s)=(t(s),q^{i}(s),p_{i}(s))
is an integral curve of
X_{\rm h}
,
then the second equation implies that
t=s+ctn.
and
c(t)

is a solution to the Hamilton equations

\frac{dq^{i}}{dt}=\frac{\partial{\rm h}}{\partial p_{i}}\circ{\bf c}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dp_{i}}{dt}=-\frac{\partial{\rm h}}{\partial q^{i}}\circ{\bf c}\ .

The connection between the Lagrangian and the
Hamiltonian contact formalisms for nonautonomous systems is performed by the Legendre map

{\rm F}{\cal L}\colon\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q

defined in Definition
4.22
.
Then, as
{\rm F}{\cal L}
is a (local) diffeomorphism,
then all the geometrical objects in

\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
are
{\rm F}{\cal L}
-projectable;
in particular we have that

{\rm F}{\cal L}^{*}{\rm h}=E_{\cal L}\hskip 10.00002pt;\hskip 10.00002pt{\rm F}{\cal L}^{*}\mbox{\boldmath$\Omega$}_{\rm h}=\mbox{\boldmath$\Omega$}_{\cal L}\ ,

and therefore, for the solutions

{\rm F}{\cal L}_{*}X_{\cal L}=X_{\rm h}\ .

Finally, the equivalence between the cosymplectic and the contact (Lagrangian) formulations is stated in the following:

Proposition 4.51

Let
(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q,{\cal L})
a time-dependent Lagrangian system.
Then equations (
4.22
) and (
4.11
) are equivalents.

(
Proof
)
From (
4.22
) we have

0=\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\cal L})\mbox{\boldmath$\Omega$}_{\cal L}=\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\cal L})\omega_{\cal L}+\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\cal L}){\rm d}E_{\cal L}\,{\rm d}t-{\rm d}E_{\cal L}\,\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\cal L}){\rm d}t=\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\cal L})\omega_{\cal L}+\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\cal L}){\rm d}E_{\cal L}\,{\rm d}t-{\rm d}E_{\cal L}\ .

Now, taking the Reeb vector field
R_{\cal L}
, we obtain that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(R_{\cal L})\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\cal L})\omega_{\cal L}+\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\cal L}){\rm d}E_{\cal L}\,\mathop{i}\nolimits(R_{\cal L}){\rm d}t-\mathop{i}\nolimits(R_{\cal L}){\rm d}E_{\cal L}=\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\cal L}){\rm d}E_{\cal L}-\mathop{i}\nolimits(R_{\cal L}){\rm d}E_{\cal L}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\ \mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\cal L}){\rm d}E_{\cal L}=\mathop{i}\nolimits(R_{\cal L}){\rm d}E_{\cal L}\ ,

and coming to the first equation we obtain that

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\cal L})\omega_{\cal L}={\rm d}E_{\cal L}-\mathop{i}\nolimits(R_{\cal L}){\rm d}E_{\cal L}\,{\rm d}t\ ,

which together with
\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\cal L}){\rm d}t=1
are equations (
4.11
).

Thus, the Lagrangian contact and cosymplectic formulations are equivalent.
The equivalence between the corresponding Hamiltonian equations is proved in the same way
and lead to the same Hamilton equations.

Remark 4.52

(
Cartan’s Theorem
):
It can be proved (see
[
1
]
) that, if
(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q,\mbox{\boldmath$\Omega$}_{\rm h})

is a regular time-dependent Hamiltonian system, then

\mbox{\boldmath$\Theta$}_{\rm h}\wedge\mbox{\boldmath$\Omega$}_{\rm h}^{\ n}
is a volume form on
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q

if, and only if, the condition
X_{\rm h}({\rm h})-{\rm h}\not=0
holds everywhere.
Then,
\mbox{\boldmath$\Theta$}_{\rm h}
is said to be a
contact form
and the pair
(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q,\mbox{\boldmath$\Theta$}_{\rm h})
is called a
contact manifold
.
(See Definition
6.1
).

In the same way, if
(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q,\mbox{\boldmath$\Omega$}_{\cal L})
is a regular time-dependent Lagrangian system,
then
\mbox{\boldmath$\Theta$}_{\cal L}\wedge\mbox{\boldmath$\Omega$}_{\cal L}^{\ n}
is a volume form on
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q

if the condition
X_{\cal L}(E_{\cal L})-E_{\cal L}\equiv-{\cal L}\not=0
everywhere.
Then
\mbox{\boldmath$\Theta$}_{\cal L}
is a contact form, and
(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q,\mbox{\boldmath$\Theta$}_{\cal L})
is a contact manifold.

All this justifies the name of
contact formulation
.

4.6.2
Extended symplectic formulation

(See
[
13
,
154
,
233
,
301
,
302
]
for details).

In this formulation, the Lagrangian formalism is done
starting from the extended configuration space
\mathbb{R}\times Q

and taking its tangent bundle

{\rm T}(\mathbb{R}\times Q)\cong{\rm T}\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q\cong\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q

which is called the
extended velocity phase space
and has the canonical projections

\nu\colon{\rm T}(\mathbb{R}\times Q)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q\hskip 10.00002pt;\hskip 10.00002pt\varpi\colon{\rm T}(\mathbb{R}\times Q)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\ .

Then, we have the following extensions of the
geometrical objects which are defined in
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
,

\displaystyle E_{{\cal L}_{ext}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\nu^{*}E_{\cal L}+\varpi

\displaystyle{\mathbf{\Theta}}_{{\cal L}_{ext}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\nu^{*}\Theta_{\cal L}+E_{{\cal L}_{ext}}\wedge{\rm d}t

\displaystyle{\mathbf{\Omega}}_{{\cal L}_{ext}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle-{\rm d}{\mathbf{\Theta}}_{{\cal L}_{ext}}=\nu^{*}\Omega_{\cal L}-{\rm d}E_{{\cal L}_{ext}}\wedge{\rm d}t\ .

Another way to obtain these geometric elements is constructing
the
extended Lagrangian

{\cal L}_{ext}:=\nu^{*}{\cal L}+\frac{1}{2}\varpi^{2}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}(\mathbb{R}\times Q))

(4.23)

and using the canonical structures (the vertical endomorphism and the Liouville vector field)
of the tangent bundle
{\rm T}(\mathbb{R}\times Q)
to construct them.
It is obvious that the Lagrangian
{\cal L}
is regular if, and only if,

\Omega_{{\cal L}_{ext}}
is a symplectic form and then

({\rm T}(\mathbb{R}\times Q),\Omega_{{\cal L}_{ext}},E_{{\cal L}_{ext}})

is a regular Hamiltonian system. This means that there exists a unique vector field

X_{{\cal L}_{ext}}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}(\mathbb{R}\times Q))

which is the solution to the Lagrangian equation of motion in this formalism, which is

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{{\cal L}_{ext}}){\mathbf{\Omega}}_{{\cal L}_{ext}}={\rm d}E_{{\cal L}_{ext}}\ ,

and whose integral curves
{\bf c}\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}(\mathbb{R}\times Q)
are the solutions to the equation

\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{\bf c})({\mathbf{\Omega}}_{{\cal L}_{ext}}\circ{\bf c})={\rm d}E_{{\cal L}_{ext}}\circ{\bf c}\ .

In coordinates, this solution is

X_{{\cal L}_{ext}}=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}-\frac{\partial{E_{{\cal L}_{ext}}}}{\partial{t}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\varpi}}+v^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}+\Bigl(\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial{v^{i}}\partial{v^{j}}}\Bigr)^{-1}\Big(\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{j}}}-v^{k}\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{k}\partial v^{j}}\Big)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\ .

Nevertheless, the real manifold of physical states is not

{\rm T}(\mathbb{R}\times Q)
but
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
.
But as
\ker\,\rho_{*}=\Big\langle\displaystyle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\varpi}}\Big\rangle
and

\displaystyle\Big[\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\varpi}},X_{{\cal L}_{ext}}\Big]=0
,
this vector field
X_{{\cal L}_{ext}}
is
\rho
-projectable and, actually, we have that

\rho_{*}X_{{\cal L}_{ext}}=X_{\cal L}\ ;

Note that
X_{\cal L}
is a
sode
on
{\rm T}(\mathbb{R}\times Q)
,
although
X_{{\cal L}_{ext}}
is not so on
{\rm T}(\mathbb{R}\times Q)
,
and the integral curves of
X_{\cal L}
are solutions to the Euler–Lagrange equations.
Thus, the Lagrangian contact (or the cosymplectic) formulation is recovered in this way.

For the Hamiltonian formalism, we take the

extended momentum phase space

{\rm T}^{*}(\mathbb{R}\times Q)\cong{\rm T}^{*}\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q\cong\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{*}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q

(or, more generally,
M\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{*}
,
where
M
is any symplectic manifold), and its canonical projections

\displaystyle pr_{1}\colon{\rm T}^{*}(\mathbb{R}\times Q)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}^{*}\mathbb{R}\cong\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{*}

;

\displaystyle\mu\colon{\rm T}^{*}(\mathbb{R}\times Q)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q

\displaystyle pr_{2}\colon{\rm T}^{*}(\mathbb{R}\times Q)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}^{*}Q

;

\displaystyle u\colon{\rm T}^{*}(\mathbb{R}\times Q)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}

If
\Omega\in{\Omega}^{2}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
and
\Omega_{\mathbb{R}}\in{\Omega}^{2}({\rm T}^{*}\mathbb{R})

are the canonical symplectic forms of
{\rm T}^{*}Q
and
{\rm T}^{*}\mathbb{R}
, respectively,
then
{\rm T}^{*}(\mathbb{R}\times Q)

can be endowed with the following symplectic structure

{\mathbf{\Omega}}_{ext}:=pr_{2}^{*}\Omega+pr_{1}^{*}\Omega_{\mathbb{R}}\ .

The dynamical information is still given by the time-dependent Hamiltonian function

{\rm h}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}^{*}(\mathbb{R}\times Q))
.
Then, we can introduce the
extended time-dependent Hamiltonian function

{\rm h}_{ext}:=\mu^{*}{\rm h}+u\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}^{*}(\mathbb{R}\times Q))

and it is immediate to prove that

{\mathbf{\Omega}}_{ext}=\mu^{*}\omega-{\rm d}{\rm h}_{ext}\wedge{\rm d}t\ .

Thus we have the symplectic (locally) Hamiltonian system

({\rm T}^{*}(\mathbb{R}\times Q),\Omega_{ext},{\rm h}_{ext})
.
The Hamiltonian dynamical equation for
X_{ext}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}^{*}(\mathbb{R}\times Q))
is

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{ext}){\mathbf{\Omega}}_{ext}={\rm d}{\rm h}_{ext}\ ,

and for its integral curves
{\bf c}\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}^{*}(\mathbb{R}\times Q)

\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{\bf c})({\mathbf{\Omega}}_{ext}\circ{\bf c})={\rm d}{\rm h}_{ext}\circ{\bf c}\ .

The unique solution to the above equation, in a chart of natural coordinates

(t,u,q^{i},p_{i})
of
{\rm T}^{*}(\mathbb{R}\times Q)
, is

\displaystyle X_{ext}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}-\frac{\partial{{\rm h}_{ext}}}{\partial{t}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{u}}+\frac{\partial{{\rm h}_{ext}}}{\partial{p_{i}}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}-\frac{\partial{{\rm h}_{ext}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{i}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}-\frac{\partial{(\mu^{*}{\rm h})}}{\partial{t}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{u}}+\frac{\partial{(\mu^{*}{\rm h})}}{\partial{p_{i}}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}-\frac{\partial{(\mu^{*}{\rm h})}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{i}}}\ .

Then the integral curves of this vector field are solutions to the Hamilton equations,

\frac{dq^{i}}{dt}=\frac{\partial{\mu^{*}{\rm h}}}{\partial{p_{i}}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dp_{i}}{dt}=-\frac{\partial{\mu^{*}{\rm h}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{du}{dt}=-\frac{\partial{\mu^{*}{\rm h}}}{\partial{t}}\ ,

where the last equation shows that the energy is not conserved.

As the physical phase space is
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q

and not
{\rm T}^{*}(\mathbb{R}\times Q))
,
the real dynamical vector field cannot be
X_{ext}
really,
but another one on
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q
which must be related to it.
But, as in the Lagrangian formalism, the vector field
X_{ext}
is
\mu
-projectable
and, in fact, we have that

\mu_{*}X_{ext}=X_{\rm h}\ .

In this way, the equivalence with the Hamiltonian contact
(or the cosymplectic) formulation is proved.

In order to establish the equivalence between the Lagrangian and Hamiltonian formalisms
in this extended symplectic picture, we need to connect the
extended velocity and momentum phase spaces through a suitable “Legendre map”;
that is, the fiber derivative of some Lagrangian function on
{\rm T}(\mathbb{R}\times Q)
.
This function cannot be
\rho^{*}{\cal L}
because its fiber derivative

{\rm F}(\rho^{*}{\cal L})
is not a (local) diffeomorphism since

\rho^{*}{\cal L}
is not a regular Lagrangian on
{\rm T}(\mathbb{R}\times Q)
.
The correct choice consists in taking the extended Lagrangian (
4.23
)
and its fiber derivative

{\rm F}{\cal L}_{ext}:{\rm T}(\mathbb{R}\times Q)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}^{*}(\mathbb{R}\times Q)\ ,

which in coordinates is

\displaystyle{\cal F}{\cal L}_{ext}^{\hskip 8.19447pt*}\,q^{i}=q^{i}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt{\cal F}{\cal L}_{ext}^{\hskip 8.19447pt*}\,p_{i}=\frac{\partial{\rho^{*}{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt{\cal F}{\cal L}_{ext}^{\hskip 8.19447pt*}\,t=t\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt{\cal F}{\cal L}_{ext}^{\hskip 8.19447pt*}\,u=\varpi\ .

Observe that
{\rm F}{\cal L}_{ext}
is just an extension of
{\cal F}{\cal L}

such that the following diagram commutes

\displaystyle{\rm T}(\mathbb{R}\times Q)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits^{{\cal F}{\cal L}_{ext}}}

\displaystyle{\rm T}^{*}(\mathbb{R}\times Q)

\displaystyle\nu\biggr\downarrow\

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\biggr\downarrow\ \mu

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits^{{\cal F}{\cal L}}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q

and
{\cal F}{\cal L}_{ext}
is a (local) diffeomorphism if, and only if,
{\cal F}{\cal L}
is so
or, what is equivalent, if
{\cal L}
is regular.
Therefore

{\rm F}{\cal L}_{ext}^{*}{\mathbf{\Omega}}={\mathbf{\Omega}}_{{\cal L}_{ext}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt{\rm F}{\cal L}_{ext}^{*}{\rm h}_{ext}=E_{{\cal L}_{ext}}

and then

{\rm F}{\cal L}_{{ext}\ *}X_{{\cal L}_{ext}}=X_{ext}\ .

4.7
Examples

In this last section,
let us consider the examples studied at the end of the previous chapter,
in which the Lagrangian functions of the systems have been modified to include time dependence.

4.7.1
The forced harmonic oscillator with periodic perturbation

We recover the system analyzed in Section
3.7.1
,
but now the oscillator is also submitted to an external periodic force.
In the cosymplectic formalism,
the configuration bundle is
\mathbb{R}\times Q=\mathbb{R}^{2}
, with coordinates
(t,q)
.

Lagrangian formalism

The cosymplectic Lagrangian formalism takes place on
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q\simeq\mathbb{R}^{3}
, with coordinates

(t,q,v)
, and the dynamics can be described by the Lagrangian function

{\cal L}=\frac{1}{2}(mv^{2}-kq^{2})+Aq\,\cos wt\hskip 10.00002pt;\hskip 10.00002ptk,w\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\ .

Now we have

E_{\cal L}=\frac{1}{2}(mv^{2}+kq^{2})-Aq\,\cos wt\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\theta_{\cal L}=mv\,{\rm d}q\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\omega_{\cal L}=m\,{\rm d}q\wedge{\rm d}v\ ,

and
{\cal L}
is a regular Lagrangian.
The Reeb vector field for this Lagrangian is just
\displaystyle R_{\cal L}=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}
.
Then, for
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\cal L}=\lambda\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}+f\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q}}+g\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v}}
,
equations (
4.11
) give
\lambda=1
and

\mathop{i}\nolimits(\Gamma_{\cal L})\omega_{\cal L}=m\,(f\,{\rm d}v-g\,{\rm d}q)=mv\,{\rm d}v+(kq-A\,\cos wt)\,{\rm d}q={\rm d}E_{\cal L}+\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{t}}\,{\rm d}t\ ,

which leads to

f=v\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptmg=-kq+A\,\cos wt\ .

So the Euler–Lagrange vector field is

\Gamma_{\cal L}=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}+v\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q}}+\frac{-kq+A\,\cos wt}{m}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v}}\ ,

and its integral curves
(q(t),v(t))
are the solutions to

\frac{dq}{dt}=v\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptm\frac{dv}{dt}=-kq+A\,\cos wt\hskip 10.00002pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptm\frac{d^{2}q}{dt^{2}}=-kq+A\,\cos wt\ ,

which is the Euler–Lagrange equation for this time-dependent forced harmonic oscillator.

Hamiltonian formalism

For the Hamiltonian formalism,
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q\simeq\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
with coordinates
(t,q,p)
. First, the Legendre transformation is

{\cal F}{\cal L}^{*}t=t\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt{\cal F}{\cal L}^{*}q=q\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt{\cal F}{\cal L}^{*}p=mv\ ,

which is a diffeomorphism (the Lagrangian is hyperregular).
The canonical Hamiltonian function is

{\rm h}=\frac{p^{2}}{2m}+kq^{2}-Aq\,\cos wt\ .

As
\omega={\rm d}q\wedge{\rm d}p
, the Reeb vector field is
\displaystyle R=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}
and,
for
\displaystyle{\cal E}_{\rm h}=\Lambda\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}+F\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q}}+G\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p}}
,
equations (
4.13
) give
\Lambda=1
and

\mathop{i}\nolimits({\cal E}_{\rm h})\omega=F\,{\rm d}p-G\,{\rm d}q=\frac{p}{m}\,{\rm d}p+(kq-A\,\cos wt)\,{\rm d}q={\rm d}{\rm h}-R\frac{\partial{{\rm h}}}{\partial{t}}\,{\rm d}t\ ,

which leads to

F=\frac{p}{m}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptG=-kq+A\,\cos wt\ .

So the evolution vector field is

{\cal E}_{\rm h}=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}+\frac{p}{m}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q}}-(kq-A\,\cos wt)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p}}\ ,

and its integral curves
(q(t),p(t))
are the solutions to

m\frac{dq}{dt}=p\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dp}{dt}=-kq+A\,\cos wt\ ,

which are the Hamilton equations for this time-dependent forced harmonic oscillator.

Using the Legendre map, we check again that the Hamilton and
the Euler–Lagrange equations of the system are equivalent.
Obviously, we have that
{\cal F}{\cal L}_{*}\Gamma_{\cal L}={\cal E}_{\rm h}
.

Unified Lagrangian–Hamiltonian formalism

The extended unified bundle is
{\cal M}=\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q\times_{Q}{\rm T}^{*}Q\simeq\mathbb{R}^{4}

with coordinates
(t,q,v,p)
. The Reeb vector field is
\displaystyle R=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}
,
the canonical forms are

\Theta_{\cal M}=p\,{\rm d}q\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\Omega_{\cal M}=-{\rm d}\Theta_{\cal M}={\rm d}q\wedge{\rm d}p

and the Hamiltonian function is

{\rm H}=pv-\frac{1}{2}(mv^{2}-kq^{2})-Aq\,\cos wt\ .

For
\displaystyle X_{\rm H}=g\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}+f\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q}}+F\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v}}+G\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p}}
,
equations (
4.15
) give
g=1
and

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\rm H})\Omega_{\cal W}=f\,{\rm d}p-G\,{\rm d}q=(kq-A\,\cos wt)\,{\rm d}q+(p-mv)\,{\rm d}v+v\,{\rm d}p={\rm d}{\rm H}-R({\rm H}){\rm d}t\ ,

which leads to

f=v\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptG=-(kq-A\,\cos wt)\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptp=mv\ .

The last equation is the constraint defining the submanifold

{\cal M}_{0}\hookrightarrow{\cal M}
and gives the Legendre map.
The evolution vector field is

X_{\rm H}|_{{\cal M}_{0}}=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}+v\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q}}+G\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v}}-(kq-A\,\cos wt)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p}}\ ,

and the tangency condition leads to

X_{\rm H}(p-mv)=-(kq-A\,\cos wt)-Gm=0\ \Longleftrightarrow\ G=-\frac{kq-A\,\cos wt}{m}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{\rm(on ${\cal M}_{0}$)}\ ;

therefore

X_{\rm H}|_{{\cal M}_{0}}=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}+v\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q}}-\frac{kq-A\,\cos wt}{m}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v}}-(kq-A\,\cos wt)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p}}\ .

and its integral curves
(t,q(t),v(t),p(t))
are the solutions to

\frac{dq}{dt}=v\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptm\frac{dv}{dt}=-(kq-A\,\cos wt)\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dp}{dt}=-(kq-A\,\cos wt)\ .

Te first two equations are equivalent to

m\frac{d^{2}q}{dt^{2}}=-kq+A\,\cos wt\ ,

which is the Euler–Lagrange equation of the system.
Furthermore, using the constraint
p=mv
(the Legendre map),
the first and third equations are

\frac{dq}{dt}=\frac{p}{m}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dp}{dt}=-kq+A\,\cos wt\ ;

which are the Hamilton equations for the system.

4.7.2
A nonautonomous system with central forces

Consider the
Kepler problem
analyzed in Section
3.7.2

in the case where the mass of the particle subjected to the central field is not constant but a (strictly positive) function of time
m(t)
.
As in the standard case, the motion is on a plane;
hence the configuration bundle is
\mathbb{R}\times Q=\mathbb{R}^{3}

and the coordinates are
(t,r,\phi)
, where
(r,\phi)

are polar coordinates on the adequate open set
U\subset Q=\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Lagrangian formalism

The velocity phase space for the cosymplectic Lagrangian formalism is

\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q\simeq\mathbb{R}^{5}
, with local coordinates

(t,r,\phi,v_{r},v_{\phi})
and the Lagrangian function is

{\cal L}=\frac{1}{2}m(t)(v_{r}^{2}+r^{2}v_{\phi}^{2})-\frac{K}{r}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptK\not=0\ ;

therefore

\displaystyle E_{\cal L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}m(t)(v_{r}^{2}+r^{2}v_{\phi}^{2})+\frac{K}{r}\ ,

\displaystyle\theta_{\cal L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m(t)(v_{r}\,{\rm d}r+r^{2}v_{\phi}\,{\rm d}\phi)\ ,

\displaystyle\omega_{\cal L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m(t)({\rm d}r\wedge{\rm d}v_{r}+r^{2}{\rm d}\phi\wedge{\rm d}v_{\phi}-2rv_{\phi}\,{\rm d}r\wedge{\rm d}\phi)-\dot{m}(t)(v_{r}\,{\rm d}t\wedge{\rm d}r+r^{2}v_{\phi}\,{\rm d}t\wedge{\rm d}\phi)\ ,

where
\displaystyle\dot{m}(t)=\frac{dm(t)}{dt}
.
The Lagrangian is regular and the Reeb vector field for this system is

R_{\cal L}=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}+\frac{\dot{m}(t)}{m(t)}\Big(v_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{r}}}+v_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{\phi}}}\Big)\ .

If
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\cal L}=\lambda\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}+f_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{r}}+f_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}+g_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{r}}}+g_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{\phi}}}
,
equations (
4.11
) give
\lambda=1
and

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(\Gamma_{\cal L})\omega_{\cal L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dot{m}(t)\,[-v_{r}\,{\rm d}r-r^{2}v_{\phi}\,{\rm d}\phi+(v_{r}f_{r}+r^{2}v_{\phi}f_{\phi})\,{\rm d}t]+

\displaystyle m(t)\,[f_{r}\,{\rm d}v_{r}+f_{\phi}r^{2}\,{\rm d}v_{\phi}-(g_{r}-2rv_{\phi}f_{\phi})\,{\rm d}r-(g_{\phi}r^{2}+2rv_{\phi}f_{r})\,{\rm d}\phi]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dot{m}(t)\,(v_{r}^{2}+r^{2}v_{\phi}^{2})\,{\rm d}t+m(t)\,v_{r}\,{\rm d}v_{r}+m(t)\,r^{2}v_{\phi}\,{\rm d}v_{\phi}+\Big(m(t)\,rv_{\phi}^{2}-\frac{K}{r^{2}}\Big){\rm d}r

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm d}E_{\cal L}+\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{t}}\,{\rm d}t\ ,

which leads to

\displaystyle f_{r}=v_{r}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptf_{\phi}=v_{\phi}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptv_{r}f_{r}+r^{2}v_{\phi}f_{\phi}=v_{r}^{2}+r^{2}v_{\phi}^{2}\ ,

\displaystyle m(t)\,g_{r}=m(t)\,(2rv_{\phi}f_{\phi}-rv_{\phi}^{2})-\dot{m}(t)\,v_{r}+\frac{K}{r^{2}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptg_{\phi}=-\frac{2v_{\phi}f_{r}}{r}-\frac{\dot{m}(t)}{m(t)}v_{\phi}\ ,

and then the Euler–Lagrange vector field is

\Gamma_{\cal L}=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}+v_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{r}}+v_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}+\Big(rv_{\phi}^{2}-\frac{\dot{m}(t)}{m(t)}v_{r}+\displaystyle\frac{K}{m(t)\,r^{2}}\Big)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{r}}}-\Big(\frac{\dot{m}(t)}{m(t)}v_{\phi}+\frac{2v_{\phi}v_{r}}{r}\Big)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{\phi}}}\ .

Then, its integral curves
(r(t),\phi(t),v_{r}(t),v_{\phi}(t))
are the solutions to

\displaystyle\frac{dr}{dt}=v_{r}\ ,\ \frac{d\phi}{dt}=v_{\phi}\ ,\ m(t)\,\frac{dv_{r}}{dt}=m(t)\,rv_{\phi}^{2}-\dot{m}(t)\,v_{r}+\frac{K}{r^{2}}\ ,\ \frac{dv_{\phi}}{dt}=\frac{\dot{m}(t)}{m(t)}v_{\phi}-\frac{2}{r}v_{\phi}v_{r}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptm(t)\,\frac{d^{2}r}{dt^{2}}=m(t)\,r\Big(\frac{d\phi}{dt}\Big)^{2}-\dot{m}(t)\,\frac{dr}{dt}+\frac{K}{r^{2}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{d^{2}\phi}{dt^{2}}=-\frac{\dot{m}(t)}{m(t)}\,\frac{d\phi}{dt}-\frac{2}{r}\,\frac{d\phi}{dt}\,\frac{dr}{dt}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{d}{dt}\Big(m(t)\,\frac{dr}{dt}\Big)=m(t)\,r\Big(\frac{d\phi}{dt}\Big)^{2}+\frac{K}{r^{2}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{d}{dt}\Big(m(t)r^{2}\frac{d\phi}{dt}\Big)=0\ ,

which are the Euler–Lagrange equations for this system.

There is an infinitesimal Lagrangian exact Noether symmetry
which is the vector field
\displaystyle Y=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}
, because

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y){\rm d}t=0
trivially,
and a calculation similar to the one done in the example of Section
3.7.2
leads to
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\theta_{\cal L}=0

and
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)E_{\cal L}=0
.
Therefore, the associated conserved quantity is

f_{Y}=\mathop{i}\nolimits\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}\right)\theta_{\cal L}=m(t)r^{2}v_{\phi}\ ;

that is, the angular momentum, as the last Euler–Lagrange equation shows.

Hamiltonian formalism

The cosymplectic Hamiltonian formalism
takes place on
\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}^{*}Q\simeq\mathbb{R}^{5}
,
with local coordinates
(t,r,\phi,p_{r},p_{\phi})
. First, the Legendre transformation is

{\cal F}{\cal L}^{*}t=t\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt{\cal F}{\cal L}^{*}r=r\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt{\cal F}{\cal L}^{*}\phi=\phi\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt{\cal F}{\cal L}^{*}p_{r}=m(t)\,v_{r}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt{\cal F}{\cal L}^{*}p_{\phi}=m(t)\,r^{2}v_{\phi}\ ,

which is a diffeomorphism because the Lagrangian is hyperregular.
The canonical time-dependent Hamiltonian function is

{\rm h}=\frac{p_{r}^{2}}{2m(t)}+\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{2m(t)\,r^{2}}+\frac{K}{r}\ .

As
\omega={\rm d}r\wedge{\rm d}p_{r}+{\rm d}\phi\wedge{\rm d}p_{\phi}
,
the Reeb vector field is
\displaystyle R=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}
.
Then, the evolution vector field obtained from equations (
4.13
) is

{\cal E}_{\rm h}=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}+\frac{p_{r}}{m(t)}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{r}}+\frac{p_{\phi}}{m(t)\,r^{2}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}+\Big(\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{m(t)\,r^{3}}+\frac{K}{r^{2}}\Big)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{r}}}\ ,

and its integral curves
(r(t),\phi(t),p_{r}(t),p_{\phi}(t))
are the solutions to

m(t)\,\frac{dr}{dt}=p_{r}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptm(t)\,r^{2}\frac{d\phi}{dt}=p_{\phi}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dp_{r}}{dt}=\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{m(t)\,r^{3}}+\frac{K}{r^{2}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dp_{\phi}}{dt}=0\ ,

which are the Hamilton equations for this system.

Although the Hamilton equations have the same aspect as in the
autonomous case (see Section
3.7.2
), one can observe that,
as
m=m(t)
, combining these equations and
using the Legendre map, we obtain, in fact,
the Euler–Lagrange equations for this nonautonomous system,
and
{\cal F}{\cal L}_{*}\Gamma_{\cal L}={\cal E}_{\rm h}
.

Once again, a Hamiltonian exact Noether symmetry is given by
the vector field
\displaystyle Y=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}
, since

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y){\rm d}t=0
,
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\theta=0
,
and
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y){\rm h}=0
.
Then, as the last Hamilton equation shows,
the associated conserved quantity is again the angular momentum

\displaystyle f_{Y}=\mathop{i}\nolimits\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}\right)\theta=p_{\phi}
.

Unified Lagrangian–Hamiltonian formalism

The extended unified bundle is
{\cal M}=\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q\times_{Q}{\rm T}^{*}Q\simeq\mathbb{R}^{7}
,
with natural coordinates
(t,r,\phi,v_{r},v_{\phi},p_{r},p_{\phi})
.
The Reeb vector field
\displaystyle R=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}
,
the canonical forms are

\Theta_{\cal M}=p_{r}\,{\rm d}r+p_{\phi}\,{\rm d}\phi\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\Omega_{\cal M}=-{\rm d}\Theta_{\cal M}={\rm d}r\wedge{\rm d}p_{r}+{\rm d}\phi\wedge{\rm d}p_{\phi}\ ,

and the Hamiltonian function is

{\rm H}=p_{r}v_{r}+p_{\phi}v_{\phi}-\frac{1}{2}m(t)(v_{r}^{2}+r^{2}v_{\phi}^{2})+\frac{K}{r}\ .

For
\displaystyle X_{\rm H}=f\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}+f_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{r}}+f_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}+F_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{r}}}+F_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{\phi}}}+G_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{r}}}+G_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{\phi}}}
,
equation (
4.15
) gives
g=1
and

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\rm H})\Omega_{\cal M}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{r}\,{\rm d}p_{r}+f_{\phi}\,{\rm d}p_{\phi}-G_{r}\,{\rm d}r-G_{\phi}\,{\rm d}\phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Big(\frac{K}{r^{2}}+m(t)\,rv_{\phi}^{2}\Big){\rm d}r+(p_{r}-m(t)\,v_{r})\,{\rm d}v_{r}

\displaystyle+(p_{\phi}-m(t)\,r^{2}v_{\phi})\,{\rm d}v_{\phi}+v_{r}\,{\rm d}p_{r}+v_{\phi}\,{\rm d}p_{\phi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm d}{\rm H}-R({\rm H}){\rm d}t\ ,

which leads to

f_{r}=v_{r}\ ,\ f_{\phi}=v_{\phi}\ ,\ G_{r}=\frac{K}{r^{2}}+m(t)\,rv_{\phi}^{2}\ ,\ G_{\phi}=0\ ,\ p_{r}=m(t)\,v_{r}\ ,\ p_{\phi}=m(t)\,r^{2}v_{\phi}\ .

The last two equations are constraints defining the submanifold

{\cal M}_{0}\hookrightarrow{\cal M}
which give the Legendre map.
The evolution vector field is

X_{\rm H}|_{{\cal M}_{0}}=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}+v_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{r}}+v_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}+F_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{r}}}+F_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{\phi}}}+\Big(\frac{K}{r^{2}}+m(t)\,rv_{\phi}^{2}\Big)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{r}}}\ ,

and the tangency condition leads to

\displaystyle X_{\rm H}(p_{r}-m(t)v_{r})=\frac{K}{r^{2}}+m(t)\,rv_{\phi}^{2}-F_{r}m(t)-\dot{m}(t)\,v_{r}=0\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{\rm(on ${\cal M}_{0}$)}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow F_{r}=\frac{K}{m(t)\,r^{2}}+rv_{\phi}^{2}-\frac{\dot{m}(t)}{m(t)}v_{r}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{\rm(on ${\cal M}_{0}$)}\ ,

\displaystyle X_{\rm H}(p_{\phi}-m(t)\,r^{2}v_{\phi})=-m(t)\,F_{\phi}r^{2}-m(t)\,2f_{r}rv_{\phi}-\dot{m}(t)\,r^{2}v_{\phi}=0\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{\rm(on ${\cal M}_{0}$)}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow F_{\phi}=-\frac{2v_{r}v_{\phi}}{r}-\frac{\dot{m}(t)}{m(t)}r^{2}v_{\phi}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{\rm(on ${\cal M}_{0}$)}\ ;

therefore

\displaystyle X_{\rm H}|_{{\cal M}_{0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}+v_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{r}}+v_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}+\Big(rv_{\phi}^{2}+\displaystyle\frac{K}{m(t)\,r^{2}}-\frac{\dot{m}(t)}{m(t)}v_{r}\Big)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{r}}}

\displaystyle-\Big(\frac{2v_{r}v_{\phi}}{r}+\frac{\dot{m}(t)}{m(t)}r^{2}v_{\phi}\Big)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{\phi}}}+\left(\frac{K}{r^{2}}+m(t)\,rv_{\phi}^{2}\right)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{r}}}\ ,

and its integral curves
(t,r(t),\phi(t),v_{r}(t),v_{\phi}(t),p_{r}(t),p_{\phi}(t))
are the solutions to

\displaystyle\frac{dr}{dt}=v_{r}\

,

\displaystyle\ \frac{d\phi}{dt}=v_{\phi}\ ,

\displaystyle\frac{dv_{r}}{dt}=\frac{K}{m(t)\,r^{2}}+rv_{\phi}^{2}-\frac{\dot{m}(t)}{m(t)}v_{r}\

,

\displaystyle\ \frac{dv_{\phi}}{dt}=-\frac{2v_{r}v_{\phi}}{r}-\frac{\dot{m}(t)}{m(t)}r^{2}v_{\phi}\ ,

\displaystyle\frac{dp_{r}}{dt}=\frac{K}{r^{2}}+m(t)\,rv_{\phi}^{2}\

,

\displaystyle\ \frac{dp_{\phi}}{dt}=0\ .

Te first four equations are equivalent to

m(t)\frac{d^{2}r}{dt^{2}}=m(t)\,r\Big(\frac{d\phi}{dt}\Big)^{2}+\frac{K}{r^{2}}-\dot{m}(t)\frac{dr}{dt}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{d^{2}\phi}{dt^{2}}=-\frac{2}{r}\,\frac{d\phi}{dt}\,\frac{dr}{dt}-\frac{\dot{m}(t)}{m(t)}r^{2}\frac{d\phi}{dt}\ ,

which are the Euler–Lagrange equation of the system.
Furthermore, using the constraints
p_{r}=m(t)\,v_{r}

and
p_{\phi}=m(t)\,r^{2}v_{\phi}
(tat is, the Legendre map),
the first, second, fifth, and sixth equations are

\frac{dr}{dt}=\frac{p_{r}}{m(t)}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{d\phi}{dt}=\frac{p_{\phi}}{m(t)\,r^{2}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dp_{r}}{dt}=\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{m(t)\,r^{3}}+\frac{K}{r^{2}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dp_{\phi}}{dt}=0\ ;

which are the Hamilton equations for the system.

Chapter 5
Riemannian mechanics: Newtonian dynamical systems

In this chapter, we analyze the geometry of Newtonian dynamical systems,
which are classical mechanical systems on a (semi)Riemannian manifold:
they are also called
purely mechanical systems
.
This is the geometrization of the classical
Newtonian Mechanics
or
(semi)Riemannian mechanics
.
As we will see, a special type of these systems, the conservative ones,
are just a particular kind of the Lagrangian systems studied in the above chapter.
Some basic references are
[
1
,
11
,
46
,
75
,
91
,
179
,
291
]
.

As in the above chapters,
we start reviewing the basic notions and properties on connections in manifolds and Riemannian geometry,
that are needed for the development of this chapter.
Next we introduce the general features about Newtonian dynamical systems and their properties:
we state geometrically the classical Newton equations of dynamics, we study conservation laws,
and describe some particular types of these kinds of systems.
This geometric framework is also used to study the case of systems with holonomic and nonholonomic constraints and their corresponding variational principles.
Finally, nonautonomous Newtonian systems are also briefly analyzed.

5.1
Connections in manifolds. Riemannian manifolds

(For a detailed account of these subjects and the proof of the results, see for example

[
97
,
199
,
238
,
241
,
242
,
179
,
328
]
).

5.1.1
Connections and covariant derivatives

Definition 5.1

A
connection
on a manifold
M
is a map

\nabla\colon\mathfrak{X}(M)\times\mathfrak{X}(M)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathfrak{X}(M)\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt(X,Y)\mapsto\nabla_{X}Y\ ,

satisfying the following properties:

(i)

\nabla_{X}Y
is
\mathbb{R}
-linear in
Y
.

(ii)

\nabla_{X}Y
is
{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
-linear in
X
.

(iii)

If
f\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
, then
\nabla_{X}(fY)=(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X)f)Y+f\nabla_{X}Y
.

Then
\nabla_{X}Y
is said to be the
covariant derivative
of
Y
with respect to
X
.

Connections and covariant derivatives
satisfy the following properties:

•

For every
p\in M
, the result of
(\nabla_{X}Y)(p)

depends only on the value of
X
at
p
and
on the value of
Y
on a neighbourhood of
p
.
Thus, we can calculate
(\nabla_{X}Y)(p)
in a local coordinate system
around
p
, and
\nabla_{u}Y
is well-defined for
u\in{\rm T}_{p}M
.

•

(Localization of a connection).
If
U\subset M
is an open set, then there exists a connection
\nabla^{U}
on
U
defined as:

\nabla^{U}_{X}|_{U}Y|_{U}:=(\nabla_{X}Y)|_{U}\ .

•

Let
(U,x^{i})
be a local chart; then the vector fields

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x^{i}}}
define a basis of

\mathfrak{X}(U)
and

\nabla_{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}=\Gamma_{ij}^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}\ ,

where
\Gamma_{ij}^{k}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(U)
are the so-called

Christoffel symbols
of the connection
\nabla

for the local basis
\left\{\displaystyle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x^{i}}}\right\}
or in the local chart
(U,x^{i})
.
Then, if
\displaystyle X=X^{i}{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}}
and

\displaystyle Y=Y^{j}{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}}
,

(\nabla_{X}Y)|_{U}=(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X)Y^{j}){\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}}+X^{i}Y^{j}\Gamma_{ij}^{k}{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}}\,.

Observe that this expression says that, at
p\in M
,
the covariant derivative depends only on the values of
Y
along a curve

\gamma\colon(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M

such that
\gamma^{\prime}(0)=X_{p}
.

This also holds if we have a local basis

(X_{i})
for vector fields on an open set
U\subset M
, not necessarily local coordinate vector fields.

•

If
M
is a parallelizable manifold, and
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}

is a basis of
\mathfrak{X}(M)
, then any family of differentiable functions

\Gamma_{ij}^{k}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
define a connection on
M
.
That is, the Christoffel symbols determine the connection
and they allow calculating the covariant derivative
in the open set where they are defined.

In particular, if
M=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the standard connection is defined
taking the Christoffel symbols equal to zero in the natural coordinate system.

•

Let
(x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})
and
(\tilde{x}^{1},\ldots,\tilde{x}^{n})
be two coordinate systems on the same open set, and
\Gamma_{ij}^{k}
and
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}
be the corresponding Christoffel symbols, then

\Gamma_{ij}^{k}=\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\frac{\partial\tilde{x}^{\alpha}}{\partial x^{i}}\frac{\partial\tilde{x}^{\beta}}{\partial x^{j}}\frac{\partial x^{k}}{\partial\tilde{x}^{\gamma}}+\frac{\partial^{2}\tilde{x}^{\delta}}{\partial x^{i}\partial x^{j}}\frac{\partial x^{k}}{\partial\tilde{x}^{\delta}}\ ,

hence they are not the components of any tensor field.
Thus, it is a nonsense to say that a connection is null.
The Christoffel symbols can vanish in a coordinate system and be different from zero in another one.

•

The set of connections on a manifold is not a vector space but an affine space.

•

Let
\nabla^{\alpha}
be a family of connections on
M
and
f_{\alpha}
be a family of differentiable functions such that their supports are a locally finite family of subsets of
M
. Then
\nabla=f_{\alpha}\nabla^{\alpha}
defined as

\nabla_{X}Y:=f_{\alpha}\nabla^{\alpha}_{X}Y

is a connection if, and only if,
\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha}f_{\alpha}=1
.

•

Using the above item, we can state that
any paracompact manifold admits a connection and, conversely,
if there exists a connection on
M
, then
M
is paracompact.
(Remember that to say that
M
is paracompact is equivalent to say
that every open covering has a subordinate partition of unity,
or that
M
is metrizable, or that every connected component has a numerable basis of open sets).

5.1.2
Covariant derivative of tensor fields

Definition 5.2

Let
(M,\nabla)
be a differentiable manifold with a connection,
and let
\mathcal{T}(M)
be the algebra of tensor fields on
M
.
For
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
the

covariant derivative
by
X
defined by the connection
is the unique map
\nabla_{X}\colon\mathcal{T}(M)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{T}(M)

verifying the following properties:

(i)

It is the Lie derivative
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X)
on
{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
.

(ii)

It reduces to the well known
\nabla_{X}
on
\mathfrak{X}(M)
.

(iii)

It is
\mathbb{R}
-linear.

(iv)

It respects the type of tensor field; that is, it maps
\mathcal{T}_{h}^{k}(M)
onto
\mathcal{T}_{h}^{k}(M)
.

(v)

(Leibniz rule)
:
\nabla_{X}(S\otimes T)=(\nabla_{X}S)\otimes T+S\otimes(\nabla_{X}T)
.

(vi)

It commutes with the internal contractions.

That is,
\nabla_{X}
is a derivation of the
\mathbb{R}
-algebra
\mathcal{T}(M)
, with zero degree, which commutes with the internal contractions.

Observe that, as a consequence of the definition, we have that,
for
f\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
and
S,T\in\mathcal{T}(M)
,

\nabla_{X}(fS\otimes T)=(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X)f)(S\otimes T)+f(\nabla_{X}(S\otimes T))=\nabla_{X}(S\otimes fT)\ .

The covariant derivative has the following properties:

•

For
p\in M
, the value of
(\nabla_{X}T)(p)
only depends on
X(p)
and the value of the tensor field
T
on an open set containing
p
. This property has the same consequences as in the case of derivation of vector fields.

•

The above properties defining
\nabla_{X}
allow us to calculate
\nabla_{X}T
for any tensor field
T
providing we know how is the actuation of
\nabla_{X}
on functions and vector fields.
For example, let
X,Y\in\mathcal{T}^{1}(M)
and
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(M)=\mathcal{T}_{1}(M)
, then,

\langle\nabla_{X}\alpha,Y\rangle=\nabla_{X}\langle\alpha,Y\rangle-\langle\alpha,\nabla_{X}Y\rangle\ .

In particular, we have that

\nabla_{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}}{\rm d}x^{j}=-\Gamma_{ik}^{j}{\rm d}x^{k}
.

•

In a local coordinate system
(U,x^{i})
, if

X=X^{j}{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}},\,\,\,T=T_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}^{{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}}{\rm d}x^{j_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes{\rm d}x^{j_{s}}\otimes{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i_{1}}}}\otimes\ldots{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i_{r}}}}\ ,

then

\nabla_{X}T=S_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}^{{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}}{\rm d}x^{j_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes{\rm d}x^{j_{s}}\otimes{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i_{1}}}}\otimes\ldots{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i_{r}}}}\ ;

where

S_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}^{{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}}=X^{k}(\partial_{k}T_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}^{{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}}+\sum_{n=1}^{r}T_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}^{{i_{1},\ldots,l,\dots,i_{r}}}\Gamma_{kl}^{i_{n}}-\sum_{n=1}^{s}T_{j_{1},\ldots,l,\dots,j_{s}}^{{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}}\Gamma_{k_{n}}^{l})\ .

Definition 5.3

Let
T\in\mathcal{T}_{s}^{r}(M)
,
X_{i},Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
, and
\beta^{j}\in\Omega^{1}(M)
.
The tensor field
\nabla T\in\mathcal{T}_{s+1}^{r}(M)
defined by

(\nabla T)(X_{1},\ldots,X_{s},Y,\beta^{1},\ldots,\beta^{r}):=(\nabla_{Y}T)(X_{1},\ldots,X_{s},\beta^{1},\ldots,\beta^{r})\ ,

is called the
covariant differential
of
T
.

With the same notation for
T
as in the above item, we have

\nabla T=S_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s};k}^{{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}}{\rm d}x^{j_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes{\rm d}x^{j_{s}}\otimes{\rm d}x^{k}\otimes{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i_{1}}}}\otimes\ldots{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i_{r}}}}\ ,

where

S_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s};k}^{{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}}=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x^{k}}}T_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}^{{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}}+\sum_{n=1}^{r}T_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}^{{i_{1},\ldots,l,\dots,i_{r}}}\Gamma_{kl}^{i_{n}}-\sum_{n=1}^{s}T_{j_{1},\ldots,l,\dots,j_{s}}^{{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}}\Gamma_{k_{n}}^{l}\ .

And for functions
f\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
, we have
\nabla f={\rm d}f
.

For a vector field
\displaystyle X=X^{i}{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}}
, we have that

\nabla X=\Big(\frac{\partial{X^{i}}}{\partial{x^{k}}}X^{i}+X^{l}\Gamma_{kl}^{i}\Big){\rm d}x^{k}\otimes{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}}\in\mathcal{T}_{1}^{1}(U)\ .

Observe that
(\nabla X)(Y)=\nabla_{Y}X
.

5.1.3
Covariant derivative along curves

Remember that a
vector field along a map

F\colon N\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M
, is another map
Z\colon N\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}M

such that
Z(q)\in{\rm T}_{F(q)}M
, for every
q\in N
; that is
\tau_{Q}\circ Z=F
.
The set of vector fields along a map
F
is a
{\rm C}^{\infty}(N)
-modulus, denoted by
\mathfrak{X}(F)
.

If
\gamma\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M
is a curve,
then we have the set
\mathfrak{X}(\gamma)
as a
{\rm C}^{\infty}(I)
-modulus.
One distinguished element of this set is the velocity vector
\gamma^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{X}(\gamma)
.
If
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
, then
X\circ\gamma\in\mathfrak{X}(\gamma)
;
but not every element of
\mathfrak{X}(\gamma)
is of this kind.

If
\displaystyle Y=Y^{j}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x^{i}}}
is a vector field,
then, by the above properties of the covariant derivative,

\nabla_{\gamma^{\prime}(t)}Y
is well-defined as

\nabla_{\gamma^{\prime}(t)}Y:=(D(Y^{j}\circ\gamma)(t){\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}}|_{\gamma(t)}+\Gamma_{ij}^{k}(\gamma(t))D\gamma^{i}(t)(Y^{j}(\gamma(t)){\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}}|_{\gamma(t)}\ ,

where
\displaystyle D=\frac{d}{dt}
on the functions
f:I\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}
, and
\gamma^{i}
are the components of
\gamma
. Then:

Definition 5.4

Given a curve
\gamma\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M
,
the
covariant derivative
along
\gamma

is the unique map
\nabla_{t}\colon\mathfrak{X}(\gamma)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathfrak{X}(\gamma)

such that

(i)

\nabla_{t}
is
\mathbb{R}
-linear.

(ii)

If
f\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(I)
and
\mathbf{w}\in\mathfrak{X}(\gamma)
, then

\nabla_{t}(f\mathbf{w})=(Df)\mathbf{w}+f\nabla_{t}\mathbf{w}
.

(iii)

If
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
, then
\nabla_{t}(X\circ\gamma)(t)=\nabla_{\gamma^{\prime}(t)}X
.

The vector field along
\gamma
given by
\nabla_{t}\mathbf{w}
is called the

covariant derivative
of
\mathbf{w}
along
\gamma
.

Other notations for
\nabla_{t}\mathbf{w}
are

\nabla_{t}^{\gamma}\mathbf{w}=\nabla_{\gamma^{\prime}(t)}\mathbf{w}=\nabla_{\frac{d}{dt}}\mathbf{w}
.

If
(U,x^{i})
is a local chart with
\gamma(I)\subset U
,
then a basis for
\mathfrak{X}(\gamma)
is given by
\displaystyle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x^{i}}}\circ\gamma
and,
for
\mathbf{w}\in\mathfrak{X}(\gamma)
, then
\mathbf{w}=w^{i}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x^{i}}}\circ\gamma\Big)
,
with
w^{i}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(I)
. Writing

\gamma^{\prime}=\dot{q}^{i}\displaystyle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x^{i}}}\circ\gamma
,
we have that

\nabla_{t}\mathbf{w}=\left(\dot{w}^{i}+(\Gamma_{ij}^{k}\circ\gamma)\dot{q}^{i}{w}^{j}\right){\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}}\circ\gamma\ .

Similarly as above, we can define the concept of tensor field along a map
or along a curve, and the covariant derivative of tensor fields along a curve
with the same rules as above.
If we know the action of
\nabla_{t}
on functions and vector fields,
we can obtain the covariant derivative of any kind of tensor field
along the curve
\gamma
.
In particular, if
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\Omega^{1}(\gamma)

and
\mathbf{w}\in\mathfrak{X}(\gamma)
, then

D\langle\boldsymbol{\beta},\mathbf{w}\rangle=\langle\nabla_{t}\boldsymbol{\beta},\mathbf{w}\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{\beta},\nabla_{t}\mathbf{w}\rangle\ ,

which, for
\boldsymbol{\beta}=b_{j}{\rm d}x^{j}\circ\gamma
, gives the following expression in a local chart

\nabla_{t}\boldsymbol{\beta}=\left(\dot{b}_{j}-(\Gamma_{ij}^{k}\circ\gamma)\dot{q}^{i}{b}_{k}\right){\rm d}x^{j}\circ\gamma\ .

5.1.4
Parallel transport along a curve. Geodesic curves

Definition 5.5

Let
(M,\nabla)
be a manifold with a connection
and
\gamma\colon I\subseteq\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M
a curve.
We say that
\mathbf{w}\in\mathfrak{X}(\gamma)
is
parallel along
\gamma

if
\nabla_{t}\mathbf{w}=0
.

The following properties hold:

•

(Existence of parallel vector fields). Given
(M,\nabla)
and

\gamma\colon I\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M
, if
t_{o}\in I
and
\mathbf{w}_{o}\in{\rm T}_{\gamma(t_{o})}M
,
then there exists an unique
\mathbf{w}\in\mathfrak{X}(\gamma)
parallel and such that
\mathbf{w}(t_{o})=\mathbf{w}_{o}
.

This result comes from the linearity of the differential equation
defining parallel vector fields along a curve
\gamma\colon I\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M
.
We say that
\mathbf{w}
is the
parallel transport
of
\mathbf{w}_{o}

along
\gamma
, with respect to the connection
\nabla
.

•

Observe that the map
{\rm T}_{\gamma(t_{o})}M\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathfrak{X}(\gamma)

given by
\mathbf{w}_{o}\mapsto\mathbf{w}
is linear and bijective.

•

If
t\in I
, then the vector
\mathbf{w}(t)
is called the
parallel transport
of
\mathbf{w}_{o}
from
\gamma(t_{o})
to
\gamma(t)
.
Thus, for
t_{o},t\in I
, we have a
parallel transport operator
along
\gamma
, denoted
\tau_{t_{o},t}\colon{\rm T}_{\gamma(t_{o})}M\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}_{\gamma(t)}M
, which is a linear isomorphism.

•

Let
(\mathbf{e_{i}})
be a basis of
{\rm T}_{\gamma(t_{o})}M

and
\mathbf{w}_{i}
the corresponding parallel transported elements
of
\mathfrak{X}(\gamma)
, then:

1.

(\mathbf{w}_{i}(t))
is a basis of
{\rm T}_{\gamma(t)}M
for every
t\in I
.

2.

(\mathbf{w}_{i})
is a basis of
\mathfrak{X}(\gamma)
as
{\rm C}^{\infty}(I)
-module.

•

For
t_{o},t\in I
, consider
\tau_{t_{o},t}\colon{\rm T}_{\gamma(t_{o})}M\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}_{\gamma(t)}M
which is the parallel transport operator along
\gamma
.
Then it is easy to show that,

(\nabla_{t}\mathbf{w})(t_{o})=\lim_{t\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 1.99306pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>t_{o}}\frac{\tau_{t_{o},t}^{-1}(\mathbf{w}(t))-\mathbf{w}(t_{o})}{t-t_{o}}\ ;

that is, the parallel transport determines the covariant derivative.

Definition 5.6

A curve
\gamma\colon I\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M
is a
geodesic
of the connection
\nabla

if
\gamma^{\prime}
, as a vector field along
\gamma
,
is parallel along
\gamma
; that is
\nabla_{t}{\gamma^{\prime}}=0
.

•

If
\gamma=(q^{i}(t))
in local coordinates,
then the curve
\gamma
is a geodesic if, and only if,
it satisfies the following second order differential equation

\ddot{q}\,^{k}+(\Gamma_{ij}^{k}\circ\gamma)\dot{q}\,^{i}\dot{q}\,^{j}=0\ .

As a consequence, given
u_{p}\in{\rm T}_{p}M
there is a unique geodesic with initial condition
u_{p}
.

•

Reparametrization of geodesics
:
Let
\gamma\colon I_{1}\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M
be a geodesic and
let
\varphi\colon I_{2}\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>I_{1}
be a diffeomorphism
between open intervals on
\mathbb{R}
.
Then the reparametrized curve
\gamma\circ\varphi
is a geodesic
if, and only if,
\varphi
is an affine map.

•

A vector field
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
is said to be
parallel

if it is parallel along any curve on
M
.
This is equivalent to each of the following expressions:

i)

\nabla_{u}X=0
, for every tangent vector
u
.

ii)

\nabla_{Y}X=0
, for every vector field
Y
.

iii)

\nabla X=0
.

•

The above notions can be extended to tensor fields,
so defining
tensor fields parallel along a curve

or
parallel tensor fields
.

5.1.5
Torsion and curvature of a connection

Definition 5.7

Let
(M,\nabla)
be a manifold endowed with a connection.
The map
{\mathit{T}}\colon\mathfrak{X}(M)\times\mathfrak{X}(M)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathfrak{X}(M)

defined by

{\mathit{T}}(X,Y):=\nabla_{X}Y-\nabla_{Y}X-[X,Y]

is
{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
-bilinear, and then it defines a tensor field

{\mathit{T}}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{2}^{1}(M)
which is called
the
torsion tensor
of the connection.

A connection
\nabla
is said to be
symmetric
if its torsion is zero.

The properties of the torsion tensor are:

•

The torsion tensor
{\mathit{T}}
is antisymmetric.

•

Let
(E_{i})
be a local reference frame of vector fields,
(E^{i})
its dual reference,

\Gamma_{ij}^{k}
the corresponding Christoffel symbols,
and
[E_{i},E_{j}]=c_{ij}^{k}E_{k}
; then

{\mathit{T}}={\mathit{T}}_{ij}^{k}\,E^{i}\otimes E^{j}\otimes E_{k}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt{\mathit{T}}_{ij}^{k}=\Gamma_{ij}^{k}-\Gamma_{ji}^{k}-c_{ij}^{k}\ .

Hence,
\nabla
is symmetric if, in any system of coordinates,
the Christoffel symbols
\Gamma_{ij}^{k}
are symmetric in
(i,j)
.

•

It is easy to show that:

i)

Two connections on
M
are the same if, and only if,
they have the same geodesics and the same torsion tensor.

ii)

Given a connection on
M
, there exist another, unique,
with the same geodesics and null torsion.

Definition 5.8

The map
{\mathit{R}}\colon\mathfrak{X}(M)\otimes\mathfrak{X}(M)\otimes\mathfrak{X}(M)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathfrak{X}(M)

defined by

{\mathit{R}}(X,Y)Z:=\nabla_{X}\nabla_{Y}Z-\nabla_{Y}\nabla_{X}Z-\nabla_{[X,Y]}Z

is
{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
-trilinear, then it is a tensor field

{\mathit{R}}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{3}^{1}(M)
which is called
the
curvature tensor
of the connection
\nabla
.

A connection is said to be
flat
if its curvature tensor is zero.

The properties of the curvature tensor are:

•

The curvature tensor
{\mathit{R}}
is antisymmetric in
(X,Y)
.

•

With the same notations as above,
in a local reference frame
(E_{i})
we have that

{\mathit{R}}={\mathit{R}}_{ijk}^{l}\;E^{i}\otimes E^{j}\otimes E^{k}\otimes E_{l}\ ,

with

{\mathit{R}}_{ijk}^{l}=\Gamma_{jk,i}^{l}-\Gamma_{ik,j}^{l}+\Gamma_{jk}^{m}\Gamma_{im}^{l}-\Gamma_{ik}^{m}\Gamma_{jm}^{l}-c_{ij}^{m}\Gamma_{mk}^{l}\ .

•

When the torsion of
\nabla
is zero, then
{\mathit{R}}(X,Y)Z+{\mathit{R}}(Z,X)Y+{\mathit{R}}(Y,Z)X=0
. This is called the
Bianchi identity
.

•

It can be proved that, for a manifold with a connection,
the following conditions are equivalent:

(i)

The connection is flat.

(ii)

On a neighbourhood of any point, there exist a local chart with coordinate vector fields which are parallel.

(iii)
