The parallel transport along a curve between two points of the manifold does not depend on the chosen curve.

5.1.6
Riemannian manifolds

Definition 5.9

Let
M
be a differentiable manifold.
A
Riemannian metric
on
M
is a 2-covariant tensor field

{\tt g}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{2}(M)
symmetric and positive defined;
that is

(i)

The map
{\tt g}\colon\mathfrak{X}(M)\times\mathfrak{X}(M)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)

is
{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
-bilinear and symmetric

(ii)

For every
p\in M
, and
u_{p}\in{\rm T}_{p}M
,
we have
{\tt g}(u_{p},u_{p})\geq 0
, and it is zero if, and only if,
u_{p}=0
.

A
Riemannian manifold

(M,{\tt g})
is a differentiable manifold endowed with a Riemannian metric.

Changing the positive defined condition by nondegeneracy of
{\tt g}
,
we say that we have a
pseudo Riemannian manifold
.
In the case that the signature of a pseudo Riemannian metric
{\tt g}

is
(1,m-1)
, or
(m-1,1)
, we say that
(M,{\tt g})
is a

Lorentzian manifold
and
{\tt g}
is a
Lorentz metric
.

In the sequel we will suppose that
(M,{\tt g})
is a Riemannian manifold unless indicated.

We have the following properties:

•

We introduce the following notations and terminology:

1.

If
u_{p},v_{p}\in{\rm T}_{p}M
, then
(u_{p}\mid v_{p}):={\tt g}(u_{p},v_{p})
is the
scalar product
of
u_{p}
and
v_{p}
.

2.

For
u_{p}\in{\rm T}_{p}M
, the
norm
of
u_{p}
is
\parallel u_{p}\parallel:={\tt g}(u_{p},u_{p})^{1/2}
.

3.

For
X,Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
, the scalar product of
X
and
Y
is
(X\mid Y):={\tt g}(X,Y):M\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}
.

4.

For
u_{p},v_{p}\in{\rm T}_{p}M
, if
{\tt g}(u_{p},v_{p})=0
we say that they are
orthogonal
.

5.

Two vector fields
X,Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
are orthogonal if
{\tt g}(X,Y)=0
.

6.

If
u_{p},v_{p}\in{\rm T}_{p}M
and
{\tt g}
is Riemannian,
then the
angle

\theta

between
u_{p}
and
v_{p}
:
\theta
is defined by

\displaystyle\cos\theta:=\frac{{\tt g}(u_{p},v_{p})}{\parallel u_{p}\parallel\parallel v_{p}\parallel}
.

•

If
(U,x^{i})
is a local chart,
the local expression of
{\tt g}
is

{\tt g}|_{U}=g_{ij}\,{\rm d}x^{i}\otimes{\rm d}x^{j}
, where

g_{ij}={\tt g}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}},\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\right)\,.

The matrix
(g_{ij})
is symmetric and positive defined.

•

In general, if
(E_{i})
is a basis for vector fields on a manifold,
then
{\tt g}=g_{ij}\,E^{i}\otimes E^{j}
, where
g_{ij}={\tt g}(E_{i},E_{j})
.
Remember that
(E^{j})
is the dual basis corresponding to
(E_{i})
.

•

The standard metric on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
:
The standard Riemannian metric on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
is given by

{\tt g}=\delta_{ij}{\rm d}x^{i}\otimes{\rm d}x^{j}
,
in the canonical coordinate system.

•

The Minkowski space
:
On the vector space

\mathbb{R}^{m}
we introduce the Minkowski metric given by

{\tt g}=\eta_{ij}\,{\rm d}x^{i}\otimes{\rm d}x^{j}
,
where the diagonal of
(\eta_{ij})
is
(-1,1,\ldots,1)
and all the other elements are zero.
This is the so-called
Minkowski metric

and
(\mathbb{R}^{m},{\tt g})
is a Lorentzian manifold.

•

Let
{\mathit{j}}\colon N\hookrightarrow M

an immersed submanifold of a Riemannian manifold
(M,{\tt g})
.
Then
(N,{\mathit{j}}^{*}{\tt g})
is a Riemannian manifold.
Observe that this can not be true in the pseudo Riemannian case.

•

Let
(M_{1},{\tt g}_{1})
and
(M_{2},{\tt g}_{2})
be Riemannian manifolds.
An
isometry
between
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
is a diffeomorphism

F\colon M_{1}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M_{2}
such that
F^{*}{\tt g}_{2}={\tt g}_{1}
.
If
F
is defined only on an open set then it is called a local isometry.

•

An
isometry
of
(M,{\tt g})
is a diffeomorphism on
M
, such that it leaves invariant the metric tensor
{\tt g}
.

An
infinitesimal isometry
of
(M,{\tt g})
is a vector field on
M

such that the diffeomorphisms defined by its flux
F^{t}

are isometries of
(M,{\tt g})
.
The infinitesimal isometries are called
Killing vector fields
.
Clearly,
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
is a Killing vector field if, and only if,
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X){\tt g}=0
.

•

In a paracompact differentiable manifold, there exists a Riemannian metric. This is a consequence of the existence of partitions of unity.

For the existence of a Lorentzian metric on a paracompact manifold
M
,
it is necessary that there exists a vector field without critical points.
This is true for connected compact manifolds with null Euler–Poincaré characteristic.

5.1.7
Some natural constructions on Riemannian manifolds

Let
(M,{\tt g})
be a Riemannian manifold.

•

For every point
p\in M
, the metric
{\tt g}
defines an isomorphism

\hat{\tt g}_{p}\colon{\rm T}_{p}M\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}_{p}^{*}M
, such that

\langle\hat{\tt g}_{p}(u_{p}),v_{p}\rangle:={\tt g}(u_{p},v_{p})
.
This family of isomorphisms collects into a global one

\hat{\tt g}\colon{\rm T}M\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}^{*}M
,
which is an isomorphism of vector bundles.

This isomorphism can be extended to the corresponding

{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
-modules of sections; that is, we have the map

{\tt g}^{\flat}\colon\mathfrak{X}(M)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\Omega^{1}(M)
defined by

{\tt g}^{\flat}(X)=X^{\flat}:=\hat{\tt g}\circ X
,
which is an isomorphism with inverse

{\tt g}^{\sharp}\colon\Omega^{1}(M)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathfrak{X}(M)
such that

\alpha\mapsto\alpha^{\sharp}=\hat{\tt g}^{-1}\circ\alpha
.

•

In a local chart
(U,x^{i})
, the isomorphism
\hat{\tt g}

and its inverse are given respectively by

(x^{i},v^{i})\mapsto(x^{i},v^{i}g_{ij}(x))\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt(x^{i},\alpha_{i})\mapsto(x^{i},\alpha_{i}g^{ij}(x))\ ,

where
(g^{ij})
is the inverse matrix of
(g_{ij})
.

•

The above isomorphisms are usually called

musical isomorphisms
, and they are written as

v^{\flat}
, for the linear form with coordinates
v_{j}=v^{i}g_{ij}
,
and
\alpha^{\sharp}
, for the tangent vector with coordinates

\alpha^{j}=\alpha_{i}g^{ij}
.

Obviously, these isomorphisms can be extended to any kind of tensor fields, and we can lift and low indexes using the metric tensor.

•

Let
f\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
; the
gradient
of
f

is the vector field
{\rm grad}\,f:=\hat{{\tt g}}^{-1}\circ{\rm d}f
.
Then we have that
({\rm grad}\,f|X)=\langle{\rm d}f,X\rangle
.
In local coordinates,

\displaystyle{\rm grad}\,f=g^{ij}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}
.

•

In a Riemannian manifold, there exists a canonical measure
V_{\tt g}

called
Riemannian volume
.
First, in the open set of a local chart
(U,x^{i})
,
the integral of a function
f\colon U\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}
is given by

\int_{U}f\,{\rm d}V_{\tt g}=\int_{\varphi(U)}\hat{f}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{m})\sqrt{{\mid\det\,(g_{ij})}\mid}\,\,{\rm d}x^{1}\ldots{\rm d}x^{m}\ ,

where
\hat{f}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{m})
is the local expression of
f
.

•

IF
M
is an oriented manifold, then this measure comes from
a canonical volume form
\Omega_{g}
usually called

canonical Riemannian volume form
.
In a local chart, this volume form is given by

\Omega_{\tt g}=\sqrt{{\mid\det\,(g_{ij})}\mid}\ {\rm d}x^{1}\ldots{\rm d}x^{m}\ .

5.1.8
The Levi-Civita connection

Definition 5.10

Let
(M,{\tt g})
be a Riemannian manifold.
A connection
\nabla
on
M
is a
Riemannian connection

if the metric tensor field
{\tt g}
is parallel; that is,
\nabla{\tt g}=0
.

This is equivalent to say that
\nabla_{X}{\tt g}=0
, for every
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
.

Another interesting way to state this property is the following:
for every
X,Y,Z\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
,

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Z)({\tt g}X,Y)={\tt g}(\nabla_{Z}X,Y)+{\tt g}(X,\nabla_{Z}Y)\ .

Using the idea of covariant derivative along a curve,
this condition is equivalent to say that,
for every curve
\gamma\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M
and

X,Y\in\mathfrak{X}(\gamma)
,
the following equation holds

D(X\mid Y)=(\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}(t)}X\mid Y)+(X\mid\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}(t)}Y)\ .

Remember that, for a function
f:I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}
, we have
Df={\rm d}f/{\rm d}t
.

Observe that, in particular, if
X,Y
are parallel along

\gamma
, then
(X\mid Y)
is constant,
then we get that the parallel transport with a Riemannian connection is an isometry.
Furthermore, if
\gamma
is a geodesic line, then
(\gamma^{\prime}\mid\gamma^{\prime})
is constant.

Finally, the
Fundamental Theorem of Riemannian geometry
states that,
if
(M,{\tt g})
is a Riemannian manifold,
there exists one, and only one, connection on
M
which is Riemannian and symmetric.

Definition 5.11

The above connection, called the
Levi-Civita connection

associated to the metric, is defined by the
Koszul formula
:

2(\nabla_{X}Y\mid Z)=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X)(Y\mid Z)+\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)(Z\mid X)-\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Z)(X\mid Y)

+([X,Y]\mid Z)+([Z,X]\mid Y)-([Y,Z]\mid X).

In the sequel, unless indicated, when we use a connection
\nabla

in a Riemannian manifold, we will assume that it is the Levi-Civita connection.

In a local chart
(U,x^{i})
in
M
, the Christoffel symbols of the Levi-Civita connection in the corresponding local basis for the vector fields are given by:

\Gamma_{ij}^{k}=\frac{1}{2}g^{kl}\left(\frac{\partial g_{jl}}{\partial x^{i}}+\frac{\partial g_{il}}{\partial x^{j}}-\frac{\partial g_{ij}}{\partial x^{l}}\right).

They are also written as
\Gamma_{ij}^{k}=g^{kl}[ij,l]

where
[ij,l]
are called
Christoffel symbols of the first class
.

5.1.9
Submanifolds of a Riemannian manifold

Let
{\mathit{j}}\colon M\hookrightarrow\widetilde{M}

be an embedded submanifold of a Riemannian manifold

(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{g})
with the Levi-Civita connection

\widetilde{\nabla}
. We know that
{\tt g}=j^{*}\widetilde{\tt g}

is a Riemannian metric on
M
. Let
\nabla
be its Levi-Civita connection.

•

For every
p\in M
, the embedding
{\mathit{j}}
allows us to identify

{\rm T}_{p}M
as a subspace of
{\rm T}_{j(p)}\widetilde{M}
,
hence we have a splitting

{\rm T}_{j(p)}\widetilde{M}={\rm T}_{p}M\oplus({\rm T}_{p}M)^{\perp}\,.

Then every tangent vector
u\in{\rm T}_{j(p)}\widetilde{M}

can be decomposed as a sum
u=u^{\top}+u^{\perp}
,
the tangent part to
M
and the orthogonal one. This splitting can be extended to vector fields on the manifold
\widetilde{M}
.

•

Let
X,Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
; on a neighbourhood of every
p\in M

we can extend the vector fields
X,Y
to vector fields
defined on an open set of
\widetilde{M}
.
Hence, we can calculate
(\widetilde{\nabla}_{X}Y)(p)\in{\rm T}_{j(p)}\widetilde{M}
,
as this value is independent of the extensions.
With this construction, we have a vector field along the embedding
{\mathit{j}}
;
that is,
\widetilde{\nabla}_{X}Y\colon M\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}\widetilde{M}
.
This vector field can be split into,

\widetilde{\nabla}_{X}Y=(\widetilde{\nabla}_{X}Y)^{\top}+(\widetilde{\nabla}_{X}Y)^{\perp}\,.

•

It is easy to prove that, for
X,Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
, the map
(X,Y)\mapsto(\widetilde{\nabla}_{X}Y)^{\top}
,
defines a connection on
(M,{\tt g})
.
This connection is precisely the Levi-Civita connection.

•

Now we consider the other component of the above splitting:
The expression
\Pi(X,Y)=(\widetilde{\nabla}_{X}Y)^{\perp}
is

{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
-bilinear and symmetric in
X,Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
.
Symmetry holds because
[X,Y]\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
, for
X,Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
, hence
[X,Y]^{\perp}=0
.

•

The above property of bilinearity implies that
the map
\Pi_{p}\colon{\rm T}_{p}M\times{\rm T}_{p}M\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>({\rm T}_{p}M)^{\perp}
, for every
p\in M
,
is well-defined and gives us a vector valued bilinear symmetric form
taking values on
({\rm T}_{p}M)^{\perp}
.
It is called the
second fundamental form
of
M
.

The decomposition
\widetilde{\nabla}_{X}Y=\nabla_{X}Y+\Pi(X,Y)
is called
Gauss formula
.

Furthermore, if
N\in\mathfrak{X}(\widetilde{M})
is orthogonal to
M
,
then we have the so-called
Weingarten equation
:

(\widetilde{\nabla}_{X}N\mid Y)=-(N\mid\Pi(X,Y))
.

•

(
Nash Embedding Theorem
): Every Riemannian manifold
with a countable basis of open sets is isometric
to a submanifold of some
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

It is interesting to analyze the following particular cases of submanifolds of Riemannian manifolds:

Hypersurfaces

Let
(\widetilde{M},{\tt g})
be a Riemannian manifold and

M\subset\widetilde{M}
be a hypersurface of
\widetilde{M}
,
and suppose that both manifolds are oriented.

If
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{m})
is an oriented positive basis of vector fields on
M
,
then, on the points of
M
, we can take a unique vector field
N
,
unitary and orthogonal to
M
, such that
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{m},N)

is an oriented positive basis for
\widetilde{M}
on the points of
M
. We say that
N
is the positive-oriented normal vector field to
M
. In this situation, the second fundamental form can be understood as

\mathbb{R}
-valued, and we write
h(X,Y)=(\Pi(X,Y)\mid N)
.
We have that
\Pi(X,Y)=h(X,Y)N
.

With this construction, we have a 2-covariant tensor field on
M
.
With this quadratic form, using the metric
{\tt g}
,
at every point
p\in M
, we can define a symmetric endomorphism

S
on
{\rm T}_{p}M
, called the
Weingarten map
, which is defined by

(S(X)\mid Y)=h(X,Y):=((\widetilde{\nabla}_{X}Y)^{\perp}\mid N)\ .

The eigenvalues of
S
are called
principal curvatures

and the product of all of them is called the
Gauss curvature
.
The eigenvectors are called
principal directions
.

Curves

Let
(M,{\tt g})
be a Riemannian manifold and
C\subset M
a connected orientable regular curve; that is, a submanifold of dimension one.

For every
p\in C
there exists a neighbourhood and
a local parametrization of
C
with a curve
c\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M

such that
\parallel c^{\prime}(t)\parallel=1
, the
arc parameter
,
and with the orientation of
C
.
If
{\mathbf{t}}=c^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{X}(c)
is the tangent vector field, for every
s\in I
,
we have that
{\mathbf{t}}(s)
is a positive basis of
{\rm T}_{c(s)}C
.

Suppose that
({\mathbf{t}},\nabla_{{\mathbf{t}}(s)}{\mathbf{t}},\ldots,\nabla_{{\mathbf{t}}(s)}^{m-1}{\mathbf{t}})

are linearly independent in some
s_{o}\in I
.
If we apply the Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization procedure,
we get an orthonormal basis
({\mathbf{f}}_{1}(s)={\mathbf{t}},\ldots,{\mathbf{f}}_{m}(s))

of
{\rm T}_{c(s)}C
, for
s
on a neighbourhood of
s_{o}
.
This is the so called
Frenet reference
.
The covariant derivatives of these vector fields
are linear combinations of themselves,
and the coefficients are the curvatures of
C
.
This linear combinations are the
Frenet–Serret formulas
.

5.1.10
Curvature and distance on a Riemannian manifold

Definition 5.12

Let
(M,{\tt g})
be a Riemannian manifold and
R
be
the curvature tensor of the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla
of
(M,{\tt g})
.
The
Riemann curvature tensor
is the tensor field

{\rm Rie}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{4}(M)
obtained by lowering
the contravariant index of
R
to the last place:

{\rm Rie}(X,Y,Z,W)=(R(X,Y)Z\mid W)\ .

The
Ricci curvature tensor
is the tensor field

{\rm Ric}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{2}(M)
obtained after the contraction
of the first covariant index with the contravariant index of
R
;
that is
{\rm Ric}=c_{1}^{1}(R)
.

The
scalar curvature
is the function

S\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(M)
obtained by taking the trace, with respect to
\bf g
, of the Ricci tensor:

{\rm S}={\rm tr}_{\tt g}({\rm Ric})={\rm tr}({\rm Ric}^{\sharp})\ .

Remark 5.13

These definitions can change by demanding that the contractions
are with other indexes, and then the only difference is a change in the sign.

•

A Riemannian manifold is
flat
if it is locally isometric
to the Euclidean space.
Then, a Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
is flat if, and only if,
its curvature tensor vanishes; that is,
its Levi-Civita connection is flat.

•

In a local reference for vector fields, we have that

{\rm Rie}=R_{ijkl}\,E^{i}\otimes E^{j}\otimes E^{k}\otimes E^{l}
, where
R_{ijkl}=R_{ijk}^{s}g_{sl}
.

{\rm Ric}=R_{ij}\,E^{i}\otimes E^{j}
with
R_{jk}=R_{ijk}^{i}
.

{\rm S}=R_{k}^{k}=g^{kj}R_{jk}
.

Now, let
(M,{\tt g})
be a Riemannian manifold.

•

Length of a curve
: Let
\gamma\colon I\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M
be a
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
-piecewise curve. The
length
of
\gamma
is

\ell(\gamma)=\int_{I}\parallel\gamma^{\prime}\parallel\ .

Observe that this length is invariant by reparametrizations;
that is, if
\varphi\colon J\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>I
is a diffeomorphism
between open real intervals, then
\ell(\gamma\circ\varphi)=\ell(\gamma)
.

•

Supposing that the manifold
M
is connected, any two points can be connected by a piecewise curve. Then,
if
p,q\in M
, the
distance
between
p
and
q
is defined as:

{\rm d}(p,q):=\inf\{\ell(\gamma)\mid\gamma\textrm{ is a }{\mathcal{C}}^{1}\textrm{-piecewise curve from}\,\,p\,\,\textrm{to}\,\,q\}.

The function
{\rm d}
is a distance defining the topology of
M
and is called
Riemannian distance
.

•

Let
M
be a connected differentiable manifold,
suppose it is Hausdorff but not necessarily paracompact.
Then the following conditions are equivalent:

1.

M
admits a Riemannian metric.

2.

M
is metrizable as topological space.

3.

The topology of
M
has a countable basis of open sets.

4.

M
is paracompact.

•

If
(M,{\tt g})
is a connected Riemannian manifold,
we say that it is
geodesically complete

if the domain of definition of all the geodesic curves on
M
is
\mathbb{R}
.

•

(
Hopf–Rinow Theorem
):
Let
(M,{\tt g})
be a connected Riemannian manifold and
{\rm d}

its Riemannian distance. Then, the following conditions are equivalent:

1.

For a subset of
M
, if it is bounded by
{\rm d}
and closed,
then it is compact.

2.

As a metric space,
M
is complete.

3.

(M,{\tt g})
is geodesically complete.

5.2
Newtonian dynamical systems

Next, in this section,
we use the above geometric structures to state the description of dynamical systems
on a (semi)Riemannian manifolds
(see, for instance,
[
1
,
11
,
46
,
75
,
91
,
179
,
291
]
as general references).

5.2.1
Newton dynamical equations. Kinetic energy

From a geometric approach, mechanical systems in Newtonian physics have a common background which, as in the above cases,
is collected in the following postulates:

Postulate 5.14

(First Postulate of Newtonian mechanics)
:
The
configuration space

Q
of the dynamical system
with
n
degrees of freedom is a
n
-dimensional differentiable manifold which is endowed with a
Riemannian metric

1
1

1

This formulation can be extended to describe relativistic systems
and, in this case, the metric is semi-Riemannian.
.

The
state space
, or
phase space
of coordinates–velocities, is the tangent bundle
{\rm T}Q
or, alternatively, the
phase space
of coordinates–momenta
is the cotangent bundle
{\rm T}^{*}Q
of the manifold
Q
.

Postulate 5.15

(Second Postulate of Newtonian mechanics)
:
The
observables
or physical magnitudes of the dynamical system
are functions of
{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)
or
{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}^{*}Q)
.

The result of the measure of an observable
is the value of its representing function at a point of the phase space.

Postulate 5.16

(Third Postulate of Newtonian mechanics)
:
The dynamics of the system is given by a
1
-form on
Q
,
the
work form
or
force form
, or equivalently, a vector field on
Q
,
the
force field
or simply the
force
.

With these ideas in mind, we define:

Definition 5.17

A
Newtonian mechanical system
is a triple
(Q,{\tt g},\omega)
, where

1.

Q
is a differentiable manifold (
\dim\,Q=n
).

2.

{\tt g}
is a
Riemannian metric
on
Q
. Then
(Q,{\tt g})
is a Riemannian manifold.

3.

\omega
is a differential 1-form on
Q
, called the
work form
.

As
{\tt g}
is a Riemannian metric, the work form
\omega\in{\Omega}^{1}(Q)

is associated to a unique vector field
{\rm F}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
such that

\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm F}){\tt g}=\omega
. We call
{\rm F}
the

the force field
of the system.
In this case, we denote the system as
(Q,{\tt g},{\rm F})
).

For a Newtonian system
(Q,{\tt g},{\rm F})
, let
\nabla
be the
Levi-Civita connection
associated to
{\tt g}
.

Postulate 5.18

(Fourth Postulate of Newtonian mechanics)
:
The dynamical trajectories of the Newtonian dynamical system

(Q,{\tt g},{\rm F})
are the curves
\gamma\colon[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q

solution to the equation

\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}={\rm F}\circ\gamma

(5.1)

which is called the
Newton equation
of the system.

Remark 5.19

Note that if
{\rm F}=0
, then the dynamical trajectories are the geodesic curves of the metric
{\tt g}
.
This corresponds to the first Newton Law (
Inertia Law
).

If
(U,x^{i})
is a local chart on
Q
, and
\{\Gamma^{k}_{ij}\}

are the corresponding Christoffel symbols of the connection
\nabla
, then the dynamical equation is locally given by

\ddot{\gamma}^{k}+\Gamma_{ij}^{k}\dot{\gamma}^{i}\dot{\gamma}^{j}={\rm F}^{k}\circ\gamma\ .

Furthermore, if,
\omega=\omega_{i}{\rm d}x^{i}
and

\displaystyle{\rm F}={\rm F}^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x^{i}}}
,
in this chart, then we have that

\omega_{i}=g_{ij}{\rm F}^{j}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt{\rm F}^{i}=g^{ij}\omega_{j}\ ,

where
g^{ij}
are the components of the inverse matrix of
{\tt g}
in this local chart (the “inverse metrics”).

This formalism has a dual counterpart as follows:

Definition 5.20

Let
(Q,{\tt g})
be a Riemannian manifold. The musical diffeomorphism associated to the Riemannian metric
{\tt g}
, defined by

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\theta&\colon&{\rm T}Q&\longrightarrow&{\rm T}^{*}Q\\
&&(q,v)&\mapsto&(q,\mathop{i}\nolimits(v)g)\end{array}

gives us the commutative diagram

\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\rm T}Q&\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(63.0,6.0){\mbox{$\theta$}}\put(0.0,3.0){\vector{1}{0}{135.0}}\end{picture}&{\rm T}^{*}Q\\
&\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(14.0,22.0){\mbox{$\tau_{Q}$}}\put(110.0,22.0){\mbox{$\pi_{Q}$}}\put(63.0,0.0){\mbox{$Q$}}\put(0.0,45.0){\vector{3}{-2}{55.0}}\put(135.0,45.0){\vector{-3}{-2}{55.0}}\end{picture}&\end{array}.

Hence
\theta
can be understood as a differential
1
-form on
Q

along

\tau_{Q}

(denoted by
\theta\in{\Omega}^{1}(Q,\tau_{Q})
),
called the
linear momentum
1
-form

associated with the metric.

Using
\theta
, the Newton equation (
5.1
) can be equivalently written as:

Proposition 5.21

(
Dual form of the dynamical equations
):
Given a Newtonian dynamical system
(Q,{\tt g},\omega)
,
a curve
\gamma\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q

is a solution to the dynamical equation if, and only if, it satisfies that

\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}(\theta\circ\dot{\gamma})=\omega\circ\gamma

(5.2)

(
Proof
)
If
\gamma\colon[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
is a curve on
Q
, then

\theta\circ\dot{\gamma}\in{\Omega}^{1}(Q,\gamma)
, and we have that

\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}(\theta\circ\dot{\gamma})=\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}(\mathop{i}\nolimits(\dot{\gamma}){\tt g})=\mathop{i}\nolimits(\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}){\tt g}+\mathop{i}\nolimits(\dot{\gamma})\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}{\tt g}=\mathop{i}\nolimits(\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}){\tt g}=\theta\circ\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}

On the other side if
\gamma
is a dynamical trajectory, that is

\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}={\rm F}\circ\gamma
, then:

\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}(\theta\circ\dot{\gamma})=\theta\circ\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}=\theta\circ{\rm F}\circ\gamma=\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm F}){\tt g}\circ\gamma=\omega\circ\gamma\ ;

which is the equation (
5.2
).

Note that as
\theta\circ\dot{\gamma}\in{\Omega}^{1}(Q,\gamma)
, then,
for every
X\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
, we have

\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}((\theta\circ\dot{\gamma})(X))=(\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}(\theta\circ\dot{\gamma}))(X)+(\theta\circ\dot{\gamma})(\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}X)\ .

Hence, we obtain the following:

Theorem 5.22

(Linear momentum conservation)
:
If the work form (or in a equivalent way, the force field) of a Newtonian mechanical system
(Q,{\tt g},\omega)
is zero, then the linear momentum is invariant, is a constant, along the trajectories of the system.
(In a more geometric way, for every trajectory of the system
\gamma
, the form

\theta\circ\dot{\gamma}\in{\Omega}^{1}(Q,\gamma)
is parallel along the trajectory
\gamma

2
2

2

We maintain the statement as in the classical form in physics:
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, with a natural Cartesian system of coordinates, we have that

\Gamma^{k}_{ij}=0
, for every
i,j,k
, hence the components of

\theta\circ\dot{\gamma}
are constants.

).

(
Proof
)
If

\omega=0
or, equivalently,
F=0
, then the Newton equation is

\displaystyle\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}(\theta\circ\dot{\gamma})=0
;
hence
\theta\circ\dot{\gamma}
is parallel along the trajectory
\gamma
.

Finally, with the Riemannian metric, we can associate the following function:

Definition 5.23

Given a Riemannian manifold
(Q,{\tt g})
, the associated
kinetic energy
is the function

K\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)
defined by

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}K&\colon&{\rm T}Q&\longrightarrow&\mathbb{R}\\
&&(q,v)&\mapsto&\frac{1}{2}{\tt g}(v,v)\end{array}\ .

Its local expression is

K(q^{i},v^{j})=\frac{1}{2}g_{ij}(q)v^{i}v^{j}\ .

5.2.2
Euler–Lagrange equations

Now, we transform the Newton equation into a new form,
which is easier to state when we know the elements defining the system.
First, we need a technical result:

Lemma 5.24

Let
(Q,{\tt g})
be a Riemannian manifold,
K\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)
its kinetic energy,
\nabla
the Levi-Civita connection of
{\tt g}
, and

\gamma\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
a curve.
If
(U,\varphi=(q^{i}))
is a local chart of
Q
with
\gamma(t)\in U
, and
(\tau_{Q}^{-1}(U),q^{i},v^{i})

is the natural chart on
{\rm T}Q
; then

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial{K}}{\partial{v^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}\right)-\frac{\partial{K}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}={\tt g}\left(\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma},\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\right)\ .

(
Proof
)
We compare the local expressions of both sides of the equation.
As
K=\frac{1}{2}g_{ij}v^{i}v^{j}
, we have that

\frac{\partial{K}}{\partial{v^{j}}}=g_{ij}v^{i}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{\partial{K}}{\partial{q^{j}}}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial{g_{ik}}}{\partial{q^{j}}}v^{i}v^{k}\ ,

thus, if
\gamma=(\gamma^{1},\ldots,\gamma^{n})
,

\frac{\partial{K}}{\partial{v^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}=(g_{ij}\circ\gamma)\circ\dot{\gamma}^{i}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{\partial{K}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\dot{\gamma}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\partial{g_{ik}}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\circ\gamma\right)\dot{\gamma}^{i}\dot{\gamma}^{k}\ .

Hence we obtain that

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial{K}}{\partial{v^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}\right)=\left(\frac{\partial{g_{ij}}}{\partial{q^{k}}}\circ\gamma\right)\dot{\gamma}^{k}\dot{\gamma}^{i}+(g_{ij}\circ\gamma)\ddot{\gamma}^{i}

and

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial{K}}{\partial{v^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}\right)-\frac{\partial{K}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}=\left(\frac{\partial{g_{ij}}}{\partial{q^{k}}}\circ\gamma\right)\dot{\gamma}^{k}\dot{\gamma}^{i}+(g_{ij}\circ\gamma)\ddot{\gamma}^{i}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\partial{g_{ik}}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\circ\gamma\right)\dot{\gamma}^{i}\dot{\gamma}^{k}\ .

(5.3)

Furthermore,

g\left(\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma},\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\right)=g\left((\ddot{\gamma}^{i}+\Gamma^{i}_{kl}\dot{\gamma}^{k}\dot{\gamma}^{l})\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}},\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\right)=(g_{ij}\circ\gamma)\ddot{\gamma}^{i}+(g_{ij}\circ\gamma)\Gamma^{i}_{kl}\dot{\gamma}^{k}\dot{\gamma}^{l}.

But we have that

[kl,j]=g_{ij}\Gamma^{i}_{kl}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\partial{g_{jk}}}{\partial{q^{l}}}+\frac{\partial{g_{jl}}}{\partial{q^{k}}}-\frac{\partial{g_{lk}}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\right)\ ,

and by substitution in equation (
5.3
), we obtain the desired result.

Theorem 5.25

(Lagrange)
:
Let
(Q,{\tt g},\omega)
a Newtonian mechanical system, and

\gamma\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
a curve contained in the domain
U\subset Q
of a chart

(U,\varphi=(q^{i}))
of
Q
. Then,
\gamma
is a solution to the dynamical equation
(
5.1
) if, and only if, it satisfies the equations

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial{K}}{\partial{v^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}\right)-\frac{\partial{K}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}=(\omega\circ\gamma)\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\right)=\omega_{j}\circ\gamma\ ,

(5.4)

which are called
Euler–Lagrange equations of the second kind

of the system.

(
Proof
)
If
\gamma
is a solution to the Newton equation (
5.1
), then

{\tt g}\left(\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma},\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\right)={\tt g}\left({\rm F}\circ\gamma,\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\right)=(\omega\circ\gamma)\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\right).

Hence, by the previous Lemma, the curve
\gamma
satisfies equations (
5.4
).

Conversely, if
\gamma
satisfies (
5.4
), then,

{\tt g}\left(\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma},\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\right)=(\omega\circ\gamma)\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\right)={\tt g}\left(F\circ\gamma,\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\right)\ ,

Therefore
\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}={\rm F}\circ\gamma
,
hence
\gamma
is a solution to the Newton equation.

Remark 5.26

•

To calculate the Newton equation in a local chart, we need to know the Christoffel symbols
of the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla
associated to
{\tt g}
;
but to write the Euler–Lagrange equations we do not need the local expression of the connection.
Moreover, observe that the way to get Euler–Lagrange equations does not depend on the local chart we use. We need only to know the local expression of
K
and
\omega
, and calculate the suitable derivatives.

•

Another way to understand Euler–Lagrange equations is as follows:
let
(U,q^{i})
be a local chart of
Q
;
the Newton equations of the system in this chart are written as

\ddot{\gamma}^{i}+\Gamma^{i}_{jk}\dot{\gamma}^{j}\dot{\gamma}^{k}={\rm F}^{i}\circ\gamma\ ,

or, as it is usual,

\ddot{q}^{i}+\Gamma^{i}_{jk}\dot{q}^{j}\dot{q}^{k}={\rm F}^{i}\ .

This is a second order system of differential equations.
To transform it into a first order system, we introduce new variables

v^{i}=\dot{q}^{i}
, and we obtain the first order system on
{\rm T}Q

\dot{q}^{i}=v^{i}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\dot{v}^{i}={\rm F}^{i}-\Gamma^{i}_{jk}v^{j}v^{k}\ .

The associated vector field is given by

X=v^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}+({\rm F}^{i}-\Gamma^{i}_{jk}v^{j}v^{k})\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}=v^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}-\Gamma^{i}_{jk}v^{j}v^{k}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}+{\rm F}^{v}\ ,

where
{\rm F}^{v}
is the vertical lift of
{\rm F}
from
Q
to
{\rm T}Q
. Observe that we obtain the geodesic vector field plus the vertical lift of the force field.

As
X\in\mathfrak{X}(\tau_{Q}^{-1}(U))
, for every point
(q,v)\in\tau_{Q}^{-1}(U)

there exists a unique solution, with
(q_{o},v_{o})
as initial condition.
Consider now the functions we need to obtain
the Euler–Lagrange equations and calculate the action of
X
on them.
Using the well known properties of Christoffel symbols, we have:

X\left(\frac{\partial{K}}{\partial{v^{k}}}\right)=X(g_{lk}v^{l})=\frac{\partial{g_{lk}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}v^{i}v^{l}+g_{lk}({\rm F}^{l}-\Gamma_{ij}^{l}v^{i}v^{j})\ ,

hence

X\left(\frac{\partial{K}}{\partial{v^{k}}}\right)-\frac{\partial{K}}{\partial{q^{k}}}=\frac{\partial{g_{lk}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}v^{i}v^{l}+g_{lk}{\rm F}^{l}-g_{lk}\Gamma_{ij}^{l}v^{i}v^{j}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial{g_{ij}}}{\partial{q^{k}}}v^{i}v^{j}=g_{lk}{\rm F}^{l}\ .

So, we have obtained:

Proposition 5.27

Let
(Q,{\tt g},\omega)
be a Newtonian mechanical system,
(U,q^{i})
a local chart on
Q
and
(\tau_{Q}^{-1}(U),q^{i},v^{i})
the corresponding natural chart on
{\rm T}Q
.
Then there exists a unique vector field
X\in\mathfrak{X}(\tau_{Q}^{-1}(U))

which satisfies,

1.

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X)q^{k}=v^{k}
.

2.

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X)\left(\frac{\partial{K}}{\partial{v^{k}}}\right)=\frac{\partial{K}}{\partial{v^{k}}}+g_{ik}{\rm F}^{i}
.

Furthermore, its integral curves
\sigma\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q

are canonical lifts of curves

\gamma\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
to
{\rm T}Q
,
which are solution to the Newton equations of the system.

(
Proof
)
The existence and unicity of the vector field
X
are consequences of being

\displaystyle\left(q^{k},\frac{\partial{K}}{\partial{v^{k}}}\right)
a local chart on
{\rm T}Q
,
because the Riemannian metric
{\tt g}
is non-degenerate.
The properties of
X
have been proved in the previous discussion.

Furthermore, the integral curves of
X
are the lifts to
{\rm T}Q
of the solutions to the system of Euler–Lagrange equations,
and hence solutions to the Newton equations.

Velocity dependent forces

It is usual that the mechanical forces, or the work forms, depend
not only on the position coordinates, but also on the velocities.
This is the case of dissipative systems or electromagnetic, Lorentz, forces.
Geometrically, this means that

\omega\in{\Omega}^{1}(Q,\tau_{Q})
and
{\rm F}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q,\tau_{Q})
.
In this case the only change we need to do in the above paragraphs are the followings:

1.

The Newton equations change to

\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}={\rm F}\circ\dot{\gamma}\ ,

or in dual form,

\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}(\theta\circ\dot{\gamma})=\omega\circ\dot{\gamma}\ .

2.

The Euler–Lagrange equations are

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial{K}}{\partial{v^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}\right)-\frac{\partial{K}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}=(g_{ij}{\rm F}^{i})\circ\dot{\gamma}=(\omega_{j}\circ\dot{\gamma})\ .

If the force field of the system depends on the velocities, the work form

\omega
cannot be the exterior differential of a function defined on the configuration space
Q
.

As can be seen, the only changes consist in the substitution of

\gamma
by
\dot{\gamma}
, when we compose with
\omega
or with
{\rm F}
,
to take into account the new domain of definition.

5.2.3
Conservative systems:
Mechanical Lagrangians and Euler–Lagrange equations

In particular, we are interested in a special kind of Newtonian systems:
those which are of conservative or mechanical Lagrangian type, called
simple mechanical systems
.

Definition 5.28

A Newtonian mechanical system
(Q,{\tt g},\omega)
is
conservative
if the work form is exact; that is, there exists
V\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(Q)
such that
\omega=-{\rm d}V\,

3
3

3

The negative sign is a customary tradition in Physics
in order to identify the potential function of the field with the potential energy of the system.

.
In this case, the function
V
is called the
potential energy
of the system and the force vector field is
{\rm F}=-{\rm grad}\ V
.

Thus, for these systems, we define:

Definition 5.29

In a conservative Newtonian mechanical system
(Q,{\tt g},\omega)
, with
\omega=-{\rm d}V\,
,
the
total energy
or
mechanical energy
of the system
is the function
E\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)
defined as

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}E&\colon&{\rm T}Q&\longrightarrow&\mathbb{R}\\
&&(q,v)&\mapsto&K(q,v)+(\tau^{*}_{Q}V)(q,v)\end{array}

(To simplify notation we usually write
E=K+V
).

As a direct consequence of the definition, we have:

Theorem 5.30

(Mechanical energy conservation)
:
Let
(Q,{\tt g},\omega)
be a conservative Newtonian mechanical system, then
the mechanical energy
E

is invariant (“constant”) along the trajectories of the system.

(
Proof
)
If
\gamma\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
is a solution to the Newton equations,

\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}={\rm F}\circ\gamma\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm F}){\tt g}=\omega=-{\rm d}V\ ,

(5.5)

then we have that

\displaystyle\frac{d(E\circ\dot{\gamma})}{dt}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}(E\circ\dot{\gamma})=\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\left(\frac{1}{2}{\tt g}(\dot{\gamma},\dot{\gamma})+V\circ\gamma\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\tt g}(\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma},\dot{\gamma})+\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}(V\circ\gamma)={\tt g}(\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma},\dot{\gamma})+{\rm d}V(\dot{\gamma})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\tt g}({\rm F},\dot{\gamma})+{\rm d}V(\dot{\gamma})=\omega(\dot{\gamma})+{\rm d}V(\dot{\gamma})=0\ .

Proposition 5.31

Let
(Q,{\tt g},\omega)
be a conservative Newtonian mechanical system with
\omega=-{\rm d}V
and
let
\gamma\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
be a curve with image on the domain
U\subset Q
of a local chart

(U,\varphi=(q^{i}))
of
Q
.
Then the Euler–Lagrange equations (
5.4
) are

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}\right)-\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}=0\ ,

(5.6)

that is, the Euler–Lagrange equations for the Lagrangian function
{\cal L}=K-\tau_{Q}^{*}V
.

(
Proof
)
In fact we have that

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial{K}}{\partial{v^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}\right)-\frac{\partial{K}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}=(-{\rm d}V\circ\gamma)\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\right)=-\frac{\partial{(\tau_{Q}^{*}V)}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\circ\gamma\ ,

and recalling that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{(\tau_{Q}^{*}V)}}{\partial{v^{j}}}=0
, we can write

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial{(K-\tau_{Q}^{*}V)}}{\partial{v^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}\right)-\frac{\partial{(K-\tau_{Q}^{*}V)}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}=0\ .

Definition 5.32

The function
{\cal L}:=K-\tau_{Q}^{*}V
is said to be a
Lagrangian function of mechanical type

or a
mechanical Lagrangian function
,
and equations (
5.6
) are the
Euler–Lagrange equations of the first class
of the conservative Newtonian system.

From now on, we commit an abuse of notation and we write simply
{\cal L}=K-V
.

Remark 5.33

•

For conservative Newtonian mechanical systems, it is immediate to prove that the vector field
X\in\mathfrak{X}(\tau_{Q}^{-1}(U))
of Proposition
5.27

satisfies the condition

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(X)\left(\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{k}}}\right)=\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{k}}}\ ,

(instead of the condition 2 in the aforementioned proposition).

•

Observe that the Lagrangian functions
of mechanical type

{\cal L}=K-V\equiv\frac{1}{2}g_{ij}(q)v^{i}v^{j}-V(q)

are regular because

\Omega_{\cal L}\equiv g_{ij}(q){\rm d}q^{i}\wedge{\rm d}v^{j}\ .

5.2.4
Coupled systems (systems in interaction)

Next we study the case of Newtonian mechanical systems which are
made of several dynamical systems in interaction; that is,
the so-called
coupled systems
.

Let
(Q_{1},{\tt g}_{1},\omega_{1})
,
(Q_{2},{\tt g}_{2},\omega_{2})
be two Newtonian mechanical systems and
{\rm F}_{1}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q_{1})
,
{\rm F}_{2}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q_{2})
the corresponding force fields. The dynamical equations for both systems are

\nabla^{1}_{\dot{\gamma}_{1}}\dot{\gamma}_{1}={\rm F}_{1}\circ\gamma_{1}\hskip 10.00002pt;\hskip 10.00002pt\nabla^{2}_{\dot{\gamma}_{2}}\dot{\gamma}_{2}={\rm F}_{2}\circ\gamma_{2}

(5.7)

(with
\gamma_{1}\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q_{1}
,
\gamma_{2}\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q_{2}
).
They constitute a non-coupled, separated, set of ordinary differential equations.
Together, they are a system of non-coupled ordinary differential equations.

Consider the system
(Q,{\tt g},\omega)
, where:

•

Q=Q_{1}\times Q_{2}
; with
\pi_{1}\colon Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q_{1}
,
\pi_{2}\colon Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q_{2}
.

•

{\tt g}={\tt g}_{1}\oplus{\tt g}_{2}
.

•

\omega=\pi_{1}^{*}\omega_{1}+\pi^{*}_{2}\omega_{2}
.

We can see that
\nabla=\nabla^{1}\oplus\nabla^{2}

is the Levi-Civita connection of the Riemannian metric
{\tt g}
, defined as follows:
if
\gamma\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q

is given by
\gamma=(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})
, then

\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}=\nabla^{1}_{\dot{\gamma}_{1}}\dot{\gamma}_{1}+\nabla^{2}_{\dot{\gamma}_{2}}\dot{\gamma}_{2}\ .

Moreover, from the expression of
\omega
we obtain that the associated force field
{\rm F}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
is

{\rm F}(q)=((q_{1},q_{2}),{\rm F}_{1}(q_{1}),{\rm F}_{2}(q_{2}))\ ,

with
q\equiv(q_{1},q_{2})\in Q
.
Then the dynamical equation of the system is

\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}={\rm F}\circ\gamma\ ,

which is equivalent to the non-coupled system (
5.7
).
From the physical point of view, this situation is a model of two

joint mechanical systems
but
without interaction
.
With this is mind we have:

Definition 5.34

We say that
N
Newtonian systems
(Q_{\mu},g_{\mu},{\rm F}_{\mu})
, with

\mu=1,\ldots,N
, are
coupled
(or
in interaction
) if

{\rm F}_{\mu}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q_{\mu},\pi_{\mu})
, that is,
we have the following commutative diagram for the force field acting on the system

\begin{array}[]{ccc}&&{\rm T}Q_{\mu}\\
&\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(7.0,20.0){\mbox{${\rm F}_{\mu}$}}\put(0.0,0.0){\vector{4}{3}{40.0}}\end{picture}&\Bigg\downarrow\tau_{\mu}\\
Q=\prod_{\mu=1}^{n}Q_{\mu}&\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(20.0,5.0){\mbox{$\pi_{\mu}$}}\put(0.0,0.0){\vector{1}{0}{40.0}}\end{picture}&Q_{\mu}\end{array}

In this case, if
N=2
, we have a system of differential equation as in (
5.7
) but now it is coupled; that is, we have:

\nabla^{1}_{\dot{\gamma}_{1}}\dot{\gamma}_{1}={\rm F}_{1}\circ\gamma={\rm F}_{1}\circ(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\hskip 10.00002pt;\hskip 10.00002pt\nabla^{2}_{\dot{\gamma}_{2}}\dot{\gamma}_{2}={\rm F}_{2}\circ\gamma={\rm F}_{1}\circ(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})

We want to formulate this system as a single Newtonian mechanical system, so we need the following result:

Lemma 5.35

Let
M_{1},M_{2}
be manifolds,
M=M_{1}\times M_{2}
and
\pi_{i}\colon M\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>M_{i}
(
i=1,2
) the natural projections. Then

1.

\mathfrak{X}(M)
is canonically isomorphic to

\mathfrak{X}(M_{1},\pi_{1})\times\mathfrak{X}(M_{2},\pi_{2})
(as
{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
-módules).

2.

{\Omega}^{1}(M)
is canonically isomorphic to

{\Omega}^{1}(M_{1},\pi_{1})\times{\Omega}^{1}(M_{2},\pi_{2})
(as
{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
-módules).

(
Proof
)
Recall that if
q\equiv(q_{1},q_{2})\in M
, we have the isomorphism

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\alpha_{q}&\colon&{\rm T}_{q}M&\longrightarrow&{\rm T}_{q_{1}}M_{1}\times{\rm T}_{q_{2}}M_{2}\\
&&v&\mapsto&({\rm T}_{q}\pi_{1}(v),{\rm T}_{q}\pi_{2}(v))\ .\end{array}

Hence we obtain the sequence

\mathfrak{X}(M)\mathrel{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits^{\phi}}\mathfrak{X}(M_{1},\pi_{1})\times\mathfrak{X}(M_{2},\pi_{2})\mathrel{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits^{\psi}}\mathfrak{X}(M)\ ,

where the maps are defined as

\displaystyle\phi(X)(q)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\alpha_{q}(X(q))\ ,

\displaystyle\psi(X_{1},X_{2})(q)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\alpha_{q}^{-1}(X_{1}(q),X_{2}(q))\ .

If
\rho_{i}\colon\mathfrak{X}(M_{1},\pi_{1})\times\mathfrak{X}(M_{2},\pi_{2})\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathfrak{X}(M_{i},\pi_{i})
(
i=1,2
)
are the natural projections, we have that:

1.

The map
\phi
is well-defined and
\phi(X)
is differentiable:

It is enough to observe that
\rho_{i}(\phi(X))
is differentiable.
To obtain this consider
f\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M_{i})
, we have that

((\rho_{i}(\phi(X)))f)(q)=\rho_{i}(\alpha_{p}(X(q)))f={\rm T}_{q}\pi_{i}(X(q))f=X(q)(f\circ\pi_{i})=(X(f)\circ\pi_{i}))(q)\ ,

which depends differentially on
q\in M
.

2.

The map
\phi
is injective, because the maps
\alpha_{q}
are isomorphisms.

3.

The map
\phi
is a
{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
-módules isomorphism as can be seen directly.

4.

\psi\circ\phi={\rm Id}_{\mathfrak{X}(M)}
;
in fact, if
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
and
p\in M
, we have that

((\psi\circ\phi)(X))(q)=\psi(\phi(X))(q)=\alpha_{p}^{-1}(\phi(X)(q))=\alpha_{q}^{-1}(\alpha_{p}(X(q)))=X(q)\ .

The statement (2) is a direct consequence of (1).

From this Lemma we obtain:

Theorem 5.36

Two Newtonian mechanical systems in interaction,

(Q_{\mu},g_{\mu},{\rm F}_{\mu})
,
\mu=1,2
, are equivalent to a unique Newtonian mechanical system

(Q,{\tt g},{\rm F})
, where

1.

Q=Q_{1}\times Q_{2}
; with
\pi_{1}\colon Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q_{1}
,
\pi_{2}\colon Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q_{2}
.

2.

{\tt g}_{1}\oplus{\tt g}_{2}
.

3.

\omega=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})
and
{\rm F}=({\rm F}_{1},{\rm F}_{2})
.

(we have identified

\mathfrak{X}(M)
with
\mathfrak{X}(M_{1},\pi_{1})\times\mathfrak{X}(M_{2},\pi_{2})
, and

{\Omega}^{1}(Q)
with
{\Omega}^{1}(Q_{1},\pi_{1})\times{\Omega}^{1}(Q_{2},\pi_{2})
,
in agreement with the above Lemma)

4
4

4

The meaning of this “equivalence” is the following:
if
\gamma\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
is a solution to the dynamical equation of
(Q,{\tt g},{\rm F})
, and
\gamma=(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})
,
then
\gamma_{\mu}\colon\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q_{\mu}
, (
\mu=1,2
),
is a solution to the coupled dynamical equations of both systems
(Q_{\mu},g_{\mu},{\rm F}_{\mu})
;
and conversely.

.

(
Proof
)
The metric
{\tt g}={\tt g}_{1}\oplus{\tt g}_{2}
is Riemannian on
Q
. Its Levi-Civita connection
\nabla
is
\nabla_{1}\oplus\nabla_{2}
;
then the dynamical equation associated to this Newtonian system can be split into the two components on
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
,
according to the dynamical equations of the two different systems in interaction
Q_{1}
,
Q_{2}
; hence we obtain the result.

Observe that

\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm F}){\tt g}=\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm F}_{1},{\rm F}_{2})({\tt g}_{1}\oplus{\tt g}_{2})=\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm F}_{1}){\tt g}_{1}+\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm F}_{2}){\tt g}_{2}=\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\ ,

and the dynamical equation
\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}={\rm F}\circ\gamma

splits in two different ones:

\nabla^{1}_{\dot{\gamma}_{1}}\dot{\gamma}_{1}={\rm F}_{1}\circ\gamma={\rm F}_{1}\circ(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\hskip 10.00002pt;\hskip 10.00002pt\nabla^{2}_{\dot{\gamma}_{2}}\dot{\gamma}_{2}={\rm F}_{2}\circ\gamma={\rm F}_{1}\circ(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})

Remark 5.37

When studying a physical complex system, it is usual that we first consider the two component systems as independent; that is,
without interaction,
(Q_{1},{\tt g}_{1},{\rm F}_{1})
,
(Q_{2},{\tt g}_{2},{\rm F}_{2})
, and in a second approach we introduce the interaction

{\rm F}^{int}=({\rm F}^{int}_{1},{\rm F}^{int}_{2})
; that is, the force fields depending on the positions of both systems, then we obtain the system
(Q_{1}\times Q_{2},{\tt g}_{1}\oplus{\tt g}_{2},{\rm F}_{1}+{\rm F}^{int}_{1},{\rm F}_{2}+{\rm F}^{int}_{2})
.

Corollary 5.38

A collection of
n
coupled Newtonian mechanical systems
(Q_{\mu},g_{\mu},{\rm F}_{\mu})
,
\mu=1,\ldots n
, is “equivalent” to a unique Newtonian mechanical system
(Q,{\tt g},{\rm F})
, where

1.

Q=Q_{1}\times\ldots\times Q_{N}
; with
\pi_{\mu}\colon Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q_{\mu}
.

2.

{\tt g}={\tt g}_{1}\oplus\ldots{\tt g}_{N}
.

3.

\omega=(\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{N})
and
{\rm F}=({\rm F}_{1},\ldots,{\rm F}_{N})
.

(observe that we have identified

\mathfrak{X}(M)
with
\displaystyle\prod_{\mu=1}^{N}\mathfrak{X}(M_{\mu},\pi_{\mu})
, and

{\Omega}^{1}(Q)
with
\displaystyle\prod_{\mu=1}^{N}{\Omega}^{1}(M_{\mu},\pi_{\mu})

in agreement with the above Lemma).

The system
(Q,{\tt g},{\rm F})
is a Newtonian mechanical system composed by different systems with interaction.

Local coordinate expressions
:
To write the dynamical equation in a local chart, consider the curve
\gamma\equiv({\gamma}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma}_{n})

(where
\gamma_{\mu}
is the component of
\gamma
on the manifold
Q_{\mu}
). The dynamical equation is written as

\nabla^{\mu}_{\dot{\gamma}_{\mu}}\dot{\gamma}_{\mu}={\rm F}_{\mu}\circ\gamma\ .

To obtain the local expression of
\nabla
, let

(U_{\mu},q_{\mu}^{i})
be local charts on
Q_{\mu}
.
We have that
(U,q_{\mu}^{j})
, with
U=U_{1}\times\ldots\times U_{N}

is a local chart on
Q
, and if
\{(\Gamma_{\mu})_{jk}^{i}\}

are the Christoffel symbols of
\nabla_{\mu}
,
we have that

\nabla_{\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{j}_{\mu}}}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{k}_{\mu}}}=(\Gamma_{\mu})_{jk}^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q_{\mu}^{i}}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\nabla_{\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{j}_{\mu}}}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{k}_{\nu}}}=0\hskip 20.00003pt(\mu\not=\nu)\ ,

because
\nabla
is the Levi-Civita connection. From these expressions we have that

\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}=\sum_{\mu=1}^{N}\left(\ddot{\gamma}_{\mu}^{j}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{j}_{\mu}}}+(\Gamma_{\mu})_{jk}^{i}\dot{\gamma}_{\mu}^{j}\dot{\gamma}_{\mu}^{k}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}_{\mu}}}\right)={\rm F}\circ\gamma\ ,

and, for every
\mu=1,\ldots,N
,

\ddot{\gamma}_{\mu}^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}_{\mu}}}+(\Gamma_{\mu})_{jk}^{i}\dot{\gamma}_{\mu}^{j}\dot{\gamma}_{\mu}^{k}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}_{\mu}}}={\rm F}^{i}_{\mu}\circ\gamma\ .

Hence, if
\dim\,Q_{\mu}=N_{\mu}
, for every
\mu=1,\ldots,N

and every
i=1,\ldots,N_{\mu}
, we have

\ddot{\gamma}_{\mu}^{i}+(\Gamma_{\mu})_{jk}^{i}\dot{\gamma}_{\mu}^{j}\dot{\gamma}_{\mu}^{k}={\rm F}_{\mu}^{i}\circ\gamma\ .

To write the Euler–Lagrange equations of the system,
we need to obtain the associated natural chart
(\tau^{-1}_{\mu}(U),q_{\mu}^{j},v_{\mu}^{j})

in
{\rm T}Q
and we have

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial{K}}{\partial{v^{j}_{\mu}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}\right)-\frac{\partial{K}}{\partial{q^{j}_{\mu}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}=(g_{\mu})_{jk}{\rm F}_{\mu}^{k}\ ,

where the kinetic energy function is
\displaystyle K=\sum_{\mu=1}^{N}K_{\mu}
,
because
{\tt g}={\tt g}_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus{\tt g}_{N}
. Hence, taking into account that

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial{K_{\nu}}}{\partial{v^{j}_{\mu}}}\right)=0\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{\partial{K_{\nu}}}{\partial{q^{j}_{\mu}}}=0\hskip 10.00002pt;\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{if $\mu\not=\nu$}\ ,

we write

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial{K_{\mu}}}{\partial{v^{j}_{\mu}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}\right)-\frac{\partial{K_{\mu}}}{\partial{q^{j}_{\mu}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}=(g_{\mu})_{jk}{\rm F}_{\mu}^{k}\ .

Moreover, if the system is conservative, then
\omega=-{\rm d}V
(with
V\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(Q)
);
that is,

\omega=-\frac{\partial{V}}{\partial{q^{j}_{\mu}}}{\rm d}q^{j}_{\mu}\ .

If we consider the Lagrangian function of the system
{\cal L}=K-V
,
with local expression

{\cal L}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mu=1}^{N}(g_{\mu})_{jk}v^{j}_{\mu}v^{k}_{\mu}-V=\sum_{\mu=1}^{N}K_{\mu}-V\ ,

we have

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{j}_{\mu}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}\right)-\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{j}_{\mu}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}=0\ .

Finally, if the force fields depend on the velocities, the expression would be the same writing
{\rm F}\circ\dot{\gamma}
instead of
{\rm F}\circ\gamma
in every equation. In this case

{\rm F}_{\mu}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q_{\mu},\pi_{\mu}\circ\tau_{\mu})
, where

\tau_{\mu}\colon{\rm T}Q_{\mu}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q_{\mu}
are the natural projections.

5.3
Systems with holonomic and nonholonomic constraints

Another relevant topic is the study of dynamical systems in classical mechanics
with holonomic or nonholonomic constraints.
Nonholonomic systems are those subjected to constraints depending on positions and velocities, and
they have been thoroughly studied
(see, for instance,

[
11
,
34
,
100
,
127
,
131
,
159
,
165
,
248
,
273
,
282
,
344
]
).

5.3.1
Holonomic constraints. Holonomic d’Alembert Principle

Let
(Q,{\tt g},\omega)
be a Newtonian mechanical system and
{\rm F}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)

its force field. Let
S
be a submanifold of
Q

(usually called
submanifold of holonomic constraints
) and

j_{S}\colon S\hookrightarrow Q
the natural embedding.
The problem we study consists in describing the dynamics of the system when it is forced to evolve on the submanifold
S
.
To force this behaviour, first it is compulsory to apply a new force field
{\rm R}
, called
constraint force
, which obliges the system to remain on
S
.
In general, this force depends, not only on the position, but also on the velocity;
then
{\rm R}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q,\tau_{Q})
and, moreover, it is a new unknown to find.

Then we have a dynamical equation for curves

\gamma\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>S
, which is

\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}={\rm F}\circ\gamma+{\rm R}\circ\dot{\gamma}\ .

(5.8)

To solve this problem, we introduce the following:

Assumption 5.39

(d’Alembert Principle
):
The constraint force
{\rm R}
is orthogonal to the submanifold
S
;
that is, for every
q\in S
and for every
u,v\in{\rm T}_{q}S
, we have
{\tt g}(u,{\rm R}(q,v))=0
.

The question is how to solve the problem and obtain some information about the constraint force.
Let
{\tt g}_{S}:=j_{S}^{*}{\tt g}
. It is obvious that
(S,{\tt g}_{S})
is a Riemannian manifold.
Let
\nabla^{S}
