be the Levi-Civita connection associated to
{\tt g}_{S}
.
We have the following natural orthogonal splitting

{\rm T}_{q}Q={\rm T}_{q}S\oplus({\rm T}_{q}S)^{\perp}\hskip 20.00003pt(\forall q\in S\subset Q)\ ,

and the projections

\pi_{S}(q)\colon{\rm T}_{q}Q\rightarrow{\rm T}_{q}S\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\pi^{\perp}_{S}(q)\colon{\rm T}_{q}Q\rightarrow({\rm T}_{q}S)^{\perp}\ ,

and, as they are defined at every point, we have

\pi_{S}\colon{\rm T}Q|_{S}\rightarrow{\rm T}S\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\pi^{\perp}_{S}\colon{\rm T}Q|_{S}\rightarrow{\rm T}S^{\perp}\ .

Then d’Alembert’s Principle reduces to
\pi_{S}\circ{\rm R}=0
.
Hence, we have that:

Proposition 5.40

\nabla^{S}=\pi_{S}\circ\nabla
.

(
Proof
)
It is a direct computation to prove that, on the vector fields tangent to
S
, the map
\pi_{S}\circ\nabla
is a connection on
S
and it is symmetrical.
Furthermore, for every
X,Y,Z\in\mathfrak{X}(S)
, we have that

(\pi_{S}\circ\nabla_{Z}){\tt g}(X,Y)={\tt g}((\pi_{S}\circ\nabla_{Z})X,Y)+{\tt g}(X,(\pi_{S}\circ\nabla_{Z})Y)\ ,

and hence
\pi_{S}\circ\nabla
is the Levi-Civita connection of
{\tt g}_{S}
.

Now, if we take the dynamical equation (
5.8
) and we split it into the tangent and orthogonal components to
S
, we obtain

\displaystyle\pi_{S}(\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi_{S}\circ{\rm F}\circ\gamma+\pi_{S}\circ{\rm R}\circ\dot{\gamma}=\pi_{S}\circ{\rm F}\circ\gamma\ ,

(5.9)

\displaystyle\pi_{S}^{\perp}(\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi_{S}^{\perp}\circ{\rm F}\circ\gamma+\pi_{S}^{\perp}\circ{\rm R}\circ\dot{\gamma}=\pi_{S}^{\perp}\circ{\rm F}\circ\gamma+{\rm R}\circ\dot{\gamma}\ .

(5.10)

Denoting
{\rm F}^{S}:=\pi_{S}\circ{\rm F}\in\mathfrak{X}(S)
, the projection of

{\rm F}
on
S
, then equation (
5.9
) is

\nabla^{S}_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}={\rm F}^{S}\circ\gamma\ ;

(5.11)

and this is the dynamical equation of the Newtonian mechanical system

(S,{\tt g}_{S},\omega_{S})
, where
\omega_{S}=\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm F}^{S}){\tt g}_{S}
.

The solutions to equation (
5.11
) are curves

\gamma\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>S
such that, if we introduce them into equation
(
5.10
), it allows us to calculate the constraint force
{\rm R}

for that trajectory, obtaining

\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}-\nabla^{S}_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}={\rm F}\circ\gamma-{\rm F}^{S}\circ\gamma+{\rm R}\circ\dot{\gamma}\ .

Then
{\rm R}\circ\dot{\gamma}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q,\dot{\gamma})
.
Observe that we can calculate the constraint force only for each trajectory of the system, but not as a vector field depending on the velocities.

We also have:

Proposition 5.41

\omega_{S}=j_{S}^{*}\omega
.

(
Proof
)
If
q\in S
and
v\in{\rm T}_{q}S
we have that

\displaystyle\omega_{S}(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm F}^{S}){\tt g}_{S})(v)={\tt g}_{S}({\rm F}^{S},v)={\tt g}({\rm F}^{S},v)={\tt g}({\rm F},v)\ ,

\displaystyle(j_{S}^{*}\omega)(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(j_{S}^{*}\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm F})g)(v)={\tt g}({\rm F},v)\ ,

and the result follows.

We have obtained that the dynamical system describing the motion of the systems
(Q,{\tt g},\omega)
constrained to move on the submanifold
S
, is the Newtonian mechanical system
(S,j_{S}^{*}{\tt g},j_{S}^{*}\omega)
.

D’Alembert’s Principle tells us that the constraint force
{\rm R}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q,\tau_{Q})
, the force that obliges the system to move on the submanifold
S
, is orthogonal to
S
, but we can set this principle in a dual way:

Proposition 5.42

(Dual d’Alembert Principle)
:
Let
\rho=\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm R}){\tt g}
. Then
j_{S}^{*}\rho=0
.

(
Proof
)
Let
q\in S
and
u,v\in{\rm T}_{q}S
, then

(j_{S}^{*}\rho_{(q,v)})(u)={\tt g}_{S}({\rm R}(q,v),u))=0\ .

Consider now the linear momentum form of the constrained system,

\theta_{S}\colon{\rm T}S\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}^{*}S
. The equation of motion using
\theta_{S}
is,

\nabla^{S}_{\dot{\gamma}}(\theta_{S}\circ\dot{\gamma})=\omega_{S}\circ\gamma

and as
\omega_{S}=j_{S}^{*}\omega
, we have

\nabla^{S}_{\dot{\gamma}}(\theta_{S}\circ\dot{\gamma})=j_{S}^{*}\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}(\theta\circ\dot{\gamma})\ .

Next, we discuss some particular cases:

Systems with one constraint

Let
S=\{q\in Q\ ;\ \varphi(q)=0\}
, with
\varphi\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(Q)

and suppose that
{\rm d}\varphi(q)=\hskip-9.95845pt/\hskip 5.69054pt0
, for every
q\in S
. This implies that
S
is a submanifold of
Q
.
Let
X\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
such that
\mathop{i}\nolimits(X){\tt g}={\rm d}\varphi
, then

X
is orthogonal to
S
. Hence

\pi^{\perp}_{S}({\rm F})=\frac{{\tt g}({\rm F},X)}{{\tt g}(X,X)}X=\frac{{\rm d}\varphi({\rm F})}{\|{\rm d}\varphi\|^{2}}X\ ,

and we have that

{\rm F}^{S}=\pi_{S}({\rm F})={\rm F}-\frac{{\rm d}\varphi({\rm F})}{\|{\rm d}\varphi\|^{2}}X\ .

As a consequence,

\omega_{S}=\omega-\frac{{\rm d}\varphi({\rm F})}{\|{\rm d}\varphi\|^{2}}{\rm d}\varphi\ ,

and this allows us to find the trajectories of the system as solutions to the differential equation

\nabla^{S}_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}={\rm F}\circ\gamma-\frac{{\rm d}\varphi({\rm F})}{\|{\rm d}\varphi\|^{2}}X\ .

We have that the constraint force along the solution
\gamma
is given by the equation

\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}-\nabla^{S}_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}=\frac{{\rm d}\varphi({\rm F})}{\|{\rm d}\varphi\|^{2}}\circ\gamma-{\rm R}\circ\dot{\gamma}\ ,

where the only unknown is
{\rm R}\circ\dot{\gamma}
.

Systems with several constraints

Consider now

S=\{q\in Q\ ;\ \varphi_{1}(q)=0,\ldots,\varphi_{h}(q)=0\}
,
with
\varphi_{1}\ldots,\varphi_{h}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(Q)
, such that

{\rm d}\varphi_{1}(q),\ldots{\rm d}\varphi_{h}(q)
are linearly independent at every point
q\in S
(we assume that
S
is not empty).
Let
{Z}_{1},\ldots,{Z}_{n-h}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
such that:

1.

\mathop{i}\nolimits(Z_{i}){\rm d}\varphi_{j}=0
.

2.

{\tt g}(Z_{i},Z_{j})=0
;
i\not=j
.

To obtain these vector fields
Z_{i}
, it is enough to take vector fields

{X}_{1},\ldots,{X}_{n-h}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
satisfying the first condition (a linear equation) and apply the well known
Gram–Schmidt method
.
In this situation, we have

\pi_{S}({\rm F})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{{\tt g}({\rm F},Z_{i})}{{\tt g}(Z_{i},Z_{i})}Z_{i}\ ;

hence, as in the previous case, we obtain the dynamical equation and the expression of the constraint force along every trajectory.

5.3.2
Euler–Lagrange equations

In the above paragraph, we have studied the dynamics of a Newtonian mechanical system
(Q,{\tt g},\omega)

constrained to move on the submanifold
j_{S}\colon S\hookrightarrow Q
. We have proved that its dynamics is given by the Newtonian mechanical system
(S,{\tt g}_{S},\omega_{S})
.
To write the corresponding Euler–Lagrange equation of this last system, take a local chart
(U,q^{i})
on
S
and the corresponding natural lift
(\tau_{Q}^{-1}(U),q^{i},v^{i})

to
{\rm T}S
. Then we have

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial{K_{S}}}{\partial{v^{k}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}\right)-\frac{\partial{K_{S}}}{\partial{q^{k}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}=({\tt g}_{S})_{ik}{(\rm F^{S})}^{i}\ ,

(5.12)

where
K_{S}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}S)
is the
kinetic energy
of the system, defined by

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}K_{S}&\colon&{\rm T}S&\longrightarrow&\mathbb{R}\\
&&(q,v)&\mapsto&\frac{1}{2}{\tt g}_{S}(v,v)\end{array}.

Then we have that:

Proposition 5.43

K_{S}=({\rm T}j_{S})^{*}K
.

(
Proof
)
If
q\in S
and
v\in{\rm T}_{q}S
, then

K_{S}(q,v)=\frac{1}{2}({\tt g}_{S})_{ik}(q)v^{i}v^{k}=\frac{1}{2}g_{ik}(q)v^{i}v^{k}

and the result follows.

Thus, equation (
5.12
) is

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial{({\rm T}j_{S})^{*}K}}{\partial{v^{k}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}\right)-\frac{\partial{({\rm T}j_{S})^{*}K}}{\partial{q^{k}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}=(\omega_{S})_{k}\circ\gamma=(j_{S}^{*}\omega)_{k}\circ\gamma\ .

In the case that the dynamical system is conservative, that is
\omega=-{\rm d}V
,
then

\omega_{S}=j_{S}^{*}\omega=-j_{S}^{*}{\rm d}V=-{\rm d}j_{S}^{*}V\ ,

and the above equation takes the expression

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial{({\rm T}j_{S})^{*}K}}{\partial{v^{j}_{S}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}\right)-\frac{\partial{({\rm T}j_{S})^{*}K}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}=-\frac{\partial{j_{S}^{*}V}}{\partial{q^{k}}}\circ\gamma\ ,

or, equivalently,

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial{{\cal L}_{S}}}{\partial{v^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}\right)-\frac{\partial{{\cal L}_{S}}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}=0\ ,

where
{\cal L}_{S}:=({\rm T}j_{S})^{*}{\cal L}
.
Observe that the constraint force is not in these equations. This was one of the innovations developed by Lagrange.

If
(W,x^{i})
is a local chart on
Q
, and
(U,q^{i})
is another on
S
,
both adapted to the map
j\colon W\hookrightarrow U
, then we have the local expression

x^{i}=f^{i}(q)
, and hence

\displaystyle\dot{x}^{i}=\frac{\partial{f^{i}}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\dot{q}^{j}
.
This shows that it is enough to know the Lagrangian function
{\cal L}
of the unconstrained system, to introduce the expression of
x^{i},\dot{x}^{i}
using the above local expressions and, by direct derivation, to obtain the Euler–Lagrange equations of the constrained system.

5.3.3
Some examples of Newtonian mechanical systems

In the following examples the question is to identify the elements defining the different systems; that is,
the
configuration manifold
, the
Riemannian metric
and the
force field
or the
work form
.

Unconstrained particle in
\mathbb{R}^{3}

Consider a particle with mass
{\rm m}
moving on an open set

Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and subjected to a force defined by a vector field

{\rm F}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
.

The
geometric metric

{\tt g}
is the original metric on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
Consider, instead, the Riemannian metric given by
\widetilde{\tt g}:={\rm m}{\tt g}
.
Observe that the corresponding Levi-Civita connections
\nabla
and
\widetilde{\nabla}
for both metrics
{\tt g}
y
\widetilde{\tt g}

are the same: it is enough to calculate their Christoffel symbols in any local chart, or recall the calculus of
\nabla_{X}Y
for the Levi-Civita connection.
Let
\displaystyle\widetilde{\rm F}:=\frac{{\rm F}}{{\rm m}}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
,
and consider the Newtonian mechanical system
(Q,\widetilde{\tt g},\widetilde{\rm F})
.
The Newton equation is

\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}=\widetilde{\rm F}\circ\gamma=\frac{{\rm F}}{{\rm m}}\circ\gamma\ ,

which, as it is usual in mechanics, can be written as

{\rm m}\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}={\rm F}\circ\gamma\ .

Observe that the work form is given by

\omega=\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{\rm F})\widetilde{\tt g}=\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm F}){\tt g}\ ,

and the linear momentum form is

\widetilde{\theta}\circ\dot{\gamma}=\mathop{i}\nolimits(\dot{\gamma})\widetilde{\tt g}\ ,

whose local expression is

\widetilde{\theta}\circ\dot{\gamma}={\rm m}g_{ij}\dot{\gamma}^{i}{\rm d}q^{j}\circ\gamma\ .

Particle constrained to a surface of
\mathbb{R}^{3}

Consider now a particle with mass
{\rm m}
on an open set

Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We have seen above that the Newtonian system describing this situation is given by
(Q,\widetilde{\tt g},\widetilde{\rm F})
.
If
j_{S}\colon S\hookrightarrow Q
is a regular surface and the particle is constrained to move on it, then the associated Newtonian system is
(S,\widetilde{\tt g}_{S},\widetilde{\rm F}^{S})
, where

\widetilde{\tt g}_{S}=j_{S}^{*}\widetilde{\tt g}
and
\widetilde{\rm F}^{S}=\pi_{S}\circ\widetilde{\rm F}
.
Hence, the dynamical equation is

\nabla^{S}_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}=\widetilde{\rm F}^{S}\circ\gamma\ .

If we know one solution
\gamma
, then the constraint force along
\gamma
is given by the equation

\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}-\nabla^{S}_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}=\widetilde{\rm F}\circ\gamma-\widetilde{\rm F}^{S}\circ\gamma+\widetilde{\rm R}\circ\dot{\gamma}\ ,

from where we can obtain
\widetilde{\rm R}\circ\dot{\gamma}
.
Recall that
{\rm R}={\rm m}\widetilde{\rm R}
.

We can write the dual formulation using the work form

\widetilde{\omega}=\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm F}){\tt g}=\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{\rm F})\widetilde{\tt g}\ .

with
\widetilde{\omega}_{S}=j_{S}^{*}\widetilde{\omega}
.
Hence, we have that the dynamical equation is

\nabla^{S}_{\dot{\gamma}}(\widetilde{\theta}_{S}\circ\dot{\gamma})=\widetilde{\omega}_{S}\circ\gamma\ ,

with
\widetilde{\theta}_{S}\circ\dot{\gamma}=\mathop{i}\nolimits(\dot{\gamma})\widetilde{\tt g}_{S}
,
and
\widetilde{\theta}_{S}=({\rm T}j_{S})^{*}\widetilde{\theta}
.

Systems of particles in
\mathbb{R}^{3}

Consider now a system made of
N
particles, denoted
{P}_{1},\ldots,{P}_{N}
,
with masses
{{\rm m}}_{1},\ldots,{{\rm m}}_{N}
. We suppose that the particle
P_{\mu}
moves in
Q_{\mu}
, open set of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, equipped with the usual metric
{\tt g}_{\mu}
and the associated dynamical metric
\widetilde{\tt g}_{\mu}:={\rm m}_{\mu}{\tt g}_{\mu}
.

If the force field acting on every particle is

{\rm F}_{\mu}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q_{\mu})
, that is
{\rm F}_{\mu}
depends only on the position of the corresponding particle;
then we have
N
uncoupled mechanical systems, and their dynamical equation can be solved separately.

But, if
\pi_{\mu}\colon\prod_{\nu=1}^{N}Q_{\nu}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q_{\mu}
,
and the forces on the system are a family of vector fields
{\rm F}_{\mu}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q_{\mu},\pi_{\mu})
;
that is, the force
{\rm F}_{\mu}
on the particle
P_{\mu}
depends on the position of the other particles, then the
N
particles are in interaction and,
as we have shown in Section
5.2.4
, the
N
systems

(Q_{\mu},\widetilde{\tt g}_{\mu},\widetilde{\rm F}_{\mu})
are equivalent to a unique Newtonian mechanical system
(Q,\widetilde{\tt g},\widetilde{\rm F})
with

1.

\displaystyle Q=\prod_{\mu=1}^{N}Q_{\mu}
,

2.

\displaystyle\widetilde{\tt g}=\oplus_{\mu=1}^{N}\widetilde{\tt g}_{\mu}
,

3.

\omega=(\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{N})
and

\widetilde{\rm F}=(\widetilde{\rm F}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\rm F}_{N})
,

and the dynamical equation is the corresponding to this last system.

Systems of particles on a submanifold

In this case, we have a system of
N
particles
{P}_{1},\ldots,{P}_{N}
,
with masses
{{\rm m}}_{1},\ldots,{{\rm m}}_{N}
, moving respectively on open sets
{Q}_{1},\ldots,{Q}_{N}
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We suppose that every one of these sets is equipped with the corresponding metric
g_{\mu}
.
If
{\rm F}
is the force field acting on the system, then the system is given by
(Q,\widetilde{\tt g},\widetilde{\rm F})
,
as we have seen in the above paragraph. Observe that it is the same situation if the particles are in interaction or not.

If the dynamics is constrained to a submanifold

j_{S}\colon S\hookrightarrow Q
, then there exists a constraint force
{\rm R}
,
and the dynamical equation is

\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}=\widetilde{\rm F}\circ\gamma+\widetilde{\rm R}\circ\dot{\gamma}\ ;

for curves
\gamma\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>S
, where

\widetilde{\rm R}=(\widetilde{\rm R}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\rm R}_{N})
,
{\rm R}_{\mu}={\rm m}_{\mu}\widetilde{\rm R}_{\mu}
,
\mu=1,\ldots,N
.

To solve this equation assuming the
d’Alembert Principle
;
that is, that
\widetilde{\rm R}
is
\widetilde{\tt g}
-orthogonal to
S
,
we need to decompose the equation projecting onto
S
and on its orthogonal complement at every point, as we did in the general study, (see Section
5.3
). Then we solve the
S
component and use the orthogonal one to compute the constraint force along every solution.

5.3.4
Nonholonomic constraints. Nonholonomic d’Alembert Principle

Let
(Q,{\tt g},\omega)
be a Newtonian mechanical system and
{\rm F}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
be the force field. Let
C
be a submanifold of
{\rm T}Q
,
such that
\tau_{Q}(C)=Q
. In this situation
C
is called the

submanifold of nonholonomic constraints
, and

j_{C}\colon C\hookrightarrow{\rm T}Q
is the natural embedding.
We want to describe the dynamics of the system when it is constrained to evolve on the submanifold
C
. The system is given by
(Q,{\tt g},F,C)
.

To solve this problem we suppose that there exists a
constraint force

{\rm R}
usually depending on the velocities, that is
{\rm R}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q,\tau_{Q})
, which forces the system to move on
C
and it is unknown.
Then the Newton dynamical equation is given, in this case, for curves

\gamma\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
such that satisfy:

1.

\dot{\gamma}(t)\in C
,
t\in I
.

2.

\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}={\rm F}\circ\gamma+{\rm R}\circ\dot{\gamma}
.

Thus, we need to state conditions allowing us to find the trajectories of the system and calculate
{\rm R}
5
5

5

Arnold Sommerfeld, in
[
326
]
, says that this force
\mathrm{R}
is a “geometric force”, versus
F
which is an “applied force”.
.

Let
(q,v)\in C
. The condition assumed on
C
,

\tau_{Q}(C)=Q
, tells us that the dimension of the subspace of

{\rm V}_{(q,v)}({\rm T}Q)
which is tangent to
C
, does not depend on the point
(q,v)
. Let

{\rm T}_{(q,v)}^{V}C={\rm V}_{(q,v)}({\rm T}Q)\cap{\rm T}_{(q,v)}C=\{w\in{\rm V}_{(q,v)}({\rm T}Q)\ ;\ w\in{\rm T}_{(q,v)}C\}

be the vertical subspace tangent to
C
. This is a vector subbundle of
{\rm T}Q
and we can write
{\rm T}^{V}C={\rm V}({\rm T}Q)|_{C}\cap{\rm T}C
as vector bundles on the manifold
C
.
Consider the vertical lift from the point
q\in Q
to
(q,v)
given by

\lambda_{q}^{(q,v)}\colon{\rm T}_{q}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm V}_{(q,v)}({\rm T}Q)\,,

defined as:

\lambda_{q}^{(q,v)}(u_{q}):\phi\mapsto\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\frac{\phi(q,v+tu)-\phi(q,v)}{t}\,,

that is, the directional derivative of
\phi
along
u_{q}
at the point
(q,v)\in{\rm T}Q
.

As
\lambda_{q}^{(q,v)}
is an isomorphism from
{\rm T}_{q}Q
to
{\rm T}_{(q,v)}({\rm T}Q)
, let
({\rm T}_{(q,v)}^{V}C)_{q}
the inverse image of

{\rm T}_{(q,v)}^{V}C\subset{\rm T}_{(q,v)}({\rm T}Q)
by
\lambda_{q}^{(q,v)}
. Then
{\rm T}_{q}Q=({\rm T}_{(q,v)}^{V}C)_{q}\oplus({\rm T}_{(q,v)}^{V}C)^{\perp}_{q}
, being this an orthogonal decomposition with respect to
{\tt g}
.

Then we state:

Assumption 5.44

(Nonholonomic d’Alembert Principle
):
The constraint force
{\rm R}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q,\tau_{Q})
satisfies that

{\rm R}(q,v)\in({\rm T}^{V}_{(q,v)}C)^{\perp}_{q}\ ,

that is,
{\tt g}({\rm R}(q,v),w)=0
, for every
w\in({\rm T}^{V}_{(q,v)}C)_{q}
.

In the classical physics literature, the elements in
({\rm T}^{V}_{(q,v)}C)_{q}
are called
virtual velocities
.

Remark 5.45

If there are no constraints, that is
C={\rm T}Q
, then for every
(q,v)\in C
we have that
{\rm T}_{(q,v)}^{V}C=V_{(q,v)}({\rm T}Q)
, hence
({\rm T}^{V}_{(q,v)}C)_{q}={\rm T}_{q}Q
and
({\rm T}^{V}_{(q,v)}C)^{\perp}_{q}=\{0\}
, that is
{\rm R}(q,v)=0
and there is no constraint force.

This principle allows us to obtain the dynamical equations of the trajectories of the system and the constraint force along every trajectory, as we will see in the sequel.

In order to obtain this, we need to characterize the subspace
({\rm T}^{V}_{(q,v)}C)_{q}
in relation with the constraints, that is the functions vanishing on the submanifold
C
.
First, let
\phi\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)
, and consider the 1-form
{\rm d}^{V}\phi\in{\Omega}^{1}(Q,\tau_{Q})
defined by

({\rm d}^{V}\phi(q,v))(u)={\rm d}\phi(\lambda_{q}^{(q,v)}(u)),\hskip 10.00002pt(q,v)\in{\rm T}Q,\hskip 10.00002ptu\in{\rm T}_{q}Q\ ;

whose expression in a local natural chart
(q^{i},v^{i})
of
{\rm T}Q
can be calculated directly applying it to
\displaystyle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}
and we obtain
\displaystyle{\rm d}^{V}\phi=\frac{\partial{\phi}}{\partial{v^{i}}}{\rm d}q^{i}
. We have the following result:

Proposition 5.46

Let
(q,v)\in C
.

1.

If
w\in{\rm T}_{q}Q
, then
\displaystyle w\in({\rm T}_{(q,v)}^{V}C)_{q}
if, and only if,

({\rm d}^{V}\phi(q,v))(w)=0
,
for every
\phi\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)
such that
j_{C}^{*}\phi=0
.

2.

Let
(({\rm T}_{(q,v)}^{V}C)_{q})^{o}=\{\alpha\in{\rm T}^{*}_{q};\alpha(w)=0,\forall w\in({\rm T}_{(q,v)}^{V}C)_{q}\}\subset{\rm T}^{*}_{q}Q
be the annihilator of
({\rm T}_{(q,v)}^{V}C)_{q}
, then
(({\rm T}_{(q,v)}^{V}C)_{q})^{o}=\{{\rm d}^{V}\phi(q,v);\forall\phi\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q),j_{C}^{*}\phi=0\}.

3.

If
w\in{\rm T}_{q}Q
, then
\displaystyle w\in({\rm T}_{(q,v)}^{V}C)_{q}
if, and only if,
\mathop{i}\nolimits(w){\tt g}\in(({\rm T}_{(q,v)}^{V}C)_{q})^{o}
.

(
Proof
)

1.

Let
w\in{\rm T}_{q}Q
, then we have

\displaystyle w\in({\rm T}_{(q,v)}^{V}C)_{q}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \lambda_{q}^{(q,v)}(w)\in{\rm T}_{(q,v)}^{V}C\ \Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\lambda_{q}^{(q,v)}(w)(\phi)=0,\forall\phi\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q),\mathrm{with}\,\,j_{C}^{*}\phi=0\ \Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle{\rm d}\phi(\lambda_{q}^{(q,v)}(w))=0,\forall\phi\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q),\mathrm{with}\,\,j_{C}^{*}\phi=0\ \Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle({\rm d}^{V}\phi(q,v))(w)=0,\forall\phi\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q),\mathrm{with}\,\,j_{C}^{*}\phi=0\ .\hskip 10.00002pt

2.

It is a consequence of the previous item.

3.

It is a direct consequence of the definitions.

Corollary 5.47

Let
{\rm R}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q,\tau_{Q})
and
(q,v)\in C
; then

{\rm R}(q,v)\in({\rm T}^{V}_{(q,v)}C)^{\perp}_{p}
if, and only if,

\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm R}(q,v))g\in(({\rm T}_{(q,v)}^{V}C)_{q})^{o}
.

Usually the submanifold
C
is given by the vanishing of a finite family of constraint functions defined on
{\rm T}Q
. We want to characterize
(({\rm T}_{(q,v)}^{V}C)_{q})^{o}
using these constraints. Then, suppose that the submanifold
C
is defined by the vanishing of
r
functions
\{\phi^{i}\}
, with
r<n=\dim\,Q
, satisfying the condition

\displaystyle\mathrm{rank}\,\left(\frac{\partial{{\phi}^{1},\ldots,{\phi}^{r}}}{\partial{{v}^{1},\ldots,{v}^{n}}}\right)=r
. Then
\dim\,C=2n-r
.
We have:

Proposition 5.48

Let
(q,v)\in C
, then

1.

\dim{\rm T}^{V}_{(q,v)}C=r
.

2.

({\rm T}^{V}_{(q,v)}C)_{q}=\{w\in{\rm T}_{q}Q;({\rm d}^{V}\phi^{i}(q,v))(w)=0,i=1,\ldots,r\}
.

3.

If
\alpha\in{\rm T}_{q}^{*}Q
satisfies
\alpha|_{({\rm T}^{V}_{(q,v)}C)_{q}}=0
, then
\alpha
is a linear combination of the elements
{\rm d}^{V}\phi^{1}(q,v),\ldots,{\rm d}^{V}\phi^{r}(q,v)
;
that is, the vector space
(({\rm T}_{(q,v)}^{V}C)_{q})^{o}
is generated by
\{{\rm d}^{V}\phi^{1}(q,v),\ldots,{\rm d}^{V}\phi^{r}(q,v)\}
.

(
Proof
)
Let
(q^{i},v^{i})
a natural coordinate system on
{\rm T}Q
.

1.

The assumed condition

\displaystyle{\rm rank}\,\left(\frac{\partial{{\phi}^{1},\ldots,{\phi}^{r}}}{\partial{{v}^{1},\ldots,{v}^{n}}}\right)=r
implies that, up to a change of order in the coordinates
{q}^{1},\ldots,{q}^{n}
, we can suppose that

\det\,\left(\frac{\partial{{\phi}^{1},\ldots,{\phi}^{r}}}{\partial{{v}^{1},\ldots,{v}^{r}}}\right)\not=0\ .

Then
(q^{1},\ldots,q^{n},\phi^{1},\ldots,\phi^{r},v^{r+1},\ldots,v^{n})
is a local coordinate system of
{\rm T}Q
by the Inverse Function Theorem. The vector space
V_{q,v}({\rm T}Q)
is generated by

\left\{\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi^{1}}},\ldots,\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi^{r}}},\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{r+1}}},\ldots,\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{n}}}\right\}_{(q,v)}\,,

and the subspace
{\rm T}^{V}_{(q,v)}C\subset V_{q,v}({\rm T}Q)
is generated by

\left\{\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi^{1}}},\ldots,\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi^{r}}}\right\}_{(q,v)}\ .

2.

The inclusion part is proved in the first item of Proposition
5.46
and the equality follows from a dimensional analysis.

3.

The previous items imply that
\{{\rm d}^{V}\phi^{1}(q,v),\ldots,{\rm d}^{V}\phi^{r}(q,v)\}
is a basis of
(({\rm T}_{(q,v)}^{V}C)_{q})^{o}
.

Then, as a corollary, we obtain:

Proposition 5.49

For every
(q,v)\in C
, the form
\eta\in{\Omega}^{1}(Q,\tau_{Q})
satisfies the condition

\displaystyle j^{*}_{C}\eta|_{({\rm T}^{V}_{(q,v)}C)_{q}}=0

if, and only if, there exist
{f}_{1},\ldots,{f}_{r}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)

such that
\eta=f_{i}{\rm d}^{V}\phi^{i}

(
Proof
)
It is a consequence that, for every
(q,v)\in C
, we have that
\eta(q,v)\in(({\rm T}_{(q,v)}^{V}C)_{q})^{o}
.

In the case that the constraints define only locally the submanifold
C
, then the above results are valid only in the corresponding open set.

The last proposition allows us to state the so called
Dual d’Alembert nonholonomic Principle which states that
“the work form
\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm R})g
corresponding to the constraint force
{\rm R}
annihilates the virtual velocities of the system”.

And, as an immediate result, we have:

Corollary 5.50

If
{\rm R}
is the nonholonomic constraint force, then there exist

{f}^{1},\ldots,{f}^{r}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)
such that

\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm R}){\tt g}=f_{i}{\rm d}^{V}\phi^{i}=f_{i}\frac{\partial{\phi^{i}}}{\partial{v^{j}}}{\rm d}q^{j}\ ,

and, as a consequence,

{\rm R}=f_{i}\frac{\partial{\phi^{i}}}{\partial{v^{j}}}g^{jk}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{k}}}\ .

Definition 5.51

The functions
{f}^{1},\ldots,{f}^{r}
are called
Lagrange multipliers

of the nonholonomic system.

Particular case
: The submanifold
C\subset{\rm T}Q
is a linear subbundle of
{\rm T}Q
.

1.

In this case
C
is defined by the vanishing of a family of differential forms, that is, we have
\omega^{i}\in\Omega^{1}(Q)
,
i=1,\ldots,r
, linearly independent at every point of
Q
, and

C=\{(q,v)\in{\rm T}Q;\,\omega^{i}_{q}(v)=0,i=1,\ldots,r\}\,.

In local coordinates, if
\omega^{i}=a^{i}_{j}(q){\rm d}q^{j}
, then
\phi^{i}=a^{i}_{j}(q)v^{j}
, that is the constraints are linear in the velocities, and the expression of the constraint force is

{\rm R}=f_{i}a^{i}_{j}g^{jk}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{k}}}\ .

2.

Alternatively, we can suppose that the subbundle
C
is given as a regular distribution
\mathcal{D}
, the distribution annihilated by
\{\omega^{i},i=1,\ldots,r\}
. If
(q,v)\in\mathcal{D}
, by linearity, we have that
{\rm T}_{(q,v)}^{V}C=\lambda_{q}^{(q,v)}(\mathcal{D}_{q})
, hence
({\rm T}_{(q,v)}^{V}C)_{q}=\mathcal{D}_{q}
and
({\rm T}_{(q,v)}^{V}C)^{\perp}_{q}=\mathcal{D}^{\perp}_{q}
, then the constraint force
{\rm R}
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{D}
.

3.

If the distribution
\mathcal{D}
is integrable and
(q,v)\in\mathcal{D}
is the initial condition of the dynamical equation for the solution
\gamma
, then the image of
\gamma
is contained in the integral submanifold of
\mathcal{D}
passing through the point
q\in Q
, because
\dot{\gamma}(t)\in\mathcal{D}_{\gamma(t)}
for every
t
. The constraint force
{\rm R}
, orthogonal to
\mathcal{D}
, forces the system to move on the integral submanifolds of the constraint distribution
\mathcal{D}
.

Remark 5.52

•

We can understand the solution as follows: if we have a constraint
\phi:{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}
, there is an associated 1-form,
{\rm d}^{V}\phi
, which gives a “constraint force”
R^{\phi}
defined by
\mathop{i}\nolimits(R^{\phi}){\tt g}={\rm d}^{V}\phi
.
If we have
r
independent constraints
\{\phi^{i}\}
, we have the corresponding constraint forces,
R^{\phi^{i}}
, and the subbundle generated by them,
\{R^{\phi^{i}}\}
, and then the resultant constraint force
{\rm R}
is on this subbundle.

•

For these systems, d’Alembert’s Principle says that, if the system moves “along the vertical fibers” of C, the work realized by the constraint force along the trajectory is null. As we have said, these vertical velocities are called “virtual” because it is not possible to move the system with these velocities on the constraint manifold
C
.

5.3.5
Dynamical equations

Following the above results,
if
C
is locally defined by the annihilation of

r
functions
\{\phi^{i}\}
, and they are independent constraints,
then the dynamical equations are

\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}={\rm F}\circ\gamma+f_{j}\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm d}^{V}\phi^{j})g^{-1}\circ\dot{\gamma}\ ,

or, in dual form,

\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}(\theta\circ\dot{\gamma})=\omega\circ\gamma+f_{j}{\rm d}^{V}\phi^{j}\circ\dot{\gamma}\ .

These equations together with the constraints defining
C
,
\phi^{1}=0,\ldots,\phi^{r}=0
,
are a system of
n+r
equations with
n+r
unknowns:
the components of the trajectory
\gamma
and the multipliers
f_{i}
. Observe that some of them are differential equations and the remaining ones are the constraint functions.

The corresponding Euler–Lagrange equations are

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial{K}}{\partial{v^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}\right)-\frac{\partial{K}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}=\omega_{j}\circ\gamma+f_{k}\frac{\partial{\phi^{k}}}{\partial{v^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt(j=1,\ldots,n)\ ,

because
\omega=\omega_{k}{\rm d}q^{k}
, with
\omega_{k}=g_{kj}{\rm F}^{j}
.

If the system is conservative, then
\omega=-{\rm d}V
,
where
V\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(Q)
is the potential function. In this case we can introduce the Lagrangian function
{\cal L}:=K-\tau_{Q}^{*}V
and we have

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}\right)-\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}=f_{k}\frac{\partial{\phi^{k}}}{\partial{v^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}\ .

In any case, these equations, together with the constraints, defining
C
, are also a system of
n+r
equations with
n+r
unknowns.

If
C
is a vector subbundle, then
\phi^{i}=a^{i}_{j}(q)v^{j}
and the Euler–Lagrange equations are

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial{K}}{\partial{v^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}\right)-\frac{\partial{K}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}=\omega_{j}\circ\gamma+f_{k}a^{k}_{j}\circ\dot{\gamma}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt(j=1,\ldots,n)\,.

Or in the case of conservative systems

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}\right)-\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{j}}}\circ\dot{\gamma}=f_{k}a^{k}_{j}\circ\dot{\gamma}\ .

Summarizing, the fundamental data in the Lagrangian formulation
of the autonomous dynamical systems are the following:

•

A Newtonian mechanical system (independent of time) is a triple
(Q,{\tt g},\omega)
, where

(Q,{\tt g})
is a Riemannian manifold where the motion happens,
which is called the
configuration space
of the system,
whose points represent the
positions
of the particles of the system,
and
the form
\omega
or, equivalently, the vector field
{\rm F}
,
are geometric elements carrying the dynamical information.

•

The dynamical trajectories, which are the solutions to Newton equations,
are curves on
{\rm T}Q
which are canonical lifts of curves on
Q
.
The points of the manifold
{\rm T}Q
,
which are the possible initial conditions for these equations
and, hence, they represent the possible
position
and
velocities

of the system) and are the
physical states
of the system, and
then
{\rm T}Q
is said to be the

state
or
phase space
(of
velocities
) of the system.

•

In the particular case of conservative systems,
the geometric element representing the dynamics is substituted
by the
(mechanical) Lagrangian function

{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)
, and the dynamical equations
are the
Euler–Lagrange equations (of the first kind)
.

Finally, if the degrees of freedom of the system
have some kind of restriction, we have two different situations:

1.

If the dynamics takes place on some submanifold

j_{S}\colon S\hookrightarrow Q
, the situation is the same, but considering

(S,j_{S}^{*}{\tt g},j_{S}^{*}\omega)
as a Newtonian system.
Observe that, in this case, the configuration space is
S

and the corresponding phase space (of velocities) is
{\rm T}S
.

2.

If the configuration space is not restricted but the velocities are, then we have a nonholonomic system, and we need to introduce new unknowns, the Lagrange multipliers, to obtain the corresponding dynamical equations.

5.4
Nonautonomous Newtonian systems

In some interesting cases, the force field acting on a Newtonian system
depends not only on the positions and the velocities, but also on time.
In the following paragraphs, we extend our geometric formulation to this situation.

5.4.1
Mechanical systems with time-dependent forces

The geometric model appropriate to this case is the following:

Definition 5.53

A
nonautonomous Newtonian mechanical system
is a triple

(\mathbb{R}\times Q,{\tt g},{\rm F})
, where

(Q,{\tt g})
is a Riemannian manifold and the force field is of the form

{\rm F}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q,\pi_{2})
(with
\pi_{2}\colon\mathbb{R}\times Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
); that is,

\begin{array}[]{ccc}&&{\rm T}Q\\
&\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(5.0,15.0){\mbox{${\rm F}$}}\put(-6.0,-8.0){\vector{4}{3}{50.0}}\end{picture}&\Bigg\downarrow\tau_{Q}\\
\mathbb{R}\times Q&\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(17.0,5.0){\mbox{$\pi_{2}$}}\put(0.0,0.0){\vector{1}{0}{40.0}}\end{picture}&Q\end{array}\ .

Moreover, if the force field depends on the velocities, then

{\rm F}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q,\tau_{Q}\circ\rho_{2})
, with
\rho_{2}\colon\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q
;
that is,

\begin{array}[]{ccc}&&{\rm T}Q_{\mu}\\
&\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(15.0,15.0){\mbox{${\rm F}$}}\put(-6.0,-8.0){\vector{2}{1}{70.0}}\end{picture}&\Bigg\downarrow\tau_{Q}\\
\mathbb{R}\times Q&\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(3.0,8.0){\mbox{$\rho_{2}$}}\put(-6.0,3.0){\vector{1}{0}{25.0}}\end{picture}{\rm T}Q\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(9.0,8.0){\mbox{$\tau_{Q}$}}\put(5.0,3.0){\vector{1}{0}{25.0}}\end{picture}&Q\end{array}\ .

The Newton equations are written in the usual way:

•

In the case that the force does not depend on the velocities,

\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}={\rm F}\circ\bar{\gamma}\ ,

where
\bar{\gamma}=(t,\gamma)\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>I\times Q
.
We can also use the dual form by means of the corresponding work form
\omega\in{\Omega}^{1}(Q,\pi_{2})
.

•

If the force field depends on the velocities, then

\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}={\rm F}\circ\bar{\dot{\gamma}}\ ,

where
\bar{\dot{\gamma}}=(t,\dot{\gamma})\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>I\times{\rm T}Q
.
As above, we can use the corresponding work form
\omega\in{\Omega}^{1}(Q,\tau_{Q}\circ\rho_{2})
and obtain the equations in the dual form.

The Euler–Lagrange equations are the same as usual, but the second term depends on time
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
In particular, if the work form depends on time,
\omega\in{\Omega}^{1}(Q,\pi_{2})
,
we say that the system is
conservative
if there exists

V\colon\mathbb{R}\times Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}
, such that
\omega=-{\rm d}V_{t}
,
where
V_{t}\colon Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
is defined by
V_{t}(p):=V(t,p)
,
for every
p\in Q
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
In this situation we can define the Lagrangian function
{\cal L}:=K-V
, depending on time, and the Euler–Lagrange equation are as usual

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\circ\bar{\dot{\gamma}}\right)-\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\circ\bar{\dot{\gamma}}=0\ .

Time dependent Newtonian systems with holonomic and nonholonomic constraints can be studied in the same way, as we see in the next section.

5.4.2
Time-dependent holonomic and nonholonomic constraints

Definition 5.54

A
nonautonomous holonomic Newtonian mechanical system

is a triple
(Q,{\tt g},\omega)
, where
(Q,{\tt g})
is a Riemannian manifold,

\omega\in{\Omega}^{1}(Q)
), and we have an embedding

j_{S}\colon S\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}\times Q
, where
S
is a submanifold of
\mathbb{R}\times Q
.

In this situation, we need to suppose that the constraint force depends not only on positions and velocities but on time also; that is
{\rm R}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q,\tau_{Q}\circ\rho_{2})
. The Newton equation is

\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}={\rm F}\circ\bar{\gamma}+{\rm R}\circ\bar{\dot{\gamma}}\ ,

and d’Alembert’s Principle can be stated as follows:

Assumption 5.55

(Nonautonomous d’Alembert principle)
:
The constraint force
{\rm R}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q,\tau_{Q}\circ\rho_{2})
satisfies that,
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
p\in S
and
u,v\in{\rm T}_{q}S
,

{\tt g}({\rm R}(t,({\rm T}_{q}j_{S})(u)),({\rm T}_{q}j_{S})(v))=0\ .

As in the autonomous case, this means that

{\rm R}(t,({\rm T}_{q}j_{S})(u))\in({\rm T}_{j_{S}(q)}S)^{\perp}
, and the results we obtain are the same as in Section
5.3
.

Definition 5.56

A
nonautonomous nonholonomic Newtonian mechanical system

is a triple
(Q,{\tt g},\omega)
, where
(Q,{\tt g})
is a Riemannian manifold,
\omega\in{\Omega}^{1}(Q)
),
and we have an embedding

j_{C}\colon C\hookrightarrow{\rm T}Q
, such that
(\tau_{Q}\circ j_{C})(C)=Q
,
where
C
is a differentiable manifold.

Once again we need to assume that the constraint force also depends on time, that is
{\rm R}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q,\tau_{Q}\circ\rho_{2})
, and this makes the dynamical trajectories of the system,

\gamma\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
, to satisfy

\dot{\gamma}(t)\in{\rm T}C
, for every
t\in I
.
Hence, the Newton equation is

\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}={\rm F}\circ\bar{\gamma}+{\rm R}\circ\bar{\dot{\gamma}}\ .

To state the
nonholonomic d’Alembert Principle
, for every
t\in\mathbb{R}

and
{\rm p}\in C
, we need to define
({\rm T}_{j_{C}({\rm p})}C)_{\tau_{Q}({\rm p})}
,
as in the time independent case (see Section
5.3.4
).

With this aim, let
{\rm p}\in C
and
j_{C}({\rm p})=(q,v)\in j_{C}(C)\subset{\rm T}Q
, we take
{\rm T}^{V}_{(q,v)}C
as the vertical subbundle tangent to
C
and in the same way
({\rm T}^{V}_{(q,v)}C)_{q}
using the vertical lift
\lambda_{q}^{(q,v)}
. Then

Assumption 5.57

(Nonautonomous nonholonomic d’Alembert Principle)
:
The constraint force
{\rm R}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q,\tau_{Q}\circ\rho_{2})
satisfies that,

{\tt g}({\rm R}(t,(q,v)),u)=0\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptu\in({\rm T}^{V}_{(q,v)}C)_{q}

for every
{\rm p}\in C
,
j_{C}({\rm p})=(q,v)\in j_{C}(C)
, and every
t\in\mathbb{R}
; that is,

{\rm R}(t,j_{C}({\rm p}))\in({\rm T}_{j_{C}({\rm p})}C)_{(\tau_{Q}\circ j_{C})({\rm p})}^{\perp}
.

The consequences of this principle on the dynamical equations and the calculus of the constraint force are similar to the autonomous case
(see Section
5.3.4
), with the only difference that now the Lagrange multipliers
f_{i}
are elements of
{\rm C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q)
.

Remark 5.58

Sometimes it is necessary to work with constraints depending on time, called
rheonomic
constraints instead of
scleronomic
constraints or not depending on time (see
[
318
]
for details). In this situation we have a submanifold
B\subset\mathbb{R}\times{\rm T}Q
such that corresponding manifolds,
B_{t}
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, are diffeomorphic. Then d’Alembert’s Principle is stated at every
t
for the submanifold
B_{t}
.

Chapter 6
An introduction to contact mechanics and dissipative dynamical systems

In Chapter
4
we have studied nonautonomous or time-dependent systems;
one of the main characteristics of which is that, as discussed there, they are not conservative , but dissipative;
that is, unlike the conservative systems studied in chapters
3
and
5
,
energy is not a conserved quantity.
However, in mechanics, there are many other systems that,
being autonomous or not autonomous indistinctly, also manifest this characteristic
[
163
,
304
]
.
They are those in which forces of a nonconservative type appear, such as those described in Section
5.2.2

In recent years, there has been a great interest in geometrically studying these types of systems, by using techniques from
contact geometry

[
3
,
20
,
43
,
174
]
.
In fact, the
contact structure
is quite similar to cosymplectic structure and allows us to give
a natural Hamiltonian description of mechanical systems with dissipation

[
38
,
40
,
41
,
68
,
189
,
191
,
235
,
251
,
354
]
.
Their Lagrangian formalism
[
93
,
117
,
169
,
191
]
,
and their unified Lagrangian-Hamiltonian formalism
[
114
]
have also been stated
in many different situations and application;
such as, damped oscillators, motion on viscous fluids, and motion with friction in general.
It should be noted, however, that the first Lagrangian formulation for these kinds of systems
was first introduced by
G. Herglotz

[
213
,
214
]

(see also
[
175
,
240
,
251
]
and
[
121
]
for a modern geometric version of this variational approach), who used a generalization of the Hamilton variational principle to obtain the
so-called
Herglotz–Euler–Lagrange equations
, which are the same equations that are obtained using contact geometry.
In all these descriptions, the Lagrangian and the Hamiltonian functions depend also on an additional variable that,
as we will see, can be identified with the “action” of the system;
and, for this reason, physicists often refer to them as
action-dependent systems
.

Contact geometry has also been used to describe different types of physical systems
in thermodynamics (for instance, in

[
38
,
39
,
321
,
342
]
),
quantum mechanics
[
93
,
215
,
224
]
, circuit theory
[
93
,
187
]
, astrophysics
[
176
]
, theoretical physics
[
224
]
, control theory
[
121
,
300
]
, etc.;
and even to describe other mechanical systems than just dissipative ones
[
145
]
.
Finally, Herglotz’s variational methods and the contact structure itself have been generalized
in different ways for the treatment of action-dependent field theories

[
115
,
168
,
170
,
171
,
353
]

(see also
[
9
,
15
,
37
,
111
,
162
,
278
,
307
,
308
,
335
]
for other contributions and less general approaches).

This whole body of doctrine, generically referred to as
contact mechanics
,
is currently a topic of active research, both in its foundation, and in its extensions and applications.

In this chapter, we review the main foundations of contact geometry
and its application to describe dissipative autonomous dynamical systems.
The extension for the treatment of dissipative nonautonomous dynamical systems (
cocontact formalism
) has been done in

[
112
,
141
,
309
]
,
as well as to other types of systems and problems, such as
higher-order dissipative systems
[
113
]
,
nonholonomic systems
[
116
,
122
]
, or reduction theorems
[
190
,
362
]
.

We start giving the basic concepts and properties of
contact manifolds
and the generic definition of
contact Hamiltonian systems
.
Next we develop the Lagrangian, Hamiltonian and unified formalisms for these kinds of systems.
We also study symmetries and the fundamental concept of
dissipated quantities
geometrically;
defining, in particular, the notion of

contact Noether symmetry
for contact Hamiltonian and Lagrangian systems,
and establishing the statements of the so-called “dissipation theorems”.
These are analogous to the conservation theorems of conservative systems,
and show how to associate dissipated and conserved quantities to these symmetries.
Finally, the examples of the damped harmonic oscillator and the Kepler problem with dissipation
are analyzed in this context.

6.1
A survey on contact geometry

(See, for instance,
[
3
,
20
,
39
,
57
,
93
,
169
,
224
,
249
]

for more information).

6.1.1
Contact manifolds

Definition 6.1

A
contact manifold
is a pair
({\rm M},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})
,
where
{\rm M}
is a
(2n+1)
-dimensional manifold and

\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\in{\Omega}^{1}({\rm M})
is a differential
1
-form
such that
\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\wedge({\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})^{n}
is a volume form in
{\rm M}
.
Then, the form
\eta
is called a
contact form

(or a
contact structure
),

As a straightforward consequence of this definition, we have that,

Theorem 6.2

Given a contact manifold
({\rm M},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})
,
the condition that
\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\wedge({\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})^{n}
is a volume form
is equivalent to have the splitting

{\rm T}{\rm M}=\ker{\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\oplus\ker\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\equiv\mathcal{D}^{\rm R}\oplus\mathcal{D}^{\rm C}\ .

(6.1)

Then, there exists a unique vector field
\mathcal{R}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm M})
such that

\mathop{i}\nolimits(\mathcal{R}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=0\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits(\mathcal{R})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=1\ ,

(6.2)

and hence it generates the distribution
{\cal D}^{\rm R}
.

Conversely, if we have two distributions
{\cal D}^{\rm R}
and
{\cal D}^{\rm C}

of ranks
1
and
2n
respectively, such that
(
6.1
) holds, they define a contact structure on
{\rm M}
.

Definition 6.3

The above vector field
\mathcal{R}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm M})

is called the
Reeb vector field
,
and
{\cal D}^{\rm R}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\rm C}

are known as the
Reeb
(or
horizontal distribution
),
and the
contact
(or
vertical distribution
of
({\rm M},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})
.

Remark 6.4

It is relevant to point out that, although Definition
6.1
is the most assumed,
there are other different terminologies in relation to the concept of
contact manifold
.
For instance, it can be defined by demanding the existence of two distributions

{\cal D}^{\rm R}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\rm C}
, satisfying the properties stated in Theorem
6.2
, instead of using differential forms
(see, for instance,
[
11
,
174
,
189
,
224
]
),
and when these distributions are associated to a fixed form,
it is called a
strict contact manifold
.
Also, in
[
1
]
, a more generic definition is given,
by demanding the existence of a
2
-form of maximal rank in an odd-dimensional manifold,
and then calling
exact contact
the case in which this form is exact
(which is the case of Definition
6.1
).

Given a contact manifold
({\rm M},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})
, as a consequence of the splitting (
6.1
), there exists a vector bundle isomorphism

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\flat_{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}\colon{\rm T}{\rm M}&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&{\rm T}^{*}{\rm M}\\
\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\cr({\rm p},X_{\rm p})&\mapsto&\big({\rm p},\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\rm p}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\rm p}+[\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\rm p})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\rm p}]\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\rm p}\big)\ .\end{array}

and its inverse
\sharp_{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}=\flat_{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}^{-1}\colon{\rm T}^{*}{\rm M}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}{\rm M}
.
Their natural extensions are the
{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm M})
-module isomorphisms
which are denoted with the same notation,

\begin{array}[]{rccl}\flat_{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}\colon&\mathfrak{X}({\rm M})&\longrightarrow&{\Omega}^{1}({\rm M})\\
&X&\longmapsto&\mathop{i}\nolimits(X){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}+(\mathop{i}\nolimits(X)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\end{array}\

(6.3)

and its inverse
\sharp_{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}=\flat_{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}^{-1}\colon{\Omega}^{1}({\rm M})\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathfrak{X}({\rm M})
.
In particular, for the Reeb vector field
we have that
\flat_{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}(\mathcal{R})=\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}
.

Remark 6.5

If in Definition
6.1
the form
\eta
is such that

\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\wedge({\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})^{n}
is not a volume form,
but the rank of the distribution
\ker{\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\cap\ker\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}
is constant
and
\dim{\rm M}-{\rm rank}(\ker{\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\cap\ker\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})
is odd,
then we say that
\eta
is a
precontact form
on
{\rm M}

and that
({\rm M},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})
is a
precontact manifold

(moreover,
{\rm dim}\,{\rm M}
could be arbitrary).
Under these hypotheses, there exist Reeb vector fields defined by (
6.2
), but they are not uniquely defined,
and the map
\flat_{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}
is not an isomorphism
[
112
]
.
(See also
[
190
]
for a more general definition of precontact structure,
using
contact distributions
).

Proposition 6.6

On a contact manifold
({\rm M},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})
, there are charts of coordinates
(U;z^{I};s)
,
I=1,\ldots,2n
,
such that

\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}|_{U}={\rm d}s-f_{I}(z^{J})\,{\rm d}z^{I}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{R}|_{U}=\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\ ,

where
f_{I}
are functions depending only on the
z^{J}
.
(They are called
adapted coordinates
of the contact structure.)

(
Proof
)
On
{\rm M}
, we can take local charts of coordinates
(U;z^{I},s)
,
I=1,\ldots,2n
, such that they rectify the vector field
\mathcal{R}
; that is,
\mathcal{R}=\displaystyle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{s}}
on
U
.
Then
\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}|_{U}=a\,{\rm d}s-f_{I}(z^{J},s){\rm d}z^{I}
; but the conditions defining
\mathcal{R}
imply that
\displaystyle\frac{\partial{f_{I}}}{\partial{s}}=0
and
a=1
, hence the result follows.

Furthermore, one can prove the existence of Darboux-type coordinates:

Theorem 6.7

(Darboux theorem for contact manifolds)

Let
({\rm M},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})
be a contact manifold.
Then, for every point
p\in{\rm M}
there exist a chart of coordinates

(U;x^{i},y_{i},s)
,
1\leq i\leq n
, such that

\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}|_{U}={\rm d}s-y_{i}\,{\rm d}x^{i}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{R}|_{U}=\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\ .

These are the
Darboux
or
canonical coordinates
of the contact manifold
({\rm M},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})
.

(
Proof
)
Taking local charts of adapted coordinates,

(U;z^{I},s)
, if we do the quotient
U/{\cal D}^{\rm R}
,
the form
{\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}|_{U}={\rm d}f_{I}(z^{J})\wedge{\rm d}z^{I}

projects to the quotient and is a symplectic form on it.
Then we can take symplectic Darboux coordinates on the quotient
and pull them back to
U
, thus obtaining contact chart Darboux coordinates
(U;x^{i},y_{i},s)
on
{\rm M}
.

Relevant examples of contact manifolds are:

Canonical model
:
The canonical model for contact manifolds
is the manifold
{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}
.
In fact, if
\Theta\in{\Omega}^{1}({\rm T}^{*}Q)

is the canonical
1
-forms in
{\rm T}^{*}Q
,

s
is the Cartesian coordinate of
\mathbb{R}
, and

\pi_{1}\colon{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}^{*}Q

is the canonical projection, then

\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}={\rm d}s-\pi_{1}^{*}\Theta={\rm d}s-p_{i}{\rm d}q^{i}
is a contact form in
{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}
,
the Reeb vector field is

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=\frac{\partial}{\partial s}
, and
(s,q^{i},p_{i})
are Darboux coordinates on
{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}
.

Contactification of a symplectic manifold
.
If
(P,\omega)
is an exact symplectic manifold such that
\omega=-{\rm d}\theta\in{\Omega}^{2}(P)
,
consider the manifold
{\rm M}=P\times\mathbb{R}
.
If
s
is the Cartesian coordinate of
\mathbb{R}
,
then the 1-form

\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}={\rm d}s-\theta\in{\Omega}^{1}({\rm M})

(where we have denoted also by
\theta
the pull-back of
\theta
to
{\rm M}
) is a contact form,
and
({\rm M},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})
is a contact manifold which is called the

contactified
of
P
.
Observe that the canonical model,
{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}
,
is the contactified of
{\rm T}^{*}Q
endowed with its canonical symplectic structure.

6.1.2
Hamiltonian, gradient, and evolution vector fields on a contact manifold

As in the case of cosymplectic manifolds,
for a contact manifold
({\rm M},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})
,
the existence of the natural
{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm M})
-modules isomorphism
\flat_{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}
,
introduced in the above section,
allows us to associate some characteristic vector fields to a function
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\rm M})
:

Definition 6.8

Let
({\rm M},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})
be a contact manifold and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\rm M})
.

The
Hamiltonian vector field
associated with
f

is the vector field
{\rm X}_{f}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm M})
defined by

\flat_{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}({\rm X}_{f}):={\rm d}f-(\mathcal{R}(f)+f)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}
.

The
gradient vector field
associated with
f

is the vector field
{\bf grad\,f}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm M})
defined by

\flat_{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}({\bf grad}\,f):={\rm d}f
.

The
evolution vector field
associated with
f

is the vector field
\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}_{f}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm M})
defined as

\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}_{f}:=f\,\mathcal{R}+{\rm X}_{f}
or, equivalently,

\flat_{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}_{f}):={\rm d}f-\mathcal{R}(f)\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}
.

Lemma 6.9

{\bf grad}\,f={\rm X}_{f}+(\mathcal{R}(f)+f)\,\mathcal{R}
.

(
Proof
)
Observe that,

\flat_{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}({\rm X}_{f})={\rm d}f-(\mathcal{R}(f)+f)\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=\flat_{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}({\bf grad}\,f)-(\mathcal{R}(f)+f)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\ ,

then, being
\flat_{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}
a diffeomorphism and bearing in mind that
\flat_{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}(\mathcal{R})=\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}
, the result follows.

Taking this into account, these vector fields can be equivalently characterized as follows:

Proposition 6.10

The Hamiltonian vector field associated with
f

is determined equivalently by the equations:

\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{f})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=-f\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{f}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}={\rm d}f-\mathcal{R}(f)\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\ .

(6.4)

The gradient vector field associated with
f
is determined equivalently by the equations:

\mathop{i}\nolimits({\bf grad}\,f)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=\mathcal{R}(f)\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits({\bf grad}\,f){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}={\rm d}f-\mathcal{R}(f)\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\ .

(6.5)

The evolution vector field associated with
f

is determined equivalently by the equations:

\mathop{i}\nolimits(\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}_{f})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=0\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits(\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}_{f}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}={\rm d}f-\mathcal{R}(f)\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\ .

(6.6)

(
Proof
)
For every
f\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm M})
,
for the Hamiltonian vector field
{\rm X}_{f}
,
using the definitions of the isomorphism
\flat_{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}
and of
{\rm X}_{f}
, first we have that,

\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{f}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}+(\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{f})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=\flat_{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}({\rm X}_{f})={\rm d}f-(\mathcal{R}(f)+f)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\ ,

(6.7)

therefore, contracting both members with the Reeb vector field and using (
6.2
), we get

(\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{f})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})\mathop{i}\nolimits(\mathcal{R})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=\mathop{i}\nolimits(\mathcal{R}){\rm d}f-(\mathcal{R}(f)+f)\mathop{i}\nolimits(\mathcal{R})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=-f\,\mathop{i}\nolimits(\mathcal{R})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{f})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=-f\ .

Now, going to (
6.7
), we obtain that

\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{f}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}={\rm d}f-\mathcal{R}(f)\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\ .

Conversely, using (
6.4
)
in the Definition (
6.3
) of
\flat_{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}
,
we get
\flat_{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}({\rm X}_{f}):={\rm d}f-(\mathcal{R}(f)+f)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}
.

For the gradient vector field
{\bf grad}\,f
,
from the definition of
{\bf grad}\,f
and the above Lemma
6.9
,
and using the above results and (
6.2
), we have

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits({\bf grad}\,f){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}

\displaystyle=\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{f}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}+(\mathcal{R}(f)+f)\,\mathop{i}\nolimits(\mathcal{R}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{f}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}={\rm d}f-\mathcal{R}(f)\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits({\bf grad}\,f)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}

\displaystyle=\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{f})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}+(\mathcal{R}(f)+f)\,\mathop{i}\nolimits(\mathcal{R})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=-f+(\mathcal{R}(f)+f)=\mathcal{R}(f)\ .

Conversely, using (
6.5
)
in the Definition (
6.3
) of
\flat_{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}
,
we obtain that
\flat_{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}({\bf grad}\,f):={\rm d}f
.

Finally, for the evolution vector field
\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}_{f}
, from the definition of
\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}_{f}
and using the above results,

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}_{f}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}

\displaystyle=\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{f}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}+f\mathop{i}\nolimits(\mathcal{R}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{f}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}={\rm d}f-(\mathcal{R}(f))\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}_{f})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}

\displaystyle=\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{f})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}+f\mathop{i}\nolimits(\mathcal{R})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=-f+f=0\ .

Once again, using (
6.6
)
in the Definition (
6.3
) of
\flat_{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}
,
we obtain that
\flat_{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}_{f}):={\rm d}f-\mathcal{R}(f)\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}
.

As an immediate consequence of Proposition
6.10
we obtain that:

Proposition 6.11

The equations for the integral curves
c\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm M}

of the Hamiltonian, the gradient, and the evolution vector fields associated with

f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\rm M})
are, respectively,

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{c})(\eta\circ c)=-f\circ c\

,

\displaystyle\ \mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{c})(\omega\circ c)=({\rm d}f-R(f)\eta)\circ c\ ,

(6.8)

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{c})(\eta\circ c)=R(f)\circ c\

,

\displaystyle\ \mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{c})(\omega\circ c)=({\rm d}f-R(f)\eta)\circ c\ ,

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{c})(\eta\circ c)=0\

,

\displaystyle\ \mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{c})(\omega\circ c))=({\rm d}f-R(f)\eta)\circ c\ ,

Local expressions
:
In Darboux coordinates
(x^{i},y_{i},s)
on
{\rm M}
, we have that
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(f)=\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{s}}
, then

{\rm d}f-\mathcal{R}(f)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{i}}+y_{i}\frac{\partial f}{\partial s}\right){\rm d}x^{i}+\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{y_{i}}}\,{\rm d}y_{i}\ ,

and, from (
6.4
), (
6.5
), and (
6.6
) (or also using (
6.4
), Lemma
6.9
and the definition of
\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}_{f}
), we obtain,

\displaystyle{\rm X}_{f}=\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}-\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{i}}+y_{i}\frac{\partial f}{\partial s}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}+\left(y_{i}\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{i}}-f\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\ ,

(6.9)

\displaystyle{\bf grad}\,f=\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}-\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{i}}+y_{i}\frac{\partial f}{\partial s}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}+\left(y_{i}\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{i}}+\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{s}}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\ ,

(6.10)

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}_{f}=\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}-\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{i}}+y_{i}\frac{\partial f}{\partial s}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}+y_{i}\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{i}}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\ .

(6.11)

Therefore, if
c(t)=(x^{i}(t),y_{i}(t),s(t))
is an integral curve of any of these vector fields, this implies that
c(t)
should satisfy,
respectively, the following systems of differential equations:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{dx^{i}}{dt}=\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{i}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\displaystyle\frac{dy_{i}}{dt}=-\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{i}}+y_{i}\frac{\partial f}{\partial s}\right)\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\displaystyle\frac{ds}{dt}=y_{i}\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{i}}-f\ ,

(6.12)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{dx^{i}}{dt}=\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{i}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\displaystyle\frac{dy_{i}}{dt}=-\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{i}}+y_{i}\frac{\partial f}{\partial s}\right)\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\displaystyle\frac{ds}{dt}=y_{i}\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{i}}+\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{s}}\ ,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{dx^{i}}{dt}=\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{i}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\displaystyle\frac{dy_{i}}{dt}=-\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{i}}+y_{i}\frac{\partial f}{\partial s}\right)\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\displaystyle\frac{ds}{dt}=y_{i}\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{i}}\ .

6.2
Contact Hamiltonian dynamical systems

As we shall see in the following sections, the contact structures and their underlying tools constitute an ideal framework for the geometric description of dissipative dynamic systems.
(See, for instance,
[
40
,
93
,
117
,
169
,
178
,
235
]
for more details).

6.2.1
Contact Hamiltonian systems

Following the same guidelines as in all previous chapters,
the geometric study of dissipative (nonconservative or action-dependent) Hamiltonian dynamical systems
in general, is based on the following set of postulates:

Postulate 6.12

(First Postulate of contact Hamiltonian mechanics)
:
The phase space of a regular (resp. singular)
dissipative dynamical system
is a differentiable manifold
{\rm M}
endowed with a contact
(resp. precontact) structure
\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\in{\Omega}^{1}({\rm M})
:

Postulate 6.13

(Second Postulate of contact Hamiltonian mechanics)
:
The observables or physical magnitudes of a dissipative dynamical system
are functions of
{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm M})
.

Postulate 6.14

(Third Postulate of contact Hamiltonian mechanics)
:
The dynamics of a dissipative dynamical system
is given by a function
h\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm M})

(or, in general, a closed
1
-form
\alpha\in Z^{1}({\rm M})
, such that
\alpha={\rm d}h
, locally)
which is called the
Hamiltonian function

(or the
Hamiltonian
1
-form
) of the system.
This function represents the energy of the system.

Postulate 6.15

(Fourth Postulate of contact Hamiltonian mechanics)
:
The dynamical trajectories of the dissipative system are the integral curves
of the Hamiltonian vector field
{\rm X}_{h}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm M})
associated with
h
;
that is, of the vector field solution to equations (
6.4
), that are now written as,

\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=-h\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}={\rm d}h-\mathcal{R}(h)\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\ .

(6.13)

Thus, these trajectories are the solutions to equations (
6.12
), which read as,

\displaystyle\frac{dx^{i}}{dt}=\frac{\partial h}{\partial y_{i}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\displaystyle\frac{dy_{i}}{dt}=-\left(\frac{\partial h}{\partial x^{i}}+y_{i}\frac{\partial h}{\partial s}\right)\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\displaystyle\frac{ds}{dt}=y_{i}\frac{\partial h}{\partial y_{i}}-h\ ,

(6.14)

It is interesting to point out the different roles of the Hamiltonian and the evolution vector fields in cosymplectic and in contact mechanics:
in cosymplectic mechanics the dynamic is given by the evolution vector field, but in contact mechanics is given by the Hamiltonian vector field

1
1

1

Although the terminology could be changed to make these aspects coherent, it has not been done because this is the usual nomenclature in the bibliography.

.

Definition 6.16

A
regular dissipative
or
contact Hamiltonian dynamical system

is a set
({\rm M},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$},h)
,
where
({\rm M},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})
is a contact manifold
and
h\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm M})
is the Hamiltonian function of the system.
If
({\rm M},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})
is a precontact manifold, then
({\rm M},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$},h)

is said to be a
singular dissipative
or
precontact Hamiltonian dynamical system

2
2

2

We write ‘(pre)contact’ to refer interchangeably to both situations (contact and precontact), and write ‘contact’ or ‘precontact’ to distinguish each of them in particular.
In any case, we will refer to this formalism as
contact mechanics
, and we will talk about
contact dynamical systems
, in general.

.

The equations
(
6.13
) and (
6.14
)
are the
(pre)contact Hamiltonian equations

for
{\rm X}_{h}
and its integral curves, respectively.

Definition 6.17

Given a dissipative Hamiltonian dynamical system
({\rm M},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$},h)
,
the
Hamiltonian problem
posed by the system
consists in finding the Hamiltonian vector field
{\rm X}_{h}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm M})

associated with
h
(if it exists).

6.2.2
Properties of contact Hamiltonian systems

Proposition 6.18

If
({\rm M},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$},h)
is a regular dissipative Hamiltonian system,
then there exists a unique Hamiltonian vector field
{\rm X}_{h}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm M})
;
that is, a unique vector field which is the solution to equations (
6.13
).

(
Proof
)
It is a straightforward consequence of the existence of the isomorphism
\sharp_{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}
in the regular case.

Remark 6.19

As it happens with singular dynamical systems,
if
({\rm M},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$};h)
is a precontact Hamiltonian system,
equations (
6.13
) are not necessarily compatible everywhere on
{\rm M}

and the corresponding constraint algorithm must be implemented in order to find
a
final constraint submanifold

P_{f}\hookrightarrow{\rm M}

(if it exists) where there are contact Hamiltonian vector fields
{\rm X}_{h}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm M})
,
tangent to
P_{f}
, which are solutions (not necessarily unique) to equations (
6.13
) on
P_{f}
.

Proposition 6.20

Let
({\rm M},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$},h)
be a contact Hamiltonian system.
Then, the contact Hamiltonian equations (
6.13
) can be equivalently written as

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=-\mathcal{R}(h)\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=-h\ .

(6.15)

and its integral curves
c\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm M}
are solutions to the equations
