\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(\widetilde{c})(\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\circ c))=-\big((\mathcal{R}(h))\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\big)\circ c\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{c})(\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\circ c))=-h\circ c\ .

(
Proof
)
As
\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=-h
, from equations (
6.13
) we obtain,

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}+{\rm d}\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}={\rm d}h-\mathcal{R}(h)\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}-{\rm d}h=-\mathcal{R}(h)\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\ .

Conversely, from (
6.15
),

-\mathcal{R}(h)\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}+{\rm d}\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}-{\rm d}h\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}={\rm d}h-\mathcal{R}(h)\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\ .

From here, the equation for the integral curves is immediate.

The first result shows that,
unlike in symplectic Hamiltonian systems, the geometric structure of contact Hamiltonian systems is not conserved by the dynamics,
and the same happens with the Hamiltonian function. Indeed,

Proposition 6.21

(Dissipation of energy):

Let
({\rm M},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$},h)
be a contact Hamiltonian system. If
{\rm X}_{h}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm M})
is solution to the equations (
6.13
) then,

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})h=-(\mathcal{R}(h))\,h\ .

(6.16)

(
Proof
)
As a consequence of the above proposition, we have,

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})h=-\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})(\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=-\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})\big((\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(\mathcal{R})h)\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\big)=-(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(\mathcal{R})h)\,h\ .

As a final result, there is a new way of writing equations (
6.13
)
without using of the Reeb vector field
\mathcal{R}
.

Proposition 6.22

Let
({\rm M},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$},h)
be a contact Hamiltonian system.
If
U=\{p\in{\rm M};h(p)\not=0\}
and
\Omega=-h\,{\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}+{\rm d}h\wedge\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}
on
U
,
equations (
6.13
) can also be written as

\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})\Omega=0\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=-h\hskip 10.00002pt;\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{\rm(on ${\rm M}$)}\ ,

(6.17)

and its integral curves
{\bf c}\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm M}
are solutions to

\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{c})(\Omega\circ c))=0\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{c})(\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\circ c))=-h\circ{\bf c}\>.

(
Proof
)
If
{\rm X}_{h}
satisfies equations (
6.17
), then,

0=\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})\Omega=-h\,\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}+\big(\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h}){\rm d}h\big)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}-{\rm d}h\,\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=-h\,\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}+\big(\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h}){\rm d}h\big)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}+h\,{\rm d}h

and hence

h\,\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=\big(\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h}){\rm d}h\big)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}+h\,{\rm d}h\ .

(6.18)

Contracting with the Reeb vector field,

0=h\,\mathop{i}\nolimits(\mathcal{R})\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=(\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h}){\rm d}{\rm h})\mathop{i}\nolimits(\mathcal{R})\eta+h\,\mathop{i}\nolimits(\mathcal{R}){\rm d}h\ ,

from which
\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h}){\rm d}h=-h\,\mathop{i}\nolimits(\mathcal{R}){\rm d}h
,
and using this in (
6.18
), we get

h\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=h\big({\rm d}h-(\mathop{i}\nolimits(\mathcal{R}){\rm d}h)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\big)=h\big({\rm d}h-(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(\mathcal{R})h)\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\big)\ .

and hence
\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}={\rm d}h-(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(\mathcal{R})h)\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}
.

Conversely, if
{\rm X}_{h}
satisfies equations (
6.13
); then, taking into account (
6.16
),

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})\Omega

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})(-h{\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}+{\rm d}h\wedge\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})=-h\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}+(\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h}){\rm d}h)\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}+h{\rm d}h

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(\mathcal{R})h)\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}+(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})h)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=\big(h(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(\mathcal{R})h)+(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})h)\big)\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=0\ ,

From here, the equation for the integral curves is immediate.

This form of the dynamical equations is especially interesting in the case of singular systems because they do not depend on the Reeb vector field and, as we pointed out,
in a precontact manifold Reeb vector fields are not uniquely determined.

6.3
Contact Lagrangian dynamical systems

In this section, we review the Lagrangian formulation for contact systems
[
93
,
117
,
169
]
.

6.3.1
Contact Lagrangian systems

If
Q
is a
n
-dimensional manifold, consider the product manifold

{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}
equipped with adapted coordinates
(q^{i},v^{i},s)
.
Now, the canonical projections are denoted

s\colon{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\tau_{1}\colon{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\tau_{0}\colon{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q\times\mathbb{R}\ .

As it was discussed in Section
4.3.1
,
the canonical geometric structures of the tangent bundle
{\rm T}Q
,
namely the canonical endomorphism and the Liouville vector field,
are extended naturally to
{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}
.
We use the same notation as in that section
to denote them:
{\cal J}\in{\cal T}^{1}_{1}({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R})
and
\Delta\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R})
, respectively;
and they have the same coordinate expressions as usual,

\displaystyle{\cal J}=\frac{\partial}{\partial v^{i}}\otimes{\rm d}q^{i}
and
\displaystyle\Delta=v^{i}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial v^{i}}
.

Similarly, the canonical lift of a curve
{\bf c}\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow Q\times\mathbb{R}
to
{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}
,
with
{\bf c}=(\mathbf{c}^{i}(t),s(t))
,
is defined as in Section
4.3.1
, and it is

\displaystyle{\bf\widehat{c}}(t)=\Big(c^{i}(t),\frac{{\rm d}c^{i}}{{\rm d}t}(t),s(t)\Big)
.
Finally, the definitions of
holonomic curves
and
sode
vector fields in
{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}

are also as in Section
4.3.1
.

Then, the foundations of the Lagrangian formalism of the contact formulation for autonomous dissipative systems
are established through the reformulation of the generic postulates stated in Section
6.2.1
for contact Hamiltonian systems:

Postulate 6.23

(First Postulate of contact Lagrangian mechanics)
:
The configuration space of a dissipative dynamical system with
n
degrees of freedom
is
Q\times\mathbb{R}
, where
Q
is a
n
-dimensional differentiable manifold, and
n
are the degrees of freedom of the system.
The phase space is the bundle
{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}
.

Postulate 6.24

(Second Postulate of contact Lagrangian mechanics)
:
The observables or physical magnitudes of a
dissipative dynamical system are functions of
{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R})
.

Postulate 6.25

(Third Postulate of contact Lagrangian mechanics)
:
There is a function
{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R})
,
called the
contact Lagrangian function
,
which contains the dynamical information of the system.

Remark 6.26

In many cases, the contact Lagrangian function is of the following type:

{\cal L}=L+\phi
,
where
L=\tau_{1}^{\,*}L_{o}
for a function

L_{o}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q)
, and
\phi=\tau_{0}^{\,*}\phi_{o}
, for
\phi_{o}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(Q\times\mathbb{R})
;
that is, in coordinates,
{\cal L}(q^{i},v^{i},s)=L(q^{i},v^{i})+\phi(q^{i},s)
.
In particular, the case where
\phi(q^{i},s)=\gamma s
, with
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
,
appears very frequently in physical applications.

Starting from a Lagrangian function the following magnitudes and structures are defined:

Definition 6.27

Let
{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R})
be a Lagrangian function.

The
Lagrangian energy

associated with
{\cal L}
is the function
E_{\cal L}:=\Delta({\cal L})-{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R})
.

The
Cartan Lagrangian forms

associated with
{\cal L}
are defined as

\theta_{\cal L}:={\cal J}({\rm d}{\cal L})\in{\Omega}^{1}({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R})\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\omega_{\cal L}:=-{\rm d}\theta_{\cal L}\in{\Omega}^{2}({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R})\ .

(6.19)

The
(pre)contact Lagrangian form
is

\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{L}={\rm d}s-\theta_{\cal L}\in{\Omega}^{1}({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R})\ ,

and it satisfies that
{\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{L}=\omega_{L}
.

The pair
({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R},{\cal L})
is a
contact Lagrangian system
.

In natural coordinates
(q^{i},v^{i},s)
on
{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm d}s-\frac{\partial L}{\partial v^{i}}\,{\rm d}q^{i}\ ,

\displaystyle{\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial^{2}L}{\partial s\partial v^{i}}{\rm d}s\wedge{\rm d}q^{i}-\frac{\partial^{2}L}{\partial q^{j}\partial v^{i}}{\rm d}q^{j}\wedge{\rm d}q^{i}-\frac{\partial^{2}L}{\partial v^{j}\partial v^{i}}{\rm d}v^{j}\wedge{\rm d}q^{i}\ .

Definition 6.28

Given a Lagrangian

{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R})
, the
Legendre map

associated with
{\cal L}
is the fiber derivative of
{\cal L}
,
considered as a function on the vector bundle

\pi_{0}\colon{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q\times\mathbb{R}

that is, the map

\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}\colon{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}

given by

\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}(q,v_{q},s)=\left(\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}_{s}(q,v_{q}),s\right)\ ,

where
{\cal L}_{s}\colon{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}
denotes the restriction of
{\cal L}
to each fiber of the bundle
{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}

(that is, the Lagrangian
{\cal L}
with
s
“freezed”).

In natural coordinates, we have:

q^{i}\circ\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}=q^{i}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptp_{i}\circ\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}=\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pts\circ\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}=s\ .

Observe that, if
\theta\in{\Omega}^{1}({\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R})

is the canonical form and
\omega=-{\rm d}\theta
,

\theta_{\cal L}=\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}^{\;*}\theta\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\omega_{\cal L}=\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}^{\;*}\omega\ .

Proposition 6.29

For a Lagrangian function,
{\cal L}
the following conditions are equivalent:

1.

The Legendre map

\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}
is a local diffeomorphism.

2.

The pair
({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal L})
is a contact manifold.

3.

The Hessian matrix
\displaystyle W_{ij}\equiv\left(\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{i}\partial v^{j}}\right)
is nondegenerate everywhere.

(
Proof
)
The proof can be done using natural coordinates.

Definition 6.30

A Lagrangian function
{\cal L}
is said to be
regular
if the equivalent
conditions in the above Proposition
6.29
hold.
Otherwise,
{\cal L}
is called a
singular
Lagrangian.
In particular,

{\cal L}
is said to be
hyperregular

if
\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}
is a global diffeomorphism.

Remark 6.31

If
{\cal L}
is a regular Lagrangian, then

({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal L},E_{\cal L})

is a contact Hamiltonian system.
When
{\cal L}
is not regular, it can induce a precontact structure, but also a structure which is neither contact nor precontact.
For example,
the Lagrangian
\displaystyle{\cal L}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}v^{i}s
in
{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}

gives a form
\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal L}\in{\Omega}^{1}({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R})

for which the condition (
6.1
) does not hold
and that has no Reeb vector fields associated to it
(see Remark
6.5
).

Definition 6.32

If
{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R})
is a Lagrangian function and
({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal L})
is
a contact or a precontact manifold, then
the pair
({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R},{\cal L})
is said to be a
(pre)contact Lagrangian dynamical system
.

Given a contact Lagrangian system
({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R},{\cal L})
, the Reeb vector field
\mathcal{R}_{\cal L}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R})
is uniquely determined by the conditions

\mathop{i}\nolimits(\mathcal{R}_{\cal L}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal L}=0\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits(\mathcal{R}_{\cal L})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal L}=1\ ,

(6.20)

and its local expression is

\mathcal{R}_{\cal L}=\frac{\partial}{\partial s}-W^{ji}\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial s\partial v^{j}}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial v^{i}}\,,

(6.21)

where
(W^{ij})
is the inverse of the partial Hessian matrix,
namely

W^{ij}W_{jk}=\delta^{i}_{k}
.
Observe that the Reeb vector field does not appear
in the simplest form
\displaystyle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{s}}
,
since the natural coordinates in
{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}

are neither adapted nor Darboux coordinates for
\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal L}
.
If
({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R},{\cal L})
is a precontact Lagrangian system then Reeb vector fields are not uniquely defined.

Finally, we state:

Postulate 6.33

(Fourth Postulate of contact Lagrangian mechanics)
:
The dynamical trajectories of the system are the integral curves
of a vector field
{\rm X}_{\cal L}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R})
satisfying the conditions:

1.

{\rm X}_{\cal L}
is the Hamiltonian vector field associated with
E_{\cal L}
;
that is, it is a solution to the equations

\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{\cal L})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal L}=-E_{\cal L}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{\cal L}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal L}={\rm d}E_{\cal L}-(\mathcal{R}_{\cal L}(E_{\cal L}))\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal L}\ ,

(6.22)

where
\mathcal{R}_{\cal L}
is a Reeb vector field, which is determined by the equations (
6.20
).

2.

{\rm X}_{\cal L}
is a
sode
:

{\cal J}({\rm X}_{\cal L})=\Delta
.

Therefore, these trajectories are the holonomic curves

{\bf c}\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}

which are the solutions to the equations

\mathop{i}\nolimits({\bf\widetilde{c}})(\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal L}\circ{\bf c})=-E_{\cal L}\circ{\bf c}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits({\bf\widetilde{c}})({\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal L}\circ{\bf c})=\big({\rm d}E_{\cal L}-(\mathcal{R}_{\cal L}(E_{\cal L}))\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal L}\big)\circ{\bf c}.

(6.23)

Equations (
6.23
) are called the

(pre)contact Euler–Lagrange equations for curves
.
Equations (
6.22
) are called the

(pre)contact Lagrangian equations for vector fields
and
a vector field solution to them (if it exists) is a

(pre)contact Lagrangian dynamical vector field
.
If, in addition,
{\rm X}_{\cal L}
is a
sode
, then
it is called a
(pre)contact Euler–Lagrange vector field
of the system,

Definition 6.34

Given a (pre)contact Lagrangian dynamical system
({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R},{\cal L})
,
the
Lagrangian problem
posed by this system
consists in finding a
sode
vector field
{\rm X}_{\cal L}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R})

solutions to (
6.22
).

6.3.2
The contact Euler–Lagrange equations

First, using (
6.21
), a simple computation in coordinates shows that

\mathcal{R}_{\cal L}(E_{\cal L})=-\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{s}}\ .

(6.24)

Taking this into account, in natural coordinates, for a holonomic curve

{\bf c}(t)=(q^{i}(t),\dot{q}^{i}(t),s(t))
on
{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}
,
equations (
6.23
) are the
Herglotz–Euler–Lagrange equations
,

\displaystyle\frac{ds}{dt}

\displaystyle={\cal L}\ ,

(6.25)

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\left(\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\right)-\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial q^{i}}=\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{j}\partial v^{i}}\,\frac{d^{2}q^{j}}{dt^{2}}+\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial q^{j}\partial v^{i}}\,\frac{dq^{j}}{dt}+\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}L}{\partial s\partial v^{i}}\,\frac{ds}{dt}-\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial q^{i}}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial s}\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial v^{i}}\ .

(6.26)

Furthermore, for a vector field

{\rm X}_{\cal L}=\displaystyle f^{i}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial q^{i}}+F^{i}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial v^{i}}+g\,\frac{\partial}{\partial s}
,
equations (
6.22
) lead to

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\cal L}+\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial v^{i}}(f^{i}-v^{i})-g

\displaystyle=0\ ,

(6.27)

\displaystyle\left(f^{j}-v^{j}\right)\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial q^{i}\partial v^{j}}+\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial q^{i}}-\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial s\partial v^{i}}g-\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial q^{j}\partial v^{i}}f^{j}-\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{j}\partial v^{i}}F^{j}+\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial s}\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial v^{i}}

\displaystyle=0\ ,

(6.28)

\displaystyle\left(f^{j}-v^{j}\right)\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{i}\partial v^{j}}

\displaystyle=0\ ,

(6.29)

\displaystyle\left(f^{j}-v^{j}\right)\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{j}\partial s}

\displaystyle=0\ ,

(6.30)

and then it is immediate to prove that:

Proposition 6.35

If
{\cal L}
is a regular Lagrangian, then
{\rm X}_{\cal L}
is a
sode
and
the equations (
6.27
) and (
6.28
) become

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle={\cal L}\,,

(6.31)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{j}\partial v^{i}}\,F^{j}+\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial q^{j}\partial v^{i}}\,v^{j}+\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial s\partial v^{i}}\,{\cal L}-\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial q^{i}}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial s}\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial v^{i}}\ .

(6.32)

which, for the integral curves of
{\rm X}_{\cal L}
, give
the equations (
6.25
) and (
6.26
).

(
Proof
)
In fact, if
{\cal L}
is a regular Lagrangian, equations (
6.29
) lead to
v^{i}=f^{i}
,
which are the
sode
condition for the vector field
{\rm X}_{\cal L}
.
Then, (
6.30
) holds identically, and
(
6.27
) and (
6.28
) give the equations (
6.31
) and (
6.32
)
and then, for the integral curves of
X_{L}
, we get equations (
6.25
) and (
6.26
).

Thus, the local expression of this Euler–Lagrange vector field is

{\rm X}_{\cal L}=v^{i}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial q^{i}}+W^{ik}\left(\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial q^{k}}-\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial q^{j}\partial v^{k}}\,v^{j}-{\cal L}\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial s\partial v^{k}}+\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial s}\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial v^{k}}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial v^{i}}+{\cal L}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\ .

(6.33)

Remark 6.36

It is important to point out that
the expression in coordinates (
6.25
) of
the second Lagrangian equation (
6.22
) relates the variation
of the “dissipation coordinate”
s
to the Lagrangian function and, from here,
we can identify this coordinate with the Lagrangian action,
\displaystyle s=\int{\cal L}\,dt
.

Remark 6.37

If
{\cal L}
is a singular Lagrangian, but
({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal L})
is a precontact manifold, although the Reeb vector fields
are not uniquely defined, the Lagrangian equations (
6.22
)
are independent on the Reeb vector field used (see
[
117
]
).
Alternatively, the Reeb independent equations (
6.17
)
for the precontact Hamiltonian system
({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal L},E_{\cal L})

can be used instead.
In any case, solutions to the Lagrangian equations
are not necessarily
sode
and
the condition
{\cal J}({\rm X}_{\cal L})=\Delta
must be added to the Lagrangian equations.
Furthermore, these equations are not necessarily compatible everywhere on
{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}

and a constraint algorithm must be implemented in order to find
a final constraint submanifold
S_{f}\hookrightarrow{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}

(if it exists) where there are
sode
vector fields
{\rm X}_{\cal L}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R})
,
tangent to
S_{f}
, which are solutions to the above equations on
S_{f}

[
117
]
.

6.3.3
Canonical Hamiltonian formalism

Consider a hyperregular Lagrangian system
({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R},{\cal L})

(the regular case is the same, taking
\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R})\subset{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}
instead of
{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}
, or locally, at least).
Then,
\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}
is a diffeomorphism
between the contact manifolds
({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal L})
and
the canonical contact manifold

({\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})
,
with

\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}^{\;*}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal L}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}_{*}{\mathcal{R}}_{\cal L}={\mathcal{R}}\ .

Furthermore, there exists a function
{\rm h}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R})

such that
\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}^{\;*}{\rm h}=E_{\cal L}
;
so we have the contact Hamiltonian system
({\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$},{\rm h})

and the contact Hamiltonian equations (
6.13
)
(or their equivalent expressions (
6.15
) or
(
6.17
)),
which read as,

\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{\rm h})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=-{\rm h}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{\rm h}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}={\rm d}{\rm h}-\mathcal{R}({\rm h})\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\ ,

(6.34)

and have a unique solution
{\rm X}_{\rm h}
.
The dynamical trajectories are the integral curves
of this contact Hamiltonian vector field and, then, they are the solutions to equations (
6.14
), which read as,

\displaystyle\frac{dx^{i}}{dt}=\frac{\partial{\rm h}}{\partial p_{i}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\displaystyle\frac{dp_{i}}{dt}=-\left(\frac{\partial{\rm h}}{\partial x^{i}}+p_{i}\frac{\partial{\rm h}}{\partial s}\right)\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\displaystyle\frac{ds}{dt}=p_{i}\frac{\partial{\rm h}}{\partial p_{i}}-h\ ,

(6.35)

Then, if
{\rm X}_{\rm h}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R})
is the
contact Hamiltonian vector field associated with
{\rm h}
,
we have that
\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}_{*}{\rm X}_{\cal L}={\rm X}_{\rm h}
.

For singular Lagrangians, as in the above chapters, we define:

Definition 6.38

A singular Lagrangian function
{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R})

is called
almost-regular
if
\mathcal{P}:=\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R})
is
a closed submanifold of
{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}
(
the natural embedding is denoted
\jmath_{\cal P}\colon\mathcal{P}\hookrightarrow{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}
),

\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}
is a submersion onto its image, and
the fibers
\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}^{-1}(FL({\rm p}))
, for every
{\rm p}\in{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}
, are
connected submanifolds of
{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}
.

In these cases, we have
({\cal P},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal P})
, where

\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal P}=j_{\cal P}^{*}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\in\Omega^{1}({\cal P})
,
Furthermore, the Lagrangian energy function
E_{\cal L}
is
\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}
-projectable;
i.e., there is a unique
{\rm h}_{\cal P}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\cal P})

which is
\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}
-related with
E_{\cal L}
.
Then, if
({\cal P},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal P})

is a contact or a precontact manifold,
then
({\cal P},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal P},{\rm h}_{\cal P})

is a (pre)contact Hamiltonian system.
whose (pre)contact Hamiltonian equations are (
6.13
) or, alternatively, (
6.17
),
adapted to this situation.
As in the Lagrangian formalism, these equations are not necessarily compatible everywhere on
{\cal P}

and a constraint algorithm must be implemented in order to find
a final constraint submanifold
P_{f}\hookrightarrow{\cal P}

(if it exists) where there are vector fields
{\rm X}_{{\rm h}_{\cal P}}\in\mathfrak{X}({\cal P})
,
tangent to
P_{f}
, which are solutions to the precontact Hamiltonian equations on
P_{f}
(see
[
117
]
for a detailed analysis on all these topics).

6.4
Unified Lagrangian-Hamiltonian formalism for contact systems

The Lagrangian-Hamiltonian unified formalism
of contact Lagrangian systems has been developed in
[
114
]
.
Next we present its main features.

6.4.1
Extended precontact unified bundle. Unified formalism

Definition 6.39

Let
Q
be a
n
-dimensional differentiable manifold.
The
extended precontact unified bundle

(or
extended precontact Pontryagin bundle
) is

\mathfrak{M}={\rm T}Q\times_{Q}{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}\ ,

and it is endowed with the natural projections

\rho_{1}\colon\mathfrak{M}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise \rho_{2}\colon\mathfrak{M}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern \rho_{0}\colon\mathfrak{M}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q\times\mathbb{R}\ ,\ s\colon\mathfrak{M}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}\ .

Natural coordinates in
\mathfrak{M}
are
(q^{i},v^{i},p_{i},s)
.

Definition 6.40

A curve
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}

is a
holonomic
in
\mathfrak{M}
if

\rho_{1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}
is holonomic curve.
A vector field
\Gamma\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{M})

satisfies the
second-order condition
in
\mathfrak{M}

(for short: it is a
sode
in
\mathfrak{M}
) if all of its integral curves
are holonomic in
\mathfrak{M}
.

In coordinates, a holonomic curve and a
sode
in
\mathfrak{M}
are expressed as

\sigma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\sigma_{1}^{i}(t),\frac{d\sigma_{1}^{i}}{dt}(t),\sigma_{2\,i}(t),\sigma_{0}(t)\right)\ ,

\displaystyle\Gamma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial q^{i}}+F^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial v^{i}}+G_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{i}}+f\,\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\ .

Definition 6.41

The bundle
\mathfrak{M}
is endowed with the following canonical structures:

1.

The
coupling function
in
\mathfrak{M}
is the
map
\mathfrak{C}\colon\mathfrak{M}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}
defined as follows: for every

w=({\rm v}_{q},{\rm p}_{q},s)\in\mathfrak{M}
, where
q\in Q
,

{\rm p}_{q}\in{\rm T}^{*}Q
, and
{\rm v}_{q}\in{\rm T}Q
, then

\mathfrak{C}(w):=\langle{\rm p}_{q},{\rm v}_{q}\rangle
.

2.

The
canonical precontact
1
-form
on
\mathfrak{M}

is the
\rho_{1}
-semibasic form

\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\mathfrak{M}}:=\rho_{2}^{*}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=ds-\pi_{1}^{*}\rho_{2}^{*}\pi_{1}^{*}\Theta\in{\Omega}^{1}(\mathfrak{M})\ ;

where
\eta
is the canonical contact form on
{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}

(and
\Theta
is the canonical
1
-form on

{\rm T}^{*}Q
).

In natural coordinates of
\mathfrak{M}
we have that

\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\mathfrak{M}}={\rm d}s-p_{i}\,{\rm d}q^{i}
.

Definition 6.42

Given a Lagrangian function
{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R})
, let

\mathfrak{L}=\rho_{1}^{*}{\cal L}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{M})
.
The
Hamiltonian function
is defined as

{\bf H}:=\mathfrak{C}-\mathfrak{L}=p_{i}v^{i}-{\cal L}(q^{j},v^{j},s)\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{M})\ .

(6.36)

Remark 6.43

Observe that
(\mathfrak{M},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\mathfrak{M}})
is a precontact manifold.
As a consequence, the Reeb vector fields are not uniquely defined and
in natural coordinates of
\mathfrak{M}
the general solution to (
6.2
) are the vector fields

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{z}}+F^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}

for arbitrary functions
F^{i}
.
Nevertheless, as we have pointed out,
the dynamics obtained from the formalism is independent of the choice of the Reeb vector fields;
therefore, as
\mathfrak{M}={\rm T}Q\times_{Q}{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}

is a trivial bundle over
\mathbb{R}
,
the canonical vector field
\displaystyle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{s}}
on
\mathbb{R}

can be lifted canonically to a vector field in
\mathfrak{M}
,
which can be taken as a representative of the family of
these Reeb vector fields.

Thus, we have that
(\mathfrak{M},\eta_{\mathfrak{M}},{\bf H})

is a precontact Hamiltonian system and then,
we can pose the dynamic problem for this system which consists of finding
a Hamiltonian vector field which is a solution to the precontact Hamiltonian equations

\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{\bf H}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\mathfrak{M}}={\rm d}{\bf H}-({\mathcal{R}}({\bf H}))\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\mathfrak{M}}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{\bf H})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\mathfrak{M}}=-{\bf H}\ .

(6.37)

Then, the integral curves
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\colon I\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathfrak{M}
of
X_{\bf H}
,
are the solutions to the equations

\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}})({\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\mathfrak{M}}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$})=\left({\rm d}{\bf H}-({\mathcal{R}}({\bf H}))\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\mathfrak{M}}\right)\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}})(\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\mathfrak{M}}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$})=-{\bf H}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\ .

(6.38)

As we shall see next,
these equations are not compatible on
\mathfrak{M}
,
and we have to implement the constraint algorithm
in order to find the final constraint submanifold of
\mathfrak{M}

where there are consistent solutions
to the equations.
In fact, in natural coordinates of
\mathfrak{M}
we have that

{\rm d}{\bf H}=v^{i}{\rm d}p_{i}+\left(p_{i}-\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\right){\rm d}v^{i}-\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\,{\rm d}q^{i}-\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{s}}\,{\rm d}s\ ,

and, if the local expression of
a vector field
{\rm X}_{\bf H}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{M})
is

{\rm X}_{\bf H}=f^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}+F^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}+G_{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{i}}}+f\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{s}}\ ,

we obtain that

\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{\bf H})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\mathfrak{M}}=f-f^{i}p_{i}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{\bf H}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\mathfrak{M}}=f^{i}\,{\rm d}p_{i}-G_{i}\,{\rm d}q^{i}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt({\mathcal{R}}({\bf H}))\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\mathfrak{M}}=-\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{s}}({\rm d}s-p_{i}{\rm d}q^{i})\ .

Then, equations (
6.37
) give

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(f^{i}-v^{i})\,p_{i}+{\cal L}\ ,

(6.39)

\displaystyle f^{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v^{i}\ ,

(6.40)

\displaystyle p_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\ ,

(6.41)

\displaystyle G_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}+p_{i}\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{s}}\ ,

(6.42)

The equations (
6.40
) are the holonomy conditions
(i.e.,
{\rm X}_{\bf H}
must be a
sode
),
which arise straightforwardly from the unified formalism.
The algebraic equations (
6.41
) are compatibility conditions
defining the submanifold
\mathfrak{M}_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{M}
,
where the equations are compatible.
This
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
is the
first constraint submanifold
of the Hamiltonian precontact system
(\mathfrak{M},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\mathfrak{M}},{\bf H})
, and is the graph of
\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}
; that is,

\mathfrak{M}_{0}=\{({\rm v}_{q},\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}({\rm v}_{q}))\in\mathfrak{M}\ ,\ \mbox{\rm for ${\rm v}_{q}\in{\rm T}Q$}\}\ .

In this way, as it is usual, the unified formalism includes the definition of
the Legendre map as a consequence of the constraint algorithm.

Thus, vector fields which are solutions to (
6.37
) are,

{\rm X}_{\bf H}=v^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}+F^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}+\left(\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}+p_{i}\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{z}}\right)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{i}}}+{\cal L}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{s}}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{\rm(on $\mathfrak{M}_{0}$)}\ .

The constraint algorithm continues by demanding that

{\rm X}_{\bf H}
must be tangent to
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
. As

\displaystyle\xi^{1}_{j}=p_{j}-\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{j}}}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{M})

are the constraints defining
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
, this condition is

{\rm X}_{\bf H}\left(p_{j}-\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{j}}}\right)=-\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial q^{i}\partial v^{j}}v^{i}-\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial v^{i}\partial v^{j}}F^{i}-{\cal L}\frac{\partial^{2}{\cal L}}{\partial z\partial v^{j}}+\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{j}}}+p_{j}\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{s}}=0\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{\rm(on $\mathfrak{M}_{0}$)}\ ,

(6.43)

and there are two options:

•

If
{\cal L}
is a regular Lagrangian, these equations determine all the
functions
F^{i}
.
Then the solution is unique and the algorithm ends.

•

If
{\cal L}
is singular, then these equations establish relations
among the arbitrary functions
F^{i}
;
some of them remain undetermined and the solutions are not unique.
Furthermore, new constraints
\xi^{2}_{\mu}\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{M})

could appear, then defining a new submanifold

\mathfrak{M}_{1}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{M}
.
Then, the algorithm continues by demanding the tangency of

{\rm X}_{\bf H}
to
\mathfrak{M}_{1}
, and so on,
until we obtain a final constraint submanifold
\mathfrak{M}_{f}
(if it exists)
where tangent solutions
X_{\bf H}
exist.

If
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}(t)=(q^{i}(t),v^{i}(t),p_{i}(t),s(t))

is an integral curve of
{\rm X}_{\bf H}
, we have that

\displaystyle f^{i}=\frac{dq^{i}}{dt}
,

\displaystyle F^{i}=\frac{dv^{i}}{dt}
,

\displaystyle G_{i}=\frac{dp_{i}}{dt}
,

\displaystyle f=\frac{ds}{dt}
,
and equations (
6.39
), (
6.40
), (
6.41
), and (
6.42
)
give to the coordinate expression of the equations
(
6.38
); in particular:

•

From (
6.40
), we have that
\displaystyle v^{i}=\frac{dq^{i}}{dt}
;
that is, the holonomy condition.

•

Using (
6.40
) again, the equation (
6.39
) gives the equation (
6.25
) again.

•

The equations (
6.42
) read,

\frac{dp_{i}}{dt}=\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}+p_{i}\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{s}}=-\left(\frac{\partial{{\bf H}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}+p_{i}\frac{\partial{{\bf H}}}{\partial{s}}\right)\ ,

which are the second group of Hamilton’s equations (
6.12
).
Then, using (
6.41
),
these equations are (on
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
),

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\right)=\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}+\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{s}}\ ,

which are the Herglotz–Euler-Lagrange equations (
6.26
).
The first group of Hamilton’s equations (
6.12
) arises
from the definition of the Hamiltonian function
(
6.36
), using the holonomy condition.

•

Using (
6.41
) and (
6.25
),
the tangency condition (
6.43
) gives
again the Herglotz–Euler-Lagrange equations (
6.26
).
If
{\cal L}
is singular, these equations can be incompatible.

6.4.2
Recovering the Lagrangian and Hamiltonian formalisms and equivalence

Now we study the equivalence of the unified formalism
with the Lagrangian and Hamiltonian formalisms
for the hyperregular case
(the regular case is the same, at least locally).
See
[
114
]
more details about the singular case.

First, observe that, in this case, denoting by

\jmath_{0}\colon\mathfrak{M}_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{M}

the natural embedding, we have that

(\rho_{1}\circ\jmath_{0})(\mathfrak{M}_{0})={\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt(\rho_{2}\circ\jmath_{0})(\mathfrak{M}_{0})={\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}\ .

Furthermore, as
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
is the graph of the Legendre map

\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}
, it is diffeomorphic to
{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}
,
being the restricted projection
\rho_{1}\circ\jmath_{0}

this diffeomorphism.
So, we have the diagram

\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{2}}

\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\jmath_{0}}

\textstyle{{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}}

\textstyle{{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}}

As in the other unified formalisms,
functions, differential forms on
\mathfrak{M}
,
and vector fields on
\mathfrak{M}
tangent to
\mathfrak{M}_{0}

can be restricted to
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
.
Then, they can be translated to the Lagrangian or the Hamiltonian side
by using that
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
is diffeomorphic to
{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}
,
or projecting to
{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}
.
In particular, a simple calculation in coordinates shows that

\rho_{1}^{*}E_{L}={\bf H}=\rho_{2}^{*}{\rm h}\ ,

Furthermore,
every curve
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\colon I\subseteq\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathfrak{M}

which takes values in
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
, can be split as

\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}=(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}_{L},\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}_{H})
, where

\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}_{L}=\rho_{1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\colon I\subseteq\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}

and
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}_{H}=\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}_{L}\colon I\subseteq\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}
.
Therefore, taking all of this into account, the results, and the discussion in the above section
lead to state:

Theorem 6.44

Let
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathfrak{M}
,
with
{\rm Im}\,(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$})\subset\mathfrak{M}_{0}
,
be a curve solution to the equations (
6.38
)
on
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
.
Then

\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}_{L}
is the lift of the projected curve

{\bf c}=\rho_{0}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q\times\mathbb{R}

to
{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}
(that is,
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}_{L}
is
a holonomic curve),
and it is a solution to the equations (
6.23
).
Moreover, the curve

\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}_{H}=\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}\circ\widetilde{\bf c}

is a solution to the equations (
6.8
) on
{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}
.

Conversely, for every curve
{\bf c}\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q\times\mathbb{R}
such that

\widetilde{\bf c}
is a solution to the equations (
6.23
), we have that the curve

\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}=(\widetilde{\bf c},\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}\circ\widetilde{\bf c})

is a solution to the equations (
6.38
) on
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
,
and the curve
\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}\circ\widetilde{\bf c}

is a solution to the equations (
6.8
) on
{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}
.

The curves
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathfrak{M}

solution to the equations (
6.38
)
are the integral curves of
sode
vector fields
{\rm X}_{\bf H}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{M})

solution to (
6.37
) on
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
.
The curves
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}_{\cal L}\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}

are the integral curves of
sode
vector fields
{\rm X}_{\cal L}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R})

solution to (
6.23
).
Finally, the curves

\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}_{\rm h}\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}

are the integral curves of the contact Hamiltonian vector field
{\rm X}_{\rm h}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R})

solution to (
6.34
) on
{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}
.
Then, an immediate corollary of the above theorem is the following:

Theorem 6.45

Let
{\rm X}_{\bf H}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{M})
be the contact Hamiltonian vector field
solution to the equations (
6.37
)
on
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
, and tangent to

\mathfrak{M}_{0}
.

Then the vector field
{\rm X}_{\cal L}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R})
, defined by

{\rm X}_{\cal L}\circ\rho_{1}={\rm T}\rho_{1}\circ{\rm X}_{\bf H}
,
is a
sode
vector field which is a
solution to the equations (
6.22
); that is, the contact Euler-Lagrange vector field,
on
{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}
.

Furthermore, the vector field
{\rm X}_{\rm h}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R})
, defined by

{\rm X}_{\rm h}\circ\rho_{2}={\rm T}\rho_{2}\circ X_{\bf H}
,
is the contact Hamiltonian vector field solution to the equations (
6.34
) on
{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}
.

These results are analogous to those obtained for the unified
formalism of nonautonomous dynamical systems.

Remark 6.46

For singular almost-regular Lagrangians, the equivalence between the constraint algorithms
in the unified and in the Lagrangian formalism
only holds when the second-order condition is imposed,
since, unlike in the unified formalism, this condition does not hold in the Lagrangian case (see
[
281
,
322
]
).

6.5
Symmetries of contact dynamical systems

(See
[
169
]
and also
[
118
]
for another complementary approach to these topics. See also
[
172
]
for a complete classification of symmetries of autonomous and nonautonomous contact systems, and
[
8
]

for a reduction scheme).

6.5.1
Symmetries of contact Hamiltonian systems. Dissipated and conserved quantities

Let
(M,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$},h)
be a contact Hamiltonian system with Reeb vector field
\mathcal{R}
,
and let
{\rm X}_{h}
be the contact Hamiltonian vector field for this system;
that is, the solution to the Hamilton equations (
6.4
).

As, for other kinds of dynamical systems,
a
dynamical symmetry
of a contact Hamiltonian system is a diffeomorphism

\Phi\colon M\longrightarrow M
such that
\Phi_{*}{\rm X}_{h}=X_{h}
.
Similarly, an
infinitesimal dynamical symmetry
of a contact Hamiltonian system is a vector field

Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
whose local flux is a dynamical symmetry; that is,

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y){\rm X}_{h}=[Y,{\rm X}_{h}]=0
.
This means that dynamical symmetries map solutions into solutions.

Nevertheless, we are mainly interested in those symmetries that let
the geometric structures invariant:

Definition 6.47

A
contact Noether symmetry
of a contact Hamiltonian system
(M,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$},h)
is a diffeomorphism

\Phi\colon M\rightarrow M
such that

\Phi^{*}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\Phi^{*}h=h\ .

An
infinitesimal contact Noether symmetry
of a contact Hamiltonian system
(M,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$},h)
is a vector field

Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
whose local flux is a contact Noether symmetry; that is,

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=0\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)h=0\ .

Proposition 6.48

Every (infinitesimal) contact Noether symmetry preserves the Reeb vector field; that is,
\Phi_{*}\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}

(or
[Y,\mathcal{R}]=0
).

(
Proof
)
We obtain that,

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(\Phi^{-1}_{*}\mathcal{R})(\Phi^{*}{\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Phi^{*}(\mathop{i}\nolimits(\mathcal{R}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})=0\ ,

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(\Phi^{-1}_{*}\mathcal{R})(\Phi^{*}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Phi^{*}(\mathop{i}\nolimits(\mathcal{R})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})=1\ ,

and, as
\Phi^{*}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}
and the Reeb vector field is unique,
from these equalities we get
\Phi^{-1}_{*}\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}
or, equivalently,

\Phi_{*}\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}
.

For the infinitesimal case, if
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
is an infinitesimal Noether symmetry, we have:

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits([Y,\mathcal{R}])\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(\mathcal{R})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}-\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(\mathcal{R})\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\mathop{i}\nolimits(\mathcal{R}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}+{\rm d}\mathop{i}\nolimits(\mathcal{R})\eta=0\ ,

(6.44)

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits([Y,\mathcal{R}]){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm d}\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits([Y,\mathcal{R}])\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=0\ ,

hence
[Y,\mathcal{R}]=\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\mathcal{R}\in\ker\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\cap\ker{\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=\{0\}
.

As in the dynamical systems studied in the previous chapters,
we have:

Proposition 6.49

Every (infinitesimal) contact Noether symmetry is a (infinitesimal) dynamical symmetry.

(
Proof
)
In fact, using that
\Phi
is a contact Noether symmetry and that, by the above proposition,
\Phi_{*}\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(\Phi^{-1}_{*}{\rm X}_{h}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(\Phi^{-1}_{*}{\rm X}_{h})(\Phi^{*}{\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})=\Phi^{*}(\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h}){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})=\Phi^{*}({\rm d}h-(\mathcal{R}(h))\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})={\rm d}h-(\mathcal{R}(h))\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(\Phi^{-1}_{*}{\rm X}_{h})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits(\Phi^{-1}_{*}{\rm X}_{h})(\Phi^{*}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})=\Phi^{*}(\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})=\Phi^{*}(-h)=-h\ ,

then
\Phi^{-1}_{*}{\rm X}_{h}
is a solution to the dynamical equation (
6.34
) and,
as the solution is unique, we conclude that
\Phi_{*}{\rm X}_{h}={\rm X}_{h}
, and
hence
\Phi
is a symmetry.

For the infinitesimal case we have that,

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits([Y,{\rm X}_{h}])\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}-\mathop{i}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=-\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)h=0\ ,

\displaystyle\mathop{i}\nolimits([Y,{\rm X}_{h}]){\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm d}\mathop{i}\nolimits([Y,{\rm X}_{h}])\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=0\ ,

then
[Y,{\rm X}_{h}]\in\ker\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\cap\ker{\rm d}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=\{0\}

and it is a dynamical symmetry.

Now, the result stated in Proposition
6.16
,

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})h=-(\mathcal{R}(h))\,h
,
induces us to define:

Definition 6.50

A
dissipated quantity
of the contact Hamiltonian system
(M,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$},h)
is a function

F\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
satisfying that

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})F=-(\mathcal{R}(h))\,F\ .

(6.45)

A
conserved quantity
of the contact Hamiltonian system
(M,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$},h)
is a function

G\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
satisfying that

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})G=0\ .

For contact Hamiltonian systems,
as we are dealing with dissipative systems,
symmetries are associated with dissipated
quantities:

Theorem 6.51

(Dissipation theorem).

If
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
is an infinitesimal dynamical symmetry,
then the function
F=-\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}

is a dissipated quantity.

(
Proof
)
In fact, Proposition
6.49
says that
[Y,{\rm X}_{h}]=0
; then,

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})F

\displaystyle=-\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=-\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}-\mathop{i}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})Y)\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{R}(h))\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}+\mathop{i}\nolimits([Y,{\rm X}_{h}])\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=-(\mathcal{R}(h))\,F+\mathop{i}\nolimits([Y,{\rm X}_{h}])\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=-(\mathcal{R}(h))\,F\ ,

where we have applied that
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=-\mathcal{R}(h))\,\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}
(by (
6.15
)).

Remark 6.52

In particular, the Hamiltonian vector field
{\rm X}_{h}
is
trivially a symmetry, since
[{\rm X}_{h},{\rm X}_{h}]=0
.
Then, Proposition (
6.16
)
establishes that its associated dissipated quantity is the energy,

h=-\mathop{i}\nolimits(X_{h})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}
.

These are “non conservation theorems”
which account for the non-conservation of these quantities associated with the symmetries.
In particular, every dissipated quantity changes with the same rate,
-\mathcal{R}(h)
,
which suggests that the quotient of two dissipated quantities should be a conserved quantity.
Indeed:

Proposition 6.53

1.

If
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are dissipated quantities and
F_{2}\not=0
, then
\displaystyle\frac{F_{1}}{F_{2}}
is a conserved quantity.

2.

If
F
is a dissipated quantity and
G
is a conserved quantity, then
FG
is a dissipated quantity.

(
Proof
)
In fact, we have

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})\left(\frac{F_{1}}{F_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{F_{2}}\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})F_{1}-\frac{F_{1}}{{F_{2}}^{2}}\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h}){F_{2}}=-\frac{1}{F_{2}}(\mathcal{R}(h))F_{1}+\frac{F_{1}}{{F_{2}}^{2}}(\mathcal{R}(h))F_{2}=0\ .

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})(FG)

\displaystyle=G\,\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})F+F\,\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})G=-(\mathcal{R}(h))\,FG.

A straightforward consequence of this proposition is that symmetries can also have associated conserved quantities:

Corollary 6.54

For every infinitesimal symmetry
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
,
if
h\not=0
, then
\displaystyle-\frac{\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}{h}
is a conserved quantity.

Contact symmetries also allow us to generate new dissipated quantities
from another one.

Proposition 6.55

If
\Phi\colon M\rightarrow M
is a contact Noether symmetry and

F\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(M)
is a dissipated quantity, then so is
\Phi^{*}F
.
Similarly, if
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
is an infinitesimal contact Noether symmetry, then

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)F
is a dissipated quantity.

(
Proof
)
In fact, we have

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})(\Phi^{*}F)=\Phi^{*}\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(\Phi_{*}{\rm X}_{h})F=\Phi^{*}\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})F=\Phi^{*}(-\mathcal{R}(h))F=-(\mathcal{R}(h))(\Phi^{*}F)\ .

For the infinitesimal case, taking into account that
Y
is a contact infinitesimal
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})F=0
, that
[Y,{\rm X}_{h}]={\rm X}_{h}
(Proposition
6.49
) and that
[Y,\mathcal{R}]=0
(Proposition
6.48
), we have:

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)F

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits([{\rm X}_{h},Y])F-\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})F+-\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\big((\mathcal{R}(h))\,F\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{h})F-F\,\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)(\mathcal{R}(h))-(\mathcal{R}(h))(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)F)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-F\,\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits([Y,\mathcal{R}])h-F\,\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(\mathcal{R})\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)h-(\mathcal{R}(h))(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)F)=-(\mathcal{R}(h))(\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)F)\ .

6.5.2
Symmetries for contact Lagrangian and canonical Hamiltonian systems

Next, we particularize the results on symmetries and dissipated quantities
to the case of Lagrangian dissipative systems and their canonical Hamiltonian formalism.

Consider a regular contact Lagrangian system
({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R},{\cal L})
,
with Reeb vector field
\mathcal{R}_{\cal L}
,
and let
{\rm X}_{\cal L}
be the contact Euler–Lagrange vector field for this system;
that is, the solution to the Lagrangian equations (
6.22
).

All the definitions and results about symmetries and dissipated quantities stated in the preceding section
hold for the contact system
({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal L},E_{\cal L})
.
In particular, the dissipation theorem states that

-\mathop{i}\nolimits(Y)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal L}
is a dissipated quantity, for every infinitesimal dynamical symmetry
Y\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R})
,
the energy dissipation theorem states that

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{\cal L})E_{\cal L}=-(\mathcal{R}_{\cal L}(E_{\cal L}))\,E_{\cal L}\ .

(6.46)

Furthermore,
if
\varphi\colon Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
is a diffeomorphism,
we can construct the
canonical lift
of
\varphi
to
{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}
as the diffeomorphism

\Phi:=({\rm T}\varphi,{\rm{\rm Id}_{\mathbb{R}}})\colon{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}
.
These kinds of diffeomorfisms
\Phi
are usually called
natural transformations
on
{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}
.
Similarly, for every vector field
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)

we can define its
complete lift

to
{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}
as the vector field

Y\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R})

whose local flux is the canonical lift of
the local flux of
Z
to
{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}
.
The infinitesimal transformation generated by
Y
are called
infinitesimal natural transformation

on
{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}
.

Then, taking into account the definitions
of the canonical endomorphism
{\cal J}

and the Liouville vector field
\Delta
in
{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}
,
it can be proved that canonical lifts of diffeomorphisms and vector fields
from
Q
to
{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}
preserve these canonical structures.
Furthermore, if these lifts leave the Lagrangian function invariant,
they also preserve the Reeb vector field
\mathcal{R}_{\cal L}
.
Therefore, as an immediate consequence,
we obtain the following relation:

Proposition 6.56

If
\Phi\in{\rm Diff}({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R})
(resp.
Y\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R})
) is a canonical lift
to
{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}
of a diffeomorphism
\varphi\in{\rm Diff}(Q)

(resp. of a vector field
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
)
that leaves the Lagrangian invariant, then
it is a (infinitesimal) contact Noether symmetry, i.e.,

\Phi^{*}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal L}=\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal L}\ ,\ \Phi^{*}E_{\cal L}=E_{\cal L}\hskip 20.00003pt({\rm resp.}\ \mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal L}=0\ ,\ \mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)E_{\cal L}=0\>)\ .

As a further consequence, it is a (infinitesimal) dynamical symmetry.

As a direct consequence, if
\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial q^{i}}=0
,
then
\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial q^{i}}

is an infinitesimal contact Noether symmetry, and its associated dissipated quantity
is the momentum

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial v^{i}}
;
that is,

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{\cal L})\left(\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial v^{i}}\right)=-(\mathcal{R}_{\cal L}(E_{\cal L}))\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial v^{i}}=\frac{\partial{{\cal L}}}{\partial{s}}\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial v^{i}}\ .

(See also
[
175
]
for a similar description).

For the canonical Hamiltonian formalism, consider
the canonical contact Hamiltonian system
({\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$},{\rm h})
.
As in the Lagrangian formalism, if
\varphi\colon Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
is a diffeomorphism,
we can construct the
canonical lift
of
\varphi
to
{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}
,
which is the diffeomorphism

\Phi:=({\rm T}^{*}\varphi,{\rm{\rm Id}_{\mathbb{R}}})\colon{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}
.
and is called a
natural transformation
on
{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}
.
In the same way, for every vector field
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)

we define its
complete lift

to
{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}
as the vector field

Y\in{\rm X}({\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R})

whose local flux is the canonical lift of
the local flux of
Z
to
{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}
.
It is called a
natural infinitesimal transformations
on
{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}
.

The canonical forms
\Theta
and
\Omega=-{\rm d}\Theta

in
{\rm T}^{*}Q
and their extensions to
{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}
are invariant under the action
of canonical lifts of diffeomorphisms and vector fields from
Q
to
{\rm T}^{*}Q
and
{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}
.
Then, taking into account the definition
of the contact form
\eta
in
{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}
, we have:

Proposition 6.57

If

\Phi\in{\rm Diff}({\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R})
(resp.
Y\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R})
) is a canonical lift to
{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}
of a diffeomorphism
\varphi\in{\rm Diff}(Q)

(resp. of
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
), then

1.

\Phi^{*}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}
(resp
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=0
).

2.

If, in addition,

\Phi^{*}{\rm h}={\rm h}
(resp.
\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y){\rm h}=0
),
then it is a (infinitesimal) contact Noether symmetry.

In particular, we have the following:

Theorem 6.58

(Momentum dissipation theorem).

If
\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\rm h}}{\partial q^{i}}=0
,
then
\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial q^{i}}

is an infinitesimal contact Noether symmetry,
and its associated dissipated quantity
is the corresponding momentum
p_{i}
;
that is,

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{\rm h})p_{i}=-(\mathcal{R}({\rm h}))\,p_{i}
.

(
Proof
)
A simple computation in local coordinates shows that

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial q^{i}}\Big)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=0

and

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial q^{i}}\Big)=0
.
Therefore,
\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial q^{i}}

is a contact Noether symmetry and, hence, a dynamical symmetry.
The other results are a consequence of the dissipation theorem.

6.6
Examples

Finally, we will consider the systems studied as examples in the previous chapters,
incorporating a standard dissipation term that accounts for dissipative forces proportional to velocity.

6.6.1
The damped harmonic oscillator

Consider a harmonic oscillator in a medium with friction.
As in Section
3.7.1
,
the configuration bundle of the system is
Q=\mathbb{R}
but, in order to develop the contact formulation, we take the manifold
Q\times\mathbb{R}
. with coordinates
(t,q)
.

Lagrangian formalism

The Lagrangian description of the one-dimensional damped harmonic oscillator is done in the phase space

{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}\simeq=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}
.
It is described by the hyperregular contact Lagrangian function,

{\cal L}=\frac{1}{2}mv^{2}-\frac{1}{2}kq^{2}-\gamma s\ ,

where
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
is the dissipation parameter.

We have the contact Lagrangian form,

\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal L}={\rm d}s-v{\rm d}q\ ,

and the energy Lagrangian function is

E_{\cal L}=\frac{1}{2}mv^{2}+\frac{1}{2}kq^{2}+\gamma s\ .

For
\displaystyle X_{\cal L}=f\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q}}+F\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v}}+g\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{s}}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R})
,
the contact Lagrangian equations (
6.31
)
and (
6.32
) are

g=\frac{1}{2}mv^{2}-\frac{1}{2}kq^{2}-\gamma s\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptf=v\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptmF=-kq-\gamma mv\ .

whose solution is the
sode
vector field,

{\rm X}_{\cal L}=v\frac{\partial}{\partial q}-\left(\frac{k}{m}\,q+\gamma v\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial v}+\left(\frac{1}{2}mv^{2}-\frac{1}{2}kq^{2}-\gamma s\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\ .

Its integral curves are the solutions to equations (
6.25
) and (
6.26
),
which for this system give the Herglotz–Euler–Lagrange equations

\frac{ds}{dt}=\frac{1}{2}m\left(\frac{dq}{dt}\right)^{2}-\frac{1}{2}kq^{2}-\gamma s\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{d^{2}q}{dt^{2}}=-\frac{k}{m}\,q-\gamma\,\frac{dq}{dt}\ ,

(6.47)

and the last equation correspond to the well-known dynamical equation of the damped harmonic oscillator.

The dissipation of the energy is given by
(
6.16
), which in the Lagrangian formalism reads

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{\cal L})E_{\cal L}=-\gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}mv^{2}+\frac{1}{2}kq^{2}+\gamma s\right)\ .

Hamiltonian formalism

For the canonical Hamiltonian formalism of the system,

{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}\simeq=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}
,
and the Legendre map is

\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}^{*}q=q\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}^{*}p=mv\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}^{*}s=s\ .

The canonical contact form is
\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}={\rm d}s-p\,{\rm d}q
, and
the canonical Hamiltonian function reads

{\rm h}=\frac{p^{2}}{2m^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}kq^{2}+\gamma s\ .

For
\displaystyle X_{\rm h}=f\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q}}+F\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p}}+g\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{s}}
,
the contact Hamiltonian equations (
6.34
) give

f=\frac{p}{m}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptF=-kq-\gamma p\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptg=\frac{p^{2}}{2m^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}kq^{2}-\gamma s\ ,

and its solution is the Hamiltonian vector field,
