{\rm X}_{\rm h}=\frac{p}{m}\frac{\partial}{\partial q}-\left(kq+\gamma p\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial p}+\left(\frac{p^{2}}{2m^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}kq^{2}-\gamma s\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\ ,

whose integral curves are the solutions to equations (
6.35
),
which for this system give the Hamilton equations,

\displaystyle\frac{dq}{dt}=\frac{p}{m}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\displaystyle\frac{dp}{dt}=-kq-\gamma p\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\displaystyle\frac{ds}{dt}=\frac{p^{2}}{2m^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}kq^{2}-\gamma s\ .

(6.48)

Finally, the dissipation of the energy is given by (
6.46
),

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{\rm h}){\rm h}=-\gamma\left(\frac{p^{2}}{2m^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}kq^{2}+\gamma s\right)\ .

Unified Lagrangian-Hamiltonian formalism

The extended unified bundle is
\mathfrak{M}={\rm T}Q\times_{Q}{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{4}

with coordinates
(q,v,p,s)
.
Then we have the precontact Hamiltonian system

(\mathfrak{M},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\mathfrak{M}},{\bf H})
,
where the canonical contact form is

\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\mathfrak{M}}={\rm d}s-p\,{\rm d}q\ ,

the Hamiltonian function is

{\bf H}=pv-\frac{1}{2}mv^{2}+\frac{1}{2}kq^{2}+\gamma s\ .

and we can take
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{s}}
as Reeb vector field.
For this system, the compatibility condition for equations (
6.37
) leads to define the submanifold
\mathfrak{M}_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{M}

defined by

\mathfrak{M}_{0}=\{(q,v,p,s)\in\mathfrak{M}\,\mid\,p-mv=0\}\ ,

and the Hamiltonian vector field solution to (
6.37
) on
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
is

{\rm X}_{\bf H}|_{\mathfrak{M}_{0}}=v\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q}}+F\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v}}-\left(kq+\gamma p\right)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p}}+\left(\frac{p^{2}}{2m^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}kq^{2}-\gamma s\right)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{s}}\ .

The tangency condition for
{\rm X}_{\bf H}
on
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
gives

\displaystyle F=-\frac{k}{m}q-\gamma\frac{p}{m}
,
and thus finally,

{\rm X}_{\bf H}|_{\mathfrak{M}_{0}}=v\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q}}-\left(\frac{k}{m}q+\gamma\frac{p}{m}\right)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v}}-\left(kq+\gamma p\right)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p}}+\left(\frac{p^{2}}{2m^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}kq^{2}-\gamma s\right)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{s}}\ .

Therefore, the integral curves of
{\rm X}_{\bf H}
are the solutions to

\frac{dq}{dt}=v\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptm\frac{dv}{dt}=-mkq-\gamma p\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dp}{dt}=-kq-\gamma p\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{ds}{dt}=\frac{p^{2}}{2m^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}kq^{2}-\gamma s\ .

Te first two equations of this system are equivalent to the
second Herglotz–Euler–Lagrange equation
(
6.47
)
and, using the constraint
p=mv
(the Legendre map),
the first and third equations are the first pair of the Hamilton equations (
6.48
).

6.6.2
The Kepler problem with friction

Consider the motion of a massive particle (of mass
m
)
under the action of Newtonian central force
in a stellar media with friction.
As in the previous cases, the motion is on a plane; hence

Q=\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and we take
(r,\phi,s)
as coordinates in
Q\times\mathbb{R}
,
(with the origin of
r
at the center of the force).

Lagrangian formalism

The Lagrangian formalism takes place in
{\rm T}Q\times\mathbb{R}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{5}
, with local coordinates

(r,\phi,v_{r},v_{\phi},s)

The contact Lagrangian function that describes the dynamics is

{\cal L}=\frac{1}{2}m(v_{r}^{2}+r^{2}v_{\phi}^{2})-\frac{K}{r}-\gamma s\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptK\not=0\ ;

which is regular as in the above situations.
The energy Lagrangian function and the contact Lagrangian form are

\displaystyle E_{\cal L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}m(v_{r}^{2}+r^{2}v_{\phi}^{2})+\frac{K}{r}+\gamma s\ ,

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm d}s-m(v_{r}\,{\rm d}r+r^{2}v_{\phi}\,{\rm d}\phi)\ ,

and the Lagrangian is regular.
For
\displaystyle{\rm X}_{\cal L}=f_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{r}}+f_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}+g_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{r}}}+g_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{\phi}}}+g\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{s}}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R})
,
the contact Lagrangian equations (
6.31
)
and (
6.32
) give

\displaystyle g=\frac{1}{2}m(v_{r}^{2}+r^{2}v_{\phi}^{2})-\frac{K}{r}-\gamma s\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptf_{r}=v_{r}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptf_{\phi}=v_{\phi}\ ,

\displaystyle mg_{r}=2mrv_{\phi}f_{\phi}-mrv_{\phi}^{2}+\frac{K}{r^{2}}-\gamma mv_{r}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptg_{\phi}=-\frac{2v_{\phi}f_{r}}{r}-\gamma r^{2}v_{\phi}\ ,

and the contact Euler–Lagrange vector field is

\displaystyle{\rm X}_{\cal L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{r}}+v_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}+\left(rv_{\phi}^{2}+\displaystyle\frac{K}{mr^{2}}-\gamma v_{r}\right)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{r}}}+\left(-\frac{2v_{\phi}v_{r}}{r}-\gamma v_{\phi}\right)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{\phi}}}

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{1}{2}m(v_{r}^{2}+r^{2}v_{\phi}^{2})-\frac{K}{r}-\gamma s\right)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{s}}\ .

Then, its integral curves are the solutions to equations (
6.25
) and (
6.26
) which read as

\displaystyle\frac{ds}{dt}=\frac{1}{2}m\left(\Big(\frac{dr}{dt}\Big)^{2}+r^{2}\Big(\frac{d\phi}{dt}\Big)^{2}\right)-\frac{K}{r}-\gamma s\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle m\frac{d^{2}r}{dt^{2}}=mr\Big(\frac{d\phi}{dt}\Big)^{2}+\frac{K}{r^{2}}-m\gamma v_{r}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{d^{2}\phi}{dt^{2}}=-\frac{2}{r}\,\frac{d\phi}{dt}\,\frac{dr}{dt}-\gamma\frac{d\phi}{dt}\ ,

(6.49)

and are the Herglotz–Euler–Lagrange equations for this system.

Similarly to the above example,
the dissipation of the energy reads

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{\cal L})E_{\cal L}=-\gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}m(v_{r}^{2}+r^{2}v_{\phi}^{2})+\frac{K}{r}+\gamma s\right)\ .

Furthermore, there exists a Lagrangian contact Noether symmetry
which is generated by the vector field
\displaystyle Y=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}
; in fact,
bearing in mind (
3.26
) and (
3.27
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}\right)\left({\rm d}s-m(v_{r}\,{\rm d}r+r^{2}v_{\phi}\,{\rm d}\phi)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}\right){\rm d}s-\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}\right)\left(m(v_{r}\,{\rm d}r+r^{2}v_{\phi}\,{\rm d}\phi)\right)=0\ ,

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)E_{\cal L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}m(v_{r}^{2}+r^{2}v_{\phi}^{2})+\frac{K}{r}+\gamma s\right)=0\ ,

and the corresponding dissipated quantity is the
angular momentum map

F=-\mathop{i}\nolimits\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}\right)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\cal L}=mr^{2}v_{\phi}\ .

Finally, using Corollary
6.54
,
we obtain that
\displaystyle\frac{F}{E_{\cal L}}
is a conserved quantity associated with
Y
.

Hamiltonian formalism

For the Hamiltonian formalism,
{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{5}
,
with local coordinates
(r,\phi,p_{r},p_{\phi},s)
. The Legendre transformation is,

\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}^{*}r=r\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}^{*}\phi=\phi\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}^{*}p_{r}=mv_{r}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}^{*}p_{\phi}=mr^{2}v_{\phi}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}^{*}s=s\ ,

which is a diffeomorphism, and the Lagrangian is hyperregular.
The canonical Hamiltonian function and the canonical contact form are

\displaystyle{\rm h}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{p_{r}^{2}}{2m}+\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{2mr^{2}}+\frac{K}{r}+\gamma s

\eta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm d}s-p_{r}\,{\rm d}r-p_{\phi}\,{\rm d}\phi\ .

For
\displaystyle{\rm X}_{\rm h}=F_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{r}}+F_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}+G_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{r}}}+G_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{\phi}}}+g\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{s}}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R})
,
the contact Hamiltonian equations (
6.34
) lead to

F_{r}=\frac{p_{r}}{m}\ ,\ F_{\phi}=\frac{p_{\phi}}{mr^{2}}\ ,\ G_{r}=\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{mr^{3}}+\frac{K}{r^{2}}-\gamma p_{r}\ ,\ G_{\phi}=-\gamma p_{\phi}\ ,\ g=\frac{p_{r}^{2}}{2m}+\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{2mr^{2}}-\frac{K}{r}-\gamma s\ .

and then, the contact Hamiltonian vector field is

{\rm X}_{\rm h}=\frac{p_{r}}{m}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{r}}+\frac{p_{\phi}}{mr^{2}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}+\left(\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{mr^{3}}+\frac{K}{r^{2}}-\gamma p_{r}\right)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{r}}}-\gamma p_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{\phi}}}+\left(\frac{p_{r}^{2}}{2m}+\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{2mr^{2}}-\frac{K}{r}-\gamma s\right)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{s}}\ .

Hence, its integral curves are the solutions to the equations (
6.35
) which are

\displaystyle m\frac{dr}{dt}=p_{r}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptmr^{2}\frac{d\phi}{dt}=p_{\phi}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dp_{r}}{dt}=\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{mr^{3}}+\frac{K}{r^{2}}-\gamma p_{r}

,

\displaystyle\frac{dp_{\phi}}{dt}=-\gamma p_{\phi}\hskip 10.00002pt,

(6.50)

\displaystyle\frac{ds}{dt}=\frac{p_{r}^{2}}{2m}+\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{2mr^{2}}-\frac{K}{r}-\gamma s

,

and are the Hamiltonian equations for this system.

As it is usual, using the Legendre map
one can easily check that
\mathfrak{F}{\cal L}_{*}X_{\cal L}=X_{\rm h}
.

Similarly to the above example,
the dissipation of the energy reads

\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits({\rm X}_{\rm h}){\rm h}=-\gamma\left(\frac{p_{r}^{2}}{2m}+\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{2mr^{2}}+\frac{K}{r}+\gamma s\right)\ .

In addition, we have a Hamiltonian contact Noether symmetry which is again the
vector field
\displaystyle Y=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}
, since

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}\right)\left({\rm d}s-p_{r}\,{\rm d}r-p_{\phi}\,{\rm d}\phi\right)=0\ ,

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits(Y){\rm h}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm L}\nolimits\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}\right)\left(\frac{p_{r}^{2}}{2m}+\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{2mr^{2}}+\frac{K}{r}+\gamma s\right)=0\ .

The associated dissipated quantity is again the angular momentum,

F=-\mathop{i}\nolimits\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}\right)\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=p_{\phi}\ .

and the corresponding dissipation law is given directly by the last Hamilton equation in (
6.50
).
As above,
\displaystyle\frac{F}{{\rm h}}
is a conserved quantity associated with
Y
.

Unified Lagrangian-Hamiltonian formalism

In the precontact extended unified bundle

\mathfrak{M}={\rm T}Q\times_{Q}{\rm T}^{*}Q\times\mathbb{R}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{7}
,
with coordinates
(r,\phi,v_{r},v_{\phi},p_{r},p_{\phi},s)
,
the canonical precontact form and the Hamiltonian function are

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}_{\mathfrak{M}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm d}s-p_{r}\,{\rm d}r-p_{\phi}\,{\rm d}\phi\ ,

\displaystyle{\bf H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{r}v_{r}+p_{\phi}v_{\phi}-\frac{1}{2}m(v_{r}^{2}+r^{2}v_{\phi}^{2})+\frac{K}{r}+\gamma s\ ,

and we can take
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{s}}
as Reeb vector field.

For
\displaystyle{\rm X}_{\bf H}=f_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{r}}+f_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}+g_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{r}}}+g_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{\phi}}}+G_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{r}}}+G_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{\phi}}}+g\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{s}}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{M})
,
equations (
6.37
) lead to

\displaystyle f_{r}=v_{r}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptf_{\phi}=v_{\phi}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptG_{r}=\frac{K}{r^{2}}+mrv_{\phi}^{2}-\gamma p_{r}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptG_{\phi}=-\gamma p_{\phi}

,

\displaystyle p_{r}=mv_{r}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptp_{\phi}=mr^{2}v_{\phi}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002ptg=\frac{p_{r}^{2}}{2m}+\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{2mr^{2}}-\frac{K}{r}-\gamma s

.

(6.51)

The first two equations in (
6.51
) are constraints defining the submanifold

\mathfrak{M}_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{M}
which give the Legendre map.
Thus, the Hamiltonian vector field is

\displaystyle{\rm X}_{\bf H}|_{\mathfrak{M}_{0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{r}}+v_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}+g_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{r}}}+g_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{\phi}}}+\left(\frac{K}{r^{2}}+mrv_{\phi}^{2}-\gamma p_{r}\right)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{r}}}-\gamma p_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{\phi}}}

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{p_{r}^{2}}{2m}+\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{2mr^{2}}-\frac{K}{r}-\gamma s\right)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{s}}\ ,

and the tangency condition of
{\rm X}_{\bf H}
on
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
leads to obtain that, finally,

\displaystyle{\rm X}_{\rm H}|_{\mathfrak{M}_{0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v_{r}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{r}}+v_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\phi}}+\left(rv_{\phi}^{2}+\displaystyle\frac{K}{mr^{2}}-\gamma\frac{p_{r}}{m}\right)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{r}}}-\left(\frac{2v_{r}v_{\phi}}{r}+\gamma\frac{p_{\phi}}{m}\right)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v_{\phi}}}

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{K}{r^{2}}+mrv_{\phi}^{2}-\gamma p_{r}\right)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{r}}}-\gamma p_{\phi}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{\phi}}}+\left(\frac{p_{r}^{2}}{2m}+\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{2mr^{2}}-\frac{K}{r}-\gamma s\right)\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{s}}\ .

Therefore, the integral curves of
{\rm X}_{\bf H}
,
on
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
, are the solutions to

\displaystyle\frac{dr}{dt}=v_{r}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{d\phi}{dt}=v_{\phi}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dv_{r}}{dt}=\frac{K}{mr^{2}}+rv_{\phi}^{2}-\gamma\frac{p_{r}}{m}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dv_{\phi}}{dt}=-\frac{2v_{r}v_{\phi}}{r}-\gamma\frac{p_{\phi}}{m}

,

(6.52)

\displaystyle\frac{dp_{r}}{dt}=\frac{K}{r^{2}}+mrv_{\phi}^{2}-\gamma p_{r}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{dp_{\phi}}{dt}=-\gamma p_{\phi}\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{ds}{dt}=\frac{p_{r}^{2}}{2m}+\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{2mr^{2}}-\frac{K}{r}-\gamma s

.

(6.53)

Equations (
6.52
) are equivalent to the Herglotz–Euler–Lagrange equation (
6.49
),
and using the constraints
p_{r}=mv_{r}

and
p_{\phi}=mr^{2}v_{\phi}
; that is, the Legendre map,
the first and second equations in (
6.52
) and (
6.53
) are
the Hamiltonian equations (
6.50
) of the system.

Appendix A
Additional contents

A.1
Tangent and cotangent bundles

(For a detailed account of these subjects and the proof of the results, see for example,
[
1
,
11
,
97
,
228
,
241
,
290
,
295
,
330
,
357
]
).

A.1.1
The tangent bundle of a manifold. Canonical lifts

Let
Q
be a differentiable manifold and
q\in Q
.
Every differentiable curve

\gamma\colon(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\subset\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q

passing through
q
; that is,

\gamma(0)=q
,
induces a derivation
{\rm D}_{\gamma}
in
{\rm C}^{\infty}(Q)
,
in the following way

\begin{array}[]{cccc}{\rm D}_{\gamma}\colon&{\rm C}^{\infty}(Q)&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&\mathbb{R}\\
&f&\mapsto&\displaystyle\lim_{t\mapsto 0}\frac{(f\circ\gamma)(t)-(f\circ\gamma)(0)}{t}\end{array}\hskip 10.00002pt.

In the set of such differentiable curves we can define the following equivalence relation

\gamma_{1}\sim\gamma_{2}\hskip 10.00002pt\Longleftrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt{\rm D}_{\gamma_{1}}={\rm D}_{\gamma_{2}}\ .

Then:

Definition A.1

1.

A
tangent vector
to
Q
at
q

is every equivalence class defined by this relation.

2.

The
tangent space
of
Q
at
q
,
denoted by
{\rm T}_{q}Q
, is the vector space of all the tangent vectors
to
Q
at
q
.

3.

The
tangent bundle
of
Q
is defined as

\displaystyle{\rm T}Q:=\bigcup_{q\in Q}{\rm T}_{q}Q
.
We denote
\tau_{Q}\colon{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
its natural projection.

Every point
{\rm p}\in{\rm T}Q
is a couple

(q,v)\equiv v_{q}
, where
q=\tau_{Q}({\rm p})\in Q

and
v\in{\rm T}_{q}Q
(it is a tangent vector).

Proposition A.2

The tangent bundle
{\rm T}Q
is a
2n
-dimensional differentiable manifold whose differentiable structure is inherited from
Q
.
In addition, the natural projection
\tau_{Q}
is a submersion.

(
Proof
)
If
{\cal A}=\{(U_{\alpha};\phi_{\alpha})\}
, with
\phi_{\alpha}\equiv({x}^{1},\ldots,{x}^{n})
,
is an atlas of local charts on
Q
,
then the induced atlas on
{\rm T}^{*}Q
is

{\rm T}{\cal A}=\{(\tau_{Q}^{-1}U_{\alpha};\psi_{\alpha})\}
,
with the coordinate functions
\psi_{\alpha}
defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\psi_{\alpha}&\colon&\tau^{-1}_{Q}(U_{\alpha})&\longrightarrow&\phi_{\alpha}(U_{\alpha})\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2n}\\
&&{\rm p}=(q,v)&\mapsto&(q^{i}(p),v^{i}({\rm p}))\end{array}\hskip 10.00002pt;

where
i=1,\ldots,n
, and:

1.

q^{i}({\rm p})=(x^{i}\circ\tau_{Q})({\rm p})

1
1

1

It is usual to commit an abuse of notation denoting by
q^{i}

both the coordinates in the base manifold
Q

and in the tangent bundle
{\rm T}Q
, and we will do in the sequel.
.

2.

If
\displaystyle v=\lambda^{j}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x^{j}}}\Big|_{q}
, then

v^{i}(p)=\lambda^{i}=v(x^{i})
;
that is,
v^{i}(p)
are the components of the tangent vector

v\in{\rm T}_{q}Q
in the basis
\displaystyle\left\{\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x^{i}}}\Big|_{q}\right\}
.

It is obvious that
\displaystyle{\rm T}Q=\bigcup_{\alpha}\tau^{-1}_{Q}(U_{\alpha})

and that
\psi_{\alpha}
are diffeomorphisms.
Then, it is immediate to prove that
{\rm T}{\cal A}
endows
{\rm T}Q

with the structure of a differentiable manifold.
If
(U;q^{i})
and
(\bar{U};\bar{q}^{i})
are two local charts
in
Q
such that

U\cap\bar{U}\not=\hbox{\rm\O}
, and
\bar{q}^{j}=\varphi^{j}(q^{i})
in
U\cap\bar{U}
,
then for the induced charts
(\tau^{-1}_{Q}(U);q^{i},v^{i})
and

(\tau^{-1}_{Q}\bar{U});\bar{q}^{i},\bar{v}^{i})

in
{\rm T}Q
we have that, on
{\rm T}U\cap{\rm T}\bar{U}
,
the relation between the coordinates
v^{i}
and
\bar{v}^{i}
is

\displaystyle\bar{v}^{j}=\frac{\partial{\varphi^{j}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}v^{i}
, since

v=v^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\Big|_{q}=v^{i}\frac{\partial{\varphi^{j}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\bar{q}^{j}}}\Big|_{q}=\bar{v}^{j}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\bar{q}^{j}}}\Big|_{q}\ ,

and hence
\displaystyle\bar{v}^{j}=\frac{\partial{\varphi^{j}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}v^{i}
.

Bearing in mind this local description of the tangent bundle,
it is evident that
\dim\,{\rm T}Q=2n
.

Finally, it is immediate to prove that
\tau_{Q}
is an surjective submersion,
since the canonical projection

\tau_{Q}\colon{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
,
is a surjective map given, in natural coordinates, by

\tau_{Q}(q^{i},v^{i})=q^{i}
, and hence its tangent map

{\rm T}\tau_{Q}\colon{\rm T}{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm T}Q
is defined as follows:
if
(q,v)\in{\rm T}Q
and
X\in{\rm T}_{(q,v)}({\rm T}Q)

we have that

\displaystyle X=\lambda^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\Big|_{(q,v)}+\mu^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\Big|_{(q,v)}
, and

[{\rm T}_{(q,v)}\tau_{Q}(X)](q^{j})=X(q^{j}\circ\tau_{Q})=X(q^{j})=\lambda^{j}\ ,

therefore

({\rm T}\tau_{Q})((q,v),X)=({\rm T}\tau_{Q})\left((q,v),\lambda^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\Big|_{(q,v)}+\mu^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\Big|_{(q,v)}\right)=\left(q,\lambda^{i}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\Big|_{q}\right)\ ,

and the associated matrix is

(I_{n\times n},0_{n\times n})=\left(\begin{matrix}1&\ldots&0&0&\ldots&0\\
\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
0&\ldots&1&0&\ldots&0\end{matrix}\right)\ .

(A.1)

In this way, we conclude that
\tau_{Q}
is a surjective submersion.

Definition A.3

The above charts of the tangent bundle
are called
natural charts
and their coordinates
natural coordinates

(
q^{i}
are the
base coordinates

and
v^{i}
the
fiber coordinates
).

Observe that the coordinate change in
{\rm T}Q
,
from
(q^{i},v^{i})
to
(\bar{q}^{i},\bar{v}^{i})
has the Jacobian matrix

\left(\begin{matrix}\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial{\varphi^{j}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\right)&0\\
\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\varphi^{j}}{\partial q^{k}\partial q^{i}}\right)v^{i}&\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial{\varphi^{j}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\right)\end{matrix}\right)\ ,

and, taking into account that its determinant is positive at every point, we conclude that:

Corollary A.4

{\rm T}Q
is an orientable manifold.

The tangent bundle of a manifold is an example of a
vector bundle
.
This structure is defined as follows:

Definition A.5

A
vector bundle
is a triple
(E,B,\pi)
,
where
E,B
are differentiable manifolds
(with
\dim\,B=m
,
\dim\,E=m+n
) and
\pi\colon E\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>B

is a surjective submersion such that, for every
p\in B
,
there exists a local chart
(U,\phi)
,
p\in U
, and a diffeomorphism

\psi\colon\pi^{-1}(U)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\phi(U)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}

satisfying that:

1.

If
\pi_{1}\colon\phi(U)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\phi(U)

is the natural projection, then
\pi_{1}\circ\psi=\pi
.

2.

E_{p}=\pi^{-1}(p)
is a vector space and,
if
\pi_{2}\colon\phi(U)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}^{n}

is the natural projection, then the maps

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\psi_{p}&\colon&E_{p}&\longrightarrow&\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
&&v&\mapsto&(\pi_{2}\circ\psi)(p,v)\end{array}

are vector space morphisms.

E
is called the
total manifold
of the vector bundle,

B
is the
base manifold
,
\pi
the
projection of the bundle
,

\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the
typical fiber
,

n
is the
rank of the bundle
and, for every
p\in B
,

E_{p}
is the
fiber
over
p
.
The pair
(U,\phi)
is said to be a
trivializing open set

and
\psi
is the associated
coordinate map
.

Observe that the family
(\pi^{-1}(U),\psi)
is a differentiable atlas of
E
which is said to be
adapted
to the projection
\pi:E\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>B
.

There is a natural way to lift diffeomorphisms, curves and vector fields
on a manifold to its tangent bundle.

Definition A.6

Let
Q
be a differentiable manifold and a diffeomorphism

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\varphi&\colon&Q&\longrightarrow&Q\\
&&q&\mapsto&\varphi(q)\end{array}\ .

The
canonical lift
of
\varphi
to
{\rm T}Q

is the diffeomorphism

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}{\rm T}\varphi&\colon&{\rm T}Q&\longrightarrow&{\rm T}Q\\
&&(q,v)&\mapsto&(q,{\rm T}_{q}\varphi(v))\end{array}\ .

This definition also holds for every map
\varphi\colon Q\longrightarrow Q
.

The following properties are immediate from the definition:

Proposition A.7

For every
\varphi,\phi\in{\rm Diff}\,Q
,

1.

\varphi^{-1}\circ\tau_{Q}\circ{\rm T}\varphi=\tau_{Q}
.

2.

{\rm T}(\varphi\circ\phi)={\rm T}\varphi\circ{\rm T}\phi
.

Using the definition of canonical lift of diffeomorphisms, we set:

Definition A.8

Let
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
be a vector field.
The
total
,
complete
or
canonical lift
of
Z
to
{\rm T}Q

is the vector field
Z^{C}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}Q)

whose local uniparametric groups of diffeomorphisms are the
canonical lifts
\{{\rm T}F_{t}\}
of the
local uniparametric groups of diffeomorphisms

\{F_{t}\}
of
Z
.

As a direct consequence of the definition and of the theorem of existence and unicity of local uniparametric groups of diffeomorphisms,
we obtain the following result:

Proposition A.9

Let
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
.
Then
Z^{C}
is
\tau_{Q}
-projectable and
\tau_{Q*}Z^{C}=Z
;
that is,

{\rm T}\tau_{Q}(Z^{C}_{(q,v)})=Z_{x}
, for every
(q,v)\in{\rm T}Q
.

Local expression
:
In a chart of coordinates
(U;q^{i})
of
Q
, if

Z|_{U}=f^{i}(q^{j})\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\ ,

then we have that, in the induced chart of natural coordinates
(\tau_{Q}^{-1}(U);q^{i},v^{i})
of
{\rm T}Q
,

Z^{C}|_{\tau_{Q}^{-1}(U)}=f^{i}(q^{j})\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}+v^{k}\frac{\partial{f^{i}}}{\partial{q^{k}}}(q^{j})\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\ .

In order to prove it, remember that, if
(q,u)\in\tau_{Q}^{-1}(U)
, then

Z^{C}(q,u)=\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}{\rm T}F_{t}(q,u)\ ,

where
\displaystyle{\rm T}F_{t}(q^{i},v^{i})=\left(F_{t}(q^{i}),\frac{\partial{F_{t}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}v^{i}\right)
and,
as a consequence,

\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}{\rm T}F_{t}(q,u)=\left(\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}F_{t}(q),\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}\left(\frac{\partial{F_{t}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}v^{i}\right)(q,u)\right)=\left(f^{i}(q),v^{j}\frac{\partial{f^{i}}}{\partial{q^{j}}}(q^{k})\right)\ ,

and the result follows.

Let
\gamma\colon(a,b)\subseteq\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
be a curve.
If
t_{0}\in(a,b)
and
x=\gamma(t_{0})
,
then
\dot{\gamma}(t_{0})
is the tangent vector to the curve
at the point
\gamma(t_{0})
; that is,

\dot{\gamma}(t_{0})\in{\rm T}_{\gamma(t_{0})}Q
.
As
\dot{\gamma}(t)
is well-defined for every
t\in(a,b)
,
we can define:

Definition A.10

The
canonical lift
of a curve
\gamma
to
{\rm T}Q
is the curve

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\widetilde{\gamma}\colon&(a,b)\subset\mathbb{R}&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&{\rm T}Q\\
&t&\mapsto&(\gamma(t),\dot{\gamma}(t))\end{array}\ ,

which is defined as

(\gamma(t_{0}),\dot{\gamma}(t_{0}))={\rm T}_{t_{0}}\gamma\frac{d}{dt}\ ;

that is, for every
f\colon Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}
, then

(\gamma(t_{0}),\dot{\gamma}(t_{0}))f=({\rm T}_{t_{0}}\gamma\frac{d}{dt})f=\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t_{0}}(f\circ\gamma)=\lim_{h\mapsto 0}\frac{f(\gamma(t_{0}+h))-f(\gamma(t_{0}))}{h}\ .

Observe that
\tau_{Q}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}=\gamma
.

Local expression
:
If
(q^{i})
are local coordinates of
Q
in a neighbourhood of

\gamma(t_{0})
, then
\gamma=(\gamma^{1},\ldots,\gamma^{n})
,
with
\gamma^{i}=q^{i}\circ\gamma
.
If
(q^{i},v^{i})
are the natural induced coordinates in
{\rm T}Q
,
then
\widetilde{\gamma}
is given by

\widetilde{\gamma}=(\gamma^{1},\ldots,\gamma^{n},\dot{\gamma}^{1},\ldots,\dot{\gamma}^{n})
,
where
\displaystyle\dot{\gamma}^{i}=\frac{d\gamma^{i}}{dt}
.

The tangent vector to
\widetilde{\gamma}
at
\widetilde{\gamma}(t_{0})

is
\displaystyle{\rm T}_{t_{0}}\widetilde{\gamma}\frac{d}{dt}

and, if
f\colon{\rm T}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}
, we have that

\left({\rm T}_{t_{0}}\widetilde{\gamma}\frac{d}{dt}\right)f=\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t_{0}}f\circ\widetilde{\gamma}\ ,

and hence

\displaystyle\left({\rm T}_{t_{0}}\widetilde{\gamma}\frac{d}{dt}\right)q^{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t_{0}}q^{i}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}=\dot{\gamma}^{i}(t_{0})\ ,

\displaystyle\left({\rm T}_{t_{0}}\widetilde{\gamma}\frac{d}{dt}\right)v^{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t_{0}}v^{i}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}=\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t_{0}}\dot{\gamma}^{i}=\ddot{\gamma}^{i}(t_{0})\ ;

that is

{\rm T}_{t_{0}}\widetilde{\gamma}\Big(\frac{d}{dt}\Big)=\dot{\gamma}^{i}(t_{0})\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\Big|_{\widetilde{\gamma}(t_{0})}+\ddot{\gamma}^{i}(t_{0})\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{v^{i}}}\Big|_{\widetilde{\gamma}(t_{0})}\ .

Remark A.11

Remember that, given a vector field
X\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
,
a curve
\gamma\colon(a,b)\subseteq\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q

is an
integral curve
of
X
at
\gamma(t)\in Q
, for
t\in(a,b)
, if

X(\gamma(t))=\dot{\gamma}(t)\ .

Taking this into account, if
\beta\in{\Omega}^{k}(Q)
,
the contraction of
\beta
with
X
, denoted by
\mathop{i}\nolimits(X)\beta
, allows us to define a new contraction

\big(\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{\gamma})(\beta\circ\gamma)
in a natural way, as follows:

\mathop{i}\nolimits(\widetilde{\gamma}(t))\big(\beta(\gamma(t))\big):=\mathop{i}\nolimits\big(\dot{\gamma}(t)\big)(\beta|_{\gamma(t)})\hskip 10.00002pt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{\rm for $t\in(a,b)$}\ .

(A.2)

A.1.2
The cotangent bundle of a manifold. Canonical lifts

Definition A.12

Let
Q
be a differentiable manifold.
The
cotangent bundle
of
Q
is the dual bundle

{\rm T}^{*}Q
of the tangent bundle
{\rm T}Q
;
that is,
\displaystyle{\rm T}^{*}Q:=\bigcup_{q\in Q}{\rm T}^{*}_{q}Q
.

We denote
\pi_{Q}\colon{\rm T}^{*}Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
the natural projection.

Thus, every point
{\rm p}\in{\rm T}^{*}Q
is a pair

(q,\xi)\equiv\xi_{q}
, where
q=\pi_{Q}({\rm p})\in Q

and
\xi\in{\rm T}^{*}_{q}Q
(it is a linear form on
{\rm T}_{q}Q
).

Proposition A.13

Let
Q
be a
n
-dimensional differentiable manifold.
The cotangent bundle
{\rm T}^{*}Q
is a
2n
-dimensional differentiable manifold,
whose differentiable structure is induced by the one of
Q
in a natural way.
Moreover, the natural projection
\pi_{Q}
is a submersion.

(
Proof
)
If
{\cal A}=\{(U_{\alpha};\phi_{\alpha}=(x^{i})\}
is an atlas of local charts in
Q
,
then the induced atlas

{\rm T}^{*}{\cal A}=\{({\rm T}^{*}U_{\alpha};\eta_{\alpha}=(q^{i},p_{i})\}

is obtained as follows:

Given one of its elements
(U;x^{i})
, we take

1.

{\rm T}^{*}U:=\pi_{Q}^{-1}(U)
.

2.

For every
{\rm p}\in{\rm T}^{*}Q
, let
q^{i}({\rm p}):=x^{i}\circ\pi_{Q}({\rm p})
2
2

2

We commit an abuse of notation, denoting
by
q^{i}
both the coordinates
q^{i}
and
x^{i}
.
.

3.

Let
{\rm p}\in{\rm T}^{*}Q
with
{\rm p}=(q,\xi)
. Then
\displaystyle p_{i}({\rm p}):=\Big\langle\xi,\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\Big|_{q}\Big\rangle
; that is,
p_{i}({\rm p})
are the components of the linear form

\xi
in the natural basis
{\rm d}q^{i}\mid_{q}
of
{\rm T}^{*}_{q}M
:

\xi=p_{i}({\rm p})\,{\rm d}q^{i}\mid_{q}
.

If
(q^{i})
and
(\bar{q}^{i})
are two local systems of coordinates
in
U\subset Q
, and
\bar{q}^{j}=\phi^{j}(q^{i})
, then
the relation between
(p_{i})
and
(\bar{p}_{i})
is the following,

p_{i}(q,\xi)=\Big\langle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{q^{i}}},\xi\Big\rangle=\Big\langle\frac{\partial{\phi^{j}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\Big|_{q}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\bar{q}^{j}}},\xi\Big\rangle=\frac{\partial{\phi^{j}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\Big|_{q}\Big\langle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\bar{q}^{j}}},\xi\Big\rangle=\frac{\partial{\phi^{j}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\Big|_{q}\bar{p}_{j}(q,\xi)\ ;

that is,
\displaystyle p_{i}=\frac{\partial{\phi^{j}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\bar{p}_{j}
.
Then, the changes of coordinates in
{\rm T}^{*}Q
,
from
(q^{i},p_{i})
to
(\bar{q}^{i},\bar{p}_{i})
, have as Jacobian matrix

\left(\begin{matrix}\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial{\phi^{j}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\right)&0\\
\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\phi^{j}}{\partial q^{k}\partial q^{i}}\right)^{-1}p_{i}&\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial{\phi^{j}}}{\partial{q^{i}}}\right)^{-1}\end{matrix}\right)\ .

From this local description of the cotangent bundle
it is evident that its dimension is
2n
as we know previously being the dual bundle of
{\rm T}Q
.

Definition A.14

The above coordinate charts are called

natural charts
of the cotangent bundle
and their elements
natural coordinates

(
q^{i}
are the
base coordinates

and
p_{i}
the
fiber coordinates
).

Observe that the determinant of the above last matrix is the unity and this is the same for any change of natural coordinates, hence the manifold
{\rm T}^{*}Q
is also an orientable manifold.

In an analogous way as for the tangent bundle, there are
natural (canonical) ways to lift
diffeomorphisms and vector fields from a differentiable manifold
Q

to its cotangent bundle
{\rm T}^{*}Q
.

Definition A.15

Consider a diffeomorphism

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\varphi&\colon&Q&\longrightarrow&Q\\
&&x&\mapsto&\varphi(x)\end{array}\ .

The
canonical lift
of
\varphi
to
{\rm T}^{*}Q
is the diffeomorphism

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}{\rm T}^{*}\varphi&\colon&{\rm T}^{*}Q&\longrightarrow&{\rm T}^{*}Q\\
&&(\varphi(x),\xi)&\mapsto&(x,{\rm T}^{*}\varphi(\xi))\end{array}

where
{\rm T}^{*}\varphi(\xi)
is defined by duality as follows: if

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}{\rm T}\varphi&\colon&{\rm T}Q&\longrightarrow&{\rm T}Q\\
&&(q,v)&\mapsto&(x,{\rm T}\varphi(v))\end{array}

then, for every
v\in{\rm T}_{q}Q
,

({\rm T}^{*}\varphi(\xi))(v):=\xi({\rm T}\varphi(v))\ .

The following properties follow straightforwardly from the definition:

Proposition A.16

If
\varphi,\phi\colon Q\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Q
are diffeomorphisms, then:

1.

\varphi\circ\pi_{Q}\circ{\rm T}^{*}\varphi=\pi_{Q}
.

2.

{\rm T}^{*}(\varphi\circ\phi)={\rm T}^{*}\phi\circ{\rm T}^{*}\varphi
,

Definition A.17

Consider a vector field
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
.
The
canonical lift
of
Z
to
{\rm T}^{*}Q

is the vector field
Z^{*}\in\mathfrak{X}({\rm T}^{*}Q)

whose local uniparametric groups of diffeomorphisms
are the canonical lifts
{\rm T}^{*}F_{t}

of the local uniparametric groups of diffeomorphisms

F_{t}
of
Z
.

As a straightforward consequence of the definition and of the
theorem of existence and unicity of uniparametric groups
of diffeomorphisms, we have that:

Proposition A.18

If
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
, then
Z^{*}
is
\pi_{Q}
-projectable and,
for every
{\rm p}\equiv(q,\xi)\in{\rm T}^{*}Q
,
we have that
{\rm T}\pi_{Q}(Z^{*}_{\rm p})=Z_{q}
.

The coordinate expression of the canonical lift of a vector field to
{\rm T}^{*}Q
is given in (
3.3
).

A.2
Lie groups and Lie algebras

(For more information about definitions and properties of Lie groups and Lie algebras, see,
for instance,
[
11
,
108
,
290
,
336
,
357
]
).

Definition A.19

A (finite-dimensional)
Lie group

is a (finite-dimensional) differentiable manifold
G
such that

(i)

G
is a group and

(ii)

the following group operations are smooth,

\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}G\times G&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&G&\quad,&G&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&G\\
(g_{1},g_{2})&\mapsto&g_{1}g_{2}&\quad,&g&\mapsto&g^{-1}\end{array}\ .

Definition A.20

Let
G
be a Lie group. A subset
H\subset G
.
is a
Lie subgroup
of
G
if

(i)

H
is a subgroup of
G
and

(ii)

H
has a differentiable structure with respect
to which it is a submanifold of
G
and is a Lie group.

Given a Lie group
G
and an element
g\in G
,
we can define the diffeomorphisms

\begin{array}[]{ccccccccc}L_{g}\colon&G&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&G&\ ,&R_{g}\colon&G&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&G\\
&g^{\prime}&\mapsto&gg^{\prime}&\ ,&&g^{\prime}&\mapsto&g^{\prime}g\end{array}\ ,

which are the so-called
left
and
right translations

respectively, and satisfy the following properties:

1.

For every
g,g^{\prime}\in G
;
L_{g}\circ L_{g^{\prime}}=L_{g}L_{g^{\prime}}
,
R_{g}\circ R_{g^{\prime}}=R_{g}R_{g^{\prime}}
.

2.

For every
g\in G
;
L_{g}^{-1}=L_{g^{-1}}
,
R_{g}^{-1}=R_{g^{-1}}
.

3.

For every
g,g^{\prime}\in G
;

L_{g}\circ R_{g^{\prime}}=R_{g^{\prime}}\circ L_{g}
.

These translations also induce translations on the set of
vector fields
\mathfrak{X}(G)
, which are denoted by

L_{g_{*}}
and
R_{g_{*}}
, respectively. Then,

Definition A.21

A vector field
X\in\mathfrak{X}(G)
is

left
(resp.
right
)
invariant

if, for every
g\in G
,

L_{g_{*}}X=X\hskip 10.00002pt(resp.\ R_{g_{*}}X=X)\ .

The sets of these vector fields are denoted

\mathfrak{X}_{L}(G)
and
\mathfrak{X}_{R}(G)
respectively.

Proposition A.22

The sets
\mathfrak{X}_{L}(G)
and
\mathfrak{X}_{R}(G)
are Lie subalgebras of
\mathfrak{X}(G)
.

(
Proof
)
The sum of invariant vector fields is also invariant,
and the same thing holds for the Lie bracket,
since, for every
g\in G
and
X_{1},X_{2}\in\mathfrak{X}_{L}(G)
(or
X_{1},X_{2}\in\mathfrak{X}_{R}(G)
),

L_{g_{*}}[X_{1},X_{2}]=[L_{g_{*}}X_{1},L_{g_{*}}X_{2}]=[X_{1},X_{2}]\ .

Definition A.23

The set
\mathfrak{X}_{L}(G)
is the

Lie algebra
of
G
and is denoted by
{\bf g}
.

Proposition A.24

If
e\in G
is the neutral element of
G
, then there is a
canonical vector space isomorphism between

\mathfrak{X}_{L}(G)
and
{\rm T}_{e}G
.

(
Proof
)
To every
X\in\mathfrak{X}_{L}(G)
corresponds a vector of

{\rm T}_{e}G
by means of the isomorphism

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\rho_{1}\colon&\mathfrak{X}_{L}(G)&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&{\rm T}_{e}G\\
&X&\mapsto&X(e):=X_{e}\end{array}\ .

Conversely, from every
X_{e}\in{\rm T}_{e}G
,
we can obtain a unique vector field
X\in\mathfrak{X}_{L}(G)

using the map

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}X&\colon&G&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&{\rm T}G\\
&&g&\mapsto&(g,{\rm T}L_{g}X_{e})\end{array}\ .

which is a vector field since, for every
f\in{\rm C}^{\infty}(G)
,
the map
X(f)\colon G\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}

is smooth and, hence, so is
X
.
Moreover,
X
is left-invariant, as you can easily see
taking into account its construction from
X_{e}
.
The map giving
X
from
X_{e}
is denoted
\rho_{2}\colon{\rm T}_{e}G\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathfrak{X}_{L}(G)
.

•

Thus, it is usual to identify
{\bf g}
with
{\rm T}_{e}G
, and then
the Lie bracket between two vectors of
{\rm T}_{e}G
is defined as

[X_{e},X_{e}^{\prime}]:=[\rho_{2}X_{e},\rho_{2}X_{e}^{\prime}](e)\ .

Then, if
\{v_{i}\}
is a basis of
{\rm T}_{e}G
,
there exist
c_{ij}^{k}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

[v_{i},v_{j}]=c_{ij}^{k}v_{k}\ ,

which are called
structure constants

of the group
G
(related to the chosen basis of
{\bf g}
).
It can be proved that

c_{ij}^{k}=0
(for all
i,j,k
)
if, and only if,
G
is locally isomorphic to

\mathbb{R}^{n}
, that is,
G
is an
Abelian Lie group
.

•

As it is obvious, we can take
\mathfrak{X}_{R}(G)
instead of
\mathfrak{X}_{L}(G)

in order to define
{\bf g}
.
Then, the identification with
{\rm T}_{e}G

would be established in an analogous way,
although the Lie algebra structure so obtained in
{\rm T}_{e}G

is the opposite one to the above.
Nevertheless, both structures are isomorphic
since the map
-{\rm Id}_{{\rm T}_{e}G}
exchanges them.

Definition A.25

Let
{\bf g}
be a Lie algebra.
Then, we have the following sequence of ideals:

{\bf g}\supseteq{\bf g^{\prime}}\supseteq{\bf g^{\prime\prime}}\supseteq\ldots\supseteq{\bf g^{k}}\supseteq\ldots

where

{\bf g^{\prime}}:=[{\bf g},{\bf g}]\ ,\ {\bf g^{\prime\prime}}:=[{\bf g^{\prime}},{\bf g^{\prime}}]\ ,\ \ldots\ ,\ {\bf g^{k}}:=[{\bf g^{k-1}},{\bf g^{k-1}}]\ .

Then,
{\bf g^{k}}
is called the

k
th-derived algebra
of
{\bf g}
.

Observe that, if
{\bf g^{k}}=0
, then
{\bf g^{k+1}}=0
.
Then we define:

Definition A.26

Let
{\bf g}
be a Lie algebra.

1.

{\bf g}
is
Abelian
if
{\bf g^{\prime}}=0
.

2.

{\bf g}
is
solvable
if
{\bf g^{k}}=0
, for some
k>0
.

3.

Consider now the sequence
{\bf g}={\bf g_{1}}\supseteq{\bf g_{2}}\supseteq\ldots\supseteq{\bf g_{k}}\supseteq\ldots

where

{\bf g_{1}}:={\bf g}\ ,\ {\bf g_{2}}:=[{\bf g},{\bf g_{1}}]\ ,\ \ldots\ ,\ {\bf g_{k}}:=[{\bf g},{\bf g_{k-1}}]

{\bf g}
is
nilpotent
if
{\bf g_{k}}=\{0\}
, for some
k>0
.

4.

{\bf g}
is
simple
if it has no ideals other than
\{0\}
and
{\bf g}
.

5.

{\bf g}
is
semisimple
if its largest solvable ideal is
\{0\}
;
that is, if
{\bf g^{k}}={\bf g}
, for every
k
.

It is easy to prove that

{\bf g}\ {\rm abelian}\ \Rightarrow{\bf g}\ {\rm nilpotent}\ \Rightarrow{\bf g}\ {\rm solvable}\ ,

and that every non-Abelian simple Lie algebra
is semisimple.

Definition A.27

Let
{\bf g}
be a Lie algebra.
The
center
of
{\bf g}
is the set

\{X\in{\bf g}\ |\ [X,Y]=0\ ,Y\in{\bf g}\}\ ,

which is a commutative ideal of
{\bf g}
.

Definition A.28

A
p
-form
\beta\in{\Omega}^{p}(G)
is

left
(resp.
right
)
invariant

if, for every
g\in G
,

L_{g}^{*}\beta=\beta\hskip 10.00002pt(resp.\ R_{g}^{*}\beta=\beta)\ .

The set of the left-invariant
1
-forms
is the dual vector space of
\mathfrak{X}_{L}(G):={\bf g}

and is denoted by
{\bf g}^{*}
.

Proposition A.29

{\bf g}^{*}
is isomorphic to
{\rm T}_{e}^{*}G
.

(
Proof
)
It is obvious from the definition.

As a consequence, from a covector
\beta_{e}\in{\rm T}_{e}^{*}G
,
we can obtain another one at every point
g\in G

doing
L_{g^{-1}}^{*}\beta_{e}:=\beta_{g}
.
Then, the map

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}{\rm B}&\colon&G&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&{\rm T}^{*}G\\
&&g&\mapsto&(g,\beta_{g})\end{array}

is a left-invariant
1
-form.
Therefore, it is easy to prove the following properties:

Proposition A.30

1.

For every
g\in G
,
\beta\in{\bf g}^{*}
,
and
X,Y\in{\bf g}
, we have that:

(a)

{\rm d}\beta
is left-invariant.

(b)

\langle X_{g},\beta_{g}\rangle=\langle X_{e},\beta_{e}\rangle
;
that is,

\langle X,\beta\rangle=ctn.

(c)

{\rm d}\beta(X,Y)=-\langle[X,Y],\beta\rangle
.

2.

If
\{v_{i}\}
is a basis of
{\rm T}_{e}G
and

\{\gamma^{i}\}
is the dual basis in
{\rm T}_{e}^{*}G
, then

(a)

{\rm d}\gamma^{k}(v_{i},v_{j})=-c_{ij}^{l}<v_{l},\gamma^{k}>=-c_{ij}^{k}

(b)

The following equations holds

{\rm d}\gamma^{k}=-\frac{1}{2}c^{k}_{ij}\gamma^{i}\wedge\gamma^{j}

The numbers
c^{k}_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}
are called the

Maurer–Cartan structure equations

of the group
G
(related to the chosen basis of
{\bf g}^{*}
).

Definition A.31

The
canonical
or
Maurer–Cartan form

of the Lie group
G
is the
1
-form with values on
{\bf g}
,

\omega\in{\Omega}^{1}(G,{\bf g})
,
defined as follows:
for every
g\in G
and
X\in\mathfrak{X}(G)
,

\omega_{g}(X_{g}):={\rm T}L_{g}^{-1}X_{g}

Obviously,
\omega
is left-invariant.

Observe that, in the definition,
X\in{\cal X}(G)
.
In particular, if
X\in{\bf g}
,
(that is,
X
is left-invariant) then we have that

{\rm T}L_{g}^{-1}X_{g}=X_{e}
.
Then an alternative definition of
\omega
is the following:

Definition A.32

The
Maurer–Cartan form

of the Lie group
G
is the only left-invariant
1
-form
with values on
{\bf g}
,

\omega\in{\Omega}^{1}(G,{\bf g})
,
such that
\omega(X)=X
, for every
X\in{\bf g}
;
that is, it gives the identity one on
{\bf g}
.

Definition A.33

The
uniparametric subgroups
of
G

are the integral curves passing through
e

of the left (resp. right) invariant vector fields,
that is, the maps

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\alpha&\colon&\mathbb{R}&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&G\\
&&t&\mapsto&g_{t}:=\alpha(t)\end{array}

such that, if
X\in\mathfrak{X}_{L}(G)
, then

(i)

\displaystyle X_{g_{t_{0}}}=\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\alpha(t)\Big|_{t_{0}}
and
(ii)

\displaystyle X_{e}=\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\alpha(t)\Big|_{0}
.

These uniparametric subgroups are complete and,
there exists a bijective correspondence between

{\bf g}
and the set of uniparametric subgroups of
G
.
The map which implements this correspondence is defined as follows:

Definition A.34

The
exponential map

is defined as,

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}{\rm exp}&\colon&{\rm T}_{e}G&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&G\\
&&tX_{e}&\mapsto&g_{t}\end{array}

for
t\in\mathbb{R}
, and such that

{\rm exp}(0)=e
; that is, if
\alpha
is the uniparametric subgroup
corresponding to
\rho_{2}X_{e}
, then
(i)

{\rm exp}(X_{e}):=\alpha(1)
and
(ii)

{\rm exp}(0):=e
.

•

Notice that the exponential map maps
a point of the line
tX_{e}
on
{\rm T}_{e}G
into
a point of the integral curve of the vector field

\rho_{2}X_{e}
on
G
.

•

Taking into account that
\mathbb{R}
is a Lie group
with respect to the sum and that

\alpha\colon\mathbb{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>G

is a Lie group homomorphism, we have that

\displaystyle{\rm exp}(t_{1}X_{e}){\rm exp}(t_{2}X_{e})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{t_{1}}g_{t_{2}}=\alpha(t_{1})\alpha(t_{2})=\alpha(t_{1}+t_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{(t_{1}+t_{2})}={\rm exp}((t_{1}+t_{2})X_{e})\ .

Furthermore,

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}{\rm exp}(tX_{e})\Big|_{t_{0}}=\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\alpha(t)\Big|_{t_{0}}=X_{g_{t_{0}}}\ ,

and, in particular,

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}{\rm exp}(tX_{e})\Big|_{t=0}=X_{e}\ .

All these properties justify the name given to this map

3
3

3

Another justification is that,
if
V
is a vector space, and we consider

G={\rm Aut}(V)
, then

{\rm exp}\colon{\rm End}(V)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\rm Aut}(V)
and, for every
a\in{\rm End}(V)
, we obtain that

{\rm exp}(a)=e^{a}:={\bf 1}+a+a^{2}/2!+a^{3}/3!+\ldots+a^{n}/n!+\ldots

where
{\bf 1}
is the identity and

a^{n}:=a\circ\ldots\circ a
(
n
times).
.
Observe that the last two equalities are natural
from the definition, since

\alpha(t)={\rm exp}(tX_{e})

is the integral curve of
X
.
Hence, the flux of
X
is

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\tau&\colon&\mathbb{R}\times G&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}&G\\
&&(t,g)&\mapsto&g\,{\rm exp}(tX_{e})\ ;\end{array}

that is,

\tau_{t}(g)=g\,{\rm exp}(tX_{e})=R_{{\rm exp}(tX_{e})}g
.

•

The exponential map allows constructing locally the group
G

from the algebra
{\bf g}
.
